Psychiatric Disorders, Chapter 7

Final Textbook 3, Psychiatric Disorders, full-length edition: chapter 7 of 11. The book goes up chapter by chapter; the A4 colour and black and white PDFs come once all 11 chapters are in.

Chapter 7 Trauma, Dissociative and Somatic Symptom Disorders

In this chapter
  1. 7.1 Hysteria: The Common Root of Three Families of Disorder: trace how hysteria was described and explained from Hippocrates through Charcot, Janet, Breuer, Freud and Hilgard, and how DSM-III, ICD-10 and ICD-11 divided it into the trauma, dissociative and somatic families used today.
  2. 7.2 Posttraumatic and Acute Stress Disorders: Diagnosis and Clinical Picture: describe the history of posttraumatic stress disorder and apply the DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11 criteria for PTSD, acute stress disorder, complex PTSD and acute stress reaction, telling them apart from the conditions they resemble.
  3. 7.3 Posttraumatic Stress Disorder: Who Develops It, Why, and How It Is Treated: explain the epidemiology, course and risk factors of PTSD, evaluate its biological, psychological and social explanations, and compare its drug and psychological treatments and the idea of post-traumatic growth.
  4. 7.4 Adjustment Disorders, Prolonged Grief Disorder and the Attachment Disorders: distinguish adjustment disorder, prolonged grief disorder, normal bereavement, reactive attachment disorder and disinhibited social engagement disorder by trigger, timing and clinical picture, and state how each is managed.
  5. 7.5 Dissociation, Depersonalisation and Dissociative Amnesia: define dissociation, describe depersonalisation/derealisation disorder and the forms of dissociative amnesia and fugue, and tell dissociative amnesia apart from the organic amnesias.
  6. 7.6 Dissociative Identity Disorder, Possession and Trance: describe dissociative identity disorder, evaluate the trauma and sociocognitive models and the recovered memory debate, and classify possession, trance and Ganser states across cultures and diagnostic systems.
  7. 7.7 Somatic Symptom Disorder and Illness Anxiety Disorder: explain how the somatoform disorders became the somatic symptom and related disorders, diagnose somatic symptom disorder and illness anxiety disorder, and outline their causes and treatment.
  8. 7.8 Functional Neurological Symptom Disorder (Conversion Disorder): describe the motor, sensory and seizure-like symptoms of functional neurological symptom disorder, the positive signs that make the diagnosis, its causes and its treatment.
  9. 7.9 Factitious Disorder and Malingering: distinguish factitious disorder imposed on self or on another from malingering, conversion and the other somatic disorders by intention and motive, and describe how deception is recognised.

Weightage. 50 of the 285 questions mapped in this book (17.5%) test this chapter, 22 of them from real past papers. 3 of its 9 sections are High yield.

In the 1880s the most famous neurologist in Europe worked in an old hospital for women in Paris. Jean-Martin Charcot (1825 to 1893) had run a medical service at the Salpêtrière since the early 1860s, in the same hospital where Philippe Pinel had introduced psychological treatment several generations before, and he had made it the leading school of neurology in the world. He taught in two ways. There were formal lectures, carefully prepared and delivered each week in the hospital's amphitheatre, and there were informal "Tuesday lessons", impromptu examinations of patients in front of his students, in which he reasoned his way to a diagnosis out loud; the students' notes were later printed as the Leçons du mardi for the years 1887 to 1889. The subject that made Charcot famous far beyond medicine was hysteria. His demonstrations of hysterical and hypnotised patients became a kind of public theatre, drawing doctors, students, writers, politicians and curious visitors from Paris society.

The star of those demonstrations was a young woman named Marie Wittmann, known to everyone as Blanche. She had been admitted in the late 1870s, at the age of eighteen, and Paris came to know her as the "Queen of the Hysterics". Charcot began to hypnotise his hysterical patients in 1878, and Blanche passed through the three stages of what his school called grand hypnotism (lethargy, catalepsy and somnambulism) with textbook regularity. Her attacks followed the sequence the Salpêtrière described for the grand hysterical attack: an epileptoid phase that looked like a seizure, a phase of "clownism" with wild contortions and the body arched backwards like a bow, a phase of "passionate attitudes" in which she seemed to act out ecstasy, terror or desire, and a final phase of delirium. In demonstrations of this kind Charcot and his assistants showed that hysterical symptoms could be made and unmade: in a hypnotised patient a suggestion could stiffen a limb, paralyse it, or take the feeling out of a patch of skin, and another suggestion could give it back.

In 1887 the painter André Brouillet showed the scene at the Paris Salon on a canvas almost the size of a wall, A Clinical Lesson at the Salpêtrière. Charcot stands beside Blanche, lecturing to rows of men in dark coats. She leans back, eyes closed, one hand cramped, into the arms of his young assistant Joseph Babinski, while a nurse reaches out to receive her; on the back wall hangs a large drawing of a woman arched in the bow of the grand attack, as though announcing what the audience has come to see. (A recent study of the electrical instruments on Charcot's table suggests that she is shown in hypnotic lethargy rather than in an attack.) Sigmund Freud, who had studied with Charcot in 1885 and 1886, kept a print of the painting; it hangs today above his famous couch in the Freud Museum in London. And Charcot's lessons were not only about women. He published more than sixty case histories of hysteria in men, nearly all of them working men (masons, bakers, carpenters, railway and factory workers) whose paralyses and lost sensations had followed accidents that sometimes barely injured them.

Charcot died in 1893, and his teaching on hysteria did not long survive him. His critics, the Nancy physician Hippolyte Bernheim first and later Babinski himself, argued that much of what had been shown on the Salpêtrière stage was the product of suggestion and of staging, and in the decades that followed the grand attack, and the diagnosis of hysteria itself, became steadily rarer in the clinics. Blanche Wittmann later returned to the hospital, no longer as a patient but as an assistant, first to its photographer and then in its new X-ray service; an intern who arrived in 1905 recalled that by then she no longer had attacks. Look again at what the single word hysteria covered on Charcot's stage: convulsions and paralyses, trances and sleepwalking states, patches of numbness, and behind many of the patients, women and men alike, frightening and sometimes violent events. Today those same phenomena are divided among the three families of this chapter: functional neurological symptom disorder (the attacks and paralyses), the dissociative disorders (the trances and altered states of identity), and the trauma- and stressor-related disorders (the events that clinicians now ask about first). The stage itself teaches a lesson that the chapter returns to again and again, with Sybil, with the sociocognitive model of multiple personality and with factitious illness: attention and suggestion can shape symptoms. Remember Blanche Wittmann; you will return to her at the end of the chapter.

7.1 Hysteria: The Common Root of Three Families of DisorderMedium yield · 4 Qs

Picture three patients. A bus conductor relives a fatal accident every night and jumps at every horn. A young woman keeps finding clothes in her cupboard that she does not remember buying, and strangers greet her by a name she never uses. A schoolgirl's right arm goes limp after a quarrel at home, although every test of her nerves is normal. Why should one chapter hold all three? The first answer is historical. A century and a half ago all three would have been called hysterical, and the disorders you will meet in this chapter grew out of that one sprawling diagnosis. The second answer is clinical: the three families still travel together. Many people with dissociative disorders and with functional neurological symptoms report earlier trauma. Amnesia, flashbacks, emotional numbing and feelings of unreality are part of posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD) and acute stress disorder. DSM-5-TR notes that dissociative symptoms are common in functional neurological symptom disorder and tells clinicians to make both diagnoses when both are present. For these reasons DSM-5 placed its chapter on the dissociative disorders next to, but not inside, the chapter on trauma- and stressor-related disorders, and followed it with the chapter on somatic symptoms. The third answer is conceptual. In each family something that is normally held together comes apart under strain: a memory from the ordinary flow of remembering, a sense of self from the rest of the personality, or a bodily function from voluntary control.

This section tells the story of how hysteria was described, explained and finally broken up. The story earns its place for three practical reasons. Examiners ask "who said what" about it constantly. The old names still turn up in case files, in ICD-10 codes and in the words families use in the outpatient department. And each explanation, from the wandering womb to the hidden observer, survives in some form in modern theories of trauma, dissociation and bodily symptoms. Figure 7.1 lays out the journey you are about to take.

The word and the womb

The idea is older than the word. Egyptian physicians had noticed that some women developed fits, paralyses, pains or a choking feeling for which no bodily cause could be found, and they blamed the womb. The Greeks inherited the idea and gave it its name, for hysteria comes from hystera, the Greek word for the uterus. In the medical writings attributed to Hippocrates (about 460 to 377 BCE) and his followers, an empty womb was thought to move about the body in search of conception, and the symptom was thought to depend on where it came to rest; a womb rising towards the chest and throat was blamed above all for attacks of choking and breathlessness. This is the famous wandering womb. The cures followed from the theory: marriage and pregnancy, to give the womb what it wanted, or fumigation of the vagina with sweet-smelling substances to lure it back into place. The philosopher Plato (about 427 to 347 BCE) gave the image its most vivid form. In his dialogue the Timaeus he pictured the womb as a living creature that longs to bear children and, if it is left barren too long, roams through the body, blocking the passages of breath and causing all kinds of disease.

Historians add an important qualification. The classicist Helen King argued in 1993 that the Hippocratic texts describe many "diseases of women" blamed on the womb, but no single disease called hysteria; the noun and the unified diagnosis were assembled by later writers who read their own ideas back into the ancient texts. So when an examiner writes that Hippocrates coined the word hysteria (Barlow and Durand say exactly this), give that answer, but know that the history is less tidy. Book 2 Section 1.2.2 places the wandering womb within Hippocratic medicine as a whole. In medieval and early modern Europe the same fits, trances and patches of numbness were often read as signs of possession or witchcraft, and outbreaks of dancing mania spread through towns like an infection; Book 2 Sections 1.2.4 and 1.2.5 tell those stories.

The first great modern step came from Thomas Sydenham (1624 to 1689), the physician known as the English Hippocrates. In a treatise of 1682, written as a letter to a colleague, he called hysteria the commonest of all chronic diseases and observed that its symptoms could imitate almost every kind of organic disease. He also held that men suffered from it, a claim that sat awkwardly with a name meaning "womb". Those two observations, that hysteria is a great imitator and that it is not confined to women, run all the way to the modern concept of functional neurological symptoms. In 1859 the French physician Paul Briquet (1796 to 1881; some sources, among them French library catalogues and Barlow and Durand, give his first name as Pierre) published a large clinical treatise on hysteria; the pattern he described, many complaints in many organs, returning year after year, later carried his name as Briquet's syndrome and became somatisation disorder (Section 7.7 teaches it).

Two ideas fixed by the old name survived long after anatomy proved that a womb cannot wander. One was that hysteria belonged to women. The other was that it was something dramatic and theatrical, perhaps not quite real. Barlow and Durand note that the habit of dismissing dramatic women as "hysterical" continued well into the 1970s, when mental health professionals finally became sensitive to the prejudice the word carried. You will see later in this section that this is one of the reasons the word was dropped.

Exam trap

Hippocrates is linked with the word hysteria (hystera, womb) and with the wandering womb; the Egyptians had the idea before him, and Plato described the womb as a restless animal. Hippocrates is a distractor in questions on la belle indifférence (usually credited to Janet, Section 7.1.3). Briquet (1859) gave his name to Briquet's syndrome, the forerunner of somatisation disorder; Sydenham (1682) said hysteria could imitate almost any disease and occurred in men. Do not credit the wandering womb to Charcot or Freud: by their time hysteria had been moved into the nervous system and the mind, and both described it in men.

Charcot at the Salpêtrière: hysteria as a disease of the nerves

By the 1870s Jean-Martin Charcot (1825 to 1893) was the most respected neurologist in France. Working at the Salpêtrière, he had helped to define multiple sclerosis and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis by matching what he saw at the bedside with what he later found in the brain and spinal cord at post-mortem, the so-called anatomo-clinical method. When he turned to hysteria, from about 1870, he brought the same habits with him: patient observation, sorting of cases into types, photography, drawings and statistics. His central claim was bold for its time. Hysteria, he said, is a real disease of the nervous system. It is not malingering, not a disorder of the womb (he rejected any causal role for the reproductive organs) and not a moral failing. It arises in people born with a hereditary weakness of the nervous system when some provoking event, often a fright or a physical shock, sets it off. And because no damage could be found in the brains of hysterical patients at post-mortem, Charcot proposed that the disease lay in a dynamic lesion: a disturbance of the way the nervous system works, invisible to the microscope, rather than a change in its structure. Notice how modern that sounds. Neurologists today describe functional neurological disorders in almost the same words, as problems of nervous system function rather than structure (Section 7.8).

Charcot divided the signs of hysteria into two kinds. The permanent hysterical stigmata were present between attacks: loss of sensation, often over one whole half of the body; narrowing of the fields of vision; and exaggerated sensitivity of particular spots. Some of those spots were hysterogenic zones, points on the body where touching, stroking or pressing was said to set off an attack, and where firm pressure could stop one. In women the classic zone lay over the ovaries, and firm "ovarian compression" was one of the ways a fit was halted at the Salpêtrière; in men the zones could be almost anywhere, and the historian Mark Micale found them recorded in nearly half of Charcot's male patients. The paroxysms were the attacks themselves. The most dramatic form, grand hysteria (grande hystérie, also called hystero-epilepsy), was said to run through a fixed sequence that Charcot's pupil, the physician and artist Paul Richer (1849 to 1933), set out in drawings and in a book of 1881. After warning sensations came four phases: an epileptoid phase that resembled a true epileptic seizure; a phase of clownism, with huge acrobatic movements and the arc de cercle, the body arched backwards so far that only the head and heels touched the bed; a phase of passionate attitudes, in which the patient seemed to act out scenes of terror, ecstasy, anger or desire; and a final phase of delirium, sometimes with hallucinations. Charcot regarded the grand attack as a natural form of disease that had always existed, and with Richer he went looking for it in old paintings of "possessed" women and ecstatic saints, reading the demoniacs of earlier centuries as undiagnosed hysterics.

In 1878 Charcot began to hypnotise his hysterical patients, and here he made the observation that examiners ask about most. In a hypnotised patient, hysterical phenomena such as loss of feeling in a limb, paralysis, a rigid contracture or a faint could be produced by suggestion, and they could be removed again by suggestion. The doctor would tell the hypnotised woman that her arm was heavy and could not move, and it would not move; he would tell her that the feeling had returned, and it did. Think about what that demonstration implies. If a word can create a paralysis and another word can take it away, the paralysis cannot be the result of a fixed injury to the nerves. It must depend on an idea. Freud, who watched these demonstrations, later put the point memorably in a paper of 1893: in its paralyses, hysteria behaves as though anatomy did not exist. A hysterical anaesthesia stops where the patient imagines the hand or the arm to stop, not where a nerve's territory ends, a clue you will meet again as "glove anaesthesia" in Section 7.8.

Charcot drew a second conclusion from hypnosis, and this one turned out to be wrong. He believed that only hysterics could be deeply hypnotised, so that hypnotisability was itself a symptom of hysteria. The hypnotic state of a hysteric, which his school called grand hypnotism, was said to pass through three stages: lethargy, a limp, sleep-like state; catalepsy, in which the limbs stayed in whatever position they were placed; and somnambulism, a sleepwalking state in which the patient could talk, move and carry out suggestions. Blanche Wittmann, as you saw in the opening story, passed through the three stages with a regularity that made her the ideal demonstration patient, and that regularity is exactly what later critics found suspicious.

Charcot's most lasting contribution was to take hysteria out of the women's wards. Against the common belief that hysteria in men was rare, he insisted that it was frequent, and Micale counted 61 published case histories of male hysteria in his work. Almost all of these men came from the working class: masons, bakers, carpenters, plumbers, locksmiths, railway workers and factory hands. Many had fallen ill after an accident at work or on the railways, and Charcot noticed that the severity of the symptoms bore little relation to the size of the injury, which ranged from a train crash to a trivial cut on a finger. He called these cases traumatic hysteria, and in the 1880s he gave increasing weight to the "psychical shock" of the accident rather than the physical blow. In his last years he described the mechanism as a kind of autosuggestion: after a frightening event, an idea (my arm is crushed, my legs are finished) takes hold of a vulnerable mind and produces the symptom it imagines. In a Tuesday lesson of February 1888 he even called hysteria, according to the students' handwritten transcript, a "psychic disease par excellence", although the printed edition softened the phrase. Charcot the neurologist was moving, a little reluctantly, towards psychology.

A modern case shows how little the picture has changed. Hari, a 41-year-old railway trackman near Hubballi, is struck on the right shoulder by a slowly moving inspection trolley. He has a bruise and nothing more. Two days later he cannot lift his right arm at all; it hangs from the shoulder as if dead. His reflexes are normal, his nerve conduction studies and scan are normal, and the numbness he reports ends in a neat ring around the top of the shoulder, at a place where no nerve territory ends. He is worried about his job and about the family's loan. Charcot would have recognised him at once: a hysterical paralysis of one arm after a minor accident is exactly the kind of traumatic hysteria he described in railway workers. Today Hari would be assessed for functional neurological symptom disorder (Section 7.8), and because there is a compensation question the clinician would also have to keep malingering in mind (Section 7.9). Traumatic hysteria is also one of the ancestors of PTSD, through the "railway spine" and "traumatic neurosis" of the late nineteenth century, a lineage traced in Section 7.2.

Charcot's view of hypnosis was challenged from the provincial town of Nancy. There the country doctor Ambroise-Auguste Liébeault had for years treated patients by hypnotic suggestion, and the professor of medicine Hippolyte Bernheim (1840 to 1919) built a school on his methods. The Nancy school argued that hypnosis is not a sign of disease but an exaggeration of ordinary suggestibility, a capacity that most people possess, and that the stages and attacks seen at the Salpêtrière were produced by suggestion rather than revealed by it. Under pressure, the Salpêtrière school conceded a distinction between the "grand hypnotism" of hysterics and the "petit hypnotism" of ordinary people, but by the end of the century Nancy's view had largely won. Book 2 Sections 1.3.8 and 3.1.1 tell the dispute and its place in the birth of psychotherapy, and Book 2 Section 3.10.5 gives the modern definition of hypnosis.

The most damaging critic came from inside Charcot's own circle. Joseph Babinski (1857 to 1932), the assistant who catches Blanche Wittmann in Brouillet's painting, was still lecturing on hysteria in his master's style in 1891, but over the next decade he changed his mind completely. In 1901 he proposed replacing the word hysteria with pithiatism, built from Greek words meaning "persuasion" and "curable": disorders that can be reproduced by suggestion and cured by persuasion. In 1909 he published what he called the "dismemberment" of traditional hysteria, stripping away everything that could not be produced and removed in this way. Babinski also gave neurology one of its most useful tests, the sign that bears his name (1896): when the sole of the foot is stroked, the big toe turns upwards instead of curling down if the motor pathways from the brain are damaged. A hysterical paralysis does not produce the sign, so it became one of the first positive tools for telling an organic paralysis from a functional one. During the First World War, Babinski's pupil Clovis Vincent took the doctrine of persuasion to a brutal extreme, combining painful electric currents with commands to recover (a method called "torpillage", torpedoing) in soldiers with hysterical paralyses. You will meet those soldiers in Section 7.1.6.

Exam trap

"Charcot demonstrated that hysterical phenomena such as loss of feeling in limbs or fainting could be produced by" both hypnosis and suggestion (IHBAS 2024). The patient was hypnotised and the symptom was then created, and removed, by a suggestion, so neither option alone is complete. Relaxation is a different procedure: Edmund Jacobson's progressive muscle relaxation lowers arousal and produces no symptoms (Book 2 Section 4.8). Pair the names and places correctly: Charcot is the Salpêtrière in Paris; Bernheim and Liébeault are Nancy; Mesmer is animal magnetism; James Braid coined "hypnotism" (1843). Charcot thought hypnotisability was a sign of hysteria; Bernheim thought it was normal suggestibility. Pithiatism is Babinski's word, and the toe sign is Babinski's too.

Pierre Janet and the splitting of consciousness

Pierre Janet (1859 to 1947) came to hysteria as a philosopher. His doctoral thesis, L'automatisme psychologique (Psychological Automatism, 1889), was built on studies of patients with hysteria, and he went on to qualify in medicine and to work under Charcot at the Salpêtrière. Janet's question was simple to state and hard to answer. Suppose a woman's hand is numb to every pinprick, yet she buttons her blouse with it without looking. Suppose a man cannot remember the night his house burned, yet he trembles at the smell of smoke. How can a person know and not know at the same time?

Janet's answer was psychological automatism. Normally, he argued, the mind is constantly binding sensations, memories and actions into a single personal consciousness, a work of synthesis that takes mental energy. In hysteria that power of synthesis is weak, especially after exhaustion, illness or a terrifying experience. The field of consciousness then narrows (one chapter of his 1889 book is called "Suggestion and the narrowing of the field of consciousness"): the patient can hold only a few things in awareness at once, and whatever falls outside the narrowed field is not lost but goes on working by itself. Janet called this coming apart désagrégation (literally disaggregation), and in English it became dissociation. A memory of a traumatic event, cut off in this way, becomes a fixed idea (idée fixe): a cluster of images, feelings and movements that lives outside personal awareness, at the level Janet called subconscious, and expresses itself automatically as a symptom. The fixed idea may show itself as an attack in which the scene is re-enacted, a trance, a paralysis or a patch of numbness. Janet explored these split-off systems with hypnosis and automatic writing, in which a patient's hand would write answers to questions while her conscious attention was engaged elsewhere.

Two features of Janet's theory matter for the rest of this chapter. First, it is a deficit theory: the split happens because the mind is too weak to hold everything together, not because the person wants to forget. Second, it puts trauma at the centre: the fixed ideas are very often memories of overwhelming events that could not be absorbed into the ordinary story of a life. When trauma researchers rediscovered Janet a century later, Bessel van der Kolk and Onno van der Hart (1989) described him as the first to study dissociation systematically as the mind's response to overwhelming experience, and the first to show that traumatic memories may be expressed as sensations, emotional states and behavioural re-enactments rather than as stories. ICD-10's definition of the dissociative (conversion) disorders, "a partial or complete loss of the normal integration between memories of the past, awareness of identity and immediate sensations, and control of bodily movements", is Janet's theory in the World Health Organization's words. Section 7.5 develops dissociation as a modern clinical concept.

Janet's name is also attached, in almost every exam guide, to a phrase you will meet again in Section 7.8: la belle indifférence, French for "the beautiful indifference", the strange calm with which some patients regard a dramatic symptom such as a paralysed leg or a blind eye. In his lectures at Harvard, published as The Major Symptoms of Hysteria (1907), Janet described how hysterical patients often seem untroubled by their paralyses and anaesthesias, as if the lost function had simply dropped out of their personal consciousness. The history of the phrase itself is more tangled. A systematic review by the neurologist Jon Stone and his colleagues (2006) found that the phrase seems to have become popular after Freud used it in Studies on Hysteria (1895) about one of his patients, Fräulein Elisabeth von R., whose cheerful air he called "the belle indifférence of a hysteric"; in 1915 Freud attributed the phrase to Charcot, which suggests it was already in use in the late nineteenth century, although Stone's team could not find it in Charcot's published lectures; and Janet, although he described the attitude, does not appear to have used the words. So the phrase is usually credited to Janet, sometimes to Charcot, and its earliest well-known use in print is Freud's. Whether the sign is any use in diagnosis (it is not) is taken up in Section 7.8.

Exam trap

"The term la belle indifférence seen in hysteria was first used by" Pierre Janet (RML 2024): give Janet whenever he is an option, because that is the keyed answer and the attribution most textbooks repeat. Freud is the option the historical evidence actually favours, but it is not the keyed answer; Anna Freud (1895 to 1982) catalogued the defence mechanisms (Book 2 Section 3.4.1); Hippocrates gave hysteria its name. If an interviewer presses you, know the fine print: the earliest well-known printed use of the phrase is in Freud's Studies on Hysteria (1895), Freud credited it to Charcot, and Janet described the attitude, without the phrase, in 1907 (Stone and colleagues, 2006). The sign belongs to conversion disorder (functional neurological symptom disorder), not to panic disorder, phobia or mania. Dissociation, désagrégation, psychological automatism and the fixed idea are Janet's terms; repression is Freud's.

Breuer, Freud and the idea of conversion

While Charcot lectured in Paris, a Viennese physician was treating the patient who would become the most famous in the history of psychotherapy. Between 1880 and 1882 Josef Breuer (1842 to 1925) visited a young woman, Bertha Pappenheim (1859 to 1936), who had fallen ill while nursing her dying father: a nervous cough, a squint, disturbances of vision and speech, and a paralysis that drew her right arm and both legs into rigid contractures. In a dreamy, self-induced trance she told Breuer about the circumstances in which each symptom had first appeared, and as she talked the symptoms eased; she herself called the procedure the "talking cure". Breuer called it the cathartic method, because the relief seemed to come from catharsis, the release of emotion that had been blocked at the moment the symptom began. Book 2 Section 3.1.2 tells the case and the method in full (Book 2 Chapter 3 opens with it), so here you need only two further points. The first is that Breuer gave up the treatment in 1882 without curing her; she was admitted to institutions several times over the following years, with severe returns of her symptoms, before a slow recovery; she later became a pioneering social worker and campaigner against the trafficking of women. The second is that Barlow and Durand read Breuer's notes as describing two alternating states of mind, one fairly ordinary and one wild and abusive, with amnesia for the second. Read that way, the founding case of psychotherapy looks like dissociative identity disorder, a reading taken up in Section 7.6.

In 1895 Breuer and Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) published the case, together with four of Freud's own, as Studies on Hysteria. Their "Preliminary Communication" of 1893 had already stated the thesis in one famous line: "hysterics suffer mainly from reminiscences". A hysterical symptom, on this view, is a memory turned into a bodily state, the frozen remnant of a traumatic moment whose emotion was never discharged. So if you are asked what Freud meant by hysteria, the answer is physical reactions to emotional trauma.

Freud then went beyond Breuer in two directions. First, he asked why a memory should be shut out of consciousness at all, and answered that it is pushed out because it is unbearable. The ego defends itself against an incompatible idea (a wish, a memory or a feeling that clashes with the person's moral standards) by repression: actively excluding it from awareness and keeping it excluded. Second, he asked where the emotional energy attached to the repressed idea goes. In hysteria, he proposed in 1894, it is transformed into something bodily, and for this transformation he suggested the name conversion. Barlow and Durand note that the word had been used off and on since the Middle Ages but that Freud made it popular, and it survives in the bracketed name of a modern diagnosis, functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder), which Section 7.8 teaches. The symptom was often thought to be symbolic of the conflict: the arm that must not strike becomes paralysed, the voice that must not speak is lost. Barlow and Durand summarise Freud's account in four steps. A traumatic event, in Freud's view an unacceptable unconscious conflict, comes first; the person represses the conflict; the anxiety threatens to break through and is converted into a physical symptom, which relieves the pressure; and the symptom then brings attention and sympathy and allows escape from a difficult situation. The last two steps are the two kinds of "gain" described in the next subsection.

What was the original trauma? In a lecture to his medical colleagues in Vienna on 21 April 1896, "The Aetiology of Hysteria", Freud claimed that in each of the 18 patients he had analysed, hysteria could be traced to sexual abuse in early childhood, memories of which had been repressed and were recovered in analysis. This was the seduction theory. Within eighteen months he had privately abandoned it. In a letter to his friend Wilhelm Fliess dated 21 September 1897 he gave his reasons: his analyses never reached a convincing conclusion, the theory would have required the abuse of children by fathers to be implausibly common, and the unconscious cannot tell a real memory from an emotionally charged fiction. He did not say so publicly until 1906. In place of real seductions Freud put the child's own wishes and fantasies, and from that turn grew the theory of infantile sexuality, the Oedipus complex and the psychosexual stages (taught in the Personality book). The decision has been fought over ever since; in 1984 Jeffrey Masson argued that Freud had suppressed an uncomfortable truth about child abuse. Underneath the argument lies a question that matters greatly in this chapter. When a memory of abuse emerges during treatment, is it a record of the past or a product of the treatment? That question returns, almost unchanged, in the recovered memory debate of the 1990s (Section 7.6).

Now set Janet and Freud side by side, as Figure 7.2 (in Section 7.1.7) does. For Janet the memory is split off sideways because the mind is too weak to integrate it; the two streams run in parallel, and treatment means strengthening synthesis and bringing the split-off idea back into the personality. For Freud the memory is pushed downwards because it is unbearable; an active force keeps it down, the force shows itself as resistance when the therapist tries to undo it, and treatment means making the unconscious conscious. Most psychodynamic writers followed Freud, which is why the standard psychodynamic answer to the question "what produces dissociative and conversion symptoms?" is repression. Modern trauma researchers, by contrast, have returned to Janet's language of dissociation. Book 2 Section 3.1.3 sets out the contrast as it bears on therapy, and Book 2 Section 3.1.4 explains why Freud gave up hypnosis.

Exam trap

"According to Freud, the term hysteria describes" physical reactions to emotional trauma (RML 2021). The distractors come from other parts of Freud's work: a phobia of horses is the case of Little Hans (1909), read by Freud as displaced Oedipal fear and later reinterpreted by behaviourists as conditioning (Chapter 6; Book 2 Section 4.1); an unresolved Oedipus complex and oral-type behaviour belong to psychosexual theory (Personality book). "In view of most psychodynamic theorists, dissociative symptoms are produced by" repression (RML 2022). Conflict is what gets repressed, not the mechanism that produces the symptom; ego states are a different idea (Paul Federn's, later the basis of ego-state therapy and of Eric Berne's transactional analysis, Book 2 Section 3.9); gender identity belongs to Chapter 9. Catharsis and the cathartic method are Breuer's; "talking cure" was the patient's own phrase; Studies on Hysteria is 1895 (The Interpretation of Dreams is 1900).

What a symptom gains: primary, secondary and tertiary gain

Freud's account carried one further idea that has outlived the rest of his theory of hysteria: a symptom can be useful to the person who has it. He distinguished two kinds of benefit. The first, primary gain, is the internal benefit. By converting a conflict into a bodily symptom, and keeping the conflict itself out of awareness, the person is spared the anxiety that facing it would bring; Barlow and Durand define primary gain as the Freudian notion that anxiety reduction is the principal reinforcement obtained from a symptom. The second, secondary gain, is the external benefit that follows once the symptom exists: attention, sympathy and care from others, and escape from unwanted duties or impossible situations. A third term, tertiary gain, was proposed by D. A. Dansak in a paper of 1973, "On the tertiary gain of illness", for the benefits that people other than the patient draw from the illness: a relative who finds in it a reason for something they already wanted, a caregiver whose role and importance depend on it, a healer or clinic that earns from it.

A case makes the three kinds concrete. Lalitha, 19, has been married for eight months and lives with her husband's family in a village near Mandya. During a loud quarrel with her mother-in-law over money, she suddenly falls to the floor, eyes screwed tightly shut, arms and legs thrashing for about twenty minutes. She does not bite her tongue or wet herself, and afterwards she says she remembers nothing. The quarrel stops at once. Neighbours crowd in, her mother-in-law fans her and sends for the local healer, and her husband takes her to her mother's house to rest for two weeks. Over the next months the "fits" recur whenever the quarrels do. In Freud's terms, the primary gain is that Lalitha never has to feel, let alone voice, an anger she cannot afford to express towards her husband's mother; the conflict becomes a fit and stays out of awareness. The secondary gain is everything that follows: the quarrel halted, the attention, the rest, the visit home. And there may be tertiary gain: her husband, who has wanted for a year to set up a separate household, now has a reason the family will accept, and the healer has a regular client. Today Lalitha's attacks would be classified as dissociative convulsions (F44.5) in ICD-10, or as functional neurological symptom disorder with attacks or seizures in DSM-5-TR (Section 7.8).

Three cautions keep these ideas honest. First, gain is a description, not a diagnosis. Secondary gain follows many genuine medical illnesses (a man with a broken leg also receives sympathy and time off work), so its presence tells you nothing about the cause of a symptom. Second, gain in conversion is not a conscious motive. If Lalitha were deliberately faking fits to stop the quarrels, the diagnosis would change: to malingering if the aim were an external reward, or to factitious disorder if the aim were to occupy the role of a sick person (Section 7.9). Third, the evidence for Freud's mechanism is thin. Barlow and Durand judge that Freud was probably right that a trauma or an impossible situation often comes first, that illness offers an escape, and that attention reinforces the symptom, but that the idea of primary gain has little support; Section 7.8 reviews the studies, and Section 7.9 returns to gain when it compares conversion with factitious disorder and malingering.

Clinical link

Never tell a patient or family that a functional symptom is "just for attention". The patient is not choosing the symptom, and the accusation destroys the trust that treatment depends on. A better approach is to explain that the symptom is real and involuntary, to ask what has been happening in the family around the time the attacks began, and to agree a plan in which attention is given for coping and recovery rather than for the attack itself. Understanding the gains is part of formulation; announcing them is not. The sick role, illness behaviour and abnormal illness behaviour are taught in Book 2 Section 9.1.4.

Exam trap

Primary gain is internal: relief from anxiety because the conflict stays unconscious. Secondary gain is external: attention, sympathy, escape from duties, sometimes compensation. Tertiary gain goes to someone else (Dansak, 1973). Secondary gain is not the same as malingering: in conversion the gain is not consciously sought, whereas the malingerer consciously fakes a symptom for an external incentive. Questions that describe a symptom that "brings attention" are testing secondary gain, not factitious disorder, unless the stem says the symptom is deliberately produced.

Shell shock: hysteria in uniform

If hysteria were a disorder of the womb, or of nerves weakened by a bad heredity, the First World War should not have produced it in fit young men. It produced it in very large numbers. From the first winter of the war, soldiers came back from the trenches shaking uncontrollably, unable to stand or walk, bent double at the waist, blind, deaf or mute, with no wound to explain any of it. In February 1915 the Cambridge psychologist Charles Samuel Myers (1873 to 1946) described in The Lancet three soldiers who had been close to bursting shells and had lost memory, vision, smell and taste. His paper is the first use of the term shell shock in the medical literature (Myers later said he had not invented the phrase), and he noted that his patients closely resembled the civilians then labelled hysterical. The name implied a physical concussion of the brain by the blast, and doctors argued for years over whether the damage came from the explosion or from the terror of modern warfare. By 1917 British medical officers were being told not to use the term at all and to record probable cases as "Not Yet Diagnosed (Nervous)". The treatments ranged from rest, talk and hypnosis to the painful electrical "torpedoing" of Clovis Vincent in France.

Shell shock matters to this chapter for two reasons. First, it finished the idea that hysteria belonged to women. Here were conversion symptoms (paralyses, tremors, mutism, bizarre gaits) and dissociative symptoms (amnesia for the battle, confused wandering) in men, produced not by any organ but by overwhelming fear in a situation from which there was no honourable escape. Barlow and Durand's reading of conversion, a socially acceptable way out of an impossible situation, fits the soldier in the trench as well as it fits Lalitha in her in-laws' house. Second, it opened the road from hysteria to trauma. The war neuroses led to The Traumatic Neuroses of War by Abram Kardiner (1941), to the "combat fatigue" of the Second World War and, after Vietnam, to posttraumatic stress disorder in DSM-III (1980). That lineage is the subject of Section 7.2. What matters here is that the trauma family of this chapter split away from hysteria on the battlefield.

Divided consciousness: Hilgard's neodissociation theory

For half a century after Janet, dissociation went out of fashion. Psychoanalysis spoke of repression, behaviourism of learned responses, and neither had much use for a mind that splits. The idea was revived in the laboratory by Ernest Ropiequet Hilgard (1904 to 2001), who led research on hypnosis at Stanford University, in his book Divided Consciousness (1977). His neodissociation theory pictures the mind as a set of subsystems of thought and control, each able to work with some independence, coordinated by an executive ego that monitors and plans. Normally the executive keeps the subsystems integrated and you experience a single stream of awareness. In hypnosis, and perhaps in some disorders, part of the processing is cut off from awareness by an amnesic barrier: the information is still registered and can still guide behaviour, but the person does not experience it as their own. The prefix "neo" signals what is new. Hilgard kept Janet's picture of parallel streams but dropped Janet's assumption of weakness. For Hilgard, dividing consciousness is a normal capacity, present in everyone and greatest in highly hypnotisable people.

Key study: Hilgard's hidden observer (1970s)

Hilgard gave highly hypnotisable volunteers the suggestion that they would feel no pain, and then exposed them to pain in the laboratory: a hand and forearm held in iced water (the cold pressor test), or a tourniquet on the upper arm while they squeezed a hand exerciser (ischaemic pain). Asked aloud, they reported little or no pain. Beforehand, Hilgard had told them under hypnosis that a "hidden part" of them might be aware of things the hypnotised part was not, and that this part could report, through automatic writing or key presses, when he gave a cue. The hidden observer, addressed this way, reported pain close to the levels felt without hypnosis; Knox, Morgan and Hilgard (1974) called this the paradox of hypnotically suggested anaesthesia. Hilgard concluded that the pain had been registered but kept out of awareness by an amnesic barrier. The criticism came from Nicholas Spanos (1942 to 1994; Section 7.6.4). Spanos and Hewitt (1980) gave eight highly hypnotisable subjects Hilgard's instructions, which imply that the hidden part feels more pain, and another eight the opposite expectation, that the hidden part feels less. Each group's hidden reports matched what it had been led to expect, which suggests that the observer is created by the instructions, as a role that a "good hypnotic subject" enacts. Book 2 Section 3.10.5 teaches the experiment and the debate between state and non-state theories of hypnosis in full.

Hilgard believed his findings shed light on clinical dissociation, and in a 1984 paper he compared the hidden observer with the alternate personalities of multiple personality, each a part of the mind that knows what the rest does not. Spanos's reply, that hidden observers and alter personalities alike are roles shaped by expectation and instruction, grew into the sociocognitive model of dissociative identity disorder, which Section 7.6 weighs against the trauma model. You can now see the three accounts of the split side by side in Figure 7.2, and the whole run of explanations, from the womb to the hidden observer, in Table 7.1.

Table 7.1 How hysteria was explained, from the Hippocratic writers to Hilgard.
Who and whenWhere the trouble liesHow symptoms ariseWhat was thought to cure them
Hippocratic writers (about 400 BCE)The wombAn empty womb wanders and presses on other organsMarriage, pregnancy, fumigation
Charcot (1870s to 1893)A hereditarily weak nervous systemA "dynamic lesion" set off by shock; later, autosuggestionHypnosis and suggestion, isolation, electrotherapy
Bernheim and the Nancy school (1880s)Ordinary suggestibilityIdeas accepted under suggestionSuggestion, with or without hypnosis
Janet (1889)A weak power of psychological synthesisFixed ideas split off from a narrowed consciousnessUncovering and changing the fixed ideas, often under hypnosis
Breuer and Freud (1893 to 1895)A traumatic memory whose emotion was never dischargedBlocked emotion expressed through the bodyCatharsis: reliving the memory with its feeling
Freud (1894 onwards)Unconscious conflict, traced first to childhood abuse, later to fantasyRepression, then conversion of its energy into a bodily symptomPsychoanalysis: making the conflict conscious
Babinski (1901)Suggestion itselfPithiatism: produced by suggestionPersuasion
Hilgard (1977)A normal capacity to divide consciousnessParallel streams behind an amnesic barrierAn account of hypnosis rather than a treatment
Exam trap

The hidden observer and neodissociation theory are Hilgard's (Divided Consciousness, 1977), not Janet's; Janet's word was désagrégation (1889). The method was hypnotic analgesia with the cold pressor test (iced water) and ischaemic pain. Spanos is the critic, and his name goes with the sociocognitive view that the observer is a role created by instructions. Do not confuse neodissociation, a theory of hypnosis and divided awareness, with repression, a psychoanalytic defence.

From one word to three families: how the classifications divided hysteria

By the middle of the twentieth century hysteria had been explained in at least five incompatible ways, and the official classifications had to decide what to do with it. They took it apart in stages, and the American and the international systems ended up dividing it differently (Figure 7.3).

The first edition of the American Psychiatric Association's manual, DSM-I (1952), followed the psychodynamic thinking of its day and listed a conversion reaction and a dissociative reaction among the psychoneurotic disorders. DSM-II (1968) merged the two into a single diagnosis, hysterical neurosis, with two types. In hysterical neurosis, conversion type, "the special senses or voluntary nervous system are affected", producing blindness, deafness, anaesthesias, paralyses and disorders of movement. In hysterical neurosis, dissociative type, alterations of consciousness or identity produce amnesia, somnambulism, fugue and multiple personality. Read DSM-II's description of the whole category and you can hear almost every theorist in this section. Hysterical neurosis is "an involuntary psychogenic loss or disorder of function"; its symptoms "characteristically begin and end suddenly in emotionally charged situations and are symbolic of the underlying conflicts"; and "often they can be modified by suggestion alone". The text for the conversion type even mentions the patient's "belle indifference" and the "secondary gains" the symptoms may bring. DSM-II also listed a neurasthenic neurosis, a depersonalisation neurosis and a hypochondriacal neurosis, and, among the personality disorders, the hysterical personality, which it already glossed as histrionic personality disorder.

DSM-III (1980) broke the diagnosis apart. Its authors set out to describe disorders by what can be observed rather than by their presumed causes, and two old words failed that test. Neurosis went because, as Barlow and Durand explain, it was too vague, applying to almost all non-psychotic disorders, and because it implied a specific but unproven cause: unconscious conflict, the anxiety it produces, and the defence mechanisms that manage it. Hysteria went too, and its contents were redistributed by the way patients present. Conditions in which a person presents with physical symptoms that suggest a medical condition, but for which no physical pathology can be found, became the somatoform disorders (Section 7.7 defines them in full): somatisation disorder (the old Briquet's syndrome), conversion disorder, psychogenic pain disorder and hypochondriasis. Conditions in which memory, identity or consciousness is disrupted became the dissociative disorders: psychogenic amnesia, psychogenic fugue, multiple personality and depersonalisation disorder. Deliberately produced symptoms got a class of their own, the factitious disorders (Section 7.9); PTSD entered the manual for the first time, among the anxiety disorders (Section 7.2); and the hysterical personality became histrionic personality disorder, from the Latin for an actor (Chapter 11). DSM-IV (1994) mostly renamed: psychogenic amnesia and fugue became dissociative amnesia and dissociative fugue, multiple personality disorder became dissociative identity disorder (Section 7.6), and the pain category ended up as pain disorder.

DSM-5 (2013) and its text revision DSM-5-TR (2022) produced the arrangement this chapter follows: three neighbouring chapters in place of one diagnosis. A new chapter of trauma- and stressor-related disorders gathered PTSD (moved out of the anxiety disorders), acute stress disorder, the adjustment disorders and the two attachment disorders of childhood, joined in 2022 by prolonged grief disorder (Sections 7.2 to 7.4). The dissociative disorders were placed, in DSM-5-TR's own words, "next to, but are not part of" that chapter, "reflecting the close relationship between these diagnostic classes", and fugue became a specifier of dissociative amnesia (Sections 7.5 and 7.6). The somatoform disorders were renamed somatic symptom and related disorders, now defined by the presence of distressing symptoms with excessive thoughts, feelings or behaviours rather than by the absence of a medical explanation (Section 7.7). Conversion disorder stayed in this somatic chapter, renamed functional neurological symptom disorder (Section 7.8), and factitious disorder moved into it (Section 7.9).

The World Health Organization took a different road. ICD-10, whose volume on mental disorders appeared in 1992, kept the old word in the title of its block F40 to F48, "neurotic, stress-related and somatoform disorders", and it kept conversion and dissociation together. Its category F44, dissociative (conversion) disorders, holds dissociative amnesia (F44.0), dissociative fugue (F44.1), dissociative stupor (F44.2), trance and possession disorders (F44.3), dissociative motor disorders (F44.4), dissociative convulsions (F44.5), dissociative anaesthesia and sensory loss (F44.6), mixed forms (F44.7) and other forms (F44.8), under which Ganser syndrome and multiple personality are listed. The WHO text states plainly that these disorders "have previously been classified as various types of 'conversion hysteria'". Bodily complaints without loss of function went to F45, the somatoform disorders, where somatisation disorder (F45.0) lists "Briquet disorder" among its inclusion terms, alongside hypochondriacal disorder (F45.2) and persistent somatoform pain disorder (F45.4). Neurasthenia (F48.0) and depersonalisation-derealisation syndrome (F48.1) went to the "other neurotic disorders", and reactions to severe stress, PTSD among them, to F43.

ICD-11, adopted by the World Health Assembly in 2019 and in force from 2022, kept the WHO principle. Its dissociative disorders (6B60 to 6B6Z) begin with dissociative neurological symptom disorder (6B60), the ICD-11 name for conversion, which is subdivided by the type of symptom (with non-epileptic seizures, with paresis or weakness, with visual disturbance and so on). It gathers PTSD, complex PTSD, prolonged grief disorder, adjustment disorder and the two attachment disorders as "disorders specifically associated with stress". It replaces almost the whole somatoform group with a single bodily distress disorder (6C20), which largely absorbs neurasthenia as well. And it moves hypochondriasis (6B23) into the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders. Chapter 1 explains the ICD-10 blocks and the structure of ICD-11.

Why do the two systems disagree about where conversion belongs? Because they classify by different principles. DSM sorts by what the patient presents with. A paralysed arm is a bodily complaint, the patient will usually be seen first by a physician or a neurologist, and so it sits with the other bodily complaints. ICD sorts by the process it assumes lies beneath the symptom. A paralysed arm with no lesion is a loss of the normal integration of bodily control, just as amnesia is a loss of the normal integration of memory, and so it sits with amnesia and fugue. Both arguments are reasonable, and neither has been proved right. For the examination the rule is simple: in DSM-5-TR conversion is a somatic symptom disorder; in ICD-10 and ICD-11 it is a dissociative disorder. Table 7.2 follows each of the old diagnoses to its present home.

Table 7.2 Where the old diagnoses sit today. ICD codes are those of the WHO versions; the US ICD-10-CM codes printed in DSM-5-TR differ in places.
Old nameDSM-5-TRICD-10ICD-11Section
Hysterical neurosis, conversion typeFunctional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder)F44.4 to F44.76B60 dissociative neurological symptom disorder7.8
Hysterical neurosis, dissociative typeDissociative disordersF44.0 to F44.3, F44.86B61 to 6B657.5, 7.6

Traumatic neurosis; shell shockPosttraumatic stress disorderF43.16B407.2
Munchausen syndromeFactitious disorder imposed on selfF68.16D507.9

The word hysteria itself had to go, for at least four reasons. It was stigmatising, a lay insult ("don't be hysterical") as much as a diagnosis. It carried a gender bias built into its very etymology, although every one of its successor disorders occurs in men. It implied a cause, first the womb and later unconscious conflict, that nobody could demonstrate. And it was hopelessly broad, lumping together presentations that turned out to have different courses and different treatments. The word survives only in a few fossil phrases: globus hystericus, the sensation of a lump in the throat (Section 7.8), and mass hysteria, the spread of symptoms with no physical cause through a group of people under stress, which Book 2 Section 1.2.4 discusses with the dancing manias.

Indian context

In Indian hospitals "hysteria" and "hysterical fits" are still the words many families, and some casualty doctors, use for the sudden attacks, faints, possession states and losses of voice that bring adolescents and young women in particular to the emergency room. Indian psychiatry has long found the ICD-10 F44 categories (dissociative convulsions, dissociative motor disorders, trance and possession disorders) a good fit for these presentations, and they remain in everyday use. When you take the history, avoid the word hysteria. Tell the family that the symptom is real, involuntary and treatable, and ask gently about stress at home, at school, at work and in the marriage. Possession states that occur within an accepted religious or cultural practice are not disorders at all (Section 7.6; Chapter 1 discusses cultural concepts of distress).

The fastest way to see what all this reclassification means is to push one patient through it. Mala, 23, a garment-factory worker in Bengaluru, has episodes in which she falls to the floor, eyes clamped shut, and shakes all four limbs for half an hour; she remembers nothing of them afterwards. An EEG recorded during one of the attacks is normal. The attacks began a few months after she was assaulted on her way home from a night shift, and since then she has had nightmares about the assault, avoids the road where it happened and startles at footsteps behind her. Table 7.3 shows what her attacks would have been called, and what would have been made of the assault, at different points in the history you have just read.

Table 7.3 Worked example: one patient, Mala, across a century and a half of classification.
System and dateWhat her attacks would be calledWhat happens to the assault
Charcot's Salpêtrière (1880s)Grand hysteria (hystero-epilepsy) in a hereditarily weak nervous systemA "psychical shock" that set the disease off
Freud (1896)HysteriaA clue to an earlier, repressed sexual trauma in childhood (the seduction theory)
DSM-II (1968)Hysterical neurosis, conversion typeNo PTSD diagnosis existed
DSM-III (1980) to DSM-IV (1994)Conversion disorder, a somatoform disorderPTSD, an anxiety disorder
DSM-5-TR (2022)Functional neurological symptom disorder, with attacks or seizuresPTSD, in the trauma- and stressor-related chapter, diagnosed alongside

ICD-11 (2022)Dissociative neurological symptom disorder, with non-epileptic seizures, 6B60.4PTSD, 6B40

Two lessons come out of Mala's table. The first is that the patient has not changed at all; only the frame has. Her attacks are the same attacks Blanche Wittmann had on Charcot's stage, and the assault is the kind of event Charcot called a psychical shock and Freud a trauma. The second is that the modern systems allow her to carry two diagnoses at once, one for the attacks and one for the trauma, which is the clinical truth that the single word hysteria concealed. Sections 7.2 to 7.4 now take up the first of the three families, the disorders that begin with a stressor.

Exam trap

DSM-III (1980) split hysteria into the somatoform and dissociative disorders and removed the word neurosis. ICD-10 kept conversion and dissociation together as dissociative (conversion) disorders, F44, inside the block "neurotic, stress-related and somatoform disorders" (F40 to F48). ICD-11 keeps conversion among the dissociative disorders as dissociative neurological symptom disorder (6B60), whereas DSM-5-TR puts it in the somatic symptom chapter. In ICD-10, depersonalisation-derealisation syndrome is F48.1 and neurasthenia F48.0, not F44. Hypochondriasis is a somatic disorder in DSM-5-TR (illness anxiety disorder or somatic symptom disorder) but an obsessive-compulsive related disorder in ICD-11 (6B23). And the DSM-5-TR dissociative chapter sits next to, not inside, the trauma chapter.

Scan Your Brain

  4. Bernheim's Nancy school held that hypnosis is an exaggeration of normal , not a sign of hysteria.
  5. Babinski's 1901 term for disorders reproduced by suggestion and cured by persuasion was .

Answers: (1) womb (uterus), (2) hypnosis, suggestion, (3) delirium, (4) suggestibility, (5) pithiatism, (6) désagrégation, (7) Pierre Janet, (8) repression, (9) primary, secondary, (10) F44, dissociative (conversion) disorders.

7.2 Posttraumatic and Acute Stress Disorders: Diagnosis and Clinical PictureMedium yield · 8 Qs

Almost every diagnosis in this book is defined by what the person experiences now: the low mood, the voices, the rituals, the binges. The cause, if anyone knows it, stays outside the criteria. The disorders of this section break that rule. Their criteria begin with an event in the world: a crash, a rape, a bomb, a flood. Without the event there is no diagnosis, however closely the symptoms match. That makes them unusual, and it raises the questions that run through this section and the next. What kind of event counts? What exactly happens to a mind after it? How long must the reaction last before it stops being a normal response to a terrible experience and becomes a disorder? This section answers those questions for posttraumatic stress disorder and its short-lived relatives; Section 7.3 asks who develops them, why, and how they are treated.

Why a family of trauma disorders?

When the American Psychiatric Association first named posttraumatic stress disorder in DSM-III (1980), it placed it among the anxiety disorders, and DSM-IV (1994) kept it there. The reasoning was plain: people who have survived a trauma are frightened, jumpy and avoidant, and fear is the core of anxiety. By the time DSM-5 appeared in 2013, the committee had decided that this picture was too narrow. It created a new chapter, trauma- and stressor-related disorders: the disorders in which exposure to a traumatic or stressful event is listed explicitly as a diagnostic criterion. In DSM-5-TR (2022) the chapter holds reactive attachment disorder, disinhibited social engagement disorder, posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD), acute stress disorder, the adjustment disorders and the newly added prolonged grief disorder, together with "other specified" and "unspecified" categories for presentations that fit none of these.

Two arguments justified the move. The first is causal. As David H. Barlow and V. Mark Durand put it in their textbook, these disorders all share a proximal instigating stressful event followed by intense emotional responses; the stressor is not a background risk, as it is for depression, but a necessary condition written into the criteria. The second is emotional. Many survivors are not mainly afraid. The DSM-5-TR describes people whose most prominent symptoms after a trauma are anhedonia and dysphoria (they cannot enjoy anything and feel flat or miserable), or angry and aggressive outbursts, or dissociation, rather than anxiety. Barlow and Durand add rage, horror, guilt and shame to fear. A soldier who cannot forgive himself for what he did, a woman ashamed of a rape that was in no way her fault, a father enraged at the driver who killed his child: none of these is well described as an anxiety disorder. The new chapter was deliberately placed between the anxiety and obsessive-compulsive chapters on one side and the dissociative chapter on the other, because its disorders share features with all three.

Notice one consequence of the logic. A stressor is a necessary cause of PTSD, but it is not a sufficient one. Most people who live through a trauma do not develop the disorder, and the reasons why some do is the subject of Section 7.3. Notice also what the chapter does not contain. Generalised anxiety disorder, panic disorder and obsessive-compulsive disorder may all begin after a hard year, but none of them requires a stressor for its diagnosis: the worry of generalised anxiety disorder roams over everyday matters, the attacks of panic disorder are by definition unexpected, and obsessions are intrusive thoughts that are not memories of an event (all three are taught in Chapter 6).

The World Health Organization reached the same conclusion by a slightly different route. ICD-11, adopted by the World Health Assembly in 2019 and in use from 2022, has a grouping called disorders specifically associated with stress, defined by the requirement that an identifiable stressor is necessary (though not sufficient) for the diagnosis. Its members are PTSD, complex PTSD, prolonged grief disorder, adjustment disorder, reactive attachment disorder and disinhibited social engagement disorder. Two differences from DSM-5-TR matter for examinations. ICD-11 has complex PTSD, which DSM-5-TR lacks, and it has no acute stress disorder: the short, intense reaction in the first days after a trauma is treated in ICD-11 as an acute stress reaction, a normal response that is classified outside the chapter on mental disorders altogether (you will meet it again in Section 7.2.8). Table 7.4 sets the two systems side by side.

Table 7.4 The trauma and stressor family in DSM-5-TR and ICD-11. Each disorder requires a stressor; the section of this chapter that teaches it is in the last column.
DisorderDSM-5-TR (trauma- and stressor-related)ICD-11 (specifically associated with stress)Taught in
Posttraumatic stress disorderYesYes7.2, 7.3
Complex PTSDNo (only an associated feature of PTSD)Yes7.2
Acute stress disorderYes (3 days to 1 month)No; acute stress reaction is a normal response, coded outside the mental disorders7.2
Adjustment disorderYesYes7.4
