Exam trap

"In ICD-11, which is a disorder specifically associated with stress, requiring exposure to an extremely threatening or horrific event?" The answer is posttraumatic stress disorder. Generalised anxiety disorder, panic disorder and obsessive-compulsive disorder sit in ICD-11's anxiety and fear-related and obsessive-compulsive groupings because no stressor is needed to diagnose them. Remember too that PTSD was an anxiety disorder in DSM-III and DSM-IV; it moved to its own chapter only in DSM-5 (2013).

From the Great Fire to DSM-5: the history of a diagnosis

The name is young; the suffering is not. In September 1666 the diarist Samuel Pepys (1633 to 1703) watched the Great Fire of London destroy most of the old city. He wrote one of the most vivid accounts of it that we have, and he went on writing about it after the flames were out. On 28 February 1667, nearly six months later, he wrote: "it is strange to think how, to this very day, I cannot sleep at night without great terrors of fire, and this very night I could not sleep till almost two in the morning through thoughts of fire." Barlow and Durand, who use the passage, point out how much of a modern diagnosis it contains: sleeplessness, intrusive thoughts and dreams of the event, guilt at having saved himself and his property while others lost everything, and a curious detachment and numbness about the fire.

Medicine began to take an interest in the nineteenth century, when a new machine produced a new kind of victim. Railway collisions left passengers who had no visible injury but who complained for months of pain, weakness, poor sleep, poor memory and fright. In 1866 the London surgeon John Eric Erichsen (1818 to 1896) described these cases in On Railway and Other Injuries of the Nervous System, and the condition became popularly known as railway spine, because he attributed it to "concussion of the spine". The question that dogged it was the one that still dogs trauma in courtrooms: was the illness caused by physical injury, by fright, or by the hope of compensation from the railway company? In 1889, in his book Die traumatischen Neurosen, the Berlin neurologist Hermann Oppenheim (1858 to 1919) gave the condition a more general name, traumatic neurosis, and argued that it arose from subtle physical changes in the nervous system; Charcot in Paris saw such patients as cases of hysteria in men brought on by the shock of an accident (Section 7.1).

The First World War multiplied the problem a thousandfold. Soldiers came out of the trenches trembling, mute, paralysed, blind or unable to stand, often with no wound at all. In February 1915 the Cambridge psychologist and physician Charles Samuel Myers (1873 to 1946) published in The Lancet the first medical paper to use the phrase already current among soldiers, shell shock, which implied that the blast of an exploding shell had jarred the brain. When it became clear that many shell-shocked men had never been near an explosion, the cause was rethought as psychological, and the cases were called war neuroses. (Section 7.1 tells how shell shock was understood as hysteria in men; here we follow the line that leads to PTSD.)

The decisive clinical description came from Abram Kardiner (1891 to 1981), an American psychiatrist and psychoanalyst who treated veterans of that war in New York. In The Traumatic Neuroses of War (1941) he described men who, years after the armistice, were still easily startled, irritable and given to explosive outbursts, fixated on the trauma, haunted by nightmares and shrunk in their interests and relationships. Kardiner thought the core of the condition was a persistent physiological state of readiness for danger. Read his list again and you have most of the modern criteria. In the Second World War the same reactions were called combat fatigue or combat exhaustion, and after the war psychiatrists studying survivors of the Nazi concentration camps found symptoms that persisted for decades.

The first edition of the DSM (1952) had a category called gross stress reaction, for previously normal people who broke down under overwhelming stress such as combat or civilian catastrophe. It was assumed to be temporary; if symptoms persisted, the clinician was told to look for another diagnosis. DSM-II (1968) dropped the category (the nearest replacement was a vague "adjustment reaction of adult life"), just as the United States was sending hundreds of thousands of young men to Vietnam. Many came home with nightmares, rage, numbness and drinking, and veterans' groups and sympathetic psychiatrists such as Chaim Shatan, who wrote of a "post-Vietnam syndrome" in 1972, and Robert Jay Lifton campaigned for their problems to be recognised. When DSM-III appeared in 1980 it included posttraumatic stress disorder for the first time, describing it as following a psychologically traumatic event "generally outside the range of usual human experience". The research on rape survivors that was growing at the same time showed that the syndrome was not peculiar to soldiers.

Two later revisions shaped what you must know today. DSM-IV (1994) defined the traumatic event in two parts: criterion A1, the event itself (actual or threatened death or serious injury, or a threat to the physical integrity of self or others), and criterion A2, the person's response, which had to involve intense fear, helplessness or horror. It grouped the symptoms into three clusters: re-experiencing, avoidance and numbing, and increased arousal. DSM-5 (2013) made four changes. It dropped A2, because soldiers, police officers and others trained to stay calm under fire often did not report fear, helplessness or horror at the time and yet went on to develop the disorder. It tightened criterion A, naming sexual violence explicitly and limiting indirect exposure. It split the old avoidance and numbing cluster into two, avoidance on one side and negative alterations in cognitions and mood on the other, so that PTSD now has four clusters. And it added new symptoms (persistent blame of self or others, persistent negative emotions, reckless or self-destructive behaviour), separate criteria for children aged 6 and younger, and a dissociative subtype. The DSM-5-TR (2022) kept the criteria, with minor clarifications, and updated the text.

Remember

The lineage in one line: Pepys (1666) → railway spine (Erichsen, 1866) → traumatic neurosis (Oppenheim, 1889) → shell shock (Myers, 1915) → The Traumatic Neuroses of War (Kardiner, 1941) → combat fatigue (Second World War) → gross stress reaction (DSM-I, 1952) → PTSD (DSM-III, 1980) → three clusters (DSM-IV, 1994) → four clusters and a new chapter (DSM-5, 2013).

Exam trap

PTSD was first named in DSM-III (1980), not DSM-I (1952), which had "gross stress reaction", and not DSM-IV (1994), which added acute stress disorder. The Traumatic Neuroses of War (1941) is Kardiner's; "traumatic neurosis" as a term is Oppenheim's (1889); "shell shock" belongs to the First World War and Myers (1915). Do not confuse the hysteria story of Charcot and Freud (Section 7.1) with this lineage, although the two meet in shell shock.

Criterion A: what counts as a trauma?

In ordinary speech, "traumatic" can describe a failed examination, a broken engagement or a bad haircut. PTSD needs something narrower. DSM-5-TR's criterion A requires exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury or sexual violence. That definition has three parts, and each excludes something. "Death or serious injury" excludes humiliation, rejection and loss that do not threaten life or body. "Threatened" includes the robbery at knifepoint in which nobody was hurt. "Sexual violence" covers not only rape but forced sexual contact, drug-facilitated assault and non-contact acts such as being forced to watch pornography or having sexual images taken and spread without consent.

The criterion then lists four routes by which a person can be exposed, and here the detail matters:

  1. Directly experiencing the event. The survivor of the assault, the passenger in the crash, the prisoner who was tortured, the family whose house collapsed in the earthquake.
  2. Witnessing it, in person, as it happened to others. The bystander who saw a man crushed under a lorry; the parent who watched a child stop breathing in a hospital emergency.
  3. Learning that it happened to a close family member or close friend. Here there is a condition: if the event was an actual or threatened death, it must have been violent or accidental. Hearing that your brother was murdered, killed in a road accident or died by suicide counts; hearing that your grandmother died peacefully of old age does not, however great the grief (grief is taught in Section 7.4).
  4. Repeated or extreme exposure to aversive details of the event. This route was written for people whose work brings trauma to them: the first responders who collect human remains after a train crash, the police officers who interview child victims of abuse day after day, the forensic staff who read the case files. DSM-5-TR extends it to drone operators, journalists covering atrocities and psychotherapists repeatedly exposed to their patients' accounts.

A note attached to the fourth route causes more confusion than any other line in the criteria: exposure through electronic media, television, films or pictures does not count unless it is part of the person's work. Consider two men in November 2008, when terrorists attacked hotels and a railway station in Mumbai. One was a teacher in Pune who watched the television coverage for three nights and could not sleep for a week afterwards; he does not meet criterion A. The other was a news editor who spent those nights reviewing raw footage of the dead for his channel; he might. The rule is not a judgement that the teacher's distress is unreal. It is a decision about where to draw a line so that the diagnosis stays tied to genuine exposure.

Arif, a 34-year-old fireman in Bengaluru, shows what the fourth route looks like in practice. When a four-storey building under construction collapsed, his team spent two days digging out the bodies of labourers, among them two children who had been sleeping on the site. He was not injured and saw nobody die. Yet for months afterwards he woke at night smelling the dust, could not look at his own children asleep without seeing the bodies, and shouted at his crew over small things. His exposure qualifies under criterion A4.

Other cases need more thought. A serious illness is not automatically a traumatic event; DSM-5-TR counts sudden, catastrophic medical events (a heart attack, anaphylactic shock, waking during surgery, the debridement of severe burns) but not the slower threat of a chronic disease. Bullying counts when there is a credible threat of serious harm or sexual violence. And many people, especially in places of war, conflict or domestic violence, have suffered not one event but many. The clinician then asks which event the person considers the worst, because the intrusion and avoidance symptoms refer to a particular event; if no single worst event can be named, the whole exposure is treated as meeting criterion A.

What about the commonest painful events of all: divorce, the loss of a job, failing an examination, a business collapse, a parent's natural death? None of them meets criterion A. A person who reacts to them with a PTSD-like picture, or with marked anxiety and low mood, is diagnosed with an adjustment disorder or, after a death, assessed for prolonged grief disorder (Section 7.4). DSM-5-TR also notes that the disorder is often especially severe and long-lasting when the stressor is interpersonal and intentional, such as torture or sexual violence, a point that Section 7.3 will confirm with numbers.

Exam trap

Criterion A has four routes: directly experiencing, witnessing in person, learning about a close family member or friend (a death must be violent or accidental), and repeated or extreme exposure to aversive details (the first responder, the police officer). Watching it on television does not count unless it is your job. A divorce or a job loss is not a criterion A event, however distressing: think adjustment disorder. DSM-IV's requirement that the person felt "intense fear, helplessness or horror" (A2) was removed in DSM-5.

The four symptom clusters

Once criterion A is met, the clinician asks about four groups of symptoms, labelled B to E in DSM-5-TR. Each must begin, or clearly worsen, after the event. A useful way to remember them is as four things the trauma does to a mind: it keeps coming back (intrusion), the person tries to keep it out (avoidance), it changes how the person thinks and feels about self and world (negative alterations in cognitions and mood), and it leaves the body on guard (alterations in arousal and reactivity). Figure 7.4 shows the whole structure; what follows is what each symptom actually looks and sounds like.

Cluster B: intrusion (one or more of five). The trauma returns uninvited. The first symptom is intrusive memories: recurrent, involuntary and distressing recollections that arrive in the middle of a meal or a conversation, usually vivid and sensory (the screech of brakes, the smell of petrol, the weight of a hand on the mouth). DSM-5-TR is careful to separate them from the rumination of depression: the depressed person deliberately and repeatedly turns over a problem, whereas the PTSD memory is involuntary and sensory and feels like the event, not like thinking about it. The second is distressing dreams whose content or feeling is related to the event; some people replay the event exactly, others dream of its themes (being chased, being trapped). The third is the famous flashback, a dissociative reaction in which the person feels or acts as if the event were happening again. DSM-5-TR stresses that flashbacks lie on a continuum. At the mild end there is a brief sensory intrusion while the person knows perfectly well where she is; in the middle, a partial loss of awareness of the present; at the extreme, a complete loss of contact with the surroundings, as when a veteran dives under a table at the sound of a car backfiring and does not recognise his own living room. Flashbacks usually last seconds, rarely longer. The fourth and fifth symptoms are reactions to reminders, or cues: intense psychological distress when something symbolises or resembles the event (a windy day after a cyclone, a man who looks like the attacker), and marked physiological reactions to the same cues (pounding heart, sweating, trembling). A cue can be inside the body as well: dizziness for a survivor of a head injury, a racing heart for a child who was once terrified.

Cluster C: avoidance (one or both of two). Faced with memories that hurt, the person tries to keep them out. Avoidance can be internal (efforts to avoid distressing thoughts, memories or feelings about the event, by keeping busy, distracting oneself, refusing to talk, or drinking) or external (avoiding the people, places, conversations, activities, objects and situations that bring it back). A woman assaulted in an autorickshaw stops using autorickshaws, then stops going out after dark, then stops going out alone. Avoidance is the symptom that does most damage, because, as Section 7.3 will explain, it prevents the fear from ever being unlearned. Only one avoidance symptom is required, but at least one is essential: a person with nightmares and jumpiness who does not avoid anything does not meet the full criteria.

Cluster D: negative alterations in cognitions and mood (two or more of seven). This is the cluster DSM-5 created, and it captures what Barlow and Durand meant by guilt, shame and the loss of a future. The seven symptoms are:

  1. Inability to remember an important aspect of the event, typically because of dissociative amnesia and not because of a head injury, alcohol or drugs. ("I remember him pulling me in, and then I was at the bus stand. I don't know what happened in between.")
  2. Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others or the world: "I am bad," "No one can be trusted," "The world is completely dangerous," "My whole nervous system is permanently ruined," "My life will be cut short."
  3. Persistent, distorted thoughts about the cause or consequences of the event that lead the person to blame himself or others. A mother says, "If I had not sent her to tuition that evening, it would never have happened."
  4. A persistent negative emotional state: fear, horror, anger, guilt or shame.
  5. Markedly diminished interest or participation in significant activities.
  6. Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others: "I am with my family, but it is as if there is a glass wall."
  7. Persistent inability to experience positive emotions: happiness, satisfaction, love, tenderness, sexual feeling.

Two of these deserve a word. The guilt of the second and third symptoms includes the special form called survivor guilt, the painful conviction that one should have saved others or should have died in their place, which Pepys hinted at and which you will see in a child in Section 7.2.6. And the inability to feel positive emotions is the modern name for what older writers called emotional numbing.

Cluster E: alterations in arousal and reactivity (two or more of six). The body behaves as if the danger had never ended. The six symptoms are irritable behaviour and angry outbursts with little or no provocation (shouting, fights, breaking things); reckless or self-destructive behaviour (drunk driving, dangerous speeds, risky sex, heavy drinking or drug use, suicidal acts), a symptom new in DSM-5; hypervigilance, a constant scanning for threat (the survivor of a road accident who watches every lorry, or the assault survivor who sits facing the door in every room); an exaggerated startle response, a large, involuntary jump at a sudden noise such as a telephone ringing; problems with concentration (losing the thread of a conversation, forgetting one's telephone number); and sleep disturbance. DSM-5-TR makes a subtle distinction here. The startle of E4 is a reflex, and the noise that triggers it need have nothing to do with the trauma. The physiological reaction of B5 needs some recognition, however fleeting, that the cue resembles the event. A veteran who jumps at every slammed door has an exaggerated startle; one whose heart races at the smell of diesel because it was the smell of the convoy has a cue reaction. The reckless behaviour of E2 also has limits: it does not include the dangers a soldier or firefighter must take on as part of the job, nor behaviour that is unwise but not physically dangerous, such as gambling or binge eating.

Exam trap

The DSM-5 PTSD clusters are four: intrusion, avoidance, negative alterations in cognitions and mood, and alterations in arousal and reactivity. Not two, three (that was DSM-IV) or five. "Flashbacks, denial, anger, bargaining" is a trap that splices one PTSD symptom onto Elisabeth Kübler-Ross's stages of dying (denial, anger, bargaining, depression, acceptance; Book 2, Section 9.5.2). "Dissociation, anxiety, depression, mania" is not a cluster set at all, and "hyperarousal only" is one cluster pretending to be the whole. Magical thinking and ideas of reference are features of schizotypal personality disorder and of psychosis (Chapters 11 and 4), not a PTSD cluster.

Duration, impairment and the two specifiers

Almost everyone who lives through something terrible has some of these symptoms in the first days and weeks. Sleep goes, memories intrude, the street where it happened is avoided. For most people they fade. That is why criterion F requires that the symptoms of clusters B, C, D and E last more than 1 month. The month is not a magic number, but it separates a common, usually self-limiting reaction from a condition that has settled in. For a current diagnosis, all four clusters must have been present together for at least the past month; for a lifetime diagnosis, there must have been a period of more than a month in which they were all present together. Examination papers often write "at least 1 month"; the intended answer is the same. Criterion G requires clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of life, and criterion H excludes symptoms caused by a substance (a medication, alcohol) or by another medical condition.

Two specifiers can be added. The first describes a subgroup who live with persistent dissociation. A person meets the dissociative subtype (DSM-5-TR's "with dissociative symptoms") when, on top of full PTSD, she has persistent or recurrent depersonalisation (feeling detached from her own mind or body, as if an outside observer, as if in a dream, as if time were moving slowly) or derealisation (the surroundings feel unreal, dreamlike, distant or distorted), not caused by alcohol, drugs or seizures. These two experiences are defined fully in Section 7.5. The subtype was added because studies cited by Barlow and Durand suggested that such patients differ from the usual picture. Erika Wolf and her colleagues (2012) found a subgroup who, instead of the surging arousal of most people with PTSD, shut down, with less arousal than normal and feelings of unreality, and Ruth Lanius and her colleagues (2012) argued that these patients respond somewhat differently to treatment.

The second specifier is with delayed expression. It applies when the full criteria are not met until at least 6 months after the event. The wording is deliberate. DSM-IV called it "delayed onset", which implied that the person had been well for months and then fell ill. Follow-up studies showed that this is rare: most people with a "delayed" disorder had some symptoms from the start, and what was delayed was crossing the full threshold, often after a new stress or a vivid reminder. DSM-5 therefore renamed it, and the DSM-5-TR text adds that symptoms usually begin within the first 3 months after the trauma, even when the diagnosis comes later.

Exam trap

Duration: PTSD needs symptoms in all four clusters for more than 1 month (papers say "at least 1 month"). Not 2 days, 3 days or 1 week (those are too short; 3 days is the start of acute stress disorder) and not 6 months (that is when delayed expression begins). DSM-IV's name "delayed onset" became "delayed expression" in DSM-5. The two DSM-5-TR specifiers are "with dissociative symptoms" and "with delayed expression"; DSM-IV's "acute" and "chronic" specifiers are gone.

PTSD in young children, in adolescents and across cultures

PTSD can begin at any age after the first year of life, but a 3-year-old cannot tell an interviewer that "the world is completely dangerous" or that she feels estranged from her family. DSM-5 therefore wrote a separate set of criteria for children aged 6 years and younger, built on what parents and teachers can see rather than on what the child can say. The routes of exposure are narrower: directly experiencing the event, witnessing it in person (especially when it happens to a primary caregiver), or learning that it happened to a parent or caregiving figure; the occupational route makes no sense for a toddler and is dropped. The child needs at least one intrusion symptom, but the notes change their meaning: intrusive memories may not look distressing and may come out as play re-enactment, and frightening dreams may have no content that can be linked to the event. Avoidance and negative cognitions are merged into one list of six (avoiding activities, places and physical reminders; avoiding people and conversations; more frequent negative emotions such as fear, guilt, sadness, shame or confusion; diminished interest, including constricted play; socially withdrawn behaviour; reduced expression of positive emotions), and only one of the six is needed. Two arousal symptoms are needed from a list of five, which includes extreme temper tantrums and leaves out reckless behaviour. Duration is again more than 1 month, and impairment is judged by relationships with parents, siblings, peers and caregivers, or by behaviour at school.

Barlow and Durand describe a family seen at their clinic, reported by Anne Marie Albano and colleagues in 1997, that shows how differently one event can show itself at different ages. Mrs Betty Jones took her four children, Jeff (8), Marcie (6), Cathy (4) and Susan (2), to visit a friend's farm. As they crossed the yard a large German shepherd charged, knocked Marcie to the ground and tore at her face while the family stood frozen. Jeff finally lunged at the dog and it backed away, but it stood nearby, growling, until it was restrained. At the hospital Marcie, hysterical with fear, had to be strapped to a padded board so that her face could be stitched.

Marcie developed PTSD, and so, unusually, did her brother. Jeff showed classic survivor guilt: he said he should have saved Marcie, or at least put himself between her and the dog. Both children regressed, wetting the bed and suffering nightmares and fears of separation, and Jeff began sucking his fingers again after years. Marcie became terrified of medical procedures and even of having her nails trimmed or being bathed, and she refused to be tucked into bed, a ritual she had always loved, probably because the tight blankets brought back the restraining board. Cathy, at 4, showed fear and avoidance when tested but told the child psychologist she had no problem at all. Susan, at 2, was too young to describe anything, but for months she would suddenly say, out of nowhere, "Doggy bit sister." Their mother had symptoms too. Notice how each child's age shaped the disorder: guilt and responsibility in the 8-year-old, avoidance of reminders in the 6-year-old, behaviour without words in the 4-year-old, and a repetitive phrase in the toddler. Marcie's treatment returns in Section 7.3.

Older children and adolescents meet the adult criteria, with their own colouring. School-age children may re-enact the trauma in repetitive play (DSM-5-TR allows this as an intrusive memory in children older than 6) and may avoid new activities. Adolescents may judge themselves cowardly, believe that the event has changed them in ways that make them socially undesirable, lose their aspirations for the future, or turn to reckless, thrill-seeking behaviour. Irritability and aggression often bring them to attention through trouble with peers or at school rather than through any complaint of fear.

Culture shapes the disorder as much as age does. DSM-5-TR notes that in many non-Western groups avoidance is less often seen, whereas somatic symptoms (dizziness, shortness of breath, heat sensations, pains) are more common, and that anger, distressing dreams and sleep paralysis are frequent across cultures. The meaning of the event matters: being unable to perform funeral rites after a mass killing, or having to live among unpunished perpetrators, can worsen the course. In cultures that place great weight on a family's social image, public shaming can magnify the impact of a sexual assault. And some symptoms may not be symptoms at all in their cultural setting. DSM-5-TR gives the example that blaming oneself after a trauma may be consistent with ideas of karma in South and East Asia, so a survivor who says "this happened because of my past deeds" is not automatically showing the distorted self-blame of criterion D3. Some cultures attribute the whole syndrome to a supernatural cause. Two associated features are worth knowing because they can mislead the unwary: auditory pseudo-hallucinations (hearing one's own thoughts spoken in one or more voices) and paranoid ideation can occur in PTSD without any psychosis.

Indian context

In Indian clinics PTSD often arrives in disguise. A woman who was assaulted may come to the general physician with headaches, body pains, "weakness" and sleeplessness, and say nothing about the event unless she is asked privately and gently. Families anxious about honour may discourage her from speaking at all. A man who survived a riot may describe palpitations and "tension" but not nightmares. The lesson for the interviewer is simple: in anyone with unexplained somatic complaints, ask whether something frightening has happened to them, and ask in a way that makes it safe to answer (trauma-informed care, Book 2, Section 9.7.7).

Exam trap

DSM-5-TR has separate PTSD criteria for children 6 years and younger (not 12 or 18): one intrusion symptom, one symptom from avoidance and negative cognitions taken together, and two arousal symptoms, for more than 1 month. Intrusive memories may appear as play re-enactment, and frightening dreams may have no recognisable content. Self-blame that fits a belief in karma is not automatically a distorted cognition, and somatic complaints are a common way for PTSD to present across cultures.

Acute stress disorder: the first month

Because PTSD cannot be diagnosed until a month has passed, DSM-IV (1994) introduced acute stress disorder for severe reactions in the first month. Barlow and Durand give the practical reason: without a diagnosis, people with severe early reactions could not be formally recognised, and in the United States that meant they could not get insurance coverage for immediate treatment. They also describe it, fairly, as "really PTSD, or something very much like it, occurring within the first month after the trauma", with a different name to emphasise how severe the early reaction can be.

The DSM-IV version was built around dissociation. Its authors believed that dissociating during and just after a trauma (feeling numb and detached, in a daze, unreal, or unable to remember the event) was the key early sign of later PTSD, so the diagnosis required at least three of five dissociative symptoms (numbing or detachment, reduced awareness of the surroundings, derealisation, depersonalisation and dissociative amnesia), with a duration of 2 days to 4 weeks. Research did not bear this out: many people with severe early reactions and later PTSD never dissociated, and they fell through the net. DSM-5 therefore rewrote the criteria. Today criterion A is identical to PTSD's (the same four routes), and criterion B requires nine or more of fourteen symptoms from any of five categories:

  • Intrusion (four symptoms): intrusive memories, distressing dreams, flashbacks, and intense distress or physiological reactions to reminders (a single item here, where PTSD has two).
  • Negative mood (one): persistent inability to experience positive emotions.
  • Dissociation (two): an altered sense of the reality of one's surroundings or oneself (seeing oneself from outside, being in a daze, time slowing down), and inability to remember an important aspect of the event.
  • Avoidance (two): of internal reminders and of external reminders.
  • Arousal (five): sleep disturbance, irritability and angry outbursts, hypervigilance, poor concentration, exaggerated startle.

The words "from any of the five categories" are the heart of the change. No category is compulsory, so a person can now have acute stress disorder without a single dissociative symptom. Criterion C sets the timing: the disturbance lasts from 3 days to 1 month after the trauma. Symptoms usually begin at once, but reactions that settle within 3 days do not count; they are the ordinary, transient shock that almost everyone shows. Criterion D requires distress or impairment, and criterion E excludes substances, medical conditions (DSM-5-TR names mild traumatic brain injury) and brief psychotic disorder.

Jaya, a 24-year-old software tester in Hyderabad, was sexually assaulted by the driver of a cab she had booked home from a late shift. She was seen ten days later at the hospital's crisis centre. "While it was happening I was watching it from somewhere near the roof of the car," she said. "Everything was slow." She could not remember how she had reached the road where a passer-by found her. Since then she had woken every night from dreams of the cab, could not stop the memory of his voice, jumped at the doorbell, and checked the locks again and again. She had not opened her phone's cab app or returned to the office, and she refused to talk about the night even with her mother. She felt nothing when her fiancé visited and snapped at her family over trifles. That is well over nine symptoms (two of them dissociative, though under DSM-5-TR they need not have been), present for more than 3 days and less than a month: acute stress disorder. If the same picture is still present at five weeks, the diagnosis changes to PTSD.

How useful is acute stress disorder as an early warning? Less than its creators hoped. Rates vary with the kind of event: in studies from Australia, the United Kingdom and the United States, fewer than 20 percent of people develop it after traumas that do not involve interpersonal assault (road accidents, mild brain injury, severe burns, industrial accidents), but 19 to 50 percent do after assault or rape (DSM-5-TR). Richard Bryant and his colleagues (2011), in work cited by Barlow and Durand, found that about half of people with acute stress disorder went on to develop PTSD, yet as many as 52 percent of trauma survivors who later developed PTSD had not met criteria for acute stress disorder in the first month. DSM-5-TR puts the same point the other way round: about half of the people who eventually develop PTSD first present with acute stress disorder. The diagnosis identifies people who are suffering badly now and deserve help, but it is not a reliable predictor of who will still be ill in six months. Among the risk factors for acute stress disorder itself, the one DSM-5-TR calls strongly predictive is a tendency to catastrophic appraisals of the trauma: exaggerated beliefs about future harm, guilt or hopelessness; people with the disorder also often read their own flashbacks or numbness as a sign that their mind is failing. Panic attacks are common in the first month, as are reckless or impulsive acts, and children may show marked separation anxiety.

Figure 7.5 puts the time rules of this section on one line, together with the ICD versions taught next.

Exam trap

"Acute stress disorder is distinguished from PTSD primarily by..." its duration of 3 days to 1 month after the trauma. "Required dissociative symptoms" was true only of DSM-IV; DSM-5 dropped the requirement, and today nine of fourteen symptoms from any of five categories will do. Acute stress disorder sits in the trauma- and stressor-related chapter, not the dissociative chapter, and it has nothing to do with "earlier onset of childhood symptoms". Do not confuse it with the ICD's acute stress reaction, which lasts hours to a few days.

The ICD view: acute stress reaction, PTSD and complex PTSD

Indian hospitals have used the World Health Organization's ICD-10 (1992) for decades, and ICD-11 is replacing it, so you need both. ICD-10 placed the trauma disorders in category F43, "reaction to severe stress, and adjustment disorders", inside the block F40 to F48 of neurotic, stress-related and somatoform disorders (Chapter 1 explains the blocks). Three diagnoses matter here.

The first is acute stress reaction (F43.0): a transient disorder of significant severity in a person with no other apparent mental disorder, in response to exceptional physical or mental stress. It begins within minutes of the event, with an initial state of "daze": a narrowing of attention, an inability to take in what is happening, and disorientation. This may be followed by withdrawal from the surroundings (in the extreme, a dissociative stupor) or by agitation and overactivity, with the racing heart, sweating and flushing of panic, and there may be partial amnesia for the episode. The reaction usually subsides within hours or days; where the stress continues, the symptoms begin to diminish after 24 to 48 hours and are usually minimal after about 3 days. Note the time scale: ICD-10's acute stress reaction is over, in most cases, before DSM-5-TR's acute stress disorder can even begin.

The second is post-traumatic stress disorder (F43.1), defined as a delayed or protracted response to a stressful event of an exceptionally threatening or catastrophic nature, likely to cause pervasive distress in almost anyone. Its typical features are repeated reliving of the trauma in intrusive memories ("flashbacks"), dreams or nightmares, against a persisting background of numbness and emotional blunting, detachment from other people, anhedonia and avoidance of reminders, usually with autonomic hyperarousal, hypervigilance, an enhanced startle reaction and insomnia. ICD-10's timing rule is the opposite of DSM's: the disorder should not generally be diagnosed unless it arose within 6 months of the event. The latency between event and onset, says ICD-10, ranges from a few weeks to months but rarely exceeds 6 months, and Oppenheim's old term, traumatic neurosis, survives as one of the terms listed under F43.1. ICD-10 adds that in a small proportion of people it runs a chronic course over many years and turns into the third diagnosis, enduring personality change after catastrophic experience (F62.0). This is a lasting change in personality, present for at least 2 years, after experiences such as concentration camps, torture, disasters or prolonged captivity under threat of death. Its features are a hostile or mistrustful attitude to the world, social withdrawal, feelings of emptiness or hopelessness, a chronic feeling of being on edge as if constantly threatened, and a sense of estrangement. Keep it in mind; it is the ancestor of complex PTSD.

ICD-11 rebuilt the diagnosis with a different philosophy. Its authors noticed that many DSM symptoms (poor sleep, poor concentration, irritability, loss of interest, low mood) are not specific to trauma; they occur just as often in depression and generalised anxiety, and counting them inflates comorbidity. ICD-11 therefore defines PTSD (6B40) by a small number of core elements that are specific to trauma. After exposure to an extremely threatening or horrific event or series of events, the person shows:

  1. Re-experiencing the event in the here and now: vivid intrusive memories, flashbacks or nightmares, typically with strong or overwhelming emotions such as fear or horror and strong physical sensations. The phrase "in the here and now" is the key; the memory must feel as though it is happening again, not merely be remembered with pain.
  2. Avoidance of thoughts and memories of the event, or of activities, situations or people that recall it.
  3. A persistent sense of current threat, shown for example by hypervigilance or an enhanced startle reaction.

The symptoms must last for at least several weeks and cause significant impairment. With only three elements, ICD-11 PTSD is simpler to diagnose than DSM-5-TR's twenty-symptom version, and the two systems do not always pick out the same people.

ICD-11's second trauma disorder is the more important innovation. The diagnosis of complex posttraumatic stress disorder (6B41) grew from the work of the American psychiatrist Judith Lewis Herman, who argued in 1992 that people who have suffered prolonged, repeated trauma from which they could not escape (in captivity, in a violent home, in a prison camp, in childhood abuse) are changed in ways that the ordinary PTSD criteria miss. ICD-11 requires all three elements of PTSD plus three further problems, severe and persistent, which researchers call disturbances in self-organisation:

  1. Affect dysregulation: severe and persistent problems in regulating emotion, such as violent outbursts, inability to calm down, or emotional numbing and dissociation under stress.
  2. Negative self-concept: persistent beliefs that one is diminished, defeated or worthless, with deep feelings of shame, guilt or failure related to the trauma.
  3. Disturbances in relationships: persistent difficulty sustaining relationships and feeling close to others.

The typical histories are prolonged or repeated traumas that are difficult or impossible to escape: torture, slavery, genocide campaigns, prolonged domestic violence, and repeated childhood sexual or physical abuse. Such a history makes complex PTSD likely, but it is not a requirement, and the diagnosis rests on the symptoms. DSM-5-TR has no complex PTSD; it mentions, among the associated features of PTSD, that after prolonged, repeated and severe trauma people may have difficulty regulating emotions and keeping stable relationships, and it offers the dissociative subtype.

Kamala, 38, was married at 17 into a family in a village near Mandya. For eighteen years her husband beat her when he drank, locked her in the storeroom, and once poured kerosene on her and held up a matchbox. She left after he broke her arm and now lives with her brother's family in Mysuru. She has nightmares of his footsteps, freezes at the sound of a motorcycle like his, sleeps lightly and wakes at every sound, and will not go near the bus stand from which the village bus leaves. That much is PTSD. But there is more. Small frustrations send her into storms of anger or into long spells of feeling nothing at all. "I am a useless woman," she says. "Even my own husband could not stand me. Whatever happened, I deserved it." She keeps everyone, even her kind sister-in-law, at a distance, and has refused two offers of work because they would mean meeting new people. These are all three disturbances in self-organisation, persistent and impairing: under ICD-11, complex PTSD. Under DSM-5-TR she would receive a diagnosis of PTSD, with the self-organisation problems described as associated features.

Finally, ICD-11 no longer treats the immediate shock as a disorder. Acute stress reaction (QE84) moved to the chapter on factors influencing health status or contact with health services, among problems associated with harmful or traumatic events: it is the development of transient emotional, somatic, cognitive or behavioural symptoms after an extremely threatening event, which ICD-11 considers a normal response given the severity of the stressor, and which usually begins to subside within a few days. A clinician can record it, and a person can be helped (psychological first aid is the right response, Book 2, Section 9.7.5), but because it sits outside the mental disorders chapter, no mental disorder is diagnosed. Table 7.5 compares the four systems you are expected to know.

Table 7.5 Posttraumatic stress disorder in four classifications.
FeatureDSM-IV (1994)DSM-5-TR (2022)ICD-10 (1992)ICD-11 (2022)
Where it sitsAnxiety disordersTrauma- and stressor-related disordersF43, reaction to severe stress (within F40 to F48)Disorders specifically associated with stress
StressorA1 death, serious injury or threat to physical integrity; A2 fear, helplessness or horrorActual or threatened death, serious injury or sexual violence, by four routes; no A2Exceptionally threatening or catastrophic, distressing to almost anyoneExtremely threatening or horrific event or series of events
SymptomsThree clusters: re-experiencing, avoidance and numbing, arousalFour clusters, 20 symptoms: intrusion, avoidance, cognitions and mood, arousalReliving; numbing and avoidance; hyperarousalThree core elements: re-experiencing here and now, avoidance, sense of current threat
TimingMore than 1 month; acute (under 3 months), chronic (3 months or more), "delayed onset" (6 months)More than 1 month; "delayed expression" at 6 monthsOnset usually within 6 months of the eventAt least several weeks
RelativesAcute stress disorder (new; 2 days to 4 weeks)Acute stress disorder; dissociative subtype; criteria for age 6 and underAcute stress reaction (F43.0); enduring personality change (F62.0)Complex PTSD; acute stress reaction as a normal response

Complex PTSD has a near neighbour that causes endless confusion: borderline personality disorder (taught in Chapter 11). Both can follow childhood abuse; both involve stormy emotions, a poor sense of self and troubled relationships. But the pattern differs. The person with borderline personality disorder fears abandonment frantically and swings between idealising and devaluing the same people; her sense of who she is shifts from week to week; impulsive acts and repeated self-harm or suicidal behaviour are central. The person with complex PTSD has a self-image that is stable and stably negative ("I am worthless"), and she tends to avoid closeness rather than cling to it; repeated self-harm is not part of the definition. A latent class analysis by Marylène Cloitre and her colleagues (2014) found that the two diagnoses picked out largely distinct groups of patients. They can coexist, and the distinction matters, because the treatments differ (dialectical behaviour therapy for borderline personality disorder, Book 2, Section 5.8; trauma-focused treatments for complex PTSD, Section 7.3). Table 7.6 sets the three conditions side by side.

Table 7.6 PTSD, complex PTSD and borderline personality disorder.
FeaturePTSDComplex PTSD (ICD-11)Borderline personality disorder
TraumaRequired; any single or repeated criterion A eventRequired; typically prolonged, repeated, inescapableNot required, though childhood adversity is common
Core symptomsRe-experiencing, avoidance, sense of threat (and in DSM, mood and cognition changes)All PTSD elements plus three disturbances in self-organisationInstability of emotion, self-image and relationships, with impulsivity
Self-imageMay hold negative beliefs about self since the traumaStable and stably negative: worthless, defeated, ashamedUnstable, shifting; chronic emptiness
RelationshipsDetached or estrangedAvoids closeness; hard to feel closeIntense and unstable; frantic efforts to avoid abandonment

Exam trap

ICD-11 PTSD has three core elements (re-experiencing in the here and now, avoidance, persistent sense of current threat); DSM-5-TR PTSD has four clusters. Complex PTSD is an ICD-11 diagnosis, not a DSM-5-TR one: PTSD plus affect dysregulation, negative self-concept and relationship difficulties. ICD-10's acute stress reaction lasts hours to about 3 days, and ICD-11 calls it a normal response and codes it outside the mental disorders chapter. ICD-10 PTSD usually begins within 6 months of the event; DSM-5-TR's delayed expression begins at or after 6 months. The ancestor of complex PTSD in ICD-10 is enduring personality change after catastrophic experience (F62.0).

Telling PTSD apart: differential diagnosis and a worked case

Sameer, 41, is a conductor on a state transport bus between Mysuru and Bengaluru. Seven weeks ago, a little after midnight, his bus struck a motorcycle that had pulled out from a side road. A young couple were thrown under the wheels. Sameer jumped down, knelt beside the woman and held her head while she died; her blood soaked his uniform. He was given a week's leave and went back to work. His wife brought him to the district hospital because, as she put it, "he is not the same man."

At interview Sameer describes, haltingly, what the weeks since have been like. When he closes his eyes he sees the woman's face, and two or three nights a week he wakes from dreams of headlights. When a vehicle sounds its horn behind the bus at night his heart pounds and his hands shake. He asked to be moved to day shifts, takes leave rather than travel the stretch of highway where the crash happened, and changes the subject whenever anyone mentions it. "It was my fault," he says. "I told the driver to hurry because we were late." He no longer goes to the temple festival he used to help organise, and he feels "far away" from his wife and sons. He has shouted at passengers over small change, jumps at every slammed door, sleeps four hours a night, and has started drinking two or three pegs of whisky to fall asleep. There was no head injury; the drinking began a fortnight after the crash, after the nightmares.

Push the case through the criteria. Criterion A: he experienced the crash directly and witnessed a death in person. Cluster B: intrusive memories, nightmares and physiological reactions to cues, three symptoms where one is needed. Cluster C: he avoids both reminders and talk of the event, two of two. Cluster D: distorted self-blame, loss of interest and detachment, three where two are needed. Cluster E: irritability, exaggerated startle and poor sleep, three where two are needed. Criterion F: seven weeks, more than a month. Criterion G: he is impaired at work and at home. Criterion H: the symptoms came before the alcohol, so they are not caused by it, although his drinking needs attention in its own right (Chapter 10). The diagnosis is posttraumatic stress disorder. Had he been seen in the second week, it would have been acute stress disorder; had the same symptoms followed his suspension from work after a quarrel with an inspector, it would have been an adjustment disorder, because a suspension is not a criterion A event.

The diagnosis is not complete until the conditions that resemble PTSD have been considered. DSM-5-TR lists the main ones, and each is distinguished by a specific question:

  • Adjustment disorder (Section 7.4): was the stressor of any severity rather than a criterion A event, or was it a criterion A event that produced only part of the PTSD picture? Either way, adjustment disorder.
  • Acute stress disorder: is it within the first month? Then acute stress disorder, which is changed to PTSD if the picture persists beyond a month.
  • Panic disorder (Chapter 6): are the panic attacks unexpected, or are they set off only by reminders of the trauma? Attacks that occur only after reminders are part of PTSD and do not justify a second diagnosis.
  • Specific phobia (Chapter 6): after a road accident, a person may simply fear and avoid driving, with no intrusions, nightmares or negative change in beliefs; that is a phobia of the situation, not PTSD.
  • Generalised anxiety disorder and obsessive-compulsive disorder (Chapter 6): GAD worry is not tied to an event; OCD's intrusive thoughts are obsessions unrelated to an experienced trauma, usually with compulsions.
  • Major depressive disorder (Chapter 5): depression can follow trauma, but it has no intrusion or avoidance symptoms. If the criteria for both are met, both are diagnosed, and they often are.
  • Psychotic disorders (Chapter 4): a flashback is directly related to the trauma and occurs without other psychotic features, whereas a hallucination is not tied to a past event and comes with other signs of psychosis. Remember that PTSD itself can bring pseudo-hallucinations and paranoid ideas. A short psychosis that follows a severe stress is brief psychotic disorder with marked stressors.
  • Dissociative disorders (Section 7.5): dissociative amnesia, depersonalisation/derealisation disorder and dissociative identity disorder may or may not follow trauma; if full PTSD criteria are also met, consider PTSD with dissociative symptoms.
  • Functional neurological symptom disorder (Section 7.8): new bodily symptoms appearing in the middle of posttraumatic distress may belong to PTSD.
  • Traumatic brain injury (Chapter 10): the same blast or crash can cause both, and both can be present. Re-experiencing and avoidance point to PTSD; persistent disorientation and confusion point to brain injury. A gap in memory for the event is usually a failure of encoding after a head injury, but dissociative amnesia in PTSD.
  • Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (Chapter 3) and personality disorders (Chapter 11): attention problems that began before age 12, or interpersonal problems that were there long before any trauma, are not PTSD; problems that began or worsened sharply after the trauma suggest it.
  • Malingering (Section 7.9): PTSD is often claimed in compensation cases and criminal trials, and its symptoms are easy to read about. Feigning should be considered when there is an external incentive, but it must not be assumed.
Clinical link

Ask about trauma directly; people rarely volunteer it. Then ask about each cluster in plain words ("Do pictures of it come into your mind when you don't want them?", "Are there places you keep away from because of it?", "Has it changed how you see yourself or other people?", "Do you jump at sudden noises?"). Always ask about alcohol and about thoughts of suicide, because both are common companions of PTSD (Section 7.3). Structured interviews and self-report checklists for PTSD exist and are taught in the Assessment book.

Exam trap

A flashback is not a hallucination: it is a dissociative re-living of a real past event, linked to reminders, usually lasting seconds. Panic attacks that occur only on exposure to trauma reminders belong to PTSD, not to panic disorder. A PTSD-like picture after a non-criterion A stressor (divorce, dismissal) is an adjustment disorder. Depression and PTSD often coexist, and both can be diagnosed.

7.3 Posttraumatic Stress Disorder: Who Develops It, Why, and How It Is TreatedLower yield · 1 Q

Barlow and Durand begin their account of the causes of PTSD with a sentence that sounds like the end of the story: PTSD is the one disorder for which we know the cause, at least in terms of the precipitating event. Someone experiences a trauma and develops a disorder. Yet the sentence is really the beginning of a puzzle. Most people who live through a trauma never develop PTSD, and some who live through the worst experiences imaginable come out of them psychologically intact, while others break down after events that seem far less severe. This section is about that puzzle: how common the disorder is, what separates the people who develop it from those who do not, what happens in the body and the mind of those who do, how they can be helped, and why some people say that the worst thing that ever happened to them also changed them for the better.

How common is PTSD?

The best figures come from large American surveys, summarised in DSM-5-TR. About 6.8 percent of adults in the United States meet the DSM-IV criteria for PTSD at some point in their lives. Using DSM-5 criteria, two national surveys put the lifetime figure at 6.1 to 8.3 percent and the 12-month figure at 4.7 percent. Across the World Mental Health Surveys of 24 countries, lifetime prevalence varied widely but was 3.9 percent overall. In populations affected by conflict, the point prevalence of PTSD with functional impairment is about 11 percent. Rates are raised among veterans and in jobs that expose people to trauma (police, firefighters, emergency medical staff), and they are highest of all, from one-third to more than one-half of those exposed, among survivors of rape, military combat and captivity, and politically or ethnically motivated internment and genocide.

Women are about twice as likely as men to develop PTSD: lifetime prevalence is 8.0 to 11.0 percent in women and 4.1 to 5.4 percent in men in two large DSM-5 surveys. Why? Part of the answer is exposure. Men experience more traumatic events overall (accidents, combat, physical assault), but women are more often exposed to childhood sexual abuse, sexual assault and other interpersonal violence, which carry the highest risk of PTSD of any trauma. Part of the answer is something else, because the difference persists even when men and women who have faced the same kind of stressor are compared. DSM-5-TR points to gender differences in how trauma is processed emotionally and cognitively, and to the effects of reproductive hormones. Women in the general population also stay ill longer than men. The symptom profile itself is the same in both sexes.

What about India? No national survey has measured PTSD in the general population, but Indian researchers have studied it after several of the country's disasters, and the figures they found illustrate everything in this section. The Bhopal gas leak of December 1984 was the first great test. A survey by B. B. Sethi and his colleagues, carried out between February and May 1985 among 855 people attending clinics in the affected area, found psychiatric disorder in 22.6 percent, mostly depressive and anxiety neuroses and adjustment reactions; PTSD was not among the diagnoses reported. After the super cyclone that struck Odisha in October 1999, Nilamadhab Kar and his colleagues (2007) examined 447 children and adolescents a year later and found PTSD in 30.6 percent: 43.7 percent in the areas that were hit hardest against 11.2 percent in the less exposed areas, a dose-response gradient of exactly the kind described below. Two months after the Indian Ocean tsunami of December 2004, a survey of the adults of a severely affected fishing village in Cuddalore district, Tamil Nadu, found PTSD in 12.7 percent (Kumar and colleagues, 2007). After the Gujarat earthquake of January 2001, 24.8 percent of 128 children aged 8 to 15 from badly affected sites scored above the cut-off for probable PTSD (Kumar and Fonagy, 2013). And in the Kashmir valley, a household survey of 5,519 adults carried out by Médecins Sans Frontières in 2015 found probable PTSD in 19 percent (Housen and colleagues, 2017). These studies used different methods, so their figures cannot be compared directly, but the pattern is consistent: high rates where exposure was heaviest and most personal, and lower rates elsewhere.

The dose-response puzzle

If trauma causes PTSD, you might expect that counting the traumatised would tell you how many have the disorder. The early studies were a surprise. During the Second World War, the people of London endured months of night bombing. Psychiatrists had predicted mass panic and a flood of breakdowns. It never came. Reviewing the evidence decades later, Stanley Rachman concluded that "a great majority of people endured the air raids extraordinarily well": short-lived fear reactions were common, but repeated bombing did not produce a significant increase in psychiatric disorder, and few lasting phobic reactions emerged. Similar results followed fires, earthquakes and floods.

Key study: the Beirut students (Saigh, 1984)

Phillip Saigh was teaching at the American University of Beirut in the early 1980s and happened to be collecting anxiety questionnaires from his students just before the Israeli invasion of Lebanon in 1982. When the invasion began, about half the students fled to the safety of the mountains; the other half stayed in the city and endured intense shelling and bombing. Saigh kept giving the questionnaires. The result was a natural experiment with a baseline that no planned study could have arranged. Over the long term there were no significant differences between the two groups. The few city students who did develop reactions that progressed to PTSD were those who had been closely exposed to danger and death. The conclusion: living in a city under bombardment is not, by itself, the dose that produces PTSD. Close, personal exposure to death and injury is.

Other studies found exactly that. Among Vietnam veterans, 18.7 percent developed PTSD, and the rates rose in direct proportion to the amount of combat each man had seen (Dohrenwend and colleagues, 2006). About 67 percent of American prisoners of war in Vietnam, men subjected to long-term deprivation and torture, developed PTSD (Foy and colleagues, 1987). This is a dose-response relationship: the more intense and the more personal the exposure, the higher the risk. Children with burns develop PTSD in proportion to the severity of the burns and the pain they cause. After the attacks of 11 September 2001 in New York, Sandro Galea and his colleagues (2002) surveyed a representative sample of adults living in Manhattan south of 110th Street: 7.5 percent reported symptoms consistent with PTSD, but among those who lived close to the World Trade Center, south of Canal Street, the figure was 20 percent. The people who experienced the disaster most personally and most directly were the most affected. And when the United States sent its forces to Iraq and Afghanistan, military planners, remembering Vietnam, feared PTSD rates of 30 percent or more. A study of more than 47,000 service members found that 4.3 percent developed PTSD: 7.6 percent of those who had been in combat against 1.4 percent of those who had not (Smith and colleagues, 2008). Barlow and Durand suggest that better recognition, earlier treatment and better social support explain why these veterans fared better than those of earlier wars.

The clearest single picture of dose and type comes from the community surveys of Naomi Breslau and her colleagues, who asked a random sample of 2,181 adults aged 18 to 45 in the Detroit area (Breslau and colleagues, 1998) which traumas they had experienced and then worked out, for each kind of trauma, what proportion of the people exposed to it developed PTSD. This proportion is the conditional risk: the risk of the disorder given that the event has happened. Table 7.7 shows the results that Barlow and Durand reproduce.

Table 7.7 Conditional risk of PTSD after specific traumas: the percentage of people exposed to each event who developed PTSD (Breslau's community survey in Detroit, as reproduced by Barlow and Durand). Some figures rest on small numbers and are only approximate.
Group of traumasEventPercent with PTSD
Assaultive violenceHeld captive, tortured or kidnapped53.8
Assaultive violenceRape49.0
Assaultive violenceBadly beaten up31.9
Assaultive violenceSexual assault other than rape23.7
Assaultive violenceShot or stabbed15.4
Assaultive violenceMugged or threatened with a weapon8.0
Assaultive violenceAll assaultive violence20.9
Sudden deathSudden, unexpected death of a loved one14.3

Other injury or shockWitnessing a killing or serious injury7.3

Other injury or shockSerious car crash2.3
