Three lessons stand out. First, the type of trauma matters more than almost anything else: deliberate violence by another person, above all rape and captivity or torture, carries risks several times those of accidents and disasters. Second, even after rape, about half of the women and men exposed do not develop PTSD. Third, a single common event can produce a large share of all cases. Sudden, unexpected death of a loved one carries only a moderate risk, but it is so common (60 percent of the people in Breslau's survey had experienced it) that it accounted for about 31 percent of all the PTSD cases (Breslau and colleagues, 1998). Repetition matters too: compared with women who have never been assaulted, the rates of PTSD are 2.4 to 3.5 times higher in women who have experienced a single sexual assault or rape and 4.3 to 8.2 times higher in those who have been assaulted again (Walsh and colleagues, 2012).

Exam trap

"PTSD is the one disorder whose precipitating cause is known." True, yet the majority of people exposed to trauma do not develop it. Exposure is necessary, not sufficient. Proximity and type matter most: assaultive violence (rape, captivity, torture) carries the highest conditional risk, natural disasters and accidents much lower risks. Do not answer that "most people exposed to a disaster develop PTSD"; resilience is the commonest outcome.

Course and outcome

For many people, PTSD is a temporary state. DSM-5-TR reports that about half of adults with PTSD recover completely within 3 months. For others it lasts: some remain symptomatic for more than 12 months, and some for more than 50 years, like the elderly veterans and Holocaust survivors who still wake from nightmares of events half a century old. Among those who do not recover in the first months, PTSD tends to run a chronic course (Breslau, 2012). Symptoms come and go in intensity, and they flare in response to reminders of the original trauma (an anniversary, a news report, a court hearing), to new life stresses and to new traumatic events. In older people, declining health, failing memory and social isolation can bring back symptoms that had been quiet for years, and PTSD in later life is linked with poor health and frequent visits to doctors.

The disorder is costly. People with PTSD are more likely than others to meet criteria for at least one other mental disorder, especially depression, other anxiety disorders, bipolar disorder and substance use disorders (Chapter 10 teaches the latter, and much drinking after trauma is an attempt to sleep or to blot out memories). PTSD is associated with poor relationships, absence from work, lower income and lower educational and occupational success, and with physical health problems and an increased risk of major neurocognitive disorder in later life. Most seriously, it raises the risk of suicide. Holly Wilcox, Carla Storr and Naomi Breslau (2009) found that a diagnosis of PTSD predicted later suicide attempts independently of other problems such as alcohol use, and DSM-5-TR adds that PTSD increases the likelihood of moving from suicidal thoughts to a plan or an attempt, independently of any mood disorder. Every person with PTSD should be asked about suicide (Chapter 5).

Set beside these figures one more finding. When George A. Bonanno (2004) reviewed studies that followed people over time after bereavement and trauma, he described four typical trajectories: resilience (functioning that stays fairly stable and healthy throughout, with only brief, mild disturbance), recovery (clear symptoms that gradually return to normal over months), chronic distress that persists, and delayed reactions in which adjustment seems normal at first and distress rises later. His central claim was that resilience is not rare and heroic but the commonest outcome, and that it is different from recovery. In this sense, resilience is the ability to keep relatively stable, healthy functioning after a highly disruptive event. The point matters for practice: if most people will do well without professional help, then pushing everyone into treatment after a disaster wastes resources and may do harm (Section 7.3.7).

Exam trap

About half of adults with PTSD recover completely within 3 months (DSM-5-TR), yet some stay ill for more than 50 years. The commonest outcome after trauma is resilience (Bonanno), not PTSD. PTSD predicts suicide attempts independently of depression and alcohol use, so a question that treats PTSD as a "minor, self-limiting" condition is wrong.

Before, during and after: risk and protective factors

Why does one person develop PTSD after an event that another shrugs off? DSM-5-TR sorts the answers by time: pretraumatic factors that the person brings to the event, peritraumatic factors that belong to the event itself and the person's reaction during it, and posttraumatic factors that come afterwards. Figure 7.6 lays them out on a time line.

Before the trauma. DSM-5-TR's pretraumatic risk factors include emotional problems in childhood by the age of 6 (anxiety or acting out), prior mental disorders (panic disorder, depression, earlier PTSD, obsessive-compulsive disorder), and personality traits of negative affectivity or neuroticism, the tendency to respond to life with anxiety and low mood. Environmental risks include lower socioeconomic status and education, earlier trauma (especially in childhood), childhood adversity such as poverty, family dysfunction or the loss of a parent, lower intelligence, discrimination, and a family history of psychiatric disorder. Some of these factors work in a surprising way. Naomi Breslau, Glenn Davis and Patricia Andreski (1995) followed a random sample of about 1,200 young adults and found that a tendency to be anxious, and limited education, predicted exposure to traumatic events in the first place. Breslau and her colleagues later showed that 6-year-olds with externalising (acting-out) problems were more likely to meet assaults and other traumas, probably because of their behaviour, and then to develop PTSD, while higher intelligence predicted less exposure. A person's vulnerabilities, some of them partly heritable, help to decide what kind of life events he will meet. Family instability is another pretraumatic risk: in a study of more than 1,600 male and female Vietnam veterans it predicted PTSD after the war (King and colleagues, 1996). Barlow and Durand argue that an unstable family teaches a child that the world is unpredictable and uncontrollable, which is exactly the psychological vulnerability that makes a later trauma more damaging. But the weight of these factors depends on the dose. Among the prisoners of war studied by Foy and his colleagues, the trauma was so extreme that prior vulnerability hardly mattered; at lower levels of trauma, it matters a great deal.

Key study: preparation for torture (Başoğlu and colleagues, 1997)

Metin Başoğlu and his colleagues studied two groups of torture survivors in Turkey. One group of 55 were political activists, committed to a cause, who had expected arrest and torture. The other group of 34 had no political involvement and no expectation of being tortured. The non-activists had been subjected to less horrific abuse, yet they showed more psychopathology. The activists, it seemed, were psychologically prepared: for them the torture was predictable, and it fitted a set of beliefs that gave it meaning. Predictability and a sense of purpose protected them, a reminder that the same event has different effects on different minds.

During the trauma. The peritraumatic factors are, first, the severity of the event (the dose), the person's perception that his life was in danger, physical injury, and interpersonal violence, especially violence by a caregiver or a threat to a caregiver witnessed by a child. For military personnel, being the perpetrator, witnessing atrocities and killing the enemy carry special risk. Second come the person's reactions during the event: fear, panic and especially peritraumatic dissociation, the experience during or immediately after the trauma of being dazed, detached or unreal, of time slowing down, or of watching oneself from outside, as Jaya did in Section 7.2.7. In a meta-analysis of 68 studies, Emily Ozer and her colleagues (2003) found that peritraumatic dissociation was the strongest single predictor of later PTSD among the factors they examined. (Dissociation as a general phenomenon is taught in Section 7.5.)

After the trauma. The posttraumatic factors include negative appraisals ("I will never be safe again", "this means I am weak"), avoidant or otherwise unhelpful coping, the development of acute stress disorder, repeated upsetting reminders, further adverse events, financial and other losses, and, across cultures, forced migration and heavy daily stress. A meta-analysis by Chris Brewin, Bernice Andrews and John Valentine (2000) found that factors operating after the trauma, above all a lack of social support and continuing life stress, predicted PTSD more strongly than most factors operating before it.

Social support is the most consistent protective factor of all, and its effect has been seen across cultures. Eric Vernberg, Annette La Greca, Wendy Silverman and Mitchell Prinstein (1996) studied 568 elementary school children 3 months after Hurricane Andrew struck south Florida in 1992. More than 55 percent reported moderate to severe PTSD symptoms. The children who were protected were those with support from parents, close friends, classmates and teachers, and those who coped by active problem solving; children who became angry and blamed others had more symptoms. When La Greca and her colleagues (2010) followed children at 9 and 21 months after Hurricane Charley hit Florida in 2004, strong social support again reduced the persistence of posttraumatic symptoms. Why should friends matter to a frightened brain? Barlow and Durand point to studies in which support from loved ones reduces cortisol secretion and the activity of the stress-hormone system in children under stress. They also suggest that the lack of a welcome for veterans returning from Vietnam, compared with those from Iraq and Afghanistan, may help to explain why so many more of them developed PTSD.

Exam trap

Peritraumatic dissociation (feeling dazed or unreal during the event) raises the risk of PTSD; it is not protective. Social support is the most consistent protective factor, and factors that operate after the trauma, such as lack of support and further stress, predict PTSD at least as strongly as most of those before it. Some pretraumatic traits, such as anxiousness and limited education, predict being exposed to trauma in the first place (Breslau, Davis and Andreski, 1995).

Genes, stress hormones and the brain

Genes. If vulnerability matters, some of it may be inherited. William True and his colleagues (1993) used the Vietnam Era Twin Registry, a register of male twin pairs who had both served in the American forces during the Vietnam era, to ask whether identical twins resemble each other in PTSD more than fraternal twins do, after allowing for how much combat each had seen. They did. In 4,042 male twin pairs, genetic factors accounted for roughly 13 to 34 percent of the variation in the different groups of symptoms. Given the same amount of combat exposure, the correlation of PTSD symptoms was 0.28 to 0.41 in identical twins against 0.11 to 0.24 in fraternal twins, which suggests a genetic influence. DSM-5-TR summarises later work in the same terms: the risk of PTSD after trauma is modestly heritable in twin and molecular studies, a genome-wide association study in a large multi-ethnic sample found three robust risk loci that vary with ancestry, and epigenetic changes (alterations in how genes are switched on and off, produced by experience) may also play a part.

No one thinks that a gene for PTSD makes people ill. The likelier story is the diathesis-stress model (Book 2, Section 1.7): genes make some people more easily stressed and anxious, and that makes a trauma more likely to tip them into the disorder. A study after a shooting on the campus of Northern Illinois University in 2008 illustrates the idea. Among female students who had witnessed the same event, those who carried two short alleles of the serotonin transporter gene (the same variant linked to depression after stress, Chapter 5) had more acute stress symptoms, even when the amount of exposure was equal (Mercer and colleagues, 2012). Single-gene findings of this kind have a poor record of replication in psychiatry, so treat this one as an illustration of the principle rather than a settled fact.

The stress-hormone system. When we are threatened, the hypothalamus releases corticotropin-releasing factor (CRF), which makes the pituitary gland release adrenocorticotropic hormone, which makes the adrenal glands release cortisol; cortisol then feeds back on the brain to switch the system off. This is the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, or HPA axis (its anatomy belongs to the Physiological book). At the same time the sympathetic nervous system releases noradrenaline, which drives the racing heart and vigilance of fear. In PTSD both systems are disturbed. Barlow and Durand describe abnormal CRF activity indicating a dysregulated HPA axis, and raised CRF has been found in the cerebrospinal fluid of people with PTSD. The cortisol story is more surprising. In the 1990s Rachel Yehuda and her colleagues reported that people with PTSD often had low rather than high cortisol, together with more glucocorticoid receptors on their white blood cells and an exaggerated suppression of cortisol after a small dose of the synthetic steroid dexamethasone: signs of an enhanced negative feedback, a stress system that switches itself off too hard. Later work has been less consistent. A meta-analysis of 37 studies (Meewisse and colleagues, 2007) found no overall difference in basal cortisol between people with and without PTSD, with lower levels only in some subgroups, such as women and survivors of abuse. The safe summary is that the HPA axis is dysregulated in PTSD, with raised CRF and altered feedback, but that "low cortisol" is not a reliable marker of the disorder. The noradrenaline system appears overactive, which is the reasoning behind two of the drugs in Section 7.3.8.

The hippocampus. The hippocampus helps to regulate the HPA axis and is central to learning and to memory for context: where and when something happened. Brain-imaging studies found smaller or damaged hippocampi in veterans with war-related PTSD (Gurvits and colleagues, 1996), adult survivors of childhood physical and sexual abuse (Bremner and colleagues, 1997) and firefighters exposed to extreme trauma, and memory problems in Gulf War veterans and in Holocaust survivors with PTSD compared with survivors without it. Barlow and Durand offer a plausible chain: chronic stress hormones damage the hippocampus, and a damaged hippocampus fails both to restrain the stress system (so arousal stays high) and to put memories in context (so the trauma memory floats free of its time and place). There is even a hint that the damage can be reversed. Monica Starkman and her colleagues (1999) studied patients whose hippocampi had been affected by Cushing's disease, in which the adrenal glands pour out cortisol; after successful treatment, hippocampal volume increased by up to 10 percent.

But is a small hippocampus the result of PTSD, or a risk factor that was there before the trauma? Mark W. Gilbertson and his colleagues (2002) answered the question with 40 pairs of identical twins from the Vietnam Era Twin Registry, pairs in which one brother had gone to war and the other had not. In pairs in which the combat veteran had severe PTSD, his brother, who had never seen combat, also had a smaller hippocampus. A small hippocampus therefore seems to be, at least in part, a pre-existing risk factor that makes a person more vulnerable to PTSD after trauma, rather than only a scar left by it. The two explanations are not exclusive: a vulnerable hippocampus may also be harmed further by years of stress.

The fear circuit. Brain imaging suggests that three regions work abnormally together in PTSD (Shin, Rauch and Pitman, 2006). The amygdala, which detects threat and triggers fear, is overactive, responding strongly to reminders and even to fearful faces. The medial prefrontal cortex, which normally damps the amygdala down and is needed for extinction (learning that a cue is now safe), is underactive. And the hippocampus, which tells the brain whether the present context is dangerous or safe, is smaller or works less well. Put together, the picture is of an alarm that is too sensitive, a brake that is too weak and a context system that cannot tell "then" from "now". That matches the symptoms closely: cue reactivity and startle, fear that will not extinguish, and memories that feel as though they are happening in the present.

Exam trap

The brain findings in PTSD are an overactive amygdala, an underactive medial prefrontal cortex and a smaller hippocampus, with a dysregulated HPA axis. Do not reverse them ("enlarged hippocampus", "underactive amygdala"). A smaller hippocampus may be partly a pre-existing risk factor rather than only a result of trauma.

Learning, alarms and broken beliefs: psychological accounts

Conditioning. The oldest psychological account treats PTSD as fear conditioning of a very powerful kind. The trauma is an unconditioned stimulus that produces terror; everything present at the time (the smell of diesel, the dark, a man's voice, the sound of a horn) becomes a conditioned stimulus that can produce the same terror later, and the fear generalises to similar cues. Orval Hobart Mowrer's two-factor theory (Book 2, Section 4.1) explains why the fear does not fade: the survivor avoids the cues, the avoidance is rewarded by relief, and because she never stays with the cues long enough to discover that nothing terrible happens, extinction never takes place. Terence Keane and his colleagues applied this two-factor account to the combat veterans they treated in the 1980s. The account explains cue reactivity and avoidance well. It explains less well the intrusive memories that come out of nowhere, the guilt and shame, and why only some of those exposed fall ill.

Barlow's model. David Barlow built a model that links PTSD to the panic attacks of Chapter 6 (it is drawn as a figure in his textbook with Durand). A panic attack, he argued, is a false alarm: the body's emergency reaction firing when there is no danger. In a trauma the alarm is true, because the danger is real. If the true alarm is intense enough (or if the trauma produces other overwhelming emotions such as rage or distress), a learned alarm becomes conditioned to anything that recalls the event, as being tucked into bed recalled the restraining board for Marcie. The person then develops anxious apprehension, a dread of the re-experienced emotions themselves (the flashbacks and surges of fear), and responds with avoidance or numbing of emotion. Whether this sequence ends in PTSD depends on the person's generalised biological and psychological vulnerabilities and is moderated, at every step, by social support and the ability to cope.

Emotional processing and the cognitive model. Two further accounts are taught in Book 2 because each produced a treatment. Edna Foa and Michael Kozak's emotional processing theory (1986) holds that the trauma lays down a fear structure in memory, and recovery requires that structure to be activated and corrected by new information; avoidance prevents both (Book 2, Section 4.3.6). Anke Ehlers and David M. Clark's cognitive model (2000) explains a puzzle: the trauma is in the past, yet the person feels under threat now. The sense of current threat comes from excessively negative appraisals of the trauma and its aftermath ("nowhere is safe", "it was my fault", "I am going mad") and from a trauma memory that is poorly elaborated and stored as vivid sensory fragments without the context of time and place, so it is easily triggered and re-lived with a sense of "nowness". Avoidance, suppression, rumination and safety behaviours keep both from being corrected (Book 2, Section 5.6.5).

Shattered assumptions. The social psychologist Ronnie Janoff-Bulman, in Shattered Assumptions (1992), asked what a trauma does to a person's view of the world. Most of us, she argued, live by three basic assumptions that we never put into words: that the world is benevolent (people are mostly good and things mostly turn out well), that the world is meaningful (what happens to people makes sense and depends on what they do), and that the self is worthy (I am a decent, capable person who deserves good things). A trauma shatters them all at once (hence the name of the theory, shattered assumptions), and recovery means building a new view of the world that can hold the trauma inside it. The theory explains two of the cognitive symptoms of cluster D. The belief that "no one can be trusted" is the benevolent world lost. And self-blame, which seems so irrational, can be an attempt to save the meaningful world: if it was my fault, then the world still makes sense, and I can stop it happening again. That is why cognitive processing therapy spends so long on the client's "stuck points", and why rebuilt assumptions are where post-traumatic growth begins (Section 7.3.9).

Clinical link

Each model points to the same practical conclusion: the things a survivor does to feel safe in the short run (avoiding reminders, refusing to talk, drinking to sleep, staying on guard) are the very things that keep the disorder going. Treatment therefore asks the person, gently and at a pace she controls, to turn towards the memory and the reminders, and to examine the conclusions she drew from the trauma. That is why the effective treatments in the next subsection all involve some form of exposure or cognitive processing, and why simple reassurance and advice to "forget it" fail.

Exam trap

In Mowrer's two-factor account, fear of trauma reminders is acquired by classical conditioning and maintained by avoidance, which is negatively reinforced by relief and blocks extinction. In Barlow's model the alarm in PTSD is a true alarm (real danger), whereas in panic disorder it is a false alarm. Ehlers and Clark explain persistence by a sense of current threat; Janoff-Bulman's three shattered assumptions are a benevolent world, a meaningful world and a worthy self.

Psychological treatment: prevention, trauma-focused therapy and special groups

Most clinicians agree on the principle of psychological treatment for PTSD: the survivor must face the trauma, process the intense emotions bound up with it, and develop effective ways of coping (Barlow and Durand). Psychoanalysts called the reliving of emotional trauma to relieve suffering catharsis, a term that goes back to Breuer (Section 7.1). The difficulty is to arrange the re-exposure so that it heals rather than re-traumatises. A traumatic event, unlike a spider or a lift, cannot be brought into the consulting room, and few therapists would want to try. So most treatments work with the memory itself: the survivor builds a detailed narrative of what happened, which is reviewed again and again in therapy, and the therapist helps her to question the conclusions she drew from it, especially self-blame and guilt. The psychotherapies themselves are taught in Book 2; here the question is what works, for whom and when.

Early intervention. Because about half of the people with early symptoms recover on their own, the first task after a trauma is not to treat everyone but to support everyone and watch for those who are not recovering. Psychological first aid (Book 2, Section 9.7.5) provides safety, comfort, practical help and connection without pushing anyone to talk. One popular approach turned out to be a mistake. The approach was psychological debriefing, a single session soon after the event in which every survivor is asked to describe the trauma and express his feelings, whether or not he is distressed. It does not prevent PTSD, and in some trials it has made people worse (Ehlers and Clark, 2003; the full story is in Book 2, Section 9.7.6). The guideline of the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) for England (2018) is explicit: psychologically focused debriefing should not be offered to prevent or treat PTSD. For people who are clearly struggling, by contrast, a short course of structured, trauma-focused treatment works. In a study by Anke Ehlers and her colleagues (2003) of people who had developed PTSD after frightening road accidents, only 11 percent still had PTSD after up to 12 sessions of cognitive therapy, compared with 61 percent of those given a detailed self-help booklet and 55 percent of those who were simply assessed repeatedly. In the Jerusalem Trauma Outreach and Prevention Study, Arieh Shalev and his colleagues (2012) offered treatment to people with early PTSD symptoms within weeks of a trauma: at 5 months about 20 percent of those given prolonged exposure or cognitive therapy had PTSD, against about 58 percent of those on a waiting list, whereas the antidepressant escitalopram did no better than placebo. (Those on the waiting list who then received prolonged exposure had caught up by 9 months, so early treatment speeds recovery rather than being the only chance of it.) Psychological prevention, it seems, beats medication.

Key study: Marcie's hierarchy (Albano and colleagues, 1997)

Six-year-old Marcie, bitten by the dog in Section 7.2.6, was treated together with her brother Jeff. Her main problem was terror of doctors and of any examination, so her therapists built a hierarchy from mild to severe: having her pulse taken, a tongue depressor in her throat, a stethoscope on her chest, a plaster on a cut, lying on an examination table, a chest X-ray, an electrocardiogram and, at the top, being strapped to a board. Because she was too young to re-create the memory in imagination, the exposure was done in real life. First she watched Jeff go through each step calmly; then she tried it herself, while the therapist photographed her, she drew pictures of the situations, and her family praised each success. Every item fell from a fear rating of 1 to 4 before treatment to 0 after it. Her PTSD was successfully treated, and Jeff's guilt fell markedly because he had helped to make his sister well. The case shows modelling, graded real-life exposure and family involvement working together in a young child, the core of trauma-focused cognitive behaviour therapy for children (Book 2, Section 10.3.3).

Established PTSD. For adults with established PTSD, the treatments with the strongest evidence are all trauma-focused; that is, they work directly with the memory of the trauma and its meaning. The first is prolonged exposure, developed by Edna Foa and her colleagues, which combines repeated imaginal revisiting of the memory with graded real-life exposure to avoided situations (Book 2, Section 4.3.6). The second, cognitive processing therapy, developed by Patricia A. Resick and her colleagues for rape survivors, works chiefly on the beliefs the trauma left behind (safety, trust, power, esteem, intimacy), and Ehlers and Clark's cognitive therapy for PTSD updates the meaning of the worst moments of the memory (both in Book 2, Section 5.6.5). In eye movement desensitisation and reprocessing (EMDR), developed by Francine Shapiro, the client holds the memory in mind while making side-to-side eye movements; the method works about as well as other trauma-focused therapies, though whether the eye movements add anything to the exposure is still debated (Book 2, Section 4.4.5). Stress inoculation training, Donald Meichenbaum's programme of coping skills, has some support, mostly from studies of women after sexual assault (Book 2, Section 5.5.2). Most guidelines recommend trauma-focused psychotherapy as the first-line treatment, ahead of medication.

The gains last. Barlow and Durand report that 144 women treated for PTSD after rape with these evidence-based therapies were reassessed 5 to 10 years later, and their large improvements had been maintained with very little change (Resick and colleagues, 2012). Because PTSD so often damages close relationships, and relationship strain can bring relapse, Candice Monson and her colleagues (2012) treated 40 couples, heterosexual and same-sex, in which one partner had PTSD, with the partner taking part in every session; PTSD symptoms improved, and so did satisfaction with the relationship.

Complex PTSD and comorbid problems. For complex PTSD, the International Society for Traumatic Stress Studies (ISTSS) published expert consensus guidelines in 2012, built on a survey of experts led by Marylène Cloitre (2011), which recommended a phase-based treatment: first safety and stabilisation (getting out of danger and learning skills to manage emotions, dissociation and daily life), then processing of the trauma memories, then consolidation and reintegration into ordinary life. Later guidelines are more cautious. The ISTSS guidelines of 2019 made no specific recommendation for complex PTSD because the trials were too few, and NICE (2018) speaks not of phases but of allowing extra time to build trust, attending to the person's safety and stability, and helping with dissociation, emotional dysregulation and interpersonal difficulties. Many people with complex presentations do benefit from the standard trauma-focused therapies, so stabilisation should prepare for trauma work, not postpone it indefinitely. When PTSD comes with heavy drinking or drug use, both problems are treated together rather than one after the other (Chapter 10), and depression and suicidal thinking need attention in their own right. Back in Mysuru, Kamala's treatment began with safety: help from a women's organisation, a protection order under the Protection of Women from Domestic Violence Act, 2005, and practical help to find work she could manage. Only then did her psychologist start trauma-focused work.

Indian context

Most survivors of disasters, riots and violence in India will never see a psychologist, and the country could never train enough specialists to see them all. The realistic model is the one the international guidelines recommend: basic services and security for everyone, community and family support for many, psychological first aid and focused, non-specialised support for some, and specialist trauma-focused treatment for the few who need it (the IASC pyramid, Book 2, Section 9.7.5).

Exam trap

Who developed what: prolonged exposure, Edna Foa; cognitive processing therapy, Patricia Resick; cognitive therapy for PTSD, Anke Ehlers and David Clark; EMDR, Francine Shapiro; stress inoculation training, Donald Meichenbaum. The first-line treatments for PTSD are trauma-focused psychotherapies. Early cognitive therapy after a road accident left 11 percent with PTSD, against 61 percent with a self-help booklet (Ehlers and colleagues, 2003).

Drug and other physical treatments

Drugs have a smaller place in PTSD than in depression or panic disorder. Most guidelines put trauma-focused psychotherapy first and reserve medication for people who prefer it, who cannot reach or cannot yet engage in psychotherapy, or who also have a depression that needs treating. Still, a psychologist needs to know what the drugs are, what they do and what they should not be used for, because many survivors in India will be seen first by a physician or psychiatrist, and some will arrive already taking the wrong medicine.

Antidepressants. The first-line drugs are the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), which block the reuptake of serotonin into the nerve terminal and so increase its action at the synapse (their pharmacology is taught with depression in Chapter 5). Barlow and Durand note that SSRIs such as fluoxetine (Prozac) and paroxetine (Paxil), effective for anxiety disorders in general, help in PTSD, perhaps by relieving the severe anxiety and panic that the disorder brings. Only two drugs have been approved by the United States Food and Drug Administration for PTSD, and both are SSRIs: sertraline (1999) and paroxetine (2001). The serotonin-noradrenaline reuptake inhibitor venlafaxine is the third drug that the United States Department of Veterans Affairs and Department of Defense guideline (2023) recommends, and the NICE guideline (2018) suggests considering venlafaxine or an SSRI such as sertraline when a person prefers drug treatment. These drugs take several weeks to work, and the benefit is real but modest; side effects include nausea, sleep disturbance, sexual dysfunction and, with paroxetine and venlafaxine especially, unpleasant symptoms if they are stopped suddenly.

Prazosin for nightmares. The drug prazosin is an old blood-pressure drug that blocks alpha-1 adrenergic receptors, one of the receptors through which noradrenaline acts, and it enters the brain. Because noradrenaline is overactive in PTSD and seems to disturb sleep, prazosin given at night was tried for trauma nightmares, and early small trials were encouraging; Barlow and Durand mention it as an effective drug treatment for nightmares. Then a large trial in 304 American veterans (Raskind and colleagues, 2018) found no difference from placebo in nightmares, sleep quality or overall improvement. The veterans' guideline of 2023 still suggests prazosin, weakly, for nightmares, but suggests against it for PTSD as a whole. Its main side effect is dizziness or fainting on standing up.

Antipsychotics, used only as add-ons. Atypical antipsychotics are sometimes added when a person with severe PTSD has not responded to antidepressants. The evidence is poor. In the largest trial, John Krystal and his colleagues (2011) added risperidone or placebo for 296 veterans whose chronic military PTSD had not responded to at least two antidepressant trials, and risperidone was not significantly better, while it caused more weight gain and sedation. Current guidelines advise against routine use.

Benzodiazepines: the commonest mistake. A frightened, sleepless survivor in an emergency department is often given a benzodiazepine such as alprazolam or diazepam, and in the first night it may seem to help. As a treatment for PTSD it fails. A systematic review of 18 studies by Jeffrey Guina and his colleagues (2015) concluded that benzodiazepines are ineffective both for treating PTSD and for preventing it; their use soon after a trauma was linked with a higher risk of later PTSD and with worse results from psychotherapy, as well as with aggression, depression and substance use. There is a theoretical reason too: exposure therapy works through new learning (extinction), and drugs that blunt anxiety and memory may interfere with that learning. Add the risk of dependence, especially in people who are already drinking to cope, and it is easy to see why the veterans' guideline recommends strongly against benzodiazepines and NICE advises that no drug, benzodiazepines included, should be offered to prevent PTSD.

Prevention with drugs, and the experimental frontier. Because noradrenaline helps to stamp in emotional memories, researchers have tried giving the beta-blocker propranolol within hours of a trauma to stop the memory being consolidated so strongly. A Cochrane review (Amos, Stein and Ipser, 2014) found no evidence to support it. The steroid hydrocortisone, given soon after trauma, did better in the same review, with moderate-quality evidence from four small trials, mostly in people who were severely physically ill, but later reviews conclude that it cannot yet be recommended for routine use. Ketamine, an anaesthetic that blocks NMDA glutamate receptors and has rapid effects on depression, remains experimental for PTSD. The most publicised experiment is MDMA-assisted therapy. MDMA (3,4-methylenedioxymethamphetamine, the recreational drug ecstasy) is a stimulant that Pinel also describes as an empathogen, a drug that produces feelings of empathy and closeness; in trials it was given in a few supervised sessions within a course of psychotherapy, on the theory that it lets patients revisit traumatic memories with less fear. It has not been approved by the FDA as a treatment for PTSD and remains experimental. Finally, whatever else is done, the companions of PTSD must be treated: depression (Chapter 5), alcohol and drug use (Chapter 10) and suicidal thinking. Table 7.8 summarises the drugs.

Table 7.8 Drug treatments for posttraumatic stress disorder.
