Exam trap

Benzodiazepines are not first-line for PTSD; they neither treat nor prevent it and may make outcomes worse. The first-line drugs are SSRIs (sertraline and paroxetine are the FDA-approved pair) and venlafaxine, and overall, trauma-focused psychotherapy is preferred to any drug. Debriefing is not a treatment for PTSD: a single compulsory session soon after the trauma does not prevent it and can harm. Prazosin is for nightmares, propranolol and hydrocortisone for experimental prevention.

Post-traumatic growth

Listen long enough to survivors and you hear something that the criteria for PTSD do not mention. A woman who survived cancer says she has stopped wasting her days on quarrels. A man who lost his home in a flood says he learned who his real friends were. A father whose son was killed in a road accident starts a campaign for safer roads and says he has found a strength he never knew he had. In the mid-1990s two psychologists at the University of North Carolina at Charlotte, Richard Tedeschi and Lawrence Calhoun, gave this a name: post-traumatic growth, positive psychological change experienced as a result of the struggle with a highly challenging life crisis. The important word is struggle. Growth does not come from the event itself, which remains terrible, but from the work of rebuilding the beliefs that the event destroyed, which is why the idea fits so naturally with the theory of shattered assumptions.

In 1996 Tedeschi and Calhoun published the Posttraumatic Growth Inventory, a 21-item self-report questionnaire (described in the Assessment book), and factor analysis of it gave five domains of growth, which are the answer to a favourite examination question:

  1. Appreciation of life: a greater sense of the value of one's own life and a change in priorities ("I don't take each day for granted any more").
  2. Relating to others: closer, more intimate relationships, more compassion, a sense of who can be counted on.
  3. Personal strength: "If I could survive that, I can face anything," often paired with a franker sense of one's own vulnerability.
  4. New possibilities: new interests, new paths in life, new roles, such as the campaigning father.
  5. Spiritual change: a deeper engagement with religious, spiritual or existential questions, whether or not the person is religious.

Three points keep the idea honest. First, growth coexists with distress; it does not replace it. Many people who report growth still have intrusive memories and grief, and growth is not a sign that the trauma "was for the best". Second, growth is not the same as resilience. A resilient person may never have been shaken enough to need to rebuild, and so may report little growth. Third, self-reports of growth may not always reflect real change: when Patricia Frazier and her colleagues (2009) measured the same people before and after a trauma, the growth they reported on the Posttraumatic Growth Inventory was largely unrelated to the change actually measured. For a clinician, the right stance is neither to promise growth nor to dismiss it, but to notice and support it when the client finds it.

Exam trap

Tedeschi and Calhoun's five domains: appreciation of life, relating to others, personal strength, new possibilities, spiritual change. A question asked "What are some areas of post-traumatic growth?" with options (b) appreciation of life, personal strength and new possibilities, and (d) appreciation of life, relating to others, personal strength and spiritual change. Both lists are incomplete and both name only real domains; (d), with four of the five, is the keyed answer. The option listing "accumulation of material possessions, financial success, personal weakness and new possibilities" is wrong: material possessions and financial success are not domains, and it is personal strength, not weakness. If an option lists all five, choose it.

Return for a moment to Sameer, the bus conductor of Section 7.2.9. With twelve sessions of trauma-focused cognitive therapy he learned to replay the night of the crash without being swept back into it, and to see that the motorcycle had pulled out without warning, whatever he had said to the driver. His nightmares became rare, his drinking stopped, and he went back to night shifts. A year later, without being asked, he told his psychologist that he now stops to help at every accident he passes on the highway, "because someone held that girl's head, and it was me, and I am glad it was me." That sentence holds the whole of this section: a precipitating event that is known, a disorder that was not inevitable, a treatment that works by facing rather than forgetting, and a change that the trauma did not cause but that the struggle with it made possible.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. DSM-5 moved PTSD out of the anxiety disorders into a new chapter called disorders.
  2. DSM-5-TR PTSD has symptom clusters; DSM-IV had three.
  3. Exposure through television or pictures meets criterion A only when it is -related.
  4. For PTSD, symptoms in all clusters must last more than ; the specifier "with expression" applies when full criteria are first met 6 months or more after the event.
  5. Acute stress disorder lasts from days to 1 month after the trauma and needs nine of symptoms; DSM-IV, but not DSM-5, required symptoms.
  6. ICD-11 complex PTSD adds three disturbances to PTSD: affect dysregulation, negative self-concept and difficulties in ; the concept is usually credited to Judith (1992).
  7. The proportion of people exposed to a particular trauma who go on to develop PTSD is its risk; it is highest after rape and after being held captive, tortured or kidnapped.
  8. Feeling dazed, unreal or detached during the trauma itself is called dissociation, and it is a strong predictor of later PTSD.
  9. The only two drugs approved by the United States Food and Drug Administration for PTSD are the SSRIs and .
  10. Tedeschi and Calhoun's five domains of post-traumatic growth are appreciation of life, relating to others, personal strength, new possibilities and change.

Answers: (1) trauma- and stressor-related, (2) four, (3) work, (4) 1 month; delayed, (5) 3; fourteen; dissociative, (6) relationships (sustaining relationships and feeling close to others); Herman, (7) conditional, (8) peritraumatic, (9) sertraline; paroxetine, (10) spiritual.

7.4 Adjustment Disorders, Prolonged Grief Disorder and the Attachment DisordersLower yield · 1 Q

Posttraumatic stress disorder is the disorder everyone has heard of, but it is not the commonest way in which people break down after something bad happens. Most of the stressors that bring people to a clinic are not bombs, rapes or train crashes but a failed examination, a broken marriage, a lost job, a move to a strange city, a cancer diagnosis, the death of a husband after thirty-eight years together. Some of the most damaging stressors are not events at all but absences: the baby in an overcrowded children's home who is fed and changed but never comforted by the same adult twice. This section covers the members of the trauma- and stressor-related family that grow from these more ordinary soils. All share the family's defining rule (Section 7.2): a stressor is written into the criteria as a necessary cause. They differ in the kind of stressor, the time rules and the picture that follows: adjustment disorders first, as the broadest and most often diagnosed; then prolonged grief disorder, the newest member; then the two attachment disorders of early childhood.

Adjustment disorder: when ordinary stress overwhelms coping

Naveen is 19. He grew up in a village near Raichur, the first in his family to finish school, and in August he moved into a hostel in Bengaluru to begin an engineering degree. Within three weeks the trouble had started. He could not follow lectures given rapidly in English, his roommates spoke Kannada with a city accent and laughed at his, and the mess food made him ill. By October he had failed two internal tests. He began to miss classes, lay on his bed with the door shut, cried on the phone to his mother every night and slept badly, waking at four with his heart racing about the next test. Twice he told a friend that it would be better if he simply went home and "finished everything". The friend took him to the college counsellor. When the counsellor asked what was wrong, Naveen said: "Nothing is wrong with me, madam. Everything here is wrong. If I could just pass this semester I would be all right."

That last sentence is the heart of an adjustment disorder: a clinically significant emotional or behavioural reaction to an identifiable stressor, out of proportion to what would be expected or causing real impairment, that does not amount to any other mental disorder and that is expected to fade once the stressor, or the person's adaptation to it, is resolved. Naveen is sad, anxious and tearful, but he is not describing a depression that has taken on a life of its own; his distress is about the hostel, the tests and the language, and it would lift (he believes, and he is probably right) if those problems were solved. The diagnosis exists for exactly this large group of people who are not coping, who need help and who would otherwise be squeezed into a diagnosis that does not fit them or sent away with nothing.

The stressor is the first thing to understand, because it most clearly separates adjustment disorder from its famous neighbour. In PTSD and acute stress disorder the stressor must meet Criterion A: actual or threatened death, serious injury or sexual violence (Section 7.2). In adjustment disorder the stressor can be of any severity. DSM-5-TR's examples show the range: a single event (the end of a romantic relationship) or several at once (business failure on top of marital trouble); recurrent stressors (seasonal business crises) or continuous ones (a slowly disabling illness, a neighbourhood ruled by crime); stressors that strike one person, a family or a whole community, as a flood does; and the ordinary transitions of life: going to school, leaving or returning to the parental home, marrying, becoming a parent, failing to reach an occupational goal, retiring. Moving from a village to a city hostel is one of these. So is the journey of a young man from Bihar or Odisha to a construction site in Kerala or the Gulf, where he knows no one and speaks no one's language.

DSM-5-TR sets out five criteria, and each one has a reason.

  • Criterion A: onset within 3 months. Emotional or behavioural symptoms develop in response to an identifiable stressor, within 3 months of the onset of the stressor. The time limit keeps the diagnosis honest. If a woman becomes depressed two years after her divorce, the link to the divorce may be real, but it is no longer obvious enough to build a diagnosis on; something else is probably going on.
  • Criterion B: clinically significant. Either the distress is marked and out of proportion to the severity or intensity of the stressor, or there is significant impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of functioning, or both. DSM-5-TR adds that the judgement of proportion must take into account the external context and the cultural factors that might shape how distress is shown. This criterion separates a disorder from what DSM-5-TR calls normative stress reactions: "When bad things happen, most people get upset. This is not an adjustment disorder." Grief at a failed examination, worry after a layoff and tears at a daughter's wedding are normal; missing every lecture for six weeks is not.
  • Criterion C: not another disorder. The disturbance does not meet the criteria for another mental disorder and is not merely the worsening of a disorder the person already had. If Naveen had five symptoms of major depression for two weeks, the diagnosis would be major depressive disorder, stressor or no stressor. Adjustment disorder steps aside whenever a more specific diagnosis fits.
  • Criterion D: not normal bereavement and not prolonged grief disorder. Grief has its own rules, taught later in this section.
  • Criterion E: it ends. Once the stressor or its consequences have ended, the symptoms do not persist for more than an additional 6 months. This is the rule that makes adjustment disorder a disorder of adaptation rather than a lasting illness.

Criterion E repays a careful reading, because the exam questions turn on it. It does not say that adjustment disorder lasts at most 6 months. It says that the symptoms must not outlast the end of the stressor, or of its consequences, by more than 6 months. When the stressor itself continues (a chronic disabling illness, a continuing legal battle, an unemployed husband) or leaves long consequences behind it (the money troubles and loneliness that follow a divorce), the adjustment disorder can last for years. DSM-5-TR records the difference with two duration specifiers. Acute is used when symptoms have lasted less than 6 months; persistent (chronic) is used when they have lasted 6 months or longer, which by definition can happen only in response to a chronic stressor or one with enduring consequences. Figure 7.7 (later in this section) draws both time rules on a time line.

The clinician also records which kind of reaction dominates, using one of six subtypes:

  • With depressed mood: low mood, tearfulness or feelings of hopelessness are predominant.
  • With anxiety: nervousness, worry, jitteriness or, in children, separation anxiety is predominant.
  • With mixed anxiety and depressed mood: a combination of the two predominates. Naveen, tearful by night and panicky before tests, fits here.
  • With disturbance of conduct: behaviour that violates the rights of others or social norms is predominant: truancy, vandalism, reckless driving, fighting, defaulting on responsibilities. Barlow and Durand note that conduct problems are a particular feature of adjustment reactions in adolescence.
  • With mixed disturbance of emotions and conduct: both emotional symptoms and a disturbance of conduct predominate.
  • Unspecified: for maladaptive reactions that fit none of the others, such as a reaction that shows itself mainly in physical complaints or in social withdrawal.

Now push Naveen through the criteria. The stressor is identifiable (moving into the hostel and the academic and social pressures that came with it). The symptoms began within 3 weeks, well inside 3 months (A). His distress is out of proportion to what most students feel in their first term, and he is failing tests and missing classes (B). He does not meet criteria for major depression, an anxiety disorder or anything else (C). No one has died (D). Whether Criterion E will be met is a prediction rather than a finding, but if he settles into college life, or decides with his family to change course, his symptoms should resolve within months. The diagnosis is adjustment disorder with mixed anxiety and depressed mood, acute. His remark about "finishing everything" also matters, and it is taken up in the Clinical link below.

Exam trap

Adjustment disorder needs no Criterion A stressor: the stressor can be of any severity (divorce, job loss, illness, migration, examination failure). The two time numbers are onset within 3 months of the stressor and resolution within 6 months after the stressor or its consequences end. "Lasts no more than 6 months" is the common wrong paraphrase: with a continuing stressor the disorder can be persistent (chronic) for much longer. A reaction that meets the full criteria for major depression is major depression, not adjustment disorder.

Adjustment disorder: its place, its boundaries and its treatment

Adjustment disorders have had an awkward history. From DSM-III (1980) through DSM-IV they had a chapter of their own and were used mainly as a residual category, the label for significant anxiety or depression tied to a life stress that met the criteria for nothing else; partly for this reason, Barlow and Durand note, they have been little researched. DSM-5 (2013) moved them into the new trauma- and stressor-related chapter, following the argument of the psychiatrists James Strain and Matthew Friedman that they are stress response syndromes, the mild end of a spectrum whose severe end is PTSD. Barlow and Durand describe them as generally milder than acute stress disorder or PTSD but still impairing, arising in people with the same vulnerabilities to anxiety that underlie the other disorders of the family.

Residual or not, they are common. DSM-5-TR reports that between about 5 and 20 percent of people in outpatient mental health treatment in the United States carry a principal diagnosis of adjustment disorder, and that in hospital psychiatric consultation services in Australia, Canada, Israel and the United States adjustment disorder was often the most common diagnosis in the 1990s, frequently reaching 50 percent of referrals. That second figure makes sense once you picture the patients: people on medical and surgical wards who have just been told they have cancer, who face an amputation or who have been in hospital for weeks, and who are not coping. Adjustment disorders, in DSM-5-TR's words, can be "the major psychological response to a medical condition". Rates may be higher in women, and people living in disadvantaged circumstances, who meet more stressors, are at higher risk.

Telling adjustment disorder from its neighbours comes down to three questions: what was the stressor, how soon did the symptoms start and how long have they lasted, and do the symptoms meet the criteria of something more specific?

  • Major depressive disorder. If the reaction to a stressor meets the full criteria for a major depressive episode, the diagnosis is major depressive disorder (Chapter 5). The presence of a stressor does not protect against that diagnosis.
  • PTSD and acute stress disorder. These need a Criterion A event and a specific symptom profile, and they have their own clocks: acute stress disorder only between 3 days and 1 month after the event, PTSD only after more than a month (Section 7.2). Adjustment disorder can be diagnosed straight away. Two rules follow from DSM-5-TR. A person who has met a Criterion A event but has PTSD-like symptoms below the threshold can receive an adjustment disorder; and a person who has the full PTSD symptom picture after a stressor that does not meet Criterion A (a humiliating divorce, say) is also diagnosed with an adjustment disorder.
  • Personality disorders. Some people with personality disorders become distressed in every stressful situation. The lifetime pattern decides: adjustment disorder is diagnosed alongside the personality disorder only when the reaction clearly exceeds what the personality disorder itself would explain (Chapter 11).
  • Psychological factors affecting other medical conditions. Here the direction runs the other way: psychological factors make a medical illness worse (anxiety worsening asthma, Section 7.7). In adjustment disorder the illness is the stressor and the psychological reaction is the problem.
  • Normal stress reactions. Distress that is expected, and expected in that culture, is not a disorder at all. DSM-5-TR reminds clinicians that in some cultural settings suffering is regarded as an ordinary part of life and distress at a hard event is not seen as maladaptive or worth treating.

What happens to reactions that break the time rules? DSM-5-TR sends them to other specified trauma- and stressor-related disorder, and the examples it lists are worth learning because they show the rules from the outside: adjustment-like disorders whose symptoms begin more than 3 months after the stressor; adjustment-like disorders that last more than 6 months without a prolonged stressor; persistent PTSD-like symptoms below the PTSD threshold that last longer than 6 months (sometimes called subthreshold or partial PTSD); and cultural syndromes such as ataque de nervios (Chapter 1 teaches the cultural concepts of distress). When there is not enough information to say why the criteria are not met, as in an emergency room, the label is unspecified trauma- and stressor-related disorder.

The two international classifications describe the same condition with slightly different clocks, and Table 7.9 sets them side by side. ICD-10 places adjustment disorders at F43.2, inside the block F43 "reaction to severe stress, and adjustment disorders", next to acute stress reaction (F43.0) and PTSD (F43.1). Its guidelines say that onset is usually within 1 month of the stressful event or life change and that the symptoms do not usually last more than 6 months, with one exception: the subtype called prolonged depressive reaction may last up to 2 years. ICD-10 also lists three older terms as included in F43.2: culture shock, grief reaction, and hospitalism in children (the withdrawal of young children kept for long periods in hospitals and institutions, which René Spitz described; Chapter 5). ICD-11 (adopted by the World Health Assembly in 2019 and in force from 2022) gives adjustment disorder the code 6B43 among its "disorders specifically associated with stress", and it sharpens the core of the diagnosis. In ICD-11 adjustment disorder is a maladaptive reaction to an identifiable psychosocial stressor or stressors that usually emerges within a month of the stressor and is characterised by two things: preoccupation with the stressor or its consequences (excessive worry, recurrent and distressing thoughts about it, constant rumination about its implications), and failure to adapt to the stressor, severe enough to impair personal, family, social, educational or occupational functioning. The symptoms typically resolve within 6 months unless the stressor persists. Notice what ICD-11 has done: instead of defining adjustment disorder only by what it is not, it gives the disorder positive features of its own, and Naveen's endless worry about the next test is exactly the preoccupation it describes.

Table 7.9 Adjustment disorder in the three classifications.
FeatureDSM-5-TRICD-10 F43.2ICD-11 6B43
StressorIdentifiable, any severitySignificant life change or stressful eventIdentifiable psychosocial stressor or stressors
OnsetWithin 3 monthsUsually within 1 monthUsually within 1 month
Core pictureDisproportionate distress or impairment; not another disorderDistress and emotional disturbance interfering with functioningPreoccupation with the stressor plus failure to adapt
DurationEnds within 6 months after the stressor or its consequences endUsually up to 6 months; prolonged depressive reaction up to 2 yearsTypically resolves within 6 months unless the stressor persists
SubtypesSix (depressed mood, anxiety, mixed, conduct, mixed emotions and conduct, unspecified)Brief and prolonged depressive reaction, mixed anxiety and depressive reaction, other emotions, conduct, mixed, otherNo subtypes
Clinical link

"Only an adjustment disorder" is a dangerous phrase. DSM-5-TR states that adjustment disorders are associated with an increased risk of suicide attempts and suicide, and it notes that among migrant groups, including South Asian and South-East Asian migrant workers in the Gulf countries, adjustment disorder was among the most common diagnoses linked to suicidal behaviour. A young person like Naveen who speaks of "finishing everything" needs a proper suicide risk assessment (Chapter 5) on the same day, whatever the diagnosis turns out to be.

How is adjustment disorder treated? Think about what the diagnosis says. The trouble is recent, it is tied to an identifiable present problem, the person's coping has been overwhelmed rather than permanently damaged, and the expected course is recovery once the problem is solved or adapted to. That is the textbook description of a crisis, and the classic answer is crisis intervention: a brief, active, present-focused contact, begun as soon as possible, that assesses safety, helps the person understand what has happened, mobilises their own coping skills and the people around them, and solves the most pressing practical problems (Book 2, Section 9.7.3 teaches the goals and steps and names adjustment disorder as its classic use). Brief, problem-focused psychotherapy of a few sessions does the same work at a slower pace. For Naveen it might mean a counsellor who meets him three or four times, makes sure he is safe, helps him talk to his teachers about the language problem, gets him into a peer study group and the college's bridge course in English, and involves his parents in deciding whether to continue. When the stressor is a single acute event, DSM-5-TR notes, the disturbance usually begins within days and lasts no more than a few months.

Why not the other options an examiner offers? Psychoanalytic psychotherapy (Book 2, Section 3.2) aims at long-standing unconscious conflicts and character over months or years, which is far more than a time-limited reaction to a present stressor needs. Group psychotherapy (Book 2, Section 7.10) can help, for instance in support groups for people with a new cancer diagnosis, but it is not the first choice for an individual crisis. Cognitive behaviour therapy (Book 2, Section 5.4) is often useful, especially for stress-related worry, and is increasingly studied for adjustment disorder; but the standard textbook answer remains crisis intervention and brief psychotherapy, because the defining feature of the disorder is its link to a current stressor. Drugs have little part to play. There are few controlled trials of any medicine in adjustment disorder, and at most a short course of a sleeping tablet or an anxiolytic is used for severe insomnia or acute anxiety while the psychological work is done; if the picture turns into major depression, it is treated as major depression.

Exam trap

"The treatment of choice for adjustment disorder" is crisis intervention (with brief, problem-focused psychotherapy). Psychoanalytic psychotherapy, group psychotherapy and CBT are the distractors: each may help some patients, but none is the keyed answer. Remember also the ICD clocks: ICD-10 and ICD-11 say onset usually within 1 month; DSM-5-TR allows 3 months. ICD-11's two core features are preoccupation with the stressor and failure to adapt.

Normal grief and prolonged grief disorder

Nearly every human society has answered grief with ritual. In many Hindu families the body is cremated within a day, the household observes a period of mourning that closes with rites on about the thirteenth day, and the dead are remembered every year at the shraddha; many Muslim families in South Asia gather again on the fortieth day. Rituals like these make the death real, gather the bereaved inside a circle of relatives, give grief a permitted shape and then signal that it is time to return to ordinary life. Most people follow that path. Barlow and Durand describe how acute grief (waves of yearning and crying, a stream of thoughts and images of the dead person, disbelief, bodily distress, a sense of being cut off from the world) usually peaks within the first 6 months, though some people grieve for a year or longer, and then gives way to integrated grief: the death is accepted, interest and the capacity for joy return, and memories of the dead remain, bittersweet but no longer dominating. Grief flares again on anniversaries and festivals, and that is normal too. Clinicians worry as much about a person who shows no grief at all as about one whose grief will not end.

The first careful clinical description of grief came from the psychiatrist Erich Lindemann's 1944 study of the bereaved after the Cocoanut Grove nightclub fire in Boston, which opens Chapter 9 of Book 2; the grief models that followed, including Elisabeth Kübler-Ross's stages (which describe dying, not grieving), are taught in Book 2, Sections 9.5.2 and 9.5.3. What concerns us here is the minority for whom grief does not become integrated. Clinicians had long recognised them. Through the 1990s and 2000s two research groups gave the problem a name and a set of criteria: the American psychiatrist M. Katherine Shear and colleagues called it complicated grief, and Holly G. Prigerson and colleagues called it prolonged grief disorder and published criteria proposed for both DSM and ICD in 2009. DSM-5 (2013) was not yet ready to add a new disorder and placed persistent complex bereavement disorder among its "conditions for further study" in Section III. ICD-11, adopted in 2019, introduced prolonged grief disorder as an official diagnosis (6B42) among its disorders specifically associated with stress, and DSM-5-TR (2022) followed, adding prolonged grief disorder to the trauma- and stressor-related chapter. DSM-5-TR's introduction names it as the new diagnosis of the revision, which is one reason examiners like it.

Ramesh is 66, a retired railway clerk in Mysuru. His wife died suddenly of a heart attack eighteen months ago, a week before they were to visit their daughter in Pune. The rites were performed properly and the first annual ceremony has come and gone. Ramesh, his son says, "has stopped living". He sits for hours in her chair. Every morning he sets out two cups for coffee before he remembers. He will not go into the kitchen, where she always was, and he has not been to the temple they visited together every Friday because "it is too clear there that she is not with me". He says he feels "as if half of me was burnt with her on the pyre", that he still cannot quite believe she is gone, that nothing has any meaning now and that he is lonely even with the grandchildren in the room. He cries most days and sleeps poorly. Yet he does not call himself worthless, he laughs when his granddaughter tells him about school, and when he thinks of death it is with the wish to be with his wife again. His relatives, who were patient for the first year, now say he should "have come out of it by now".

DSM-5-TR's criteria map almost line by line onto Ramesh.

  • Criterion A: time since the death. The death of someone close occurred at least 12 months ago in adults, or at least 6 months ago for children and adolescents. The long wait is deliberate. Intense grief in the first year is common and usually resolves, so a 12-month threshold avoids turning normal mourning into a disorder. Ramesh is at 18 months.
  • Criterion B: the core. Since the death there has been a persistent grief response marked by intense yearning or longing for the dead person, or by preoccupation with thoughts or memories of them (in children and adolescents the preoccupation may centre on the circumstances of the death), present most days to a clinically significant degree and nearly every day for at least the last month.
  • Criterion C: at least three of eight further symptoms, also nearly every day for at least the last month: identity disruption (feeling that part of oneself has died: Ramesh's "half of me was burnt with her"); a marked sense of disbelief about the death; avoidance of reminders that the person is dead (the kitchen, the Friday temple); intense emotional pain such as anger, bitterness or sorrow; difficulty reintegrating into relationships and activities; emotional numbness; a feeling that life is meaningless; and intense loneliness. Ramesh has at least six.
  • Criterion D: clinically significant distress or impairment.
  • Criterion E: beyond cultural norms. The duration and severity of the reaction clearly exceed the social, cultural or religious norms of the person's culture and context. The clinician must know what is expected. A widow who wears white and avoids festivities for a year in a community where that is customary is following a norm, not showing a symptom.
  • Criterion F: not better explained by another mental disorder, such as major depressive disorder or PTSD, and not due to a substance or medical condition.

ICD-11 describes the same syndrome, a persistent and pervasive grief response marked by longing for or preoccupation with the deceased together with intense emotional pain, which clearly exceeds the person's social, cultural or religious norms and impairs functioning. It differs mainly in its clock: the grief response must have persisted for an atypically long period, "more than 6 months at a minimum", after the loss, at every age. Figure 7.7 shows the difference beside the adjustment disorder time rules. ICD-10 had no grief disorder at all: an abnormal grief reaction was coded as an adjustment disorder (F43.2), while ordinary bereavement went under the Z codes, the "factors influencing health status" that are not disorders (Chapter 1). DSM-5-TR keeps a similar line: uncomplicated bereavement is listed among the other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention, and adjustment disorder can still be diagnosed in the first year when a grief reaction is clearly out of proportion or impairs self-care.

How common is prolonged grief disorder? DSM-5-TR says that the prevalence of the disorder as it is now defined is unknown. The best estimate comes from a 2017 meta-analysis by Marie Lundorff and colleagues in Denmark, which pooled 14 studies of adults bereaved by non-violent deaths and found that about 9.8 percent met criteria for a prolonged grief syndrome defined with a 6-month duration; older age went with higher rates. Barlow and Durand, citing a German population survey (Kersting and colleagues 2011), put the figure at about 7 percent of bereaved people. Both figures describe a minority, which is the point: most bereaved people, however devastated at first, do not develop a disorder. Risk is higher when the bereaved person depended heavily on the deceased, when the dead person was a child or a spouse, when the death was violent or unexpected, and when money troubles follow. Symptoms usually begin in the first months after the death, although the full syndrome may appear later; the course may be especially long in parents who have lost a child. Prolonged grief disorder carries a raised risk of suicidal thinking even after depression and PTSD are taken into account, and DSM-5-TR links it to heart disease, high blood pressure, cancer, weakened immunity and worse quality of life, partly through neglected self-care, smoking and drinking.

Culture shapes the expected length and form of grief. DSM-5-TR adds that being unable to carry out funeral rites may worsen grief, perhaps because of what that failure is felt to mean for the dead person's spiritual state, a finding many Indian families came to understand during the COVID-19 pandemic, when last rites often could not be performed in the customary way. History offers a royal example. When Prince Albert died in December 1861, Queen Victoria withdrew into a mourning so deep that, as Barlow and Durand put it, she was unable to perform as monarch for several years; she wore black for the rest of her life. Whether such grief, in a culture of elaborate Victorian mourning, would meet Criterion E is exactly the question the criteria force a clinician to ask.

Telling grief apart and treating prolonged grief

Prolonged grief disorder sits between three other conditions, and Table 7.10 lays them out. The first is normal grief. DSM-5-TR is careful here: grief that is severe in the first year, grief that surges on anniversaries and festivals, and even seeing or hearing the dead person are all compatible with normal mourning. What makes the disorder is severe grief that persists beyond the time limit, interferes with functioning and exceeds the norms of the person's culture.

The second is major depressive disorder. Since DSM-5 removed the old bereavement exclusion, a major depressive episode can be diagnosed in the weeks after a death if its criteria are met (Chapter 5 tells that controversy). DSM-5-TR gives clinicians a set of clues for telling grief from depression. In grief the main feeling is emptiness and loss; in depression it is a persistent low mood and an inability to anticipate pleasure. The pain of grief comes in waves, the so-called pangs of grief, set off by thoughts or reminders of the dead person, and it can sit alongside moments of humour and warmth, as Ramesh's laughter with his granddaughter shows; depressed mood is more constant and not tied to particular thoughts. In grief self-esteem is usually preserved, and any self-reproach concerns failings towards the deceased ("I should have made her see the doctor"); in depression worthlessness and self-loathing are common. Thoughts of death in grief are about joining the dead person; in depression they are about ending a life that feels worthless or unbearable. The two can coexist, and when they do both are diagnosed.

The third is PTSD, which can arise alongside prolonged grief when the death was violent: a murder, a suicide, a road accident the person witnessed. DSM-5-TR separates the two by what the symptoms are about. Intrusions in PTSD replay the traumatic event; intrusive memories in prolonged grief roam over the whole relationship, including its happy parts. Avoidance in PTSD is of reminders of the trauma (the road where the accident happened); avoidance in prolonged grief is of reminders that the person is gone (the kitchen, the temple). PTSD memories feel as if they are happening in the here and now; grief memories do not. And yearning for the dead person belongs to grief, not to PTSD. Two further neighbours need one line each: separation anxiety disorder is fear of separation from living attachment figures (Chapter 6), and a psychotic disorder needs other psychotic symptoms besides the reassuring, often hypnagogic, visions and voices of the dead that are common across cultures in normal grief.

Table 7.10 Reactions after a death: normal grief, prolonged grief disorder, major depression and PTSD.
FeatureNormal griefProlonged grief disorderMajor depressive disorderPTSD after a violent death
Centre of distressLoss of the person, in wavesYearning for or preoccupation with the deceasedPervasive low mood, loss of pleasureFear and horror linked to the event
Self-esteemPreservedPreserved; identity feels brokenWorthlessness, guiltSelf-blame about the event possible
IntrusionsMemories of the personMemories of the whole relationshipSelf-critical ruminationsHere-and-now replays of the event
AvoidanceLittleOf reminders that the person is goneWithdrawal from everythingOf reminders of the trauma
TimingPeaks in months, then integratesAt least 12 months (adults, DSM-5-TR)2 weeks, any timeMore than 1 month

Treatment of prolonged grief has been tested more rigorously than you might expect. Shear's complicated grief treatment borrows from interpersonal therapy and from the exposure methods used for PTSD: over 16 sessions the bereaved person retells the story of the death, gradually revisits avoided places and activities, holds imaginal conversations with the dead person, and works on goals for a future without them. In a randomised trial published in 2005, Shear and colleagues assigned 95 bereaved adults with complicated grief to complicated grief treatment or to standard interpersonal psychotherapy; 51 percent responded to complicated grief treatment against 28 percent to interpersonal therapy, and they responded faster. A larger trial of 395 bereaved adults, published in 2016, confirmed the benefit (82.5 percent responded to complicated grief treatment with a placebo tablet against 54.8 percent with the placebo alone) and tested an antidepressant at the same time: the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor citalopram did not improve grief outcomes, although it did reduce depressive symptoms when added to the psychotherapy. The practical lesson is that antidepressants treat the depression that often accompanies prolonged grief, not the grief itself. Book 2, Section 9.5.4 teaches complicated grief treatment and bereavement care.

Exam trap

Prolonged grief disorder: 12 months after the death in adults and 6 months in children and adolescents under DSM-5-TR (2022); more than 6 months at every age under ICD-11. Its core is yearning or preoccupation, nearly every day for at least the last month. DSM-5 (2013) had only "persistent complex bereavement disorder" as a condition for further study. Elisabeth Kübler-Ross's denial, anger, bargaining, depression and acceptance describe dying patients, not a diagnostic criterion set. Seeing or hearing the dead person can occur in normal grief and is not by itself psychosis.

Reactive attachment disorder and disinhibited social engagement disorder

The last two members of the family are disorders of very young children, and their stressor is not an event but a long absence: the absence of a dependable adult who comforts the child when the child is frightened. Picture two children who spent their first three years in the same overcrowded children's home, where each carer looked after a dozen infants on rotating shifts and nobody had time to pick up a crying child twice. Both were adopted at three. The boy is now four and a half. When he falls and cuts his knee he does not run to his adoptive mother; he sits where he fell and whimpers, and when she hugs him he goes stiff and turns his face away. He rarely smiles, plays alone, and has sudden spells of fearfulness or tearful irritability that no one can explain, even during quiet times at home. The girl, Sneha, is the opposite. She climbs into the lap of any visitor, calls every woman "amma", chatters to strangers in the market and wanders off with the gas cylinder delivery man at the gate without a backward glance. In a crowded mall she never once looks back to check where her mother is. Her new parents found her "so friendly" at first; now they are frightened.

The boy has reactive attachment disorder; Sneha has disinhibited social engagement disorder. The two share a cause and almost nothing else. DSM-5-TR requires, for both, that the child has experienced a pattern of extremes of insufficient care, shown by at least one of three things: social neglect or deprivation, a persistent failure to meet the child's basic emotional needs for comfort, stimulation and affection; repeated changes of primary caregiver that limit the chance to form stable attachments, as with frequent changes of foster home; or rearing in unusual settings that severely limit the chance to form selective attachments, such as institutions with high child-to-caregiver ratios. The neglect must be presumed to be responsible for the disturbed behaviour. In DSM-5-TR's words, the two disorders share an aetiology but the first is expressed as an internalising disorder, with depressive symptoms and withdrawn behaviour, and the second as one of disinhibition and externalising behaviour.

DSM-IV (1994) had one disorder, reactive attachment disorder of infancy or early childhood, with an inhibited type and a disinhibited type. DSM-5 (2013) split them into two separate disorders, following a review by the child psychiatrists Charles H. Zeanah and Mary Margaret Gleason (2010) and later work (Gleason and colleagues 2011) showing, as Barlow and Durand summarise, that the two pictures differ markedly in presentation; as the evidence below shows, they also differ in course and in their response to better care. ICD-10 had already drawn the line, with reactive attachment disorder of childhood (F94.1) and disinhibited attachment disorder of childhood (F94.2) among the disorders of social functioning beginning in childhood. ICD-11 keeps both (6B44 and 6B45) and, like DSM-5, moves them into its stress-associated group because a known adversity is required.

Reactive attachment disorder in DSM-5-TR has two core features. The first (Criterion A) is a consistent pattern of inhibited, emotionally withdrawn behaviour towards adult caregivers: the child rarely or minimally seeks comfort when distressed, and rarely or minimally responds to comfort when it is offered. The second (Criterion B) is a persistent social and emotional disturbance with at least two of three features: minimal social and emotional responsiveness to others, limited positive affect, and episodes of unexplained irritability, sadness or fearfulness evident even in non-threatening interactions with caregivers. Then come the boundaries. The criteria for autism spectrum disorder must not be met (Criterion E). The disturbance must be evident before age 5 (Criterion F). And the child must have a developmental age of at least 9 months (Criterion G). The reason for that last rule is developmental: babies begin to show selective attachments, with stranger wariness and protest at separation from a preferred carer, at roughly 7 to 9 months of developmental age, so a child younger than that cannot be said to have failed to form one. A child can be specified as persistent when the disorder has lasted more than 12 months, and as severe when all symptoms are present at high levels.

Disinhibited social engagement disorder (DSM-5-TR Criterion A) is a pattern in which the child actively approaches and interacts with unfamiliar adults and shows at least two of four behaviours: reduced or absent reticence in approaching and interacting with unfamiliar adults; overly familiar verbal or physical behaviour that breaks the culture's age-appropriate social boundaries; diminished or absent checking back with the caregiver after venturing away, even in unfamiliar places; and willingness to go off with an unfamiliar adult with little or no hesitation. Sneha shows all four. Criterion B insists that this is social disinhibition, not merely the impulsivity of attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD, Chapter 3). The neglect rule applies as before, and so does the developmental age of at least 9 months, for the same reason: at the youngest ages, children across cultures are normally wary of strangers. There is no upper age of onset, but DSM-5-TR notes that the disorder has not been identified in children whose neglect began only after age 2. It can persist into adolescence, when indiscriminate behaviour extends to peers and friendships stay superficial, and even into adulthood as excessive self-disclosure and poor wariness of strangers. A striking point is that a child with this disorder may be attached to the new caregiver, even securely; the disinhibition with strangers can exist alongside a real attachment at home.

Both disorders are rare even after terrible neglect. DSM-5-TR says that reactive attachment disorder usually occurs in fewer than 10 percent of neglected children, even severely neglected ones, and that disinhibited social engagement disorder appears in a minority of severely deprived children, with a prevalence of up to 2 percent in low-income community populations in the United Kingdom. Neglect is a necessary condition but not a sufficient one. Because neglect harms many things at once, both disorders often come with delays in cognition and language, stereotyped movements such as rocking, and signs of poor physical care such as malnutrition.

Key study: the Romanian orphanages and the Bucharest Early Intervention Project

After the fall of the Ceauşescu government in 1989 the world saw the vast state orphanages of Romania, where tens of thousands of infants lay in cots with almost no individual care. Two research programmes turned this tragedy into evidence. The English and Romanian Adoptees study, led by Michael Rutter (1933 to 2021) and later by Edmund Sonuga-Barke, followed children adopted from these institutions into British families. In its young adult follow-up (2017), children who had spent more than 6 months in an institution still showed raised levels of disinhibited social engagement, autism-like symptoms and inattention and overactivity in their twenties, whereas those adopted before 6 months looked like British adoptees who had never been deprived. The Bucharest Early Intervention Project (Charles Nelson, Nathan Fox and Charles Zeanah) went further and randomly assigned 136 institutionalised children either to newly created foster families or to care as usual. Smyke and colleagues (2012) reported that signs of the inhibited, withdrawn pattern fell quickly after placement in foster care and by 30 months were indistinguishable from those of children never institutionalised, whereas signs of the disinhibited pattern fell less, and least when placement came after 24 months. The lesson: reactive attachment disorder responds to a loving carer; disinhibited social engagement responds partly, and timing matters.

The study fits what DSM-5-TR says about course. Reactive attachment disorder can remit when the child receives normal caregiving, although without it the signs may persist for years, and DSM-5-TR advises caution in diagnosing it after age 5. Disinhibited social engagement disorder is only modestly related to the quality of later care and often persists even when the child's home has become markedly better.

Each disorder has its own main differential. For reactive attachment disorder it is autism spectrum disorder (Chapter 3), since both can show dampened positive emotion, language delay and poor social reciprocity. The history settles most cases (severe neglect is the rule in reactive attachment disorder and rare in autism), and so do three features: children with autism show restricted interests and repetitive rituals, have a specific deficit in social communication out of line with their general ability, and usually show attachment behaviour normal for their developmental level, seeking out their parent when distressed. Children with reactive attachment disorder do none of these things, although both groups may rock or flap. Intellectual disability is the second differential: a child with developmental delay shows social and emotional skills in line with mental age, and once that mental age reaches 7 to 9 months, shows selective attachments. Depression in a young child lowers positive affect but leaves comfort-seeking intact. For disinhibited social engagement disorder the main differential is ADHD: the impulsive child may also rush up to strangers, but the disinhibited child's problem is a specific absence of social wariness, together with the history of neglect. Table 7.11 compares the two disorders.

Table 7.11 Reactive attachment disorder and disinhibited social engagement disorder (DSM-5-TR).
FeatureReactive attachment disorderDisinhibited social engagement disorder
Required causeExtremes of insufficient careExtremes of insufficient care
With caregiversRarely seeks or responds to comfortMay be attached, even securely
With strangersWithdrawn, unresponsiveOverfamiliar; goes off with them; no checking back
Emotional pictureLimited positive affect; unexplained irritability, sadness or fear (internalising)Disinhibited, attention-seeking (externalising)
Age rulesDevelopmental age at least 9 months; evident before age 5Developmental age at least 9 months; not seen when neglect began after age 2
After good careUsually improves quicklyOften persists
Main differentialAutism spectrum disorder, intellectual disabilityADHD
ICD-10 and ICD-11F94.1; 6B44F94.2 (disinhibited attachment disorder); 6B45

Treatment follows directly from the cause. The essential treatment is a stable, sensitive and responsive caregiver: placement in a good foster or adoptive family, as the Bucharest project showed, and then help for that caregiver. Interventions that coach foster and adoptive parents to notice and respond to the child's signals, often using video feedback of their play together, are the main approaches being tested, although a 2022 British pilot trial began by noting that there are still no established evidence-based interventions specifically for reactive attachment disorder. No drug treats either disorder; medication is used only for clearly separate conditions such as ADHD. The neglect itself is a child protection matter (Book 2, Section 10.6.6). In India the Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act 2015 lists, among its general principles, that institutionalisation is a measure of last resort, which is the same lesson written into law: family-based care, through restoration to the family, kinship care, foster care or adoption, comes first. Two related ideas belong elsewhere: René Spitz's hospitalism and anaclitic depression in institutionalised infants are taught in Chapter 5, and John Bowlby's attachment theory, with the Strange Situation, belongs to the Developmental book.

Clinical link

The label "attachment disorder" has attracted dangerous treatments. Coercive "holding" and "rebirthing" therapies, which restrain a child until he or she "submits", were promoted for adopted children in the United States; in April 2000 a ten-year-old girl, Candace Newmaker, suffocated during a rebirthing session in Evergreen, Colorado, and in 2006 a task force of the American Professional Society on the Abuse of Children warned against all treatments that use restraint, coercion or provoked rage. Effective help is gentle, patient and built on a carer's everyday sensitivity, never on force.

Exam trap

Reactive attachment disorder is inhibited and emotionally withdrawn (rarely seeks or accepts comfort); disinhibited social engagement disorder is indiscriminately sociable (goes off with strangers, does not check back). Both need severe insufficient care and a developmental age of at least 9 months; reactive attachment disorder must also be evident before age 5 and the autism criteria must not be met. DSM-IV had one disorder with two types; DSM-5 split them. Withdrawn behaviour plus restricted, repetitive behaviour points to autism, not reactive attachment disorder.

With the attachment disorders the trauma- and stressor-related family is complete. Table 7.12 lines up all of its members by their trigger, their time rule and their core picture; the two disorders defined by a Criterion A event, PTSD and acute stress disorder, are taught in Section 7.2.

Table 7.12 The trauma- and stressor-related disorders of DSM-5-TR at a glance.
DisorderRequired stressorTime ruleCore picture
Reactive attachment disorderExtremes of insufficient care in early childhoodDevelopmental age 9 months or more; evident before age 5Withdrawn; rarely seeks or accepts comfort
Disinhibited social engagement disorderExtremes of insufficient care in early childhoodDevelopmental age 9 months or moreIndiscriminate, overfamiliar with strangers
PTSD (Section 7.2)Actual or threatened death, serious injury or sexual violenceMore than 1 month; delayed expression at 6 months or moreIntrusion, avoidance, negative cognitions and mood, arousal
Acute stress disorder (Section 7.2)As for PTSD3 days to 1 month after the event9 or more of 14 symptoms in five categories

Prolonged grief disorderDeath of a person close to the bereaved12 months after the death (6 months in children and adolescents)Yearning or preoccupation, beyond cultural norms
