7.5 Dissociation, Depersonalisation and Dissociative AmnesiaHigh yield · 10 Qs

Have you ever ridden home along a familiar road and arrived with no memory of the last few kilometres? Sat so absorbed in a film that the hall and your own body seemed to disappear? Woken after a night of cramming and felt that the world was flat and far away, as though seen through glass? Then you have had a small taste of dissociation. Most of the time the parts of our mental life run together so smoothly that we never notice they are parts: we know who we are, remember how we came to be where we are, feel that our body and actions are our own, and find the world solid and real. Dissociation is what happens when that integration loosens. A little of it is ordinary; a great deal of it, or the wrong kind, produces the disorders of this section and the next. This section defines dissociation and the dissociative family, then teaches depersonalisation/derealisation disorder, in which the self or the world feels unreal, and dissociative amnesia, in which a stretch of one's own life cannot be remembered. Dissociative identity disorder follows in Section 7.6.

What dissociation is

Pierre Janet gave the idea its first scientific form in 1889, as désagrégation, the splitting off of a cluster of memories and functions from consciousness (Section 7.1 tells his story). The modern definitions keep his core intuition. DSM-5-TR defines the dissociative disorders as characterised by a disruption of, or discontinuity in, the normal integration of consciousness, memory, identity, emotion, perception, body representation, motor control and behaviour, and adds that dissociative symptoms can potentially disrupt every area of psychological functioning. ICD-11 speaks of an involuntary disruption or discontinuity in the normal integration of identity, sensations, perceptions, affects, thoughts, memories, control over bodily movements or behaviour. ICD-10 put it most simply: a partial or complete loss of the normal integration between memories of the past, awareness of identity and immediate sensations, and control of bodily movements. Read the lists slowly and you will see why the dissociative disorders look so different from one another. If memory is cut off from the rest of the mind, the result is amnesia; if identity, the result is dissociative identity disorder; if the felt reality of the self or the world, depersonalisation or derealisation; if control of the body, the paralyses and fits that ICD calls dissociative and DSM calls functional neurological symptoms (Section 7.8).

DSM-5-TR sorts dissociative symptoms into two kinds. Positive dissociative symptoms are unbidden intrusions into awareness and behaviour, with a loss of continuity in experience: the division of identity, depersonalisation and derealisation. Negative dissociative symptoms are an inability to reach information or to control mental functions that are normally easy to reach or control: amnesia is the chief example. (The words "positive" and "negative" are used here in the same sense as in schizophrenia, Chapter 4: something added to normal experience versus something taken away. They do not mean good and bad.)

Why does DSM-5-TR place the dissociative disorders immediately after the trauma- and stressor-related disorders? Because, it says, they are frequently found in the aftermath of a wide variety of psychologically traumatic experiences in children, adolescents and adults, and because the trauma disorders themselves contain dissociative symptoms: PTSD and acute stress disorder include amnesia, flashbacks, numbing, depersonalisation and derealisation, and PTSD has a dissociative subtype (Section 7.2). The chapter is placed next to but not inside the trauma chapter, because a traumatic event is not written into the dissociative criteria as a requirement. Across cultures, DSM-5-TR lists the risk factors for pathological dissociation as earlier onset of trauma; neglect and sexual, physical and emotional abuse by parents; cumulative early adversity; and repeated, sustained trauma or torture in captivity, as suffered by prisoners of war and victims of trafficking. (By "traumatic" DSM-5-TR means psychologically traumatic; physical injury to the head is a different matter, and it comes up again under the organic amnesias.)

Mild dissociation is part of normal life, a point made more than a century ago by the American neurologist Morton Prince (1854 to 1929), founder of the Journal of Abnormal Psychology, in writings of 1906 to 1907. Prince is better remembered for The Dissociation of a Personality (1906), his study of the patient he called "Miss Beauchamp", one of the classic early cases of multiple personality (Section 7.6). The most studied normal form is absorption, the capacity to become so engaged in a book, a song, a film or a prayer that everything else falls away; Auke Tellegen and Gilbert Atkinson described it as a trait in 1974 and linked it to hypnotic susceptibility. Daydreaming and the "highway hypnosis" of the familiar drive are others. Barlow and Durand summarise how common stronger experiences are: transient feelings of unreality occur in about half of the general population at some point in life, and in 31 to 66 percent of people at the moment of a traumatic event; they are more likely when we are sleep deprived (Giesbrecht and colleagues 2007). Russell Noyes and Roy Kletti (1977) questioned more than 100 survivors of life-threatening danger and found that most had felt unreality, a blunting of emotional and physical pain, even a sense of separation from their bodies. In that moment dissociation seems to protect: the mind steps back from what it cannot bear.

Key study: the journalists at the execution (Freinkel, Koopman and Spiegel 1994)

On 21 April 1992 Robert Alton Harris, convicted of the murder of two 16-year-old boys, was executed in the gas chamber of San Quentin prison, the first execution in California for a quarter of a century. Eighteen journalists had been invited as witnesses. Because of a series of last-minute legal stays, they spent the whole night at the prison while Harris was led into and out of the chamber before he was finally executed near daybreak. Researchers at Stanford University (Freinkel, Koopman and the psychiatrist David Spiegel) sent the journalists a questionnaire on acute stress about a month later, and 15 returned it. On average each endorsed five dissociative experiences. Sixty percent had felt estranged or detached from other people; others reported that things around them seemed unreal or dreamlike, that time had stopped, that they were distant from their own emotions, even that they were strangers to themselves. No one reported seeing or hearing things that were not there. The rate was comparable to that among survivors of the 1991 Oakland and Berkeley firestorm. Healthy professionals, then, who were only witnesses, dissociated under the combined weight of horror and a sleepless night. The study is the clearest demonstration that dissociative experiences in themselves are not a sign of mental disorder.

If almost everyone dissociates a little, is a dissociative disorder simply "a lot" of the everyday kind? This is the question of continuum versus taxon, and Figure 7.8 shows the two answers. The continuum view says dissociation is a single dimension, like height, and the patients are the people at its far end. The taxon view says there is a distinct type (a taxon, from the Greek for an arrangement or class) of person whose dissociation differs in kind. In 1996 the American psychologist Niels G. Waller, the psychiatrist Frank W. Putnam and the psychologist Eve Carlson applied taxometric statistics to scores on the Dissociative Experiences Scale, the 28-item self-report questionnaire that Eve Bernstein (later Carlson) and Putnam had published in 1986 (the scale itself belongs to the Assessment book). Their analysis found that the items about absorption and imaginative involvement spread out smoothly across everyone, but a small set of items about amnesia, depersonalisation and derealisation, and identity alteration marked off a separate group. As Barlow and Durand put it, "normal" dissociative reactions differ substantially from the pathological experiences seen in the disorders, and some people never develop pathological dissociation however extreme their stress. This fits a diathesis-stress account (Book 2, Section 1.7): only those with the vulnerability respond to stress with pathological dissociation.

One more distinction is useful, because "dissociation" is used for two rather different things. In a 2005 review the British psychologists Emily A. Holmes, Richard J. Brown and colleagues argued that the evidence supports two qualitatively different forms. Detachment is an altered state of consciousness with a sense of separation from the self, the body or the world: depersonalisation, derealisation and out-of-body experiences, and the numbed "spaced-out" state of many trauma survivors. Compartmentalisation is a loss of the normal ability to bring processes or actions under deliberate control, even though they continue to operate and influence behaviour: the memories in dissociative amnesia that are stored but cannot be retrieved, and the functions lost in conversion paralysis or anaesthesia (Section 7.8). Holmes and Brown suggested that the two need different treatments, which is one reason the distinction has caught on with clinicians.

Exam trap

Dissociation is a disruption of the normally integrated functions of consciousness, memory, identity, emotion, perception, body representation, motor control and behaviour. It is not psychosis: reality testing is usually intact and the "split" is between mental functions, not between thought and emotion as in Eugen Bleuler's schizophrenia (Chapter 4). Positive dissociative symptoms are intrusions (identity division, depersonalisation, derealisation); negative symptoms are losses (amnesia). Transient dissociation is common in healthy people, especially during trauma or sleep deprivation.

The dissociative family across the classifications

DSM-5-TR's dissociative chapter holds five diagnoses: dissociative identity disorder, dissociative amnesia (with or without dissociative fugue), depersonalisation/derealisation disorder, other specified dissociative disorder and unspecified dissociative disorder. The "other specified" category is used for four named presentations: chronic and recurrent syndromes of mixed dissociative symptoms (identity disturbance with less-than-marked discontinuities, or possession episodes without amnesia); identity disturbance after prolonged and intense coercive persuasion (brainwashing, torture, long political imprisonment, recruitment by cults or terror organisations); acute dissociative reactions to stressful events that last less than a month; and dissociative trance, an acute narrowing or complete loss of awareness of the immediate surroundings with profound unresponsiveness, outside any broadly accepted cultural or religious practice. Possession, trance and their cultural setting are taught in Section 7.6, together with dissociative identity disorder and Ganser syndrome.

The three classifications divide the same territory differently, and Table 7.13 shows how. ICD-10 kept the old hysterical unity intact under F44, dissociative (conversion) disorders, which contains not only amnesia, fugue, dissociative stupor, trance and possession disorders, multiple personality and Ganser syndrome (F44.80) but also the motor, convulsive and sensory symptoms that DSM puts in the somatic chapter. ICD-10's general guidelines for F44 require that there be no physical disorder that can explain the symptoms and that there be evidence of psychological causation, in the form of a clear link in time with stressful events, problems or disturbed relationships. One disorder, however, sits outside F44: depersonalisation-derealisation syndrome is F48.1, among the "other neurotic disorders" (DSM-5-TR itself uses the code F48.1 for depersonalisation/derealisation disorder, because its codes come from the American ICD-10-CM). ICD-11 brings everything into one dissociative grouping, codes 6B60 to 6B66, including depersonalisation-derealisation disorder and the conversion symptoms, now called dissociative neurological symptom disorder (6B60, Section 7.8), and it adds partial dissociative identity disorder (Section 7.6).

Table 7.13 The dissociative disorders in DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11.
ConditionDSM-5-TRICD-10ICD-11Core feature
Identity division (7.6)Dissociative identity disorderMultiple personality disorder, F44.81DID, 6B64; partial DID, 6B65Two or more personality states with amnesia
AmnesiaDissociative amnesiaF44.06B61Cannot recall important personal information
FugueSpecifier: with dissociative fugueSeparate disorder, F44.1Subtype 6B61.0Amnesia plus travel or wandering
Unreality of self or worldDepersonalisation/derealisation disorderDepersonalisation-derealisation syndrome, F48.16B66Self or world unreal, reality testing intact
Trance and possession (7.6)Other specified (dissociative trance); possession-form DIDTrance and possession disorders, F44.3Trance disorder 6B62; possession trance disorder 6B63Narrowed awareness, or control by a spirit
Motor and sensory symptoms (7.8)Functional neurological symptom disorder (somatic chapter)F44.4 to F44.7Dissociative neurological symptom disorder, 6B60Paralysis, fits, sensory loss

The older classifications explain one favourite question. Before 1980 these conditions were "hysterical": DSM-II (1968) had a hysterical neurosis of the dissociative type, in which alterations of consciousness or identity produced amnesia, somnambulism (sleepwalking), fugue and multiple personality. So when an examiner asks which of multiple personality, fugue, amnesia and déjà vu is not a hysterical dissociative state, the answer is déjà vu. Déjà vu ("already seen") is the feeling that a new experience has been lived before, a disturbance of the sense of familiarity in recognition memory, and it belongs among the paramnesias described in Chapter 2. It is common in healthy people, especially when tired, and as a recurrent, stereotyped experience it can be the aura of a temporal lobe seizure. It involves no loss of integration of memory, identity or awareness, and no one has ever been diagnosed with a dissociative disorder for having it.

Exam trap

Multiple personality, fugue and amnesia are classic hysterical dissociative states; déjà vu is the exception, a disorder of recognition (Chapter 2). ICD-10 files depersonalisation-derealisation syndrome under F48.1, not F44, but ICD-11 moves it into the dissociative group (6B66). ICD-10 F44 includes conversion symptoms ("dissociative (conversion) disorders"); DSM-5-TR puts them in the somatic symptom chapter as functional neurological symptom disorder. Fugue is a separate disorder in ICD-10 and DSM-IV but only a specifier (DSM-5-TR) or subtype (ICD-11) of dissociative amnesia today.

Depersonalisation and derealisation: when the self or the world feels unreal

Priyanka is 22, in the first year of an MSc in Chennai. Eight months ago, the night before a major examination, she had her first panic attack: her heart pounded, she could not breathe, and in the middle of it "everything went strange", as if she were watching herself from somewhere behind her own head. The panic attacks stopped after a few weeks. The strangeness did not. "It is as if there is a glass wall between me and everything," she says. "I look at my hands typing and they don't feel like my hands. When I talk, I hear my voice, but it sounds like a recording of someone else. My mother hugs me and I know I love her, but I can't feel it. The campus looks like a film set, flat, like a painting. Even my memories feel as if they happened to some other girl." She checks herself in the mirror many times a day, to see whether she still looks real, and spends hours online reading about schizophrenia and brain tumours. "Doctor, am I going mad? I know it's me. I know the world is real. It just doesn't feel real." That last sentence is the key to her diagnosis.

Priyanka is describing two related experiences. Depersonalisation is, in DSM-5-TR's words, an experience of unreality, detachment, or being an outside observer with respect to one's thoughts, feelings, sensations, body or actions. The term was introduced in 1898 by the French psychologist Ludovic Dugas, who borrowed it from the journal of the Swiss writer Henri-Frédéric Amiel, and its essence is captured by the patient who says "I am no longer my normal natural self". DSM-5-TR shows how many facets it has. The person may feel detached from the whole self ("I am no one"); from feelings, the hypoemotionality of "I know I have feelings, but I don't feel them"; from thoughts ("my thoughts don't feel like my own", "my head is filled with cotton"); from the body or parts of it; or from sensations such as touch, hunger, thirst or sexual desire. There may be a reduced sense of agency, feeling robotic, like an automaton, not in control of one's speech or movements. In its most extreme form the self seems split, one part observing and the other taking part, which at its extreme is an out-of-body experience. Time may seem distorted, and memories may feel as if they belong to someone else. Derealisation is the matching experience of unreality or detachment with respect to the surroundings: people or objects seem unreal, dreamlike, foggy, lifeless or visually distorted. People describe a fog, a dream, a bubble, a veil or a glass wall between themselves and the world; surroundings look artificial, colourless or two-dimensional; objects may look blurred or unusually sharp, too near or too far, too large or too small (macropsia and micropsia); sounds may be muted or too loud. Self unreal is depersonalisation; world unreal is derealisation. Priyanka has both, as many people do.

The most important clinical fact about both experiences is that reality testing stays intact. Priyanka knows that she exists and that the campus is real; it only feels as if she does not and it is not. This "as if" quality is what separates depersonalisation from psychosis. DSM-5-TR gives the example of a person who complains that he is dead or that the world is not real: this could be a subjective experience that the person knows is untrue, or it could be a delusional conviction. If it is held as a fixed false belief ("my organs have rotted, I am already dead"), it is a nihilistic delusion, the core of the Cotard syndrome seen in severe psychotic depression (Chapters 2 and 5), and the diagnosis is a psychotic one. Table 7.14 separates these experiences from their look-alikes, and Book 2, Section 2.5.8 teaches how to ask about them in a mental status examination.

Table 7.14 Depersonalisation, derealisation and the experiences they are confused with.
ExperienceWhat is alteredWhat the person saysReality testingTypical settings
DepersonalisationFelt reality of the self, body, feelings or actions"I'm not my normal self; I watch myself like a robot"Intact ("as if")Panic, trauma, exhaustion, drugs, DPDR, PTSD
DerealisationFelt reality of the surroundings"The world looks like a film set, through glass"Intact ("as if")As for depersonalisation
Déjà vuFamiliarity: a new scene feels already lived"I've been here before, exactly like this"IntactHealthy people; temporal lobe epilepsy aura
Jamais vuFamiliarity: a known scene feels never seen"My own room looks strange and new"IntactFatigue, epilepsy, anxiety
HallucinationPerception without an external stimulus"Voices are talking about me"Usually lostPsychoses, delirium, substances
Nihilistic delusionBelief, held with conviction"I am dead; my insides have rotted"LostPsychotic depression, schizophrenia

When these experiences become persistent or recurrent and cause clinically significant distress or impairment, the diagnosis is depersonalisation/derealisation disorder. DSM-5-TR's criteria are short, and every one of them is there to rule something out:

  • Criterion A: persistent or recurrent experiences of depersonalisation, derealisation or both. The "or both" was added in DSM-5. DSM-III and DSM-IV had a depersonalisation disorder only, but DSM-5-TR states that there is no evidence of any distinction between predominantly depersonalised and predominantly derealised patients, so the two now share one disorder.
  • Criterion B: reality testing remains intact during the experiences, which excludes psychosis.
  • Criterion C: clinically significant distress or impairment, which excludes the brief and harmless episodes that half of all adults have had.
  • Criterion D: not attributable to a substance (a drug of abuse or a medication) or another medical condition (such as seizures).
  • Criterion E: not better explained by another mental disorder, such as schizophrenia, panic disorder, major depressive disorder, acute stress disorder, PTSD or another dissociative disorder. Depersonalisation and derealisation occur as symptoms in all of these; the disorder is diagnosed only when they stand on their own.

People with the disorder also share some typical associated features. They find their experience hard to put into words and are often afraid that they are "going crazy" or have irreversible brain damage. Many feel that time moves too fast or too slow and that their memories are not vividly their own. Vague bodily sensations (a full head, tingling, light-headedness) are common, and so are obsessive rumination and checking: constantly wondering whether they really exist, or looking repeatedly in the mirror as Priyanka does. They often look flat and robotic to others, which can hide how intense their suffering is.

Exam trap

Self unreal = depersonalisation ("no longer my normal natural self", estranged from one's own thoughts, feelings or identity). World unreal = derealisation (surroundings unreal, distorted or dreamlike). "Stress" and "depression" are not experiences of self-unreality, although either may trigger them. Dissociative amnesia (memory loss) and fugue (travel with amnesia) are different dissociative disorders. A hallucination is a perception without a stimulus, and déjà vu is a feeling of false familiarity: neither is derealisation. In every depersonalisation or derealisation experience reality testing is intact.

Depersonalisation/derealisation disorder: who develops it, why, and what helps

Transient depersonalisation is extremely common; the disorder is not. DSM-5-TR estimates that about half of all adults have had at least one episode in their lives, but that the disorder itself is far rarer, with a one-month prevalence of about 1 to 2 percent in the United Kingdom; Barlow and Durand cite survey estimates of 0.8 to 2.8 percent of the population. The best clinical picture comes from Daphne Simeon and her colleagues in New York, who in 2003 described 117 people with the disorder. Men and women were about equally represented, unlike most dissociative disorders. The mean age of onset was 16, the course tended to be chronic, and every patient was substantially impaired; 73 percent had had a mood disorder and 64 percent an anxiety disorder at some point in their lives. DSM-5-TR confirms the youthful onset: the mean is 16 years, fewer than 20 percent begin after age 20 and only 5 percent after 25, and onset in the forties or later is so unusual that it should prompt a search for a medical cause such as a brain lesion, a seizure disorder or sleep apnoea. Onset may be sudden or gradual. The course is often persistent and falls into rough thirds: about one-third of cases have discrete episodes, one-third have continuous symptoms from the start, and one-third begin episodically and become continuous. Stress, worsening mood or anxiety, new or overstimulating places, harsh lighting and lack of sleep all make the symptoms worse.

What starts it? DSM-5-TR lists the commonest immediate precipitants as severe stress (interpersonal, financial or occupational), depression, anxiety (especially panic attacks) and illicit drugs. Panic is a particularly common doorway, as in Priyanka's case: Barlow and Durand note that about half of people in an intense panic attack experience feelings of unreality, and depersonalisation and derealisation are among the listed symptoms of a panic attack (Chapter 6). Drugs are the other doorway. Cannabis (its active ingredient tetrahydrocannabinol), hallucinogens, ketamine, MDMA ("ecstasy") and salvia can all induce the symptoms, and in about 15 percent of all cases the disorder is precipitated by a drug; most of these people become so frightened of the drug that they never take it again, which makes the diagnosis easy once the symptoms persist without it. Barlow and Durand's case of Bonnie, a dance teacher in her late twenties, shows the pattern. Her "flipping out" had begun ten years earlier, the first time she smoked marijuana. Now, while demonstrating steps in front of her class, she would suddenly feel that it was "not really me", that she did not control her legs, that she was standing behind herself watching, with tunnel vision cutting her off from the room, and then panic would follow. Further back, DSM-5-TR reports a clear association with childhood interpersonal trauma in a substantial portion of cases, although the trauma is usually less extreme than in dissociative identity disorder; emotional abuse and emotional neglect are the antecedents most strongly and consistently linked to the disorder. Temperament also plays a part: people with the disorder tend to be harm avoidant.

The biology points to one theme: emotion switched down. Mauricio Sierra and his colleagues in London (2002) measured skin conductance, a sensitive index of emotional arousal, while 15 patients with depersonalisation disorder, 11 patients with anxiety disorders and 15 healthy controls looked at pictures. The depersonalised patients showed greatly reduced emotional responding compared with both other groups, specifically to unpleasant pictures, which matched their complaint that they "know" they should feel but do not. Yet they reacted faster than the controls to a sudden handclap, a sign of heightened alertness, so the dulling was not a general sluggishness but a selective inhibition of emotional processing. Imaging studies have found the same picture of reduced activity in emotion-processing areas, and Sierra and the psychiatrist Germán Berrios proposed that in depersonalisation the prefrontal cortex actively damps down the limbic system's emotional response, perhaps as an emergency reaction to overwhelming threat that has become stuck. DSM-5-TR lists the neural regions of interest as the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenocortical axis, the inferior parietal lobule and the prefrontal-limbic circuits. On neuropsychological tests, Ophir Guralnik and colleagues found that patients of normal intelligence were easily distracted and slow to perceive and process new information, with deficits in attention, short-term memory and spatial reasoning that fit their reports of tunnel vision and "mind emptiness". Simeon's group also found dysregulation of the stress hormone axis.

Several other conditions must be excluded before the diagnosis is made, and each exclusion follows from Criteria D and E.

  • Panic disorder and other anxiety disorders. If unreality occurs only during panic attacks, the diagnosis is the anxiety disorder alone. Depersonalisation/derealisation disorder is added when the unreality is prominent from the start and clearly outlasts the attacks, or continues after the panic disorder has been treated, which is Priyanka's situation.
  • Major depressive disorder. Numbness, deadness and dreamlike feelings are common in depression; the separate diagnosis is made only if the unreality clearly precedes the depressive episode or continues after it ends.
  • PTSD and acute stress disorder. Persistent depersonalisation or derealisation in someone with PTSD is the dissociative subtype of PTSD (Section 7.2), not a second disorder.
  • Psychotic disorders. Reality testing is intact in depersonalisation and lost in psychosis; the nihilistic delusion is the hard case.
  • Obsessive-compulsive disorder and illness anxiety disorder. People with depersonalisation may ruminate obsessively about whether they exist, or fear brain damage, but they lack the other obsessions and compulsions of OCD and the wider health worries of illness anxiety (Chapter 6 and Section 7.7).
  • Substances, head injury and medical conditions. Unreality during intoxication or withdrawal is not the disorder. After traumatic brain injury depersonalisation is common and is attributed to the injury. Onset after 40 or an unusual course calls for a full medical and neurological work-up, which may include an electroencephalogram (EEG), because temporal lobe epilepsy in particular, and parietal and frontal lobe epilepsy, can produce these experiences.

Culture adds one more exclusion that matters in India. Meditation in many religious and spiritual traditions deliberately induces states of detachment from the self and the world, and DSM-5-TR is clear that such voluntarily induced experiences should not be diagnosed as a disorder. Some people who first induce these states on purpose, at a meditation retreat for instance, later find that they cannot switch them off, and it is then that a disorder may develop. Cultural explanations of the experience (spiritual or supernatural) can also soften the fear of going mad.

Treatment is the weakest part of the story. No drug has been shown to work. In the largest trial, Simeon and colleagues (2004) randomised 50 people with depersonalisation disorder to fluoxetine (a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor, at a mean of 48 mg a day) or placebo for 10 weeks, and fluoxetine was no better than placebo, although depersonalisation tended to improve when a coexisting anxiety disorder improved. A small placebo-controlled trial of lamotrigine (an anticonvulsant that reduces glutamate release) by Sierra and colleagues also failed. An open trial of the opioid antagonist naltrexone in 14 patients (Simeon and Knutelska 2005) reported an average symptom reduction of about 30 percent, but without a control group that is only a hint. In practice, drugs are used to treat comorbid depression and anxiety. The psychological approach begins with explanation: being told that the experience has a name, that it is common, that it is not the beginning of madness and that it does not cause brain damage brings great relief to people like Priyanka, and it reduces the anxious self-monitoring that keeps the symptoms in the foreground. Cognitive behavioural approaches built on this idea (Book 2, Section 5.6) target the catastrophic interpretations, the checking and the avoidance, and help the person re-engage with life despite the symptoms.

Clinical link

People with depersonalisation rarely volunteer it, because they cannot find the words and fear being called mad. Ask directly and in plain language: "Do you ever feel as if you are not real, or as if you are watching yourself from outside? Does the world around you ever look unreal, like a film or a dream?" Then ask the reality-testing question: "When it happens, do you know that you and the world are really there?" A clear "yes, I know, it just doesn't feel that way" places the experience on the dissociative side of the line rather than the psychotic side (Book 2, Section 2.5.8).

Dissociative amnesia and its forms

Barlow and Durand describe a woman in her early fifties who brought her daughter to their clinic because the girl was refusing to go to school. At home the father was a quarrelsome, sometimes abusive drinker, and the adult son was a burden on everyone. Several times a week the family erupted in shouting, pushing and blame. The mother was the peacemaker who held it all together. Roughly every 6 months, usually after one of these battles, she lost her memory, and the family had her admitted to hospital. After a few days away from the turmoil her memory returned and she went home, only for the cycle to repeat. In the end the problem resolved itself when the children moved out and the stress at home fell. Nothing was wrong with her brain. Her memories were intact, stored where memories are stored; what failed, under pressures she could neither escape nor resolve, was her ability to retrieve them.

That is the essence of dissociative amnesia. DSM-5-TR defines it by four criteria:

  • Criterion A: an inability to recall important autobiographical information, usually of a traumatic or stressful nature, that is inconsistent with ordinary forgetting. The information is of the kind that should have been stored and would normally be recalled freely: one's own wedding, the year spent in a particular job, one's name. Forgetting where you left your keys or the details of a lecture is ordinary forgetting; forgetting the whole day you were assaulted, or the whole of your childhood home, is not.
  • Criterion B: clinically significant distress or impairment.
  • Criterion C: not attributable to a substance (alcohol, other drugs, medication) or to a neurological or other medical condition, such as complex partial seizures, transient global amnesia, the after-effects of a closed head injury or other neurological disease. This is the exclusion that makes the organic amnesias the favourite distractors.
  • Criterion D: not better explained by dissociative identity disorder, PTSD, acute stress disorder, somatic symptom disorder, or major or mild neurocognitive disorder.

DSM-5-TR calls the condition a potentially reversible memory retrieval deficit, and this is what sets it apart from the amnesias caused by damage or toxicity to the brain, which can impair the storage of memories and not only their retrieval. In general the memory loss is retrograde, that is, for events before the onset, and except in rare cases the person can lay down new memories normally. The lost memories need not all be traumatic: they often include ordinary stretches of everyday life around the trauma. And a strange feature is that many people with dissociative amnesia are unaware of their amnesia, or only partly aware of it. They minimise or explain away the gaps, and become uncomfortable when asked about them; they discover the gap only when they are shown evidence, or told about past events, that they cannot recall. ICD-10's version (F44.0) says the same in fewer words: amnesia, partial or complete, for recent events of a traumatic or stressful nature, with no organic brain disorder, intoxication or excessive fatigue to explain it.

Clinicians describe dissociative amnesia by its extent, and the five classic forms, listed in DSM-IV and still described in DSM-5-TR's text, are drawn on a life line in Figure 7.9.

  • Localised amnesia is a failure to recall events during a circumscribed period of time: a woman who cannot remember anything from the moment she was pulled into a car until she woke in hospital the next day. It is the commonest form (older books sometimes call it circumscribed amnesia).
  • Selective amnesia is a failure to recall some, but not all, of the events of a circumscribed period: a soldier who remembers the ambush and the evacuation but not the moment his friend was killed. Localised and selective amnesia together make up most cases, and the note to Criterion A says so.
  • Generalised amnesia is a complete loss of memory for most or all of the person's life history, often including personal identity. The person may also lose knowledge about the world (recent political events, how to use a mobile phone) and, less often, well-learned skills. It is rare and its onset is sudden; the perplexity, disorientation and purposeless wandering of these patients usually bring them to the police or to a psychiatric emergency department. DSM-5-TR notes that it may be more common among combat veterans, survivors of sexual assault and people under extreme emotional stress or conflict.
  • Systematised amnesia is a failure to recall a specific category of information, wherever it falls in time: everything to do with a violent older brother, all memories of one room in the childhood home, or the family home but not school.
  • Continuous amnesia is forgetting each new event as it occurs, from a particular point onwards. Because it is anterograde, it is the rare exception to the retrograde rule, and DSM-5-TR warns that it can be confused with delirium; medical, laboratory and neurological tests are all normal, and careful repeated assessment shows no true cognitive deficits.

Dissociative amnesia often arrives with company. Histories of trauma, especially child abuse, are common. Many patients have dissociative flashbacks, depersonalisation, high hypnotisability, depressive and functional neurological symptoms, sexual difficulties, and a history of self-injury, suicide attempts and other high-risk behaviour, and many find it hard to form and keep satisfying relationships. Mild traumatic brain injury may precede the amnesia, but the memory loss is then out of proportion to the injury and follows a dissociative rather than a neurological pattern. The suicide risk deserves emphasis. DSM-5-TR warns that it may be highest when the amnesia lifts suddenly and the person is overwhelmed by memories they had been unable to face.

The 12-month prevalence of dissociative amnesia in a small community study in the United States was 1.8 percent (DSM-5-TR); Barlow and Durand cite estimates from 1.8 to 7.3 percent and call it the most prevalent of all the dissociative disorders. It has been observed from early childhood to old age, though it is hard to assess in children under 12; Barlow and Durand note that it seldom appears before adolescence and, citing Sackeim and Devanand (1991), that it rarely appears for the first time after age 50. The main risk factor is severe, acute or chronic trauma. Cumulative childhood abuse, especially sexual abuse by a relative with betrayal by an attachment figure, extends the gaps in childhood memory; repeated combat, trafficking, imprisonment as a prisoner of war and concentration camp experiences in adult life can produce extensive localised, selective and systematised amnesia; and generalised amnesia is more likely after extreme acute trauma from which escape was impossible (brutal combat, rape, torture) or in refugees and asylum seekers. Genetic studies suggest that about half of the variation between people in dissociative symptoms is genetic. Not every study agrees: in the twin study of adolescents described in Section 7.6.4, Waller and Ross (1997) found no heritable share in pathological dissociation, a difference that reflects the different measures and samples of the studies.

Culture changes the triggers, and this matters in India. DSM-5-TR observes that where people feel highly constrained by social circumstances or cultural tradition, dissociative amnesia is often preceded not by frank trauma but by severe psychological conflict: marital conflict, other family disturbances, attachment problems or oppression. The mother in Barlow and Durand's case is an example from the United States; Indian clinicians see the same pattern in young married women caught between a husband and his family, or in young people facing a marriage they cannot refuse. DSM-5-TR adds that where possession is part of normal religious practice, amnesia and fugue may be interpreted by the family as pathological possession, so the first person consulted may be a priest or a healer rather than a psychiatrist (possession is taught in Section 7.6).

Exam trap

Generalised amnesia = failure of recall for the person's entire life (most or all of the life history, often with identity). "The past week", "childhood" and "traumatic events" are the distractors: a circumscribed period is localised, some events of that period selective, one category of information (a person, a place) systematised, and forgetting each new event as it happens continuous. Systematised does not mean "everything about one event", which is localised. Dissociative amnesia is mainly retrograde; continuous amnesia is the anterograde exception.

Dissociative fugue: amnesia in flight

In January 1887 Ansel Bourne, a preacher from Rhode Island, drew money from a bank in Providence and disappeared. Two months later, on 14 March, a shopkeeper in Norristown, Pennsylvania, who for the past six weeks had been running a small store under the name A. J. Brown, woke one morning in terror, not knowing where he was or how he had come there. He was Bourne, and he remembered nothing of the weeks he had spent as Brown. The philosopher and psychologist William James told the story in The Principles of Psychology (1890). A more recent case, reported in the news in 2006 and retold by Barlow and Durand, is that of Jeffrey Ingram, a 40-year-old man from Washington state. On his way to Canada to visit a friend who was dying of cancer, he vanished. Four days later he found himself in Denver, Colorado, not knowing who he was. He walked around for about six hours asking people for help and ended up at a hospital, where he was diagnosed with dissociative fugue. His identity came to light about a month later, only after he appeared on television news appealing to anyone who recognised him to let somebody know. His fiancée believed that the grief of facing a best friend's death had triggered it. He had had a similar episode in 1995, when he disappeared on a trip to a grocery store and was found in a Seattle hospital nine months later; his mother said he never fully regained his memory.

Cases like these are a dissociative fugue, from the Latin fuga, flight (the same root as "fugitive"). DSM-5-TR defines it as apparently purposeful travel or bewildered wandering that is associated with amnesia for identity or for other important autobiographical information, and treats it as a specifier of dissociative amnesia: the full diagnosis is "dissociative amnesia, with dissociative fugue". In DSM-IV and in ICD-10 (F44.1) fugue was a disorder of its own, defined by sudden, unexpected travel away from home or work with an inability to recall one's past and confusion about personal identity or the assumption of a new one. ICD-10's guidelines add a telling detail: during the fugue the person maintains basic self-care (eating, washing) and simple social interaction with strangers, such as buying tickets or petrol, ordering meals. To an onlooker the person in a fugue looks quite ordinary, which is how Bourne could keep a shop for six weeks without anyone finding him odd. ICD-11 makes fugue a subtype of dissociative amnesia (6B61.0), describing a loss of the sense of personal identity and sudden travel away from home, work or significant others for an extended period of days or weeks, sometimes with the assumption of a new identity.

Fugue usually goes with generalised amnesia and usually follows an intolerable situation: Barlow and Durand note that the person has usually left one behind, and many are escaping severe stress at home or at work, or legal trouble. Fugues usually end fairly abruptly, with the person recalling most of what happened, although DSM-5-TR notes that the memory loss in fugue can be particularly resistant to treatment, as Ingram's was. In children the flight is limited by the child's world: a child "comes to" after cycling into an unfamiliar neighbourhood, a teenager after taking a bus to a nearby town. Famous disappearances are often argued over; the crime writer Agatha Christie's eleven-day disappearance in December 1926 is still debated as a fugue or a deliberate escape. Many cultures also have "running" syndromes, trance-like states in which a person runs or flees for a long time, such as pibloktoq among Arctic peoples (Chapter 1); Barlow and Durand suggest that most resemble dissociative fugue.

Consider a typical presentation. Railway police bring a 28-year-old man to a hospital in Vijayawada. He is clean, polite and calm, and he bought a ticket and travelled 600 kilometres from his home town, but he cannot say who he is, where he lives or how he came to be there. A neurological examination and a CT scan are normal, there is no smell of alcohol, and he answers general knowledge questions well. Over three days of rest and gentle questioning he recalls his name, then his home, then the fact that he ran from a family meeting at which his elders had insisted he marry a woman he did not wish to marry. The diagnosis is dissociative amnesia with dissociative fugue. Three alternatives must be considered. In transient global amnesia (the next subsection) the person keeps his identity and cannot learn new information, which is the reverse of this picture. In epilepsy, wandering during or after a seizure is aimless and confined to the period of seizure activity, whereas behaviour during a fugue is purposeful, complex and goal-directed and may last days or weeks; serial or telemetric EEG recordings usually reveal the seizures. And in malingering the amnesia is feigned for an external gain, such as escaping a debt or a criminal charge (Section 7.9).

Exam trap

A person found far from home, having travelled with apparent purpose, with no memory of his identity, has dissociative fugue: the travel is what distinguishes it from plain dissociative amnesia. (Strictly, in DSM-5-TR the diagnosis is "dissociative amnesia with dissociative fugue", so if both options appear, pick fugue because it names the whole picture.) Transient global amnesia keeps identity and loses new learning; depersonalisation disorder involves no memory loss at all.

Dissociative amnesia versus the organic amnesias

Memory can be lost for many reasons that have nothing to do with dissociation, and Criterion C exists to make sure the brain is not overlooked. A good clinician, faced with a patient who cannot remember, asks the neurological questions first: Was there a head injury? A seizure? Alcohol or drugs? Is the person old enough for a dementia? Is there confusion, a fluctuating level of consciousness, a neurological sign? Then the pattern of the memory loss itself gives the answer in most cases, because the organic amnesias and dissociative amnesia fail in almost opposite directions.

The single most useful contrast concerns personal identity and new learning. In nearly every organic amnesia the person keeps their sense of who they are until very late, and the main failure is anterograde: they cannot lay down new memories. DSM-5-TR puts it plainly: in the neurocognitive disorders awareness of personal identity is generally spared until late in the course, and retrograde amnesia is almost always accompanied by anterograde amnesia. In dissociative amnesia the pattern is reversed. The loss is retrograde and autobiographical, new learning is intact, the ability to handle neutral facts (money, current affairs) is generally normal, and personal identity can be the first thing to go. Barlow and Durand's comparison adds two more points. Dissociative amnesia is linked in time to psychological trauma or extreme stress; organic amnesia is due to a known medical or physical cause. And dissociative amnesia can be reversed under hypnosis, or improved in an interview helped by a sedative drug, whereas organic amnesia is not, and a sedative may make it worse. Table 7.15 sets out the main organic amnesias against dissociative amnesia; Chapter 10 teaches the neurocognitive disorders in full, and Book 2, Section 2.6 the bedside testing of memory.

  • Transient global amnesia is a sudden attack, usually in a person between about 50 and 70, often after physical exertion, a plunge into cold water or an emotional upset. For a few hours the person cannot form new memories and has a patchy loss of the recent past; anxious and bewildered, they ask the same questions again and again ("What day is it? Why are we here?"), forgetting each answer within a minute. Crucially, they never lose their personal identity: they know their own name and recognise their family throughout. The attack resolves within 24 hours, leaving only a gap for the hours it lasted. The standard diagnostic criteria (Hodges and Warlow 1990) actually require that identity be preserved, which is exactly why it is a distractor for dissociative amnesia.
  • Korsakoff syndrome is the amnesia of thiamine (vitamin B1) deficiency, usually in chronic heavy drinkers and often after an acute Wernicke encephalopathy. Its hallmark is a severe anterograde amnesia with gaps in older memories, and confabulation, the filling of memory gaps with fabricated accounts given without any intention to deceive (Chapters 2 and 10).
  • The other organic amnesias each have their own setting. Alzheimer's disease begins gradually in later life with failure to retain new information, other abilities decline with it, and identity goes only in the late stages. Post-traumatic amnesia after a head injury covers the confused period after the injury and a short period before it, with loss of consciousness and sometimes neurological signs (Chapter 10). Alcohol blackouts ("grayouts" when partial) are gaps only for periods of intoxication. Epileptic amnesia follows complex partial (focal impaired awareness) seizures, with aimless wandering and abnormal EEG findings; it can coexist with dissociative amnesia. Amnesia after electroconvulsive therapy mostly covers the day of each treatment and remits after the course.
Table 7.15 Dissociative amnesia and the organic amnesias.
ConditionCause and onsetMain memory lossPersonal identityClues
Dissociative amnesiaTrauma or severe conflict; often suddenRetrograde, autobiographical; new learning intactMay be lost, even firstStressor in time; normal tests; may lift with hypnosis
Transient global amnesiaUnknown; sudden; often after exertion or emotion; age about 50 to 70Anterograde for hours, patchy retrogradeAlways keptRepeats the same questions; full recovery within 24 hours
Korsakoff syndromeThiamine deficiency, chronic alcohol useSevere anterograde, retrograde gapsKeptConfabulation; alcohol history
Alzheimer's diseaseNeurodegeneration; gradual, older ageNew learning first, then other abilitiesLost only lateProgressive decline in several domains
Post-traumatic amnesiaHead injuryAnterograde after injury, short retrogradeKeptLoss of consciousness, neurological signs
Alcohol blackoutIntoxicationGaps only for drinking episodesKeptLongitudinal drinking history
Epileptic amnesiaComplex partial seizuresGaps for seizures and afterKeptAimless wandering; abnormal EEG

Two psychiatric disorders also include amnesia and must be kept apart. In PTSD, one of the negative cognition and mood symptoms is an inability to remember an important part of the traumatic event (Section 7.2): a woman who was raped may be unable to recall most of that day. That gap is part of the PTSD and is not diagnosed separately; but when the amnesia extends to events beyond the immediate time of the trauma, DSM-5-TR says that a comorbid diagnosis of dissociative amnesia may be warranted. In dissociative identity disorder the amnesia is recurrent and goes with "time loss" in everyday life, finding unexplained possessions, puzzling swings in skills and frequent brief gaps during conversations, all against a background of pervasive discontinuity in the sense of self (Section 7.6). By contrast schizophrenia and bipolar disorder, the other options examiners list, are not disorders of memory at all, although people with them may have cognitive problems.

Finally, amnesia can be faked. Claimed loss of memory for a crime is a familiar problem in forensic work, and DSM-5-TR is honest that no test or set of tests invariably distinguishes dissociative from feigned amnesia. Feigning is more likely when the person has financial, sexual or legal problems, wishes to escape a stressful situation, wants to seem an interesting patient or plans litigation over "recovered memories"; yet genuine dissociative amnesia can arise in exactly the same circumstances, and the two can even coexist. Many people who malinger amnesia confess, spontaneously or when confronted. How deception is recognised is taught in Section 7.9.

Exam trap

Sudden inability to recall important autobiographical information, beyond ordinary forgetting, with no medical cause = dissociative amnesia. The organic distractors are transient global amnesia (identity kept, new learning lost for hours), Korsakoff syndrome (thiamine deficiency, confabulation), Alzheimer's disease (gradual, new learning first) and "anterograde amnesia due to a medical condition". Dissociative amnesia is retrograde and spares new learning. PTSD includes amnesia for part of the trauma, but the disorder defined by an inability to remember important personal information is dissociative amnesia; schizophrenia and bipolar disorder are not memory disorders. Remember the rule of thumb: organic amnesia loses identity last; dissociative amnesia can lose it first.

Clinical link

A diagnosis of dissociative amnesia is made only after the brain has been considered. Red flags that call for urgent medical and neurological assessment include any head injury, a first episode after middle age, confusion or drowsiness, headache, fever, focal weakness, a history of fits, heavy drinking or drug use, and anterograde rather than retrograde loss. Missing a subdural haematoma, an encephalitis or a stroke because the patient "had a family quarrel" is a far worse error than delaying a psychological diagnosis by a day.

Course and treatment of dissociative amnesia and fugue

The good news is that most people with dissociative amnesia or fugue recover, often on their own, and remember what they had forgotten. Acute generalised amnesia often lifts quickly once the person is removed from the stressful situation, such as combat, or is simply brought into the calm of a hospital, as the mother in Barlow and Durand's case showed every time she was admitted. Some people, however, develop a chronic, debilitating loss of autobiographical memory that is not helped even when they "relearn" their life history from others, and fugue can be particularly persistent, as Jeffrey Ingram's never-recovered memories show.

Treatment therefore begins with safety and the present. Barlow and Durand point out that because the episodes are so clearly tied to current life stress, preventing future episodes usually means helping the person resolve the distressing situation and strengthening their coping. When recall is needed, therapy helps the person reconstruct what happened during the amnesia or fugue, often with the help of family or friends who know, so that the person can face the information and integrate it into conscious experience. For more difficult cases hypnosis or a benzodiazepine (a minor tranquilliser) has been used, together with the therapist's suggestion that it is safe to remember (Maldonado, Butler and Spiegel 1998). This is the modern descendant of the barbiturate "amytal interview" of narcoanalysis, whose history, clinical uses and abuses, and position in Indian law are taught in Book 2, Sections 3.10.8 and 3.10.9.

Three cautions apply. First, memories recovered under hypnosis or a drug may be accurate, partly accurate or shaped by the questions asked; they need corroboration before anyone acts on them, a point at the centre of the recovered memory debate taught in Section 7.6. Second, the return of memory can be dangerous: DSM-5-TR warns that suicidal behaviour may be a particular risk when amnesia lifts suddenly and floods the person with intolerable memories, and as amnesia remits, depression, grief, rage, shame and guilt often surface, and many patients go on to develop PTSD. Third, no drug treats dissociative amnesia itself; medication is for the comorbid depression, anxiety or PTSD (Section 7.3 describes the drug treatment of PTSD). Trauma-focused psychological treatment, given once the person is safe and stable, addresses the experiences behind the amnesia.

Dissociative amnesia, fugue and depersonalisation share a structure: one part of ordinary experience (memory, the felt reality of the self, the felt reality of the world) splits off from the rest, usually under the pressure of trauma or conflict, while reality testing and most other functions carry on. The most dramatic disorder of the family pushes the split one step further, so that whole identities, each with its own memories, seem to take turns in one body. That is dissociative identity disorder, the subject of Section 7.6.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. In DSM-5-TR the symptoms of an adjustment disorder must begin within months of the onset of the stressor.
  2. ICD-11 defines adjustment disorder by preoccupation with the stressor and to the stressor.
  3. The keyed treatment of choice for adjustment disorder is .
  4. Under DSM-5-TR, prolonged grief disorder can be diagnosed in an adult only if the death occurred at least months ago.
  5. A neglected child who rarely seeks or responds to comfort, with limited positive affect, has disorder.
  6. Both attachment disorders require a developmental age of at least months.
  7. A persistent feeling that one's surroundings are unreal, foggy or dreamlike, with reality testing intact, is .
  8. ICD-10 codes depersonalisation-derealisation syndrome as , outside the F44 dissociative block.
