Answers: (1) 3, (2) failure to adapt, (3) crisis intervention, (4) 12, (5) reactive attachment, (6) 9, (7) derealisation, (8) F48.1, (9) generalised, (10) transient global amnesia.

7.6 Dissociative Identity Disorder, Possession and TranceHigh yield · 7 Qs

Of all the diagnoses in this book, none has been fought over as fiercely as the one this section opens with. Its patients have been written up as medical curiosities, turned into bestsellers and Hollywood films, cross-examined in murder trials and, by critics, described as the creations of their own therapists. The argument matters because the stakes are high on both sides. If dissociative identity disorder is what its defenders say it is, a mind that divided itself in order to survive unbearable childhood abuse, then disbelieving these patients repeats the injury. If it is what its critics say it is, a role learned in a suggestive therapy, then a treatment that goes hunting for hidden personalities manufactures the illness it means to cure. You will meet both positions, the evidence for each, and the way most clinicians now hold them together. The section then widens to possession and trance, which are among the commonest dissociative presentations in India, and ends with Ganser's strange syndrome of approximate answers. The general idea of dissociation, depersonalisation and dissociative amnesia was taught in Section 7.5; here we build on it.

One person, several selves: the clinical picture

Rukmini, a 31-year-old schoolteacher in Pune, came to the outpatient clinic because of headaches and what she called "lost time". She dressed in plain cotton saris and spoke softly in Marathi. Over the past year, she said, she had kept finding things she could not explain: two pairs of jeans and a leather jacket in her cupboard that she did not remember buying, bank messages for purchases in a mall she had never visited, and a notebook full of English poems in a slanting handwriting quite unlike her own. Twice a man she had never met greeted her warmly at a bus stop as "Ruby". Her husband said that at times she "became someone else": she laughed loudly, smoked, spoke fluent English and called him by his first name, which Rukmini never did, and afterwards she remembered nothing of it. Only at the third visit did she admit that at night she heard "a little girl crying" inside her head, and that she had told no one for fear of being called mad.

Rukmini shows the two pillars on which the diagnosis of dissociative identity disorder (DID) rests. The first is a division of identity: more than one distinct personality state takes turns at being "her". The second is amnesia: she has gaps in her memory for ordinary events that are far too large to be ordinary forgetting, and the clues to them turn up in her cupboard and her bank account. DSM-5-TR builds its five criteria around these two pillars, and each criterion exists for a reason.

  • Criterion A, a disruption of identity. There are two or more distinct personality states, which in some cultures are described as an experience of possession. The disruption involves a marked discontinuity in the sense of self and in the sense of agency, the ordinary feeling that "I am the one doing this". It comes with related changes in emotion, behaviour, consciousness, memory, perception, thinking or sensory and motor functioning, and it may be observed by others or reported by the person. The stress on agency is deliberate: what patients most often describe is not "there are several people in me" but "this is not me speaking", "these are not my feelings", "my hand is writing and I cannot stop it". The phrase "observed by others or reported" exists because most switching is not dramatic, so the clinician often has to rely on what the patient experiences.
  • Criterion B, recurrent amnesia. There are recurrent gaps in the recall of everyday events, important personal information or traumatic events that are inconsistent with ordinary forgetting. This criterion is what separates DID from the ordinary fact that everyone behaves differently at the office, at home and at a wedding. You change your manner, but you remember all three.
  • Criterion C, distress or impairment. As with every disorder in this book, the experience must cause clinically significant distress or disrupt social, occupational or other functioning.
  • Criterion D, not an accepted cultural or religious practice. A woman through whom the goddess speaks at a temple festival, with the whole village expecting and welcoming it, does not have a disorder (this distinction is taken up in Section 7.6.7). In children, the symptoms must not be better explained by imaginary playmates or other fantasy play, which are a normal part of growing up.
  • Criterion E, not a substance or a medical condition. Blackouts and chaotic behaviour during alcohol intoxication, and the automatic behaviour and amnesia of complex partial (focal impaired awareness) seizures, can both imitate the disorder and must be excluded.

DSM-5-TR's description of the amnesia in Criterion B is worth learning because exam vignettes are built from it. The gaps show up in three domains: gaps in autobiographical memory (no memory of one's own wedding or of a child's birth, or of all of school before high school); lapses for recent events or well-learned skills (suddenly not knowing how to do one's job, use a computer, cook or drive); and the discovery of possessions one has no memory of acquiring (clothing, tools, weapons, or writings and drawings one must have made). Fugues, in which the person "comes to" in another part of the city with no memory of travelling there, are common. The amnesia is not limited to traumatic events, as it largely is in dissociative amnesia (Section 7.5): in DID it eats into ordinary days, which patients describe as "time loss" or "blackouts". Most patients minimise or explain away these gaps, so the clinician has to ask about them directly.

DSM-5-TR distinguishes two forms. In possession-form DID the other identities are experienced as external agents, a spirit, a deity, a demon or a dead person, who take control; the change is usually obvious to onlookers. In non-possession-form DID, which is the usual form in Western clinics, the identities are experienced as parts of oneself, and in most such patients the changes are subtle. The elaborate picture of the films, in which each personality has its own name, wardrobe, hairstyle, handwriting and accent, occurs in only a minority and is not required for the diagnosis. Rukmini, with her "Ruby", belongs to that more florid minority. DSM-5-TR also notes a cultural twist that fits her exactly: through acculturation, identities in India may speak only English and wear Western clothes.

ICD-11 describes the same disorder (6B64) as two or more distinct personality states that take executive control of the person's functioning in turn, with episodes of amnesia that are typical and may be severe. It adds a category DSM-5-TR lacks, partial dissociative identity disorder (6B65), in which one personality state stays dominant and runs daily life but is intruded upon by non-dominant states that do not regularly take full control, though they may briefly do so (during self-harm, for example). DSM-5-TR would place the same presentation under other specified dissociative disorder (Section 7.6.7).

Hosts, alters and switches. The clinical vocabulary of DID is small but heavily examined. The host is the identity most frequently encountered, the one who carries the person's legal name and usually the one who comes for treatment; Rukmini is the host. Barlow and Durand add two observations that surprise students. Hosts usually try to hold the fragments of identity together and end up overwhelmed, and the first personality to seek treatment is seldom the original one: the host usually developed later (Frank W. Putnam, 1992). The other identities are the alters, short for alternate identities. Some recurring types appear across case series: child alters, frozen at the age at which a trauma happened; protector alters, often of the opposite sex (a small, slight woman may have a powerful male protector); persecutor alters, who punish or ridicule the host and may urge self-harm; and an impulsive alter who handles sexuality or risk-taking. Clinicians report an average of about 15 alters, and cases with as many as 100 have been described (Colin A. Ross, 1997), although how many identities there are matters far less than the fact that identity has divided.

The change from one identity to another is a switch. On screen a switch is drawn out over a minute of grimacing; in real patients it is usually instantaneous. Posture, facial expression, voice, vocabulary and even apparent physical abilities can change with it. In a series of 100 cases described by Putnam and his colleagues in 1986, 37 percent showed a change of handedness across identities. Switches are often set off by reminders of trauma, by stress or by an intense emotion, and DSM-5-TR notes that most switching is subtle, with the person experiencing several states as overlapping and interfering with one another rather than taking neat turns.

The identities do not all know about each other, and this inter-identity amnesia comes in two forms. Some identities are separated by two-way amnesia: neither remembers what happened while the other was in control. Others are linked by one-way amnesia, in which one state is co-conscious, watching and remembering what another does, while the second knows nothing of the first. Figure 7.10 draws Rukmini's system in these terms.

Rukmini's "little girl crying" points to one more feature that misleads clinicians. Auditory hallucinations are common in DID: a child's voice, voices commenting on what the person does, persecutory voices and voices giving commands. DSM-5-TR adds that hallucinations may occur in every sense. Patients usually hear these voices inside the head rather than through the ears, usually know that they are not ordinary perceptions, and tend to hide them, which is one reason DID is so often misdiagnosed as a psychotic disorder (Section 7.6.6).

Barlow and Durand illustrate the whole picture with the case of Jonah, reported by Ludwig and his colleagues in 1972. Jonah, aged 27, came to hospital with severe headaches during which a great deal of time seemed to pass that he could not remember. What brought him in was what other people told him he had done: he had fought a man and tried to stab him, chased his wife and three-year-old daughter out of the house with a butcher's knife, and on one occasion tried to drown a man in a river, waking next morning in his own bed, soaking wet, with no memory of it. On the ward the staff identified three alters. Sammy was rational and calm, the most aware of the others, and had first appeared when Jonah was about 6, immediately after he saw his mother stab his father. King Young took charge of sexual activity. Usoffa Abdulla, "son of Omega", was violent and announced that his only reason for existing was to protect Jonah; he had emerged when Jonah, aged 9 or 10, was savagely beaten by a group of white youths. Jonah knew nothing of any of them. Notice the structure: each alter appears at a moment of trauma and takes on a job the host cannot do, and awareness runs one way, from Sammy towards Jonah, never back.

Until 1994 the condition was called multiple personality disorder. DSM-IV renamed it dissociative identity disorder because the name misled: the identities are only partly independent, often sharing a great deal, and the disorder is better understood as a failure to integrate one identity than as several complete people sharing a body. The older name survives in ICD-10, which lists multiple personality disorder under F44.8, other dissociative (conversion) disorders, and in ICD-11 as a synonym of DID.

Exam trap

Clothes she does not remember buying, together with distinct personalities with their own names, is the classic sign of inter-identity amnesia, so the answer is DID. Dissociative amnesia also has memory gaps but no distinct identities; dissociative fugue adds purposeful travel or wandering but again no alternating identities; depersonalisation/derealisation disorder has unreality without amnesia or identities (Section 7.5). The identity most often met, with the person's real name, is the host; the others are alters; "ghost identity" and "mixed identity" are not terms at all. DID needs both distinct identity states (or possession) and recurrent amnesia. It does not need loss of consciousness on an EEG (that would suggest a seizure, which Criterion E excludes), a single fugue lasting under 24 hours (not a criterion of anything), or onset only after a sexual assault (the trauma usually lies in early childhood, and no particular trauma is written into the criteria). Conversion disorder can include amnesia for an attack but has no identity states.

Eve, Sybil and the rise and fall of multiple personality

Cases of "double consciousness" were described throughout the nineteenth century, and the most famous of the early cases, "Miss Beauchamp", was described by Morton Prince (whom you met in Section 7.5) in The Dissociation of a Personality (1906). Even Breuer's Anna O., whose conversion symptoms launched the talking cure (Section 7.1), fits the pattern on a rereading of Breuer's notes: Barlow and Durand point out that there were two Anna O.s, one depressed and anxious but otherwise ordinary, the other dark, abusive and hallucinating, who spoke only English or a garbled mixture of languages, and neither remembered what happened while the other was "out". But the modern story of DID is really the story of two books, and of what happened between them.

The first is The Three Faces of Eve. In 1954 two psychiatrists in Augusta, Georgia, Corbett H. Thigpen and Hervey M. Cleckley (the same Cleckley whose The Mask of Sanity defined psychopathy, Chapter 11), published "A case of multiple personality" in a psychology journal, and in 1957 they told the story to the public in a bestselling book that was filmed the same year; Joanne Woodward won the Academy Award for Best Actress for playing all three women. Eve White was a timid, self-effacing wife and mother troubled by severe headaches and blackouts. During one interview a different woman appeared: Eve Black, wild and fun-loving, who knew everything about Eve White while Eve White knew nothing of her, the one-way amnesia of Figure 7.10. Later in treatment a third personality emerged, steadier than either, who chose the name Jane. Thigpen and Cleckley treated the case as a rarity, and the woman behind it, Chris Costner Sizemore (1927 to 2016), later told her own story in I'm Eve (1977), saying that she had in fact had more than 20 personalities over the years before they were finally unified. Physiological studies kept the case alive: Condon and colleagues (1969), examining a film of Sizemore, found that Eve Black alone showed a transient microstrabismus, a slight divergence of the eyes that is hard to produce at will.

The second book is Sybil (1973), by the journalist Flora Rheta Schreiber. It told how the psychiatrist Cornelia B. Wilbur treated a young woman with 16 separate personalities and traced them to horrifying abuse by her mother. Sybil became a bestseller, and more than any other text it fixed in professional and public minds the template that would dominate the next two decades: a woman with many alters, created in childhood as a defence against extreme abuse, uncovered in long therapy that used hypnosis and drugs to reach the hidden memories. In 1998 the historian Peter J. Swales identified "Sybil" as Shirley Ardell Mason (1923 to 1998), an art teacher, and the case began to come apart. The psychiatrist Herbert Spiegel, an authority on hypnosis who had seen Mason while Wilbur was away and had used her as a demonstration subject in his classes at Columbia University, told the scholar Mikkel Borch-Jacobsen in an interview published in 1997 that Mason was highly hypnotisable, at the top of his scale (a "grade five"), that the multiple-personality label was Wilbur's and Schreiber's idea, not his, and that when he declined to call her a multiple personality, Schreiber replied that without the label "we don't have a book". His verdict was simply "It was hysteria." In Sybil Exposed (2011) the journalist Debbie Nathan used Schreiber's papers to argue that the case had been largely constructed, citing among other documents a letter in which Mason told Wilbur that she had made the personalities up; Schreiber's own book had mentioned the letter and presented it as an attempt to escape a painful stage of therapy. Defenders of the diagnosis reply that in letters to a friend, Nancy Preston, Mason affirmed that she had multiple personalities, and that one doubtful case cannot settle the status of a disorder. Both points are fair; what the Sybil story shows beyond doubt is how much a powerful therapist, a hypnotisable patient and a publishing deal can shape what a "case" looks like.

Between the two books, the numbers exploded. In 1982 the psychologist M. Boor reviewed the literature under the title "The multiple personality epidemic", describing a marked rise in reported cases during the 1970s. DSM-III (1980) gave multiple personality its own diagnostic entry, specialist clinics and a professional society followed, and through the 1980s and early 1990s thousands of patients were diagnosed in North America, many with dozens of alters and with memories, recovered in therapy, of incest and sometimes of satanic ritual abuse. Then the tide turned. Thigpen and Cleckley themselves published a short note in 1984 warning that the diagnosis was being made far too readily. Lawsuits against therapists, the recovered memory controversy (Section 7.6.5) and the collapse of the satanic abuse claims followed. When Harrison G. Pope and his colleagues (2006) counted publications, they found that papers on dissociative amnesia and DID rose from low levels in the 1980s to a sharp peak in the mid-1990s and then fell to about a quarter of that peak by 2002 and 2003, a "bubble" seen for none of the 25 comparison diagnoses they tracked. The psychiatrist Joel Paris (2012) called this the trajectory of a medical fad. Clinicians who treat DID answer that structured-interview studies keep finding the disorder in several countries, including the Netherlands and Turkey, among patients who had never heard of it. Hold that disagreement in mind: it is exactly the disagreement between the two models in Section 7.6.4.

Exam trap

The Three Faces of Eve (Eve White, Eve Black and Jane) is Thigpen and Cleckley, 1957; the patient was Chris Costner Sizemore. Sybil (16 personalities) is a book by the journalist Flora Rheta Schreiber (1973) about the psychiatrist Cornelia Wilbur's patient Shirley Mason. Herbert Spiegel and Debbie Nathan are the critics of Sybil, not its authors. Morton Prince's Miss Beauchamp is the early twentieth-century case, and Jonah is Ludwig's (1972).

Who develops dissociative identity disorder, and what course it takes

Good population figures are scarce, because the diagnosis needs a long, skilled interview. DSM-5-TR gives two: a 12-month prevalence of 1.5 percent among adults in a small community study in the United States, and a lifetime prevalence of 1.1 percent in a representative sample of women living in the community in mid-eastern Turkey. Among severely ill psychiatric inpatients, semi-structured interviews have found DID in 3 to 6 percent in North America and about 2 percent in the Netherlands (Barlow and Durand). Reports of the disorder have come from at least 21 countries, and in many of them it presents in possession form.

The sex ratio is a lesson in how samples mislead. In adult clinical case series women outnumber men by as much as 9 to 1, but these figures come from accumulated case reports, and DSM-5-TR notes that women do not predominate in child and adolescent clinics or in general population studies. Men may simply be missed: the original DSM-5 (2013) noted that adult men with DID may deny their symptoms and trauma histories, which leads to false-negative diagnoses, and that they show more criminal or violent behaviour than women, so some reach the courts rather than a therapist's office.

The disorder almost always begins in childhood, often as early as 4 years of age, but children seldom show obvious switching. They present instead with imaginary companions who act independently, with personified "moods", with problems of memory, concentration and attachment, or with traumatic play. Adolescents tend to come to attention because of self-harm, aggression or rapid shifts of behaviour that are mistaken for ADHD or bipolar disorder (Chapters 3 and 5). On average about 7 years pass between the first symptoms and the diagnosis. Without treatment the disorder tends to last for life, though switching may become less frequent with age. New stresses can bring the identities into the open: a later rape, a minor road accident, the death or illness of the abuser, or one's own child reaching the age at which one was abused.

Patients with DID rarely have DID alone. In one sample of more than 100 patients, Ellason and Ross (1997) found more than seven additional diagnoses on average. PTSD is present in most, and depression, substance use, eating disorders, self-injury, functional neurological symptoms such as non-epileptic seizures, and personality disorder features (avoidant, borderline and obsessive-compulsive) are all common. The risk to life is real: DSM-5-TR reports that over 70 percent of outpatients with DID have attempted suicide, often many times, and that dissociation is itself an independent risk factor for repeated attempts.

Indian context

Classic non-possession DID has always been reported far less often in India than in North America. At the All India Institute of Medical Sciences in New Delhi, Adityanjee, G. S. Raju and S. K. Khandelwal (1989) described only three consecutive cases in three years, a rate of about 0.15 per 1,000 patients a year, and pointed out that patients with many of the same features were usually diagnosed instead with possession syndrome. The same symptoms, in other words, are read through a different cultural lens: what a Toronto clinic calls an alter, a family in Uttar Pradesh may call a spirit. Section 7.6.7 takes this up.

Exam trap

DID begins in childhood, usually after abuse before the age of about 5 or 6, though it is diagnosed on average about 7 years after symptoms appear. The female excess (up to 9 to 1) is found in adult clinical samples only, not in children or the community. DSM-5-TR prevalence: 1.5 percent (12-month, a small US community study) and 1.1 percent (lifetime, women in Turkey). Over 70 percent of outpatients have attempted suicide.

Trauma or role? The two models of dissociative identity disorder

Why would a mind divide? Two answers have been on offer since the 1980s, and they predict very different things about where the alters come from and what therapists should do.

The trauma model. The trauma model (also called the posttraumatic model) holds that DID is a developmental response to severe, repeated trauma in early childhood, usually abuse by the people the child depends on. Barlow and Durand put the logic simply. Imagine being a small child abused again and again. You are too young to run away or to call for help, and you may not even know that what is happening is unusual. But you can do one thing: you can escape into fantasy, become somebody else, let "another girl" take it. If the escape blunts the pain for even a minute, you will escape again, and over years the escapes harden into separate states, each holding its own memories, emotions and jobs. The evidence for this account starts with histories. DSM-5-TR reports that about 90 percent of people with DID, in studies from many regions, describe multiple kinds of early neglect and abuse, often continuing into late adolescence. In Putnam's 100 cases (1986), 97 percent had experienced significant trauma, usually sexual or physical abuse, and 68 percent reported incest; in Ross's 97 cases (1990), 95 percent reported physical or sexual abuse. The obvious objection is that these are patients' own reports, gathered by therapists who expected them. The strongest reply came from Dorothy Otnow Lewis and colleagues (1997), who studied 12 murderers with DID and checked their histories against medical, school, social service, military and prison records and interviews with relatives. Dissociative symptoms in childhood and adulthood were independently corroborated in all 12, objective evidence of severe abuse was found in 11, and handwriting or signatures changed markedly across documents in 10. The subjects had amnesia for most of the abuse and under-reported it.

Two refinements make the trauma model more precise. The autohypnotic model (Putnam, 1991) adds a vulnerability: people who are highly suggestible, or easily hypnotised, may be able to use self-hypnosis as a defence, dissociating from the abuse as a hypnotised subject dissociates from a painful stimulus. About half of DID patients clearly remember imaginary playmates in childhood (Ross and colleagues, 1990), a sign of a rich and absorbing fantasy life, and DSM-5-TR notes that people with DID have the highest hypnotic capacity of any clinical group. The model also proposes a developmental window: as children learn to separate fantasy from reality, the capacity to split off new identities seems to close at about the age of 9, after which severe trauma may produce PTSD but not DID. The second refinement sees DID as an extreme subtype of PTSD, weighted towards dissociation rather than anxiety (Butler and colleagues, 1996): most patients meet criteria for PTSD as well, and both disorders start from overwhelming trauma meeting a vulnerable person.

Biological findings fit the trauma model without proving it. In a twin study of 428 adolescents (280 identical and 148 fraternal twins), Niels G. Waller and Ross (1997) found that none of the variance in pathological dissociation was attributable to heredity: all of it was environmental, about 45 percent from the environment the twins shared (Barlow and Durand point to a chaotic, unsupportive family) and the rest from experiences unique to each twin. DSM-5-TR, summarising later twin studies of dissociative symptoms in general, puts the genetic share at about 45 to 50 percent (the "about half" given for dissociative symptoms in Section 7.5.5), with non-shared stressful and traumatic experience accounting for most of the rest; the two statements concern different measures and samples, and the safe conclusion is that environment, especially trauma, carries a large part of the weight. People with DID have been found to have smaller hippocampal and amygdala volumes than healthy controls (Vermetten and colleagues, 2006), as in PTSD, and DSM-5-TR lists the orbitofrontal cortex, hippocampus, parahippocampal gyrus and amygdala among the regions implicated. Functional imaging of one patient during a switch showed changes in hippocampal and medial temporal activity (Tsai and colleagues, 1999), and different alters have shown different patterns of skin conductance, EEG and visual function, including the microstrabismus of Eve Black and, in a larger study by S. D. Miller (1989), four and a half times as many changes in visual measures across alters as in controls asked to simulate alters.

The sociocognitive model. The sociocognitive model starts from the opposite end. Its chief architect, the American-born psychologist Nicholas P. Spanos (1942 to 1994) of Carleton University in Ottawa, had spent his career showing that hypnotic behaviour is not the product of a special trance state but of people enacting the role that the setting and the hypnotist expect, even while it feels involuntary to them. In his last book, Multiple Identities and False Memories, published in 1996, two years after his death in a plane crash, he applied the same logic to DID. The alters, he argued, are social roles: ways of being a patient that a culture makes available (through books like Sybil, films and talk shows), that therapists elicit by asking leading questions, using hypnosis and naming the "parts" they find, and that suggestible, distressed people then enact and come to believe in. On this view the disorder is largely iatrogenic, produced by its treatment, and the epidemic of the 1980s is explained without needing an epidemic of abuse.

Key study: the Hillside Strangler and the student murderers (Spanos, Weekes and Bertrand, 1985)

In the late 1970s Kenneth Bianchi, one of the "Hillside Strangler" killers who raped and murdered young women around Los Angeles, insisted on his innocence despite overwhelming evidence. A defence psychologist hypnotised him and asked whether there was another part of Ken he could talk to. "Steve" answered, said he had done the killings, and said Ken knew nothing about them, and an insanity plea followed. The prosecution called Martin T. Orne, a leading expert on hypnosis. Orne mentioned in passing that genuine cases usually had at least three personalities, and Bianchi soon produced a third. Orne found no one who had seen any alter before the arrest, no real differences between the personalities on psychological tests, textbooks on psychopathology in Bianchi's room, and a performance that looked like someone simulating hypnosis rather than someone deeply hypnotised. The defence collapsed, and Bianchi was convicted and sentenced to life imprisonment.

Spanos, Weekes and Bertrand then asked a laboratory question: could ordinary people produce an alter if the situation invited one? University students were told to play an accused murderer who claimed innocence and were given, word for word, the interview Bianchi had received. More than 80 percent of them produced an alternate personality to escape conviction, whereas students given vaguer instructions, with no hint that another personality might exist, rarely did. Criticism: students knowingly playing a role are not patients who have suffered for years without any legal motive, so the study shows that alters can be produced on demand, not that clinical alters are produced that way.

Memory research supplies the sociocognitive model's best evidence. If alters are truly walled off from one another, material learned by one should be unavailable to another. Patients say that it is. But when cognitive psychologists test memory objectively, especially implicit memory, the walls leak. Huntjens and colleagues (2006) found that DID patients behaved like simulators with respect to material their other identities had learned, the very material they said they could not recall. Kong, Allen and Glisky (2008) had patients learn words in one identity and then switch: they remembered the words just as well as before, contrary to their own report of inter-identity amnesia. Add the evidence that people with DID are highly suggestible and the survey by Pope and colleagues (1999) in which only about a third of American psychiatrists thought DID should have been included in DSM without reservation, and the sociocognitive case is strong.

It is not complete, however. Lewis's corroborated childhood symptoms began long before any therapist was involved; alters in many reports differ in physiological measures that are hard to fake; and the sociocognitive model has little to say about why some people take up the role and others do not. Richard P. Kluft (1999) points out a clinical difference too: people who fake DID tend to display their symptoms eagerly and fluently, while genuine patients tend to hide them (Section 7.6.6). Table 7.16 sets the two models side by side.

Table 7.16 The trauma model and the sociocognitive model of dissociative identity disorder.
QuestionTrauma (posttraumatic) modelSociocognitive model
What are alters?States split off in childhood to escape unbearable abuseSocial roles learned and enacted by suggestible, distressed people
Main proponentsPutnam, Ross, Kluft; autohypnotic version (Putnam 1991)Spanos (1985, 1996); Lilienfeld, Lynn and others
Evidence forAbout 90 percent report early abuse; corroborated abuse in 11 of 12 cases (Lewis 1997); high hypnotisability; hippocampal and amygdala findings80 percent of students produce alters on cue (1985); memory transfers between identities (2006, 2008); diagnostic "bubble" of the 1990s; Sybil
Evidence againstAbuse histories are mostly retrospective self-report; some are confabulated (Kluft)Childhood symptoms documented before therapy; physiological differences between alters; cannot say who is vulnerable
Implication for therapyWork through trauma and integrate the identitiesAvoid hypnosis, leading questions and naming of "parts"; treat current problems

Most clinicians now combine the two in the diathesis-stress terms of Book 2, Section 1.7. Severe early trauma, in a child whose temperament gives a high capacity for dissociation and absorption, and who lacks a supportive adult, produces a real and lasting tendency to dissociate; the form that tendency takes, how many identities there are, what they are called, whether they appear as parts of the self or as possessing spirits, is then shaped by culture, by the media and, sometimes, by the questions a therapist asks. Both models can be right about different parts of the same patient.

Exam trap

The sociocognitive model is Nicholas Spanos's; it says alters are socially reinforced roles, often iatrogenic. The autohypnotic model is Putnam's; it is a version of the trauma model that adds suggestibility. "More than 80 percent of students produced an alter" is Spanos, Weekes and Bertrand (1985), modelled on the Bianchi interview; the expert who unmasked Bianchi was Martin Orne. The developmental window for DID is said to close at about age 9.

Lost and found memories: the recovered memory debate

The trauma model raises a question that spilled out of the clinic into courtrooms, newspapers and families during the 1990s. If a child dissociates from abuse, can the memory of it be lost for years and then come back? And if it comes back during therapy, how do we know it is true? This is the recovered memory debate, sometimes called the memory wars. Barlow and Durand frame it by asking you to count the harms on each side. If abuse really happened and was forgotten, then helping the person to remember and work it through may relieve years of suffering, and the abuser, who has committed a crime, should be held to account. If instead the memories were created by a careless therapist's repeated suggestions, yet feel completely real to the patient, then families are broken, innocent parents are accused and sometimes imprisoned, and the patient is left with a false and terrible past. Both harms have happened. In the United States, families who said they had been falsely accused formed the False Memory Syndrome Foundation in the early 1990s, and therapists were sued for large damages by former patients who retracted their memories.

The evidence that false memories can be created is solid, and Book 2, Section 3.10.7 sets it out: Elizabeth F. Loftus and her colleagues showed in the mid-1990s that about a quarter of adults could be led, through suggestions from a trusted relative, to "remember" being lost in a shopping mall as a child, an event that never happened, and later work showed that a planted memory of falling ill after eating egg salad could make people avoid egg salad months later. Young children are especially vulnerable to repeated suggestive questioning (Book 2, Section 10.6.4). A striking study by Susan Clancy, Richard McNally and their colleagues (2002) examined people who had "recovered" memories of being abducted by aliens, an event few would accept as real: compared with controls, they made more false recalls and false recognitions on laboratory memory tasks and scored higher on suggestibility. Memory, in short, is reconstructive, and some people, especially those high in absorption and fantasy, reconstruct more freely than others.

Key study: women whose abuse was on record (Williams, 1994)

Linda Meyer Williams traced 129 women whose sexual abuse in childhood had been documented at the time, in hospital records, when they were brought for treatment as girls. At least 17 years later she interviewed them at length about their childhoods, without telling them that the abuse was already on record. Thirty-eight percent did not recall the documented abuse, even with extensive probing, and lack of recall was commoner when the girl had been very young and had known the abuser. The study showed that real, documented abuse can be absent from adult memory. Criticism and counterweight: not reporting is not the same as not remembering (some women may have chosen not to disclose), and very early events are forgotten by everyone. Goodman and colleagues (2003), interviewing 175 people with documented abuse, found that 81 percent remembered and reported it, and that disclosure was likelier when the abuse had ended at an older age and when the child had been supported after first telling someone.

A third line of work dissolves part of the debate. Richard J. McNally and Elke Geraerts (2009) described people who had not thought about childhood abuse for many years and then recalled it on meeting a reminder outside therapy, such as a film or a visit to the old neighbourhood. Their memories were often corroborated, and they did not need repression, dissociation or false memory to explain them: they had simply not thought about the events, perhaps because as children they had not understood them as abuse, and a cue brought them back. Barlow and Durand conclude that among people who report memories of childhood sexual abuse there are at least four groups: those who have remembered all along; those with false memories created by suggestion; those who have recovered memories in therapy of abuse that may well have happened; and those who forgot ordinarily and remembered later. The clinician's task is not to decide in advance which group a patient belongs to, but to avoid creating the second.

Clinical link

The lessons for practice are concrete. Ask about trauma with open questions ("Has anything happened to you that still troubles you?") rather than leading ones ("Your symptoms are typical of abuse survivors; what do you remember?"). Do not use hypnosis, guided imagery or a drug-assisted interview to "recover" memories, since they raise confidence more than accuracy (Book 2, Sections 3.10.7 and 3.10.8). Do not treat a symptom as proof of a hidden event, and record what the patient said in their own words. In suspected dissociative disorders, ask about the experiences themselves (lost time, being told of things one has no memory of doing, finding objects one cannot account for, being called by another name) and never ask, as Bianchi's examiner did, "Is there another part of you I can talk to?"

Exam trap

The "lost in the mall" false memory study is Elizabeth Loftus's. The finding that 38 percent of women with documented childhood abuse did not recall it is Williams (1994); the finding that 81 percent did remember is Goodman and colleagues (2003). Recovered memories are neither always true nor always false, and hypnosis increases confidence in memories more than their accuracy.

Telling dissociative identity disorder apart, and treating it

Because the symptoms of DID overlap with so many other conditions, it is misdiagnosed in both directions: real cases are called schizophrenia, bipolar disorder or borderline personality disorder for years, and some patients with those disorders, or with nothing more than suggestibility and distress, are wrongly called DID. The examiners' favourite trap is the oldest confusion of all.

Schizophrenia. In everyday speech "schizophrenia" means a "split personality", but when Eugen Bleuler coined the word he meant a split between thought, emotion and behaviour, not a division into several people (Chapter 4). The confusion is understandable, because DSM-5-TR notes that DID can produce experiences that superficially resemble the first-rank symptoms once thought typical of schizophrenia: different identities talking about the person sound like voices arguing or commenting; the thoughts and feelings of an intruding identity feel like thought insertion; their sudden disappearance feels like thought withdrawal. The differences lie in the details. A person with schizophrenia usually builds a delusional explanation around the experience (a transmitter in the wall is putting thoughts in), whereas a person with DID experiences the intrusions as alien and frightening but without such a belief. Hallucinations in schizophrenia are mainly auditory, whereas in DID they occur in every sense and are often fragments of flashbacks. DID has dissociative amnesia, which psychosis does not, and lacks the negative symptoms and formal thought disorder of schizophrenia. And the two disorders sit at opposite ends of a measurable trait: people with schizophrenia have low hypnotic capacity, people with DID the highest of any clinical group.

Bipolar disorder. DSM-5-TR says DID is commonly misdiagnosed as bipolar disorder, typically bipolar II with mixed features. But what alternates in bipolar disorder is mood and energy, not identity, and the episodes last days to weeks, whereas the state changes of DID occur within minutes or hours and are tied to particular identities. The person with bipolar disorder remembers the episode, and usually shows the classic decreased need for sleep of mania; the person with DID instead loses sleep to nightmares and nocturnal flashbacks (Chapter 5).

Borderline personality disorder. This is the hardest boundary, because many patients have features of both. Borderline personality disorder brings an unstable self-image, rapid shifts of mood within a day, self-harm and stormy relationships (Chapter 11), and some critics, Scott Lilienfeld and Steven Jay Lynn among them (2003), have argued that most DID symptoms are best explained as borderline features. DSM-5-TR draws the line this way: in DID the variability over time reflects inconsistency between identity states, each of which may be quite stable in itself, and there is inter-identity amnesia; in personality disorder there is a pervasive and persistent dysfunction in managing emotion and relationships without separate states or amnesia. People with DID also typically lack the frantic efforts to avoid abandonment that define borderline personality disorder, even when they are attached to an abusive partner.

The other near neighbours. Dissociative amnesia has the memory gaps without the identity disruption, and depersonalisation/derealisation disorder has neither (Section 7.5). In PTSD, including its dissociative subtype and complex PTSD, amnesia is limited to aspects of the trauma, and depersonalisation is tied to reminders, whereas in DID amnesia is chronic and spreads through ordinary days (Section 7.2). Functional neurological symptom disorder may involve amnesia for a non-epileptic attack, but no identity states (Section 7.8). Major depression can be mimicked by the lifelong negative mood of many DID patients, but it has no shifts of self and agency. Substance blackouts, complex partial seizures and traumatic brain injury produce memory gaps too; head injury also brings loss of consciousness, disorientation and lasting cognitive deficits (Chapter 10).

Feigned DID. Because a second personality is an obvious defence in a criminal case, feigned DID must always be considered in forensic settings (Book 2, Section 10.9.5). DSM-5-TR lists the clues. People who fake the disorder tend to over-report the dramatic, media-familiar symptoms (sudden total amnesia, theatrical switching) and under-report the less publicised ones such as depression; they seem relatively untroubled by "having" the disorder or even to enjoy it, and may ask the clinician to "find" their traumatic memories; their alters are few and stereotyped, often an "all-good" and an "all-bad" identity offered in the hope of exculpation for a crime; their amnesia covers only the events for which gain is sought; and switching appears only while they are being observed. Genuine patients, by contrast, are usually ashamed of their symptoms, deny the diagnosis, under-report and avoid talking about their trauma. Kluft adds that malingerers display their symptoms eagerly and fluently, while patients try to hide them. The general problem of detecting deception is taught in Section 7.9. Table 7.17 collects the comparisons.

Table 7.17 Dissociative identity disorder and the conditions most often confused with it.
ConditionWhat changesAmnesia between states?Telling clue
Dissociative identity disorderIdentity and sense of agency: two or more personality statesYes, recurrent, for everyday eventsLost time, unexplained possessions, being called by another name
SchizophreniaThought, emotion and behaviour come apart (Bleuler's "split")NoDelusional explanations, negative symptoms, low hypnotisability
Bipolar disorderMood and energy, over days to weeksNo; episodes are rememberedDecreased need for sleep; one continuous identity
Borderline personality disorderSelf-image and mood, unstably, within a dayNo inter-identity amnesiaPervasive pattern; frantic fear of abandonment
Possession in accepted ritualA deity or spirit speaks, as expectedOften brief, for the episode onlySought, time-limited, welcomed by the community
Feigned dissociative identity disorderDisplayed, stereotyped "alters"Claimed only for events tied to gainEager, dramatic display; external motive

Treatment. There are no controlled trials of any treatment for DID, so what follows rests on case series and clinical consensus. The usual approach, set out in the guidelines of the International Society for the Study of Trauma and Dissociation (third revision, 2011), is long-term, phase-oriented psychotherapy. The first phase establishes safety, stabilisation and symptom reduction: control of self-harm and suicidal behaviour, grounding skills for dissociative episodes, management of comorbid problems, and building trust, which Barlow and Durand call essential. The second phase confronts and works through the traumatic memories, at a pace the person can bear, so that a past event becomes "simply a terrible memory instead of a current event". The third phase aims at integration of the identities, or at least smooth cooperation among them, and at rehabilitation in work and relationships. The same phased logic is used for complex PTSD (Section 7.3). Integration is hard to achieve: Coons (1986), following 20 patients for an average of 39 months, found that nine reached partial or full integration but only 5 kept full integration, and Ellason and Ross (1997) reported integration in 12 of 54 patients (22.2 percent) two years after starting treatment (the 54 were those who could be traced out of 135 inpatients), results that cannot be credited to therapy without a control group. Hypnosis has often been used to reach dissociated memories and identities, but there is no evidence that it is necessary, and Section 7.6.5 explains why it carries real risks. The sociocognitive critics add that a therapy which names, interviews and elaborates the alters may enlarge the disorder, and they recommend keeping the focus on present-day functioning. The techniques themselves (supportive psychotherapy and hypnotherapy, trauma-focused work, and dialectical behaviour therapy for self-harm) are taught in Book 2, Sections 3.10, 5.6.5 and 5.8.

No drug treats DID itself. Medication is aimed at what accompanies it: antidepressants for comorbid depression and for PTSD symptoms (Section 7.3 teaches the drug treatment of PTSD), with caution about sedatives in patients who already lose time. Barlow and Durand note that there is little evidence that medication helps much.

Exam trap

"Multiple distinct personalities within one body" is dissociative identity disorder, not schizophrenia (whose "split" is between thought and emotion, with delusions and negative symptoms), not bipolar disorder (alternating moods, remembered, over days or weeks) and not borderline personality disorder (an unstable self-image with no separate identities and no inter-identity amnesia). The core feature is two or more distinct personality states plus recurrent gaps in recall; dissociative amnesia has the gaps without the states, and schizophrenia is a psychotic, not a dissociative, disorder.

Possession, trance and Ganser states

Every year at the jatre, the temple festival, of her village near Mandya, Bhavna, a 24-year-old woman married into a large joint family, waits with the other women before the shrine of the goddess. As the drums quicken her body begins to sway, her hair comes loose and the goddess "comes" to her. She speaks in a deep voice, blesses children and answers questions about a lost cow and a marriage proposal. Afterwards she is tired, remembers little, and is treated with new respect. No one, Bhavna included, regards this as illness, and neither should a clinician. The state is sought and expected, it happens at the right time and place, it is welcomed by the community, and it causes no distress. This is exactly what Criterion D of DID, and the equivalent rules of ICD-10 and ICD-11, exclude: DSM-5-TR stresses that the majority of possession states around the world are part of broadly accepted cultural or religious practice and are not disorders.

Two years later her husband brings Bhavna to the district hospital. For three months she has been "caught", at night or alone in the kitchen, by the spirit of his first wife, who died in childbirth. She falls to the floor, her eyes roll up, and in a harsh voice the spirit abuses her mother-in-law and demands that the family stop pressing Bhavna for a son and let her visit her parents. The episodes come without warning, Bhavna cannot stop them, she is terrified of them, and the household is in uproar. Between episodes she is herself, and she remembers only fragments of what the spirit said. The same culture that honours her festival possession sees this as an affliction; so does she.

The contrast shows the four marks DSM-5-TR uses to separate pathological possession from culturally accepted possession. The pathological state is involuntary, distressing and uncontrollable; it brings the person into conflict with family, community or work; and it appears at times and in places that violate cultural or religious norms. Notice, too, what Barlow and Durand point out about possession states in many cultures: the spirit often demands, and receives, gifts or favours from the family. Bhavna's "first wife" voices, in a form the family cannot dismiss, demands that Bhavna herself could never make as a young daughter-in-law, a vivid example of the secondary gain described in Section 7.1. Trance and possession states are most common in women and are often linked to stress that is current rather than in the distant past.

Indian context

When S. Saxena and K. V. Prasad (1989) tried to fit 62 outpatients with DSM-III dissociative disorders at the All India Institute of Medical Sciences in New Delhi into DSM-III's categories, 56 of them (90.3 percent) fell into the leftover "atypical" category, because their presentations, many of them possession states, matched none of the Western subtypes. They proposed two Indian subcategories, simple dissociative disorder and possession disorder. Barlow and Durand cite this work, alongside reports from Nigeria and Thailand, for the point that dissociative trances are common in India and other Asian and African countries, while almost unknown in Western clinics. Possession syndrome as a cultural concept of distress, and the running syndromes such as amok and pibloktoq, are taught in Chapter 1.

How would Bhavna be diagnosed? It depends on the system, which is why the classification is examined. ICD-10 had a ready category: F44.3, trance and possession disorders, defined as a temporary loss of the sense of personal identity and of full awareness of the surroundings, counting only states that are involuntary or unwanted and that occur outside religious or culturally accepted situations. ICD-11 splits this into two. Trance disorder (6B62) is a marked alteration of consciousness or loss of the usual sense of identity, with a narrowed awareness of the surroundings and movements, postures and speech restricted to a small repertoire, but without replacement by another identity; the episodes are recurrent or, if single, last at least several days. Possession trance disorder (6B63) is a trance in which the usual sense of identity is replaced by an external "possessing" identity, and the person's behaviour or movements are experienced as controlled by the possessing agent; it too must be involuntary, unwanted and not part of collective cultural or religious practice. Bhavna fits 6B63 exactly. DSM-5-TR has no named trance or possession disorder. If possessing identities came with the recurrent amnesia for everyday events of Criterion B, Bhavna would have possession-form DID. She does not: her memory gaps are confined to the episodes, so DSM-5-TR would record other specified dissociative disorder, naming the reason (a possession-type trance).

That residual category, other specified dissociative disorder, is used when dissociative symptoms cause distress or impairment but do not meet the criteria of a named disorder, and the clinician states why. DSM-5-TR gives four examples. The first is chronic and recurrent syndromes of mixed dissociative symptoms: identity disturbance with less than marked discontinuities in self and agency, or alterations of identity or episodes of possession in someone who reports no dissociative amnesia. The second is identity disturbance due to prolonged and intense coercive persuasion, in people subjected to brainwashing, thought reform, indoctrination while captive, torture, long political imprisonment, or recruitment by cults or terror organisations, who may show lasting changes in, or conscious questioning of, their identity. The third is acute dissociative reactions to stressful events, transient states lasting less than a month and sometimes only hours or days, with constriction of consciousness, depersonalisation, derealisation, perceptual disturbances such as time slowing, small gaps in memory, transient stupor or changes in sensation and movement. The fourth is dissociative trance: an acute narrowing or complete loss of awareness of the immediate surroundings, shown as profound unresponsiveness or insensitivity to stimuli, sometimes with small stereotyped movements (of the fingers, for example) of which the person is unaware, and sometimes with transient paralysis or loss of consciousness, outside any accepted collective practice. When the clinician chooses not to give the reason, or there is too little information, as in an emergency department, the diagnosis is unspecified dissociative disorder.

ICD-10 also described a state close to dissociative trance: dissociative stupor (F44.2), a profound reduction or absence of voluntary movement and of normal responses to light, noise and touch, with no physical cause, and with positive evidence of a psychological cause in recent stressful events or problems. The person lies or sits motionless for long periods, mute, though muscle tone, posture and breathing show they are not unconscious. The differentials matter, because each has a different treatment: catatonic stupor in schizophrenia or mood disorder (Chapter 4), depressive stupor (Chapter 5) and organic stupor from a neurological or medical cause. DSM-5-TR would record a brief stress-related stupor as an acute dissociative reaction under other specified dissociative disorder.

The last of the old "hysterical" states belongs to the prison. In 1898 the German psychiatrist Sigbert Ganser (1853 to 1931) described, under the title "A peculiar hysterical twilight state", several prisoners awaiting trial in Halle who behaved in a way no one had seen before. Asked simple questions, they gave answers that were wrong but close to the mark, in a way that showed they had understood the question. The examples still used to teach the sign are of this kind: asked how many legs a horse has, "five"; asked what two and two make, "five". This is the symptom of approximate answers, in German Vorbeireden, "talking past the point", defined with the other disorders of speech in Chapter 2. Ganser described it as part of a syndrome, now called Ganser syndrome, with three further features: a clouding of consciousness (a twilight state in which the person seems dazed and disoriented), hallucinations, usually described as pseudohallucinations, and somatic conversion symptoms such as patches of lost sensation. The state ended suddenly and was followed by amnesia for it.

From the beginning, Ganser's colleagues argued about what they were seeing. Was it hysteria, a genuine dissociative twilight state brought on by the terror of trial, or simulation by men who stood to gain from appearing insane? The very nature of the approximate answer fuels the suspicion: to miss the right answer so consistently by a little, you must know the right answer. Against simulation stand the dazed state, the later amnesia, and reports of the syndrome outside prison, after head injury and in people with no obvious gain. ICD-10 lists Ganser syndrome under F44.8, other dissociative (conversion) disorders, alongside multiple personality disorder, and DSM-IV listed it as an example of dissociative disorder not otherwise specified. DSM-5 dropped the name, so a case today would be recorded as other specified or unspecified dissociative disorder, or, where deliberate feigning is shown, as factitious disorder or malingering (Section 7.9). Table 7.18 places all these states in the three systems.

Table 7.18 Possession, trance, identity and Ganser states across DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11.
PresentationDSM-5-TRICD-10ICD-11
Possession or trance within accepted ritualNot a disorderNot a disorderNot a disorder
Involuntary trance, no new identityOther specified: dissociative tranceF44.3 trance and possession disorders6B62 trance disorder
Involuntary possession, no everyday amnesiaOther specified dissociative disorderF44.3 trance and possession disorders6B63 possession trance disorder
Identities or possession plus recurrent everyday amnesiaDissociative identity disorderF44.8 multiple personality disorder6B64 dissociative identity disorder
One dominant state with intrusionsOther specified dissociative disorderF44.8 (other)6B65 partial dissociative identity disorder
Ganser syndromeNot named (other specified or unspecified)F44.8 Ganser syndromeNot named (6B6Y, other specified)
Exam trap

Possession within an accepted religious ritual is not a disorder; it becomes one when it is involuntary, distressing, in conflict with family or community and out of place. ICD-10's category is F44.3 trance and possession disorders; ICD-11 has trance disorder and possession trance disorder; DSM-5-TR has neither by name and uses other specified dissociative disorder. Ganser syndrome (Sigbert Ganser, 1898, prisoners awaiting trial) means approximate answers plus clouding of consciousness, pseudohallucinations and conversion symptoms, followed by amnesia. Approximate answers are not confabulation (filling memory gaps, Chapter 10) and not the flight of ideas of mania. Dissociative stupor (ICD-10 F44.2) must be told apart from catatonic, depressive and organic stupor.

7.7 Somatic Symptom Disorder and Illness Anxiety DisorderMedium yield · 3 Qs

Every general practitioner in India knows the patient who arrives with a plastic bag of old prescriptions and reports. The complaint may be "full body pain", "gas", "weakness" or "burning in the hands and feet"; the tests have been normal, or abnormal in ways that do not explain the suffering; and the patient has already seen four other doctors and will soon see a fifth. Other patients have no symptoms worth the name but cannot shake the certainty that a cancer is growing somewhere inside them. Bodily worry of this kind fills clinics everywhere in the world, yet it reaches mental health professionals late, if at all. This section asks when concern about the body becomes a disorder, and why the answer to that question changed completely in 2013. The disorder whose symptoms imitate neurological disease, conversion, is taught in Section 7.8, and deliberate deception, factitious disorder and malingering, in Section 7.9.

From the somatoform disorders to the somatic symptom disorders

The DSM-5-TR chapter called somatic symptom and related disorders holds seven diagnoses: somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety disorder, functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder), psychological factors affecting other medical conditions, factitious disorder, and the other specified and unspecified categories. What they share, in DSM-5-TR's words, is the prominence of somatic symptoms or illness anxiety associated with significant distress and impairment. The word somatic comes from the Greek soma, the body. Because their complaints are bodily, people with these disorders are met mainly in primary care and hospital clinics, not in psychiatric ones, and DSM-5 rebuilt the chapter partly to make it usable by the physicians who actually see them.

From DSM-III (1980) to DSM-IV-TR (2000) the same territory was called the somatoform disorders, literally "body-shaped" disorders: conditions with physical symptoms and complaints that suggest a medical condition but are not fully explained by any medical condition, by a substance or by another mental disorder, so that there is no evidence of the physical pathology the symptoms suggest. DSM-IV listed somatisation disorder, undifferentiated somatoform disorder, conversion disorder, pain disorder, hypochondriasis and body dysmorphic disorder, with a not otherwise specified category. Body dysmorphic disorder, a preoccupation with an imagined or slight defect in appearance, has since moved to the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders (Chapter 6), and conversion has become functional neurological symptom disorder (Section 7.8). The other members are worth meeting one at a time, because their names still appear in exam options.

Briquet's syndrome and somatisation disorder. In 1859 the Paris physician Paul Briquet (1796 to 1881) published a treatise on hysteria based on 430 patients he had studied over ten years. (Some sources, among them French library catalogues and Barlow and Durand, give his first name as Pierre; he is more usually known as Paul.) Many of his patients came with seemingly endless lists of complaints in many parts of the body for which he could find no physical basis, and despite his negative findings they soon returned with the same list or a new one with slight variations. A century later the psychiatrists of Washington University in St Louis, among them Samuel B. Guze, set out to give "hysteria" a precise, countable definition: many unexplained symptoms across several organ systems, beginning early in life. Perley and Guze (1962) showed that 39 patients diagnosed this way kept the same picture over six to eight years of follow-up, and the St Louis group revived Briquet's name for it, Briquet's syndrome. DSM-III (1980) renamed it somatisation disorder. In DSM-IV the criteria demanded many physical complaints beginning before the age of 30 and continuing for several years, with at least four pain symptoms in different sites, two gastrointestinal symptoms other than pain, one sexual or reproductive symptom other than pain, and one pseudoneurological symptom such as a paralysis or a lost sense, none fully explained medically. So strict a definition caught very few people, under 1 percent of the population, while the leftover undifferentiated somatoform disorder, one or more unexplained complaints lasting at least 6 months, caught about 19 percent (DSM-5-TR). ICD-10 keeps somatisation disorder (F45.0), requiring multiple, recurrent and frequently changing physical symptoms for at least two years, and lists "Briquet's disorder" as its inclusion term.

Hypochondriasis. The word comes from the hypochondrium (Greek hypo, under, and chondros, cartilage), the region of the abdomen beneath the cartilage of the lower ribs, where ancient physicians located the source of low spirits and vague bodily complaints. In DSM-IV, hypochondriasis meant a preoccupation with fears of having, or the idea that one has, a serious disease, based on the misinterpretation of bodily symptoms, persisting for at least 6 months despite appropriate medical evaluation and reassurance. ICD-10 calls it hypochondriacal disorder (F45.2), a category that also includes body dysmorphic disorder.

Pain disorder was diagnosed when pain was the main complaint and psychological factors were judged to play an important part in its onset, severity or maintenance. It survives in DSM-5-TR as a specifier of somatic symptom disorder, and in ICD-10 as persistent somatoform pain disorder (F45.4). ICD-10 also has somatoform autonomic dysfunction (F45.3), for symptoms presented as if due to disease of an organ under autonomic control, such as the heart or the gut ("cardiac neurosis" and irritable bowel are among its inclusion terms).

Neurasthenia. In 1869 the American neurologist George Miller Beard (1839 to 1883) named a condition of exhaustion, aches, poor concentration and irritability neurasthenia, literally a lack of nerve strength, and blamed it on the pressures of modern life. It was a fashionable diagnosis for decades and then faded in the West, but survived in China as shenjing shuairuo, the subject of Arthur Kleinman's famous study of depression expressed as bodily weakness (Chapter 5). DSM-5-TR lists neurasthenia-like idioms of distress in several cultures, including ashaktapanna in India. ICD-10 keeps neurasthenia (F48.0, mental or physical fatigue after minimal effort) among the other neurotic disorders; DSM-5-TR has no such diagnosis, and ICD-11 dropped it.

Why DSM-5 changed the rules. DSM-5-TR explains the reasons in unusually frank terms. The term "somatoform" confused people; the categories overlapped heavily and had unclear boundaries; the physicians who saw these patients found the diagnoses hard to understand and use. Above all, the old criteria rested on a negative: the symptom had to be medically unexplained. That judgement is unreliable (what is unexplained today may be explained by a test next year), it reinforces a dualism of mind versus body, and patients experienced it as an insult, as if their symptoms were not "real". It is not appropriate, DSM-5-TR says, to give someone a mental disorder diagnosis solely because a medical condition cannot be demonstrated, and the presence of a medical condition does not rule out a mental disorder. The new main diagnosis is defined instead by positive features: distressing somatic symptoms plus abnormal thoughts, feelings and behaviours in response to them. DSM-5-TR reports that about two-thirds to three-quarters of people who would have received DSM-IV hypochondriasis now receive somatic symptom disorder, and the remaining quarter to third, who have high health anxiety without significant somatic symptoms, receive illness anxiety disorder. Not everyone welcomed the change: Allen Frances, who had chaired the DSM-IV task force, warned in 2013 that the new criteria could label as mentally ill many people with real medical illnesses who are understandably worried about them. Table 7.19 maps the old categories onto the new ones.

Table 7.19 The somatic family across DSM-IV, DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11.
Old categoryDSM-IV (1994)DSM-5-TR (2022)ICD-10ICD-11
Briquet's syndromeSomatisation disorderSomatic symptom disorder (usually severe)F45.0 somatisation disorder6C20 bodily distress disorder
Fewer unexplained complaintsUndifferentiated somatoform disorderSomatic symptom disorderF45.1 undifferentiated somatoform6C20 bodily distress disorder
HypochondriasisHypochondriasisSomatic symptom disorder or illness anxiety disorderF45.2 hypochondriacal disorder6B23 hypochondriasis (obsessive-compulsive group)
Pain disorderPain disorderSomatic symptom disorder, with predominant painF45.4 persistent somatoform pain6C20, or a chronic pain code
ConversionConversion disorderFunctional neurological symptom disorder (7.8)F44.4 to F44.7 (dissociative)6B60 dissociative neurological symptom disorder
Body dysmorphic disorderSomatoformObsessive-compulsive group (Chapter 6)Within F45.26B21 (obsessive-compulsive group)
NeurastheniaNot a diagnosisNot a diagnosisF48.0 neurastheniaDropped
