ICD-11 went further than DSM-5 in simplifying. It replaced somatisation disorder, undifferentiated somatoform disorder, most of the other somatoform categories and neurasthenia with a single diagnosis, bodily distress disorder (6C20): distressing bodily symptoms together with excessive attention directed towards them, often shown by repeated contact with health services, which appropriate examination, investigation and reassurance do not relieve. The symptoms are persistent, present on most days for at least several months, and affect functioning. Severity is graded by how much the symptoms dominate life: mild (frequent medical visits but no real preoccupation, for example less than an hour a day spent on the symptoms), moderate (persistent preoccupation, for example more than an hour a day) and severe (pervasive preoccupation that may become the focal point of the person's life). Hypochondriasis kept its old name in ICD-11 but moved out of this family altogether, into the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders (6B23), on the reasoning that its intrusive health fears and repetitive checking resemble obsessions and compulsions more than they resemble bodily distress. One coding warning: DSM-5-TR prints ICD-10-CM codes, the American clinical modification, which do not always match the WHO's ICD-10. Somatic symptom disorder, for example, carries F45.1 in DSM-5-TR, the WHO code for undifferentiated somatoform disorder.

Exam trap

"A group of conditions with symptoms and complaints suggesting a medical condition, without any evidence of physical pathology" is the definition of the somatoform disorders (DSM-III to DSM-IV). Dissociative disorders disrupt memory, identity and consciousness rather than producing bodily complaints (Sections 7.5 and 7.6). Alzheimer's disease and Parkinson's disease are the opposite case: neurodegenerative diseases with clear physical pathology (plaques and tangles; loss of dopamine neurons in the substantia nigra), taught in Chapter 10. Briquet is Paul Briquet, 1859; neurasthenia is Beard, 1869. ICD-11 puts hypochondriasis among the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, and replaces the rest of the somatoform group with bodily distress disorder.

Somatic symptom disorder

Yusuf, a 46-year-old cloth merchant in Hyderabad, has had type 2 diabetes for eight years. His sugar is reasonably controlled, and his doctor has found early, mild neuropathy, which explains the burning he feels in his feet at night. But the burning has taken over his life. He checks his blood sugar eight or ten times a day, examines his feet each evening with a torch for signs of ulcers, and spends hours reading about amputations. He has had three scans of his spine for back pain and has consulted six doctors in the past year, each of whom reassured him; the relief lasts a day. He now closes his shop early because "walking will damage the nerves", and his sons run the business. When his physician suggested he talk to a psychologist he was offended: "My pain is real, not in my head."

Yusuf is right that his pain is real, and under DSM-5-TR that is no obstacle to the diagnosis. Somatic symptom disorder has three criteria, each written to capture something specific.

  • Criterion A: one or more somatic symptoms that are distressing or result in significant disruption of daily life. The symptoms can be specific (localised pain) or vague (fatigue), and they can be ordinary bodily sensations that do not usually mean serious disease.
  • Criterion B: excessive thoughts, feelings or behaviours related to the symptoms or to associated health concerns, shown by at least one of three: disproportionate and persistent thoughts about the seriousness of one's symptoms; a persistently high level of anxiety about health or symptoms; or excessive time and energy devoted to the symptoms or health concerns. This is the heart of the new diagnosis. It replaces the old negative criterion ("no medical explanation") with positive psychological features that can be observed and asked about.
  • Criterion C: although any one symptom may not be continuously present, the state of being symptomatic is persistent, typically for more than 6 months. Brief bodily worry after a scare is common and normal; the disorder is a persistent pattern.

Three kinds of specifier refine the diagnosis. With predominant pain (the old pain disorder) is used when the symptoms are mainly pain. Persistent describes a course with severe symptoms, marked impairment and long duration (more than 6 months). Severity is rated from Criterion B: mild when only one of the three B features is present, moderate when two or more are, and severe when two or more are present along with multiple somatic complaints or one very severe symptom. Yusuf, with high anxiety, catastrophic thoughts and hours of daily checking, is severe.

The most important sentence in DSM-5-TR's description is that the symptoms may or may not be associated with another medical condition. Somatic symptoms without an evident medical explanation are not enough to make the diagnosis, and a real medical illness does not exclude it. DSM-5-TR's own example is a person seriously disabled by somatic symptom disorder after an uncomplicated heart attack that caused no physical disability. A woman with genuine rheumatoid arthritis who researches her joints for hours a day, refuses to move for fear of damage and cannot be reassured has somatic symptom disorder too. Conversely, many people with medically unexplained syndromes such as irritable bowel syndrome or fibromyalgia do not qualify, because they lack the excessive response of Criterion B. What matters is not whether the symptom is explained but how the person responds to it.

The associated features fill out the picture. Thinking is dominated by attention to bodily sensations, by attributing normal sensations to illness (often catastrophically), by a self-concept of bodily weakness and by intolerance of any bodily discomfort. Behaviour includes repeated checking of the body, repeated seeking of medical help and reassurance, and avoidance of physical activity. Reassurance from a doctor tends to be short-lived or to be heard as the doctor not taking the symptoms seriously. Many patients are unusually sensitive to the side effects of medication, and the suggestion of a psychological referral is often met with surprise or refusal, as it was by Yusuf.

Barlow and Durand's patient Linda shows the severe, lifelong end of the spectrum. An intelligent woman in her 30s, she arrived at their clinic short of breath and in pain, and handed over a five-page summary of her contacts with the health-care system for major problems only, with dates, diagnoses and days in hospital, and a page and a half of the medications she had taken. Her problems had begun in her teens. Drawn to hospitals, she entered nursing school, but there she seemed to "pick up" each disease she studied, and after a series of stresses she left. She later developed an unexplained paralysis of the legs (a conversion symptom) and spent a year in a psychiatric hospital before walking again. She was now on disability benefits and was being followed by a family doctor and six specialists, and was seeing two ministers for pastoral counselling. She told her therapist that her symptoms were her identity: without them she would not know who she was or how to relate to other people. Linda's case is an extreme example of the sick role (Book 2, Section 9.1.4).

How common is it? Because the diagnosis is new, prevalence estimates still lean on studies of the older categories. DSM-5-TR expects somatic symptom disorder to be more common than DSM-IV somatisation disorder (under 1 percent) and less common than undifferentiated somatoform disorder (about 19 percent), and approximates the general adult prevalence at 4 to 6 percent, with 10 to 20 percent of primary care patients plausibly affected and 40 to 60 percent in clinics specialising in psychosomatic or functional disorders. Women report more somatic symptoms than men, and Barlow and Durand note that when the problem is severe enough to be a disorder the sex ratio is about 2 to 1, female to male. The disorder is commoner in people with fewer years of education and lower income, and in those who have recently had stressful or health-related life events. Negative affectivity (neuroticism) is an independent risk factor, and childhood adversity such as sexual abuse probably adds to the risk.

Key study: the WHO primary care study of somatisation (Gureje and colleagues, 1997)

Is turning distress into bodily complaints a peculiarity of particular cultures? For decades it was said that patients in non-Western countries "somatise" while Western patients "psychologise". Oye Gureje and his colleagues tested the claim in a World Health Organization study of 5,438 patients attending primary care in 15 centres in 14 countries, among them Bangalore. They used two definitions: full ICD-10 somatisation disorder, and a broader somatic symptom index requiring four medically unexplained symptoms in men or six in women. Full somatisation disorder was uncommon (2.8 percent overall; 1.8 percent in Bangalore), but the broader pattern was common in every centre (19.7 percent overall; 19.6 percent in Bangalore), with similar overall rates in men and women on this sex-adjusted index. Rates did vary, from under a tenth of patients in Ibadan and Nagasaki to about a third in Rio de Janeiro and Santiago, but the variation did not follow geography or the level of economic development. Conclusion: medically unexplained bodily distress is common in primary care across the world; Barlow and Durand conclude that the old idea that it belongs to "developing" cultures came largely from how earlier studies had been done. Limitation: the study used the "medically unexplained" definition that DSM-5 has since abandoned, so it measures the old somatoform concept, not somatic symptom disorder itself.

Course. Somatic symptom disorder is usually chronic and fluctuating. Severe forms typically begin in adolescence, as Linda's did. In children, the commonest symptoms are recurrent abdominal pain, headache, fatigue and nausea, often a single prominent symptom, and the parents' reaction to the symptoms strongly shapes how much distress, investigation and school absence follow. In older people, pain in several parts of the body is most common, and the disorder is probably underdiagnosed because pain and worry are wrongly accepted as part of ageing. Anxiety and depressive disorders each occur in up to half of patients, and somatic symptom disorder is associated with suicidal thoughts and attempts, partly through depression and partly independently.

Exam trap

Asked for "a defining feature of somatic symptom disorder", the keyed answer is often "physical symptoms without a medical cause". That is the old somatoform idea, and DSM-5-TR explicitly rejects it: unexplained symptoms are neither required nor sufficient. The DSM-5-TR feature is distressing somatic symptoms with excessive thoughts, feelings or behaviours about them, for typically more than 6 months, whether or not a medical condition is present. Pick the keyed option only when, as in the usual version, the alternatives are plainly wrong: hallucinations are perceptions without a stimulus (Chapter 2), delusions of grandeur are fixed false beliefs of special power or identity seen in mania and psychosis (Chapters 4 and 5), and obsessive behaviours belong to OCD (Chapter 6). The with predominant pain specifier is the old pain disorder.

Illness anxiety disorder

Tarun, a 27-year-old software engineer in Bengaluru, was well until a colleague of his age died suddenly of a brain haemorrhage. Since then every headache has been a tumour. He reads medical websites late into the night, checks his pupils in the mirror, and asks his wife several times a day whether his face looks lopsided. He has had two normal CT scans; after each he felt relief for a few days, until a new doubt arrived ("perhaps it is too small to show"). Last month, after reading about an actor's lymphoma, the fear shifted to the glands in his neck, which he now presses so often that they are sore. He has no real symptoms beyond the occasional ordinary headache. When his friends joke that he should "stop googling", he agrees that he probably worries too much, but the fear returns within the hour.

Tarun has illness anxiety disorder, the DSM-5 diagnosis for the quarter to third of old hypochondriasis in which the anxiety is about being or becoming ill, not about troublesome symptoms. Its criteria are built to separate it from its neighbours.

  • A. Preoccupation with having or acquiring a serious illness.
  • B. Somatic symptoms are absent or, if present, only mild. If another medical condition is present, or there is a high risk of one (a strong family history, for example), the preoccupation is clearly excessive or disproportionate. This criterion is the line between illness anxiety disorder and somatic symptom disorder.
  • C. A high level of anxiety about health; the person is easily alarmed about personal health status, by hearing that someone else is ill or by reading a news story.
  • D. Excessive health-related behaviours (repeatedly checking the body for signs of illness, researching the disease, seeking reassurance) or maladaptive avoidance (avoiding doctors' appointments, hospitals or visiting sick relatives).
  • E. The preoccupation has lasted at least 6 months, although the specific illness feared may change during that time, as Tarun's did from tumour to lymphoma.
  • F. It is not better explained by another mental disorder: somatic symptom disorder, panic disorder, generalised anxiety disorder, body dysmorphic disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder or delusional disorder, somatic type.

Two types are specified. In the care-seeking type, medical care, including visits, tests and procedures, is used frequently; Tarun is care-seeking. In the care-avoidant type, medical care is rarely used, because the person is too frightened of what a doctor might find. The distress, DSM-5-TR explains, comes not from the physical sensation itself but from anxiety about its meaning. When there is a sensation at all, it is usually a normal one (dizziness on standing up), a benign and passing dysfunction (a moment of ringing in the ears) or a discomfort no one would think of as disease (belching). Barlow and Durand stress a second core feature that it shares with somatic symptom disorder: disease conviction, a difficult-to-shake belief that one has a disease, which is why reassurance helps so briefly. Yet the belief stops short of a delusion: people with illness anxiety disorder can acknowledge that the feared disease may not be present, and their fears, though unfounded, are plausible. Searching the internet for symptoms, sometimes called cyberchondria, feeds the cycle in exactly the way Tarun shows.

Barlow and Durand's patient Gail shows how such fears start and how they change. Gail had grown up feeling weak and neglected as one of many children in a lower-middle-class household, belittled by a stepbrother when he was drunk, and she married at 21 hoping that marriage would make her "someone special". In the first year she discovered that her husband was continuing an affair, and it was then that the fears began. A headache meant a brain tumour, breathlessness an impending heart attack; other sensations became AIDS or cancer. She was afraid to fall asleep in case she stopped breathing, avoided exercise, alcohol and even laughing because of the sensations they caused, and feared public toilets as sources of infection. Each newspaper or television story about the "disease of the month" drew her in; for days afterwards she watched for its symptoms in herself, in others and even in her dog. For years she spent more money than the couple could afford on doctors, always hearing "There's nothing wrong with you". Finally she stopped going, convinced her worries were excessive, but the fears did not stop and she remained chronically miserable. Gail had moved, in effect, from the care-seeking to the care-avoidant pattern, without any lessening of the disorder.

DSM-5-TR's prevalence figures are borrowed from studies of hypochondriasis and health anxiety: a 1- to 2-year prevalence of 1.3 to 10 percent in community surveys in high-income countries, 2.2 to 8 percent in ambulatory medical populations (a weighted mean of 3 percent), and about one-fifth of patients in specialty clinics. Unlike somatic symptom disorder, it is equally common in men and women. It usually begins in early or middle adulthood and runs a chronic, episodic, relapsing course, although one-third to one-half of patients have a transient form with less psychiatric comorbidity. It may be set off by a major life stress or by a health scare that turns out to be benign, and a history of childhood abuse, serious childhood illness, or serious illness or death of a parent during childhood predisposes to it. About two-thirds of patients have at least one other major mental disorder, most often an anxiety disorder, OCD or depression.

The diagnosis has different homes in the three systems. DSM-5-TR places it with the somatic symptom disorders "for utility", because the patients are met in medical settings, while noting that it could equally be considered an anxiety disorder. ICD-10 had hypochondriacal disorder (F45.2) among the somatoform disorders. ICD-11 calls it hypochondriasis (6B23) and places it in the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, with specifiers for fair to good insight and for poor to absent insight, just as it does for OCD and body dysmorphic disorder.

Exam trap

The two DSM-5-TR heirs of hypochondriasis differ in Criterion B: illness anxiety disorder has absent or mild somatic symptoms and high anxiety about having or getting an illness; somatic symptom disorder has distressing symptoms and an excessive response to them. Both need about 6 months. Illness anxiety disorder has care-seeking and care-avoidant types, and its sex ratio is equal. "Hypochondriasis" is still the ICD-11 name, filed under obsessive-compulsive and related disorders.

Telling health anxiety apart

Before any of these diagnoses can be made, the clinician faces the question every patient with bodily worry fears most: what if there really is something wrong? DSM-5-TR lists an underlying medical condition as the first consideration, naming neurological and endocrine disorders, hidden cancers and diseases that affect several body systems at once, any of which can produce vague, shifting symptoms long before a test finally explains them. The presence of a medical condition does not rule out a somatic symptom disorder, but its absence must never be assumed just because a patient seems anxious. With that caution in place, the remaining distinctions are among mental disorders, and they turn on three questions: are there real distressing symptoms, what exactly does the person fear, and over what time course?

Panic disorder (Chapter 6) is the closest neighbour. A person having a panic attack also misreads bodily sensations as signs of catastrophe, but the catastrophe is immediate, a heart attack or death in the next few minutes, and the fear subsides between attacks. In the somatic disorders the feared outcome is a slow disease, cancer or AIDS, and the worry is persistent. The behaviour differs too: Barlow and Durand note that people with panic disorder usually learn fairly quickly to stop going to doctors and emergency rooms, where they are told repeatedly that nothing is wrong, while people with somatic symptom or illness anxiety disorder keep looking for a doctor who will find what the others missed. Panic attacks can occur within illness anxiety disorder when they are triggered by the illness fears.

Generalised anxiety disorder (Chapter 6) involves worry about many things, of which health is only one. In obsessive-compulsive disorder the health-related thoughts are intrusive and usually concern getting a disease in the future (from contamination, for example), they come with compulsions, and most patients have other obsessions too; in illness anxiety disorder the fear is of having a disease now. In body dysmorphic disorder the preoccupation is a perceived defect in appearance, not an illness. In delusional disorder, somatic type (Chapter 4) the belief is held with delusional conviction and is often bizarre (that an organ is rotting, that the body is infested), whereas the fears of illness anxiety disorder are plausible and can be questioned. In major depression (Chapter 5) people often ruminate about their health; if the worry occurs only during depressive episodes, illness anxiety disorder is not diagnosed. Finally, worry about a real, newly diagnosed illness is a normal response, and if it is severe but time-limited it is an adjustment disorder (Section 7.4); illness anxiety disorder becomes the diagnosis if disproportionate worry persists beyond 6 months. The contrast between hypochondriasis and conversion disorder, which is asked as a separate question, is taught in Section 7.8: conversion produces a loss of function such as a paralysed limb, whereas the somatic symptom disorders centre on distress about symptoms. Table 7.20 lays the comparisons out.

Table 7.20 Somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety disorder and the conditions they resemble.
DisorderBodily symptomsWhat is feared or believedCourseInsight
Somatic symptom disorderPresent and distressingThat the symptoms are seriousPersistent, usually over 6 monthsFirm but not delusional
Illness anxiety disorderAbsent or mildHaving or getting a serious illnessAt least 6 months; the feared illness may changeNot delusional
Panic disorderAcute surges during attacksImmediate catastrophe: dying nowEpisodic, minutesGood between attacks
Obsessive-compulsive disorderUsually noneCatching a disease in future; compulsionsChronic, with other obsessionsUsually some

Delusional disorder, somatic typeA false belief about the bodyOften bizarre: an organ rottingAt least 1 monthAbsent

Exam trap

Panic disorder fears an immediate catastrophe during an attack; illness anxiety disorder fears a slow, serious disease and the worry is persistent. Delusional disorder, somatic type, holds the belief with delusional conviction; illness anxiety disorder does not. Body dysmorphic disorder is about appearance, not illness. Health anxiety that appears only during depressive episodes is part of the depression.

Why bodily worry takes hold: causes

Why does one person shrug off a headache while another sees a tumour in it? Investigators who disagree about much else agree on the central process. Somatic symptom disorder and illness anxiety disorder are, as Barlow and Durand put it, disorders of cognition and perception, of how bodily sensations are noticed and interpreted, with strong emotional contributions. Start from a fact that is easy to forget: the healthy body is noisy. On any ordinary day most people could notice a skipped heartbeat, a twinge in the back, dizziness on standing up, a rumble in the gut or a momentary ringing in the ear. Most of us barely register these sensations, or explain them away. The person with health anxiety notices them, reads them as danger, and is then caught in a loop that keeps the danger alive.

The clearest account of that loop came from Hilary M. Warwick and Paul M. Salkovskis (1990), whose cognitive model of hypochondriasis, drawn in Figure 7.12, Barlow and Durand apply to both of the DSM-5-TR disorders. Something triggers the process: a sensation, a news story, an illness in a friend, a doctor's ambiguous remark. The person perceives a threat ("Is this a tumour?"), and the threat produces apprehension. Apprehension then feeds three processes, each of which makes the threat seem more real. First, anxiety raises physiological arousal, which produces genuine new sensations (a racing heart, a tight chest, a churning stomach), and these seem to confirm the fear. Second, anxiety turns attention towards the body, and attending to a part of the body makes its sensations more intense; Arthur J. Barsky called this tendency somatosensory amplification. Third, the person checks and seeks reassurance: prodding the lump, searching the internet, asking a spouse, booking another test. Salkovskis and Warwick had argued in 1986 that reassurance in these patients works like a compulsion in OCD: it brings relief for hours or days, then doubt returns ("Did the doctor examine me properly? Can a scan miss something that small?"), and each round of reassurance makes the next one more necessary. Checking can even create symptoms, as Tarun's sore glands show. All three processes heighten the preoccupation with bodily sensations, the sensations are misinterpreted as signs of serious illness, and the perceived threat grows.

Laboratory studies support each link. On Stroop-type tasks, in which people name the ink colour of words, people with these disorders are slowed by illness words, a sign that such words capture their attention (Hitchcock and Mathews, 1992; Pauli and Alpers, 2002). They tend to interpret ambiguous stimuli as threatening (Haenen and colleagues, 2000). Smeets, de Jong and Mayer (2000) found that they take a "better safe than sorry" approach to even minor symptoms, wanting them checked at once. And they hold a restrictive concept of health as the complete absence of symptoms (Rief and colleagues, 1998), so that any sensation at all counts as evidence of illness.

Where does the pattern come from? Barlow and Durand argue that the vulnerabilities are much the same as for the anxiety disorders (Chapter 6). There is a biological contribution: somatic symptom disorders run in families, and twin studies show a modest genetic contribution, probably nonspecific, such as a tendency to over-respond to stress. There is a general psychological vulnerability, a sense that bad events are unpredictable and uncontrollable and must be guarded against at all times. And there is a specific learned focus on the body. Children with these concerns often report the same symptoms that other family members have had; people who develop the disorders often grew up with a disproportionate amount of illness in the family; and in some families an ill person receives a great deal of attention, so that the benefits of the sick role are learned early (Book 2, Section 9.1.4). The disorders often begin after a stressful life event, frequently one involving illness or death, like Tarun's colleague's sudden death or Gail's discovery of the affair. DSM-5-TR adds further contributors: biological sensitivity (to pain, for example), early trauma such as violence, abuse and deprivation, medical iatrogenesis (doctors who reinforce the sick role and order needless referrals and tests), families and cultures that reward bodily complaints but not the expression of emotional distress, and social stigma that makes physical suffering more acceptable than psychological suffering.

One finding about severe somatic symptom disorder, the old somatisation disorder, startles most students. In family and adoption studies it is strongly linked to antisocial personality disorder (Chapter 11): Guze, Cloninger and colleagues found the two clustering in the same families, with severe somatisation in the women and antisocial behaviour in the men. The two seem to be opposites, one dependent and complaining, the other aggressive and callous, but they share an early onset, a chronic course, lower social class, marital discord, substance misuse and suicide attempts. Scott O. Lilienfeld (1992) reviewed the explanations. One with some support is that both express a shared, partly heritable disinhibition, a preference for short-term gain over long-term cost: the stream of new symptoms brings immediate sympathy and attention, though eventually isolation. C. Robert Cloninger (1987) and Cathy Spatz Widom (1984) proposed that gender socialisation then channels the same vulnerability differently, towards aggression in men and dependence in women; in support, Lilienfeld and Hess (2001) found that female students with antisocial traits reported more somatic symptoms. These ideas remain speculative, but they are a good example of integrative thinking about why one diathesis can produce two very different disorders.

Culture shapes the language of bodily distress. Gureje's WHO study showed that somatic complaints are common in every country's primary care, but which complaints people make, and what they think causes them, varies. DSM-5-TR calls such culturally patterned complaints idioms of distress, and its examples include sensations of heaviness, complaints of "gas", too much heat in the body and burning in the head, as well as explanations in terms of family stress, suppressed anger or semen loss. Barlow and Durand cite a burning sensation in the hands and feet as a characteristic complaint of Indian and Pakistani patients (Kirmayer and Weiss, 1993). The culture-bound syndromes of anxious bodily preoccupation, dhat (distress attributed to loss of semen) and koro (fear that the genitals are retracting into the body), are taught in Chapter 1.

Indian context

In Indian general practice, distress very often arrives as a bodily complaint: "weakness", "full body pain", "gas", "burning", "heaviness in the head", "no strength in the hands and legs". Many of these patients have depression or anxiety that they do not name or will not name, because a bodily illness is more respectable, earns more sympathy from the family and leads to a familiar kind of help (an injection, a tonic, a scan). Somatic symptom disorder is diagnosed when the response to the symptoms is persistently excessive and disabling, but the first clinical task is to recognise the depression or anxiety that may lie underneath (Chapter 5), and the second is never to dismiss the complaint. Telling such a patient "there is nothing wrong with you" or "it is all in your mind" usually sends them to the next doctor.

Exam trap

The vicious circle of health anxiety is Warwick and Salkovskis (1990); somatosensory amplification is Barsky's term. Reassurance seeking and checking are maintaining factors that work like compulsions, not cures. The surprising family link of severe somatisation is with antisocial personality disorder (Guze, Cloninger, Lilienfeld), not with schizophrenia. Somatic complaints are common in primary care everywhere, not only in non-Western countries (Gureje, 1997).

Psychological factors affecting other medical conditions, and the other somatic categories

Somatic symptom disorder asks whether the response to a symptom is excessive. A different question is whether psychological factors are making a real disease worse, and DSM-5-TR answers it with a separate diagnosis, psychological factors affecting other medical conditions. Its criteria require a medical symptom or condition (other than a mental disorder), and psychological or behavioural factors that adversely affect it in one of four ways: by influencing its course, shown by a close timing between the psychological factor and the onset, worsening or delayed recovery of the illness; by interfering with its treatment, as in poor adherence; by adding a well-established health risk; or by influencing the underlying pathophysiology so as to set off or worsen symptoms or require medical attention. The factors must not be better explained by another mental disorder such as panic disorder, depression or PTSD. Severity runs from mild (raising medical risk, as with inconsistent adherence to blood pressure tablets) through moderate (aggravating the condition, as when anxiety worsens asthma) and severe (leading to hospital admission or an emergency visit) to extreme (life-threatening, as when someone ignores the symptoms of a heart attack). DSM-5-TR's other examples include denying the need for treatment of acute chest pain and a person with diabetes who manipulates insulin in order to lose weight; the effects can be acute, as in Takotsubo cardiomyopathy, a sudden and usually reversible weakening of the heart muscle after acute emotional stress, or slow, as when chronic occupational stress raises the risk of hypertension.

Two boundaries define the diagnosis. If the psychological symptoms develop in response to the medical illness (depression after a diagnosis of cancer, for example), the diagnosis is adjustment disorder (Section 7.4), not this one; here the arrow runs from the psychological factor to the disease. And unlike somatic symptom disorder, this diagnosis requires a medical condition and makes no claim that the person's thoughts about it are excessive. In United States insurance data it has been a commoner diagnosis than the DSM-IV somatoform disorders. ICD-10 codes it as F54 and ICD-11 as 6E40. Its intellectual ancestor, psychosomatic medicine with Franz Alexander's "holy seven" diseases, and the modern evidence on stress and illness, are taught in Book 2, Sections 9.3.1 and 9.3.2.

The residual category other specified somatic symptom and related disorder collects presentations that do not meet full criteria. DSM-5-TR's examples are brief somatic symptom disorder and brief illness anxiety disorder, each lasting less than 6 months; illness anxiety disorder without excessive health-related behaviours or maladaptive avoidance (Criterion D not met); and pseudocyesis, a false belief of being pregnant accompanied by objective signs and reported symptoms of pregnancy, such as an enlarging abdomen and missed periods. Pseudocyesis stands out because, like conversion, it is diagnosed by showing a clear incompatibility with the recognised physiology (here, the absence of a pregnancy) rather than by an excessive response. When the clinician cannot or does not specify the reason, the diagnosis is unspecified somatic symptom and related disorder, which DSM-5-TR reserves for decidedly unusual situations with insufficient information.

One condition is often mistaken for a member of this family, and examiners have used the confusion. Chronic fatigue syndrome, also called myalgic encephalomyelitis (ME/CFS), is a long-lasting illness of disabling fatigue, pain, unrefreshing sleep and poor concentration whose cause remains unknown; viral, immune and psychological explanations have all been proposed and none has been established. In 2015 a committee of the United States Institute of Medicine reviewed the evidence and proposed a new name, systemic exertion intolerance disease, to emphasise its central feature: a worsening of symptoms after exertion. The WHO classifies the condition as a disorder of the nervous system, not a mental disorder: in ICD-10 it is postviral fatigue syndrome (G93.3), which the neurasthenia category (F48.0) explicitly excludes, and in ICD-11 it is 8E49, which bodily distress disorder excludes. DSM-5-TR has no diagnosis for it at all.

Exam trap

A question that asks about "DSM-5-TR criteria for chronic fatigue syndrome (systemic exertion intolerance disease)" has a false premise: DSM-5-TR has no such criteria. The criteria are the US Institute of Medicine's (2015). Three are required: (1) a substantial reduction in the ability to carry on pre-illness work, study, social or personal activities, lasting more than 6 months, with fatigue that is often profound, of new onset, not the result of ongoing excessive exertion and not substantially relieved by rest; (2) post-exertional malaise; (3) unrefreshing sleep. At least one more must be present: cognitive impairment or orthostatic intolerance (symptoms on standing upright). Weight loss or gain is not a criterion, in these or in the older 1994 criteria of the US Centers for Disease Control (Fukuda and colleagues), which required 6 months of unexplained fatigue plus four of eight symptoms: impaired memory or concentration, sore throat, tender lymph nodes, muscle pain, joint pain, headaches, unrefreshing sleep, and malaise lasting more than 24 hours after exertion. Nor is chronic fatigue syndrome a somatic symptom disorder by definition, though a person with it may also meet those criteria if the response to the symptoms is excessive.

Treating somatic symptom and illness anxiety disorders

Until recently, Barlow and Durand note, relatively little was known about treating these disorders. Psychodynamic therapy that sought the unconscious conflicts behind the symptoms was common, but its results were rarely reported. The controlled evidence that now exists points to a mix of better medical management, psychological treatment aimed at the vicious circle, and in some cases medication.

Explanation and reassurance, done properly. It seems paradoxical that reassurance should help people defined by their failure to be reassured, but the reassurance they usually receive is brief and dismissive. Giovanni A. Fava and colleagues (2000) tested a fuller version. Twenty patients with DSM-IV hypochondriasis were assigned either to explanatory therapy, in which the clinician went through the sources and nature of their symptoms in detail within an educational framework, or to a waiting list; both groups continued their usual medical care. Explanatory therapy reduced fears and beliefs about illness and cut the use of health care, gains that lasted to a 6-month follow-up, and the waiting-list group improved only once it received the treatment. The study was small and probably helps mainly the milder cases, but it shows that how a doctor explains matters as much as what the tests show.

Key study: six sessions of CBT for hypochondriasis (Barsky and Ahern, 2004)

Arthur Barsky and David Ahern studied 187 people with marked hypochondriasis (all scored above a cut-off on a hypochondriasis questionnaire on two occasions; 80 were primary care patients and 107 were volunteers who answered public announcements). Randomising by primary care physician, so that all of one doctor's patients received the same condition, they assigned 102 to six sessions of cognitive behaviour therapy with trained therapists and 85 to usual care from their primary care physicians. The CBT taught patients to identify and challenge their misinterpretations of bodily sensations, showed them that focusing attention on a body part creates "symptoms" there (a demonstration that the sensations were under their control), and coached them to seek less reassurance. At follow-up 6 and 12 months after treatment, the CBT group had fewer hypochondriacal symptoms, beliefs and attitudes on the Whiteley Index (a health anxiety questionnaire; Assessment book), less health anxiety and better role functioning. Limits: the effects were modest, the bodily symptoms themselves did not improve significantly, and many eligible patients refused to enter the trial because they were sure their problem was medical, not psychological. (Barlow and Durand date this trial 2005; it was published in 2004.)

Other trials have extended the result. Allen and colleagues (2006) treated patients with the severe, multi-symptom form (what is now severe somatic symptom disorder): 40 percent were rated much or very much improved after CBT, against 5 percent (2 patients) with standard medical care augmented by psychiatric consultation, and the gains lasted at least a year. (Barlow and Durand give 7 percent; the trial report gives 5.) The techniques of cognitive therapy, behavioural experiments and the reduction of safety behaviours such as checking and reassurance seeking are taught in Book 2, Sections 5.4 and 5.6.

Drugs. Antidepressants are the drugs used, partly because depression and anxiety so often accompany these disorders. The best test is the trial of Greeven and colleagues (2007), who randomly assigned 112 patients with hypochondriasis to 16 weeks of CBT, the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor (SSRI) paroxetine, or placebo. Among all patients who entered the study, 45 percent responded to CBT, 30 percent to paroxetine and 14 percent to placebo; in this intention-to-treat analysis only CBT was significantly better than placebo, although among those who completed treatment both CBT and paroxetine were. For the wider somatoform group the evidence is weaker. A Cochrane review by Kleinstäuber and colleagues (2014) of 26 randomised trials with 2,159 participants found no clear advantage of tricyclic antidepressants over placebo, only very low-quality evidence that newer antidepressants reduce symptom severity, and high drop-out rates from side effects in the antidepressant groups, a real problem in patients who are sensitive to bodily sensations. In practice antidepressants are used for illness anxiety and for somatic symptom disorder with prominent pain, depression or anxiety, while repeated prescriptions of painkillers, sedatives and "tonics" for each new complaint are avoided, since they reinforce the sick role and some carry a risk of dependence.

Managing the medical side. Much of the cost of these disorders lies in repeated consultations and investigations, and Barlow and Durand describe the arrangement their clinic uses: one gatekeeper physician screens all physical complaints, and visits to specialists must be authorised by that doctor. In a good relationship most patients accept this. Treatment also tries to change how the person relates to others, reducing the attention that flows only from symptoms and building other ways of earning care; in Linda's case, because she was receiving disability payments, the goals included part-time work and, eventually, giving up disability status.

Clinical link

Guidance for the physician who looks after a patient like Yusuf follows directly from the vicious circle. Take the symptoms seriously and examine the patient properly; give a positive explanation of what is happening (nerves that have become sensitised, a body on high alert) rather than "nothing is wrong"; see the patient at regular scheduled appointments that do not depend on producing a new symptom; order investigations for objective signs, not to reassure; treat depression and anxiety; encourage graded return to activity and work; and keep a single coordinating doctor. The psychologist's referral is easier to accept when it is offered as help for the stress of living with symptoms, not as a sign that the doctor thinks the symptoms are imaginary.

Exam trap

The best-supported treatment for somatic symptom and illness anxiety disorders is CBT: in Greeven and colleagues' trial 45 percent responded to CBT, 30 percent to paroxetine and 14 percent to placebo. Explanatory therapy is Fava's. A gatekeeper physician and scheduled visits limit doctor shopping. Repeated reassurance and repeated tests are part of the problem, and no drug has strong evidence for somatic symptom disorder itself.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  5. Linda Meyer Williams (1994) found that percent of women with documented childhood sexual abuse did not recall it 17 years later.

Answers: (1) host, alters, (2) dissociative identity disorder, (3) playmates (or fantasy play), (4) sociocognitive, Bianchi, (5) 38, (6) possession trance, (7) Ganser, (8) somatisation, (9) 6, (10) care-avoidant, obsessive-compulsive and related.

7.8 Functional Neurological Symptom Disorder (Conversion Disorder)High yield · 10 Qs

Seema was 27, a primary-school teacher in Hubballi, when she woke one morning unable to move her right leg. The evening before there had been a bitter quarrel with her husband's family about whether she should give up her job after the birth of her first child. By the time her brother carried her into the casualty department of the district hospital, she could not lift the leg off the bed, could not press her foot down and could not stand. Yet her knee and ankle reflexes were normal, her big toe went down when the sole of her foot was stroked, as it should, and a scan of her brain and spine showed nothing at all. What struck the young duty doctor most was her manner. She lay calmly, smiling a little, and said, "It is not really bothering me, doctor. It will come back." Then the neurologist arrived. He cupped his hand under her right heel and asked her to push down into his palm; she could barely do it. Then he moved his other hand under her left heel and asked her to lift her good leg off the bed as hard as she could. As she strained to raise the left leg, the "paralysed" right heel drove down into his palm with full strength.

Seema's leg is the modern face of hysteria, the condition that filled Charcot's wards and Freud's consulting room (Section 7.1). Two puzzles sit inside her story, and this section is built around them. The first is how a doctor can say with confidence that a symptom is not caused by neurological disease, rather than simply failing to find one. The answer, as you will see, is the positive sign, and the test the neurologist used on Seema is the most famous of them. The second puzzle is how a real, disabling symptom can arise without damage to the nervous system and without being put on. That question has had more than a century of answers, from Freud's "conversion" of conflict into the body to the brain scans of today.

From conversion to "functional": what the disorder is called and where it sits

From DSM-III (1980) to DSM-IV-TR (2000) the condition was called conversion disorder and listed among the somatoform disorders. The word comes from psychoanalysis. As Section 7.1 explained, Freud proposed that anxiety arising from an unconscious conflict is "converted" into a physical symptom, which lets the person discharge the anxiety without having to feel it. Barlow and Durand point out that the word had been used off and on since the Middle Ages, but it was Freud who made it popular. The trouble with the name is that it carries a theory inside it. Calling a paralysis a "conversion" says that a buried conflict caused it, and in many patients no such conflict can be found. Patients, for their part, heard the label as "it is all in your mind", or worse, "you are putting it on", and many neurologists stopped using it.

In 2011 a group of psychiatrists, psychologists and neurologists led by the Edinburgh neurologist Jon Stone set out what was wrong with the DSM-IV criteria and proposed four changes for DSM-5: a theoretically neutral name that patients and doctors could both accept; moving the requirements for "association of psychological factors" and "exclusion of feigning" out of the criteria and into the accompanying text; a new criterion requiring clinical findings of internal inconsistency or incongruity with recognised disease; and a subtype for cognitive symptoms. DSM-5 (2013) adopted most of this. It kept the old name but added a subtitle, "conversion disorder (functional neurological symptom disorder)", because, as Barlow and Durand explain, "functional neurological symptom disorder" is the term neurologists, who see most of these patients, prefer, and the one patients find more acceptable. DSM-5-TR (2022) completed the change by reversing the order: the disorder is now functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder), and the old word survives only in brackets.

What does "functional" mean? It means a problem in how the nervous system is working rather than in its structure. Neurologists often explain it to patients with a computer analogy: the hardware (the brain, spinal cord, nerves and muscles) is intact, but the software that runs it has developed a fault, so a command to move the leg does not get through, or the signals from the eyes do not reach awareness. The analogy matters because it tells the patient two true things at once: the symptom is real, and the machinery is undamaged and can recover.

The ICD tradition has always placed the disorder somewhere else. ICD-10 (1992) kept the old unity of hysteria (Section 7.1) and listed the motor and sensory symptoms with amnesia and fugue under dissociative (conversion) disorders, F44: dissociative motor disorders (F44.4), dissociative convulsions (F44.5), dissociative anaesthesia and sensory loss (F44.6) and mixed dissociative (conversion) disorders (F44.7). For the whole F44 group ICD-10 also expected evidence of psychological causation: a convincing association in time between the onset of the symptoms and stressful events, problems or disturbed relationships. ICD-11 (in use from 2022) renamed the condition dissociative neurological symptom disorder (6B60) and kept it among the dissociative disorders. Its definition is worth reading slowly: motor, sensory or cognitive symptoms that imply an involuntary discontinuity in the normal integration of motor, sensory or cognitive functions, not explained by a disease of the nervous system, another mental disorder, a substance or medication, or a sleep-wake disorder. ICD-11 lists twelve subtypes: with visual disturbance, auditory disturbance, vertigo or dizziness, other sensory disturbance, non-epileptic seizures, speech disturbance, paresis or weakness, gait disturbance, movement disturbance, cognitive symptoms, other specified and unspecified symptoms. Factitious disorders are explicitly excluded.

Which placement is right? Both have a case. DSM puts the disorder in its somatic symptom chapter because these patients arrive in medical clinics with bodily complaints, like the other members of that family (Section 7.7). ICD puts it with the dissociative disorders because the core of the symptom is a loss of integration: a function that should be available to the will has split off from it, much as a block of memories splits off in dissociative amnesia (Section 7.5). DSM-5-TR itself notes that dissociative symptoms such as depersonalisation, derealisation and dissociative amnesia are common in these patients, especially at the onset of symptoms or during attacks, and that when both disorders are present both should be diagnosed. Table 7.21 lays out the history.

Table 7.21 One disorder, five sets of rules: functional neurological symptom disorder across the classifications.
SystemNameWhere it sitsWhat the rules demand
DSM-III (1980) to DSM-IV-TR (2000)Conversion disorderSomatoform disordersPsychological factors linked in time to onset; symptom not intentionally produced or feigned
DSM-5 (2013)Conversion disorder (functional neurological symptom disorder)Somatic symptom and related disordersClinical findings incompatible with recognised disease; stressor and feigning moved to the text
DSM-5-TR (2022)Functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder)Somatic symptom and related disordersAs DSM-5; stressor recorded only as a specifier
ICD-10 (1992)Dissociative motor disorders, convulsions, anaesthesia and sensory loss (F44.4 to F44.7)Dissociative (conversion) disorders, F44No physical disorder to explain it; association in time with stress
ICD-11 (2022)Dissociative neurological symptom disorder (6B60)Dissociative disordersInvoluntary loss of integration of motor, sensory or cognitive function; adds cognitive symptoms
Exam trap

ICD-11's dissociative neurological symptom disorder is the old conversion disorder, kept among the dissociative disorders. Its core is neurological symptoms incompatible with known disease and not intentionally produced. The distractors are all outsiders: "intentional production of symptoms for external gain" is malingering (Section 7.9); "persecutory delusion with intact reality testing" is a contradiction in terms, since a delusion is by definition a failure of reality testing (Chapters 2 and 4); and "persistent memory deficit from organic brain injury" is an amnestic neurocognitive disorder (Chapter 10). Note also the direction of the DSM renaming: DSM-5 put "functional neurological symptom disorder" in brackets; DSM-5-TR moved it to the front.

The diagnostic criteria and the reasons behind them

DSM-5-TR asks for four things, and each has a reason.

  • Criterion A: one or more symptoms of altered voluntary motor or sensory function. "Voluntary" refers to the kind of function affected, not to the symptom. These are the functions normally under the control of the will or available to awareness: moving a limb, walking, speaking, swallowing, seeing, hearing, feeling touch. A racing heart, a churning stomach or chronic pain do not count here; persistent bodily complaints of that kind are the territory of somatic symptom disorder (Section 7.7). The word "voluntary" does not mean the patient is producing the symptom on purpose, which is the opposite of what the disorder describes.
  • Criterion B: clinical findings provide evidence of incompatibility between the symptom and recognised neurological or medical conditions. This is the heart of the modern diagnosis. It is not enough that the tests are normal; the clinician must find something positive that shows the symptom cannot be due to a known disease, such as the strong downward push of Seema's "paralysed" leg. DSM-5-TR adds that the findings should usually be elicited and interpreted by a professional with expertise in neurological diagnosis, and that the diagnosis should not be made simply because investigation results are normal or because a symptom seems "bizarre".
  • Criterion C: the symptom is not better explained by another medical or mental disorder. The heaviness of the limbs in depression, the tingling of a panic attack, a neurological disease that really does explain the picture: all of these must be considered first.
  • Criterion D: the symptom causes clinically significant distress or impairment, or warrants medical evaluation. The last phrase is there for a good reason. Some patients, like Seema, appear unconcerned, and a sudden blindness or paralysis needs a doctor whatever the patient's mood.

Three sets of specifiers follow. The first names the symptom type: with weakness or paralysis; with abnormal movement (tremor, dystonia, myoclonus or gait disorder); with swallowing symptoms; with speech symptoms (dysphonia, slurred speech); with attacks or seizures; with anaesthesia or sensory loss; with special sensory symptoms (visual, olfactory or hearing disturbance); or with mixed symptoms. The second gives the duration: an acute episode means symptoms present for less than 6 months, and persistent means 6 months or more. Notice what this implies: there is no minimum duration. Seema could be diagnosed on the day she arrived, whereas somatic symptom disorder typically requires symptoms that persist for more than 6 months. The third specifier records whether the presentation came with or without a psychological stressor.

Two things that the older criteria demanded have gone, and both removals are examined. DSM-IV required that psychological factors be judged to be associated with the symptom because its onset or worsening followed a conflict or stressor. DSM-5 dropped this requirement because, as Barlow and Durand explain, a stressor is not a reliable sign: many disorders follow stressful events, and stressful events happen to people with no disorder at all. Stress may in fact be absent in up to half of patients, and DSM-5-TR says plainly that the diagnosis should not be withheld if none is found. The stressor that is present is often physical rather than emotional: in one large survey, 324 of 869 patients (37 percent) reported a physical injury before their functional symptoms began. DSM-IV also required that the symptom not be intentionally produced or feigned. Following Stone's proposal, DSM-5 moved this judgement out of the criteria and into the text, because no clinician can see directly into another person's intentions and the absence of feigning cannot be proved. The text still describes the symptoms as genuinely experienced and not intentionally produced, and it says that definite evidence of feigning points instead to factitious disorder or malingering (Section 7.9).

The result is what neurologists call a "rule-in" diagnosis: DSM-5-TR states plainly that the diagnosis is not one of exclusion. It rests on what the clinician finds, not on what the tests fail to find, and so it can be made in someone who also has a recognised neurological disease. A person with multiple sclerosis can develop a functional weakness in a limb that the disease has never touched, and a person with epilepsy can also have functional seizures; DSM-5-TR estimates that about one in five people with functional seizures also have epilepsy.

Exam trap

Asked for the clinical features of conversion disorder, choose the option that says the symptoms affect motor or sensory function, begin suddenly and develop after a significant psychosocial stressor. That is the classic textbook picture, and it is the keyed answer, but remember that DSM-5 no longer requires the stressor. The near-miss "suggest a medical or neurological disorder" fits every somatic disorder and says nothing about motor or sensory function. "Physical examination and investigations reveal no abnormalities" is not enough on its own: normal tests never make the diagnosis. An option with "intentional symptom production" describes factitious disorder or malingering. Asked what the disorder requires, choose one or more neurological symptoms incompatible with recognised neurological or medical conditions, not intentional production, concurrent substance use or onset before age 5. Some coaching notes give it a duration of "6 months or more": that is only the persistent specifier.

Positive signs: the logic of inconsistency

How, then, does a clinician show that a symptom is incompatible with disease? DSM-5-TR names the principle: internal inconsistency. A physical sign that is present when the body is examined one way disappears when it is examined another way, usually when the person's attention is drawn elsewhere. Real disease does not behave like that. A leg weakened by a stroke is weak whichever way you test it, because the damaged motor pathway carries every command, attended or not. A functionally weak leg is weak when the person tries to move it, and strong when the movement happens automatically. There are dozens of such positive signs, and the oldest and best known is the one that unmasked Seema's leg.

Charles Franklin Hoover (1865 to 1927), a physician in Cleveland, Ohio, described it in 1908 in the Journal of the American Medical Association, in a short paper based on four patients. Its title tells you a great deal about the medicine of the time: "A new sign for the detection of malingering and functional paresis of the lower extremities". Hoover treated faking and functional weakness as near neighbours, and a century of neurology has worked to separate them. His sign rests on an ordinary piece of body mechanics. Lie on your back and lift one straight leg: the heel of the other leg presses down into the bed without your thinking about it, to anchor the pelvis. In Hoover's sign, as DSM-5-TR describes it, weakness of hip extension (pushing the heel down) returns to normal strength when the person flexes the opposite hip against resistance. Figure 7.13 shows the two halves of the test.

How good is the sign? In 2011 Laura McWhirter, Jon Stone and their colleagues tested Hoover's sign in 337 consecutive patients brought to hospital with a suspected stroke. Of the 124 who had leg weakness, 8 turned out to have a functional disorder. The sign was positive in 63 percent of the functional patients (its sensitivity) and in none of the patients with stroke or other disease (a specificity of 100 percent). With only eight functional patients the sensitivity is an imprecise estimate, but the pattern is the one found for positive signs in general. A systematic review by Daum, Hubschmid and Aybek in 2014 found only 14 signs (7 motor, 5 sensory and 2 of gait) that had been tested in controlled studies; their sensitivity was low and variable (8 to 100 percent) but their specificity was high (92 to 100 percent). In plain words, when a good positive sign is present it is strong evidence of a functional disorder, but its absence does not rule the disorder out.

The other motor signs that DSM-5-TR lists work on the same principle. In the hip abductor sign, described by the Japanese neurologist Sonoo in 2004, weakness of moving the weak thigh outwards (abduction) returns to normal when the person abducts the opposite thigh against resistance. A second kind of inconsistency is a discrepancy between tasks: a person who cannot point the foot downwards against the examiner's hand on the bed (weak ankle plantar flexion) may nevertheless be able to walk on tiptoe, which needs exactly that movement. For a shaking limb there is the tremor entrainment test. The examiner asks the person to copy a rhythmical tapping movement with the unaffected hand or foot. A tremor caused by disease, such as the tremor of Parkinson's disease, keeps its own frequency. A functional tremor typically "entrains" to the rhythm of the tapping hand, or stops, or the person finds that they cannot copy a simple rhythm at all. Functional tremor also tends to vary in its frequency and direction. An abnormal sustained posture, or functional dystonia, has a characteristic look: a fixed inward-turned ankle, a clenched fist or a one-sided contraction of the platysma muscle of the neck, often appearing suddenly.

For vision, DSM-5-TR gives two signs. The first is a tubular visual field, or tunnel vision of a particular kind. Real visual fields are cone-shaped: an object at the edge of the field at one metre is inside a much wider circle at two metres, so the measured field gets bigger as the test screen is moved further away. In a functional field the width stays the same at every distance, like looking down a tube, which no damage to the visual system can produce. The second is the fogging test. A person who reports poor vision in one eye reads an eye chart with both eyes open while the examiner subtly blurs ("fogs") the good eye with a lens; whatever the person reads must then be read with the "bad" eye. For speech, the sign is internal inconsistency in articulation and voice. A person with functional aphonia, who can speak only in a whisper, can usually cough normally, and a normal cough needs the vocal cords to close.

Sensory signs are weaker, and the classic one deserves care. In glove anaesthesia the person reports loss of feeling over the whole hand, ending at a sharp line around the wrist like the cuff of a glove. No single nerve produces this: the median, ulnar and radial nerves each supply part of the hand and carry on up the forearm, so a nerve lesion leaves a patch that follows the nerve's territory, not a neat cuff. ICD-10 makes the general point that the boundaries of dissociative sensory loss tend to follow the patient's ideas about the body rather than anatomy. But two cautions apply. First, a symmetrical "glove and stocking" loss of feeling in both hands and feet, fading gradually towards the fingers and toes, is the ordinary sign of a polyneuropathy such as diabetic neuropathy, and it is organic. Second, the "non-anatomical" sensory signs have done badly when tested. In 1986 Gould and his colleagues looked for seven accepted "positive" features of hysteria, including la belle indifférence, secondary gain, non-anatomical sensory loss and the splitting of sensation exactly at the midline, in 30 consecutive neurology admissions with acute structural damage to the nervous system, such as strokes. Every patient showed at least one of the features, and most showed three or four. Two years later Rolak (1988) tested 100 patients with numbness of one side of the face: sensory loss splitting exactly at the midline occurred in 20 percent of the 20 patients with psychogenic complaints but also in 7.5 percent of the 80 with structural lesions, and vibration sense felt weaker on the numb side of the forehead in 95 percent and 86 percent respectively. Neither sign told the groups apart. This is why DSM-5-TR insists that the diagnosis rest on the overall clinical picture and not on a single finding. Table 7.22 gathers the main symptom types and their signs.

Table 7.22 The symptom types of functional neurological symptom disorder and the positive signs that show incompatibility with disease.
Symptom typeWhat the person experiencesPositive sign (what reveals the inconsistency)
Weakness or paralysisA limb that will not move or gives wayHoover's sign; hip abductor sign; weak on the bed but able to walk on tiptoe
Abnormal movementTremor, jerks, fixed postures, an odd gaitTremor entrains to a copied rhythm or stops; varying frequency; fixed inverted ankle or clenched fist of sudden onset
Attacks or seizuresEpisodes of unresponsiveness with or without shakingEyes closed and resisting opening; bilateral shaking with awareness kept; over 5 minutes; normal EEG during the attack
Speech symptomsWhispering voice, loss of voice, slurred or stuttering speechInconsistent articulation and voice; normal cough in aphonia
Swallowing symptomsA lump in the throat (globus)Normal examination and swallowing; no weight loss
Visual symptomsBlindness, blurring, tunnel vision, double visionTubular field; fogging test; eyes follow a moving striped drum or mirror
Sensory lossNumbness of a hand, a limb or half the bodyLoss ending at a sharp line that fits no nerve territory (a weak sign on its own)
Clinical link

The positive signs are not only diagnostic tools; they are the first step of treatment. When the neurologist showed Seema that her right leg pushed hard while she lifted the left, he was showing her that the wiring was intact and that the leg could work, which is the most hopeful thing a person with a functional paralysis can hear. Explaining the diagnosis this way, as something seen rather than something left over when the tests were normal, makes it far easier for the patient to accept, and acceptance predicts recovery (see the course of the disorder, later in this section).

Exam trap

Hoover's sign (Hoover, 1908) shows that leg weakness is functional; despite the title of his paper, it does not show that the person is faking. Do not confuse it with the Babinski sign, an upgoing big toe when the sole is stroked, which points to organic damage of the motor pathway. Glove anaesthesia ending at a sharp line at the wrist suggests a functional disorder; symmetrical glove-and-stocking loss in all four limbs is the organic pattern of polyneuropathy. Midline splitting of sensation and la belle indifférence are poor signs, found in patients with strokes as well.

Weakness, abnormal movement, speech and swallowing

Weakness is the commonest motor presentation. It usually affects one side or one limb, comes on suddenly or after a minor injury, and is often accompanied by heaviness and a feeling that the limb "does not belong". Seema's paralysis is the textbook case, and the textbook case is not rare: in many patients the onset follows a quarrel, an accident or a frightening medical event by hours or days. A gradual onset is also possible, and Barlow and Durand's best-known case shows how slowly a functional disability can grow.

Eloise was 20 when her mother and a friend all but carried her into the clinic. She sat on a chair with her legs tucked under her, refusing to put her feet on the floor, while her mother sat close by, ready to help her move. She was of borderline intelligence, friendly and personable, and answered every question with a big smile; she obviously enjoyed the company. Her difficulty had developed over five years. She had been schooled through special education programmes until about 15, when no further programme was available, and once she began staying at home her walking began to deteriorate. Her right leg gave way and she began falling, and by six months before her admission she could move around only by crawling on the floor. Physical examination found no physical problem. She was not paralysed: her symptoms were weakness in the legs and poor balance, so that she fell often. This picture has a name, astasia-abasia, literally "without standing, without stepping": an inability to stand or walk normally in a person whose legs may work well when tested lying down. She lived with her mother, who ran a gift shop in the front of their house in a small rural town. You will see later in this section why that last fact mattered so much.

Abnormal movements are the second large group. Functional tremor, jerks (functional myoclonus), fixed postures (functional dystonia) and odd gaits make up a large share of the patients seen in movement-disorder clinics, and they show the positive signs described above: they change with distraction, entrain to other rhythms and vary from moment to moment in a way that the tremor of Parkinson's disease or the jerks of epilepsy do not. A functional gait may be slow and effortful, dragging one leg like a weight, or swaying and lurching; it tends to look worse when the person is watched and better when the person is distracted.

Speech and swallowing symptoms complete the motor family. A hoarse or strained voice without disease of the larynx is functional dysphonia, and functional aphonia is its extreme, speech only in a whisper; total mutism, the loss of all speech, was common among soldiers in the First World War. Speech may also become slurred, halting, stuttering or oddly accented without any lesion in the speech areas. The swallowing symptom is globus, a sensation of a lump in the throat; Barlow and Durand call it by its old name, globus hystericus, and note that it can make it difficult to swallow, eat or sometimes talk. Examination of the throat and swallowing studies are normal. Because real disease of the throat and oesophagus can also cause a sensation of obstruction, pain on swallowing, food sticking or weight loss are warning signs that call for investigation rather than reassurance.

Functional seizures

Nazia was 19, a second-year BA student living with her parents, grandparents and her uncle's family in a large joint household in Hyderabad, when the "fits" began. A few weeks after her engagement was fixed, she collapsed at a cousin's wedding, eyes screwed shut, arms and legs thrashing from side to side, her head rolling left and right. The episode went on for nearly twenty minutes. Afterwards she wept, and later she could describe some of what had been said around her. Over the next year the attacks came several times a week, often after arguments at home. Two doctors diagnosed epilepsy and added a second and then a third anticonvulsant, with no effect except drowsiness, and the family also took her to a dargah for prayers. Finally she was admitted to a tertiary hospital for video-electroencephalography (video-EEG): continuous video recording of her behaviour together with the electrical activity of her brain. On the third day she had a typical attack. The video showed the side-to-side thrashing and the closed eyes; the EEG during the attack showed the normal rhythm of a brain that was awake.

Nazia has functional seizures, episodes that resemble epileptic seizures, fainting or coma but are not caused by abnormal electrical discharges in the brain. They have many names, and the names carry history. The term most often used in research is psychogenic non-epileptic seizures (PNES); dissociative seizures, non-epileptic attack disorder and functional seizures are the others in current use. ICD-10 calls them dissociative convulsions and ICD-11 dissociative neurological symptom disorder with non-epileptic seizures. The older word "pseudoseizures" has been abandoned because "pseudo" suggests something fake, and these attacks are not under the person's control.

Functional seizures are one of the main reasons people are wrongly treated for epilepsy. Barlow and Durand put the figure at about 30 percent of patients referred to epilepsy centres. The paper they cite, by Selim Benbadis and W. Allen Hauser (2000), in fact worked from a more conservative 10 to 20 percent of referrals to epilepsy centres, and from it estimated that between 2 and 33 people in every 100,000 in the general population have the condition, which makes it, in their words, a significant neurological condition. DSM-5-TR gives the mean age of onset of non-epileptic attacks as 20 to 29 years, younger than the mean onset of motor symptoms.

How are the two kinds of attack told apart? Asking someone who has seen an attack, or watching a video taken on a relative's phone, is often the first step, and DSM-5-TR lists the features that suggest a functional seizure: persistent closure of the eyes, sometimes with resistance to opening; bilateral shaking movements of the limbs while awareness is preserved, which is unusual in epilepsy, because seizure activity that spreads across both hemispheres normally abolishes awareness (seizures arising in the supplementary motor area of the frontal lobe are a rare exception); and a duration of more than 5 minutes. In 2010 Andreja Avbersek and Sanjay Sisodiya reviewed 34 video-EEG studies and found good evidence for a longer list. Table 7.23 summarises it. No single feature is decisive; the clinical features usually need to be combined, and the strongest support comes from a normal EEG recorded during a typical attack. Even that, DSM-5-TR notes, does not exclude every form of epilepsy or fainting, because some seizures arising deep in the frontal lobes barely disturb the scalp EEG.

Table 7.23 Features that help to separate functional (psychogenic non-epileptic) seizures from epileptic seizures. Each is a tendency, not a rule; the diagnosis rests on the whole picture and, ideally, video-EEG.
FeatureFavours a functional seizureFavours an epileptic seizure
Eyes during the attackClosed, often resisting openingUsually open
Duration and courseLong (over 5 minutes); waxes and wanesBrief (usually a minute or two); stereotyped
MovementsOut of step between limbs; side-to-side head or body movement; pelvic thrustingRhythmic, in step; stiffening then jerking
AwarenessMay be kept despite shaking of both sides; attack may be recalledLost in a generalised seizure; no memory of it
Onset from sleepFrom apparent sleep, with an awake EEGFrom true sleep
After the attackWeeping; quick recoveryConfusion, drowsiness, noisy (stertorous) breathing
EEG during the attackNormal waking rhythmEpileptic discharges in most seizure types

Two features that people expect to help do not reliably do so: injury and wetting oneself occur in both kinds of attack. (ICD-10 describes tongue biting, serious bruising from falling and incontinence of urine as rare in dissociative convulsions, but rare is not never.) And the distinction matters in both directions. A person with functional seizures who is labelled epileptic may spend years on drugs that cannot help, while the stresses behind the attacks go unaddressed. A person with genuine epilepsy whose attacks look unusual may be wrongly told the seizures are psychological. That is why video-EEG is the standard of diagnosis wherever it is available.

Exam trap

Psychogenic non-epileptic seizures are a form of functional neurological symptom disorder ("with attacks or seizures"; ICD-10 dissociative convulsions, F44.5), not faking: the old term "pseudoseizure" is discouraged for that reason. They can coexist with epilepsy (about one person in five with functional seizures also has epilepsy, DSM-5-TR). Separate them from panic attacks, which also bring tremor and tingling: in panic, the neurological symptoms come with a pounding heart and breathlessness, and awareness is kept; loss of awareness with amnesia for the attack occurs in functional seizures but not in panic attacks (Chapter 6).

Functional blindness and the triangle test

The term functional blindness means loss of vision explained by psychological factors, without damage to the eyes, the optic nerves, the visual pathways or the visual cortex. The person may be completely unable to see, or see only a blur, or report a narrowed tunnel of vision. Barlow and Durand note a curious thing about such patients: they can usually avoid objects in their path, yet tell you they cannot see them. In the same way, a person with a functional paralysis of the legs may suddenly get up and run in an emergency, and then be astounded at having done so.

The examination shows the visual system working. The pupils constrict normally to light. When a drum painted with vertical stripes is rotated in front of the eyes, the eyes make the involuntary flicking movements called optokinetic nystagmus, which cannot occur unless the stripes are seen; a large mirror tilted slowly in front of the face similarly draws the eyes after the person's own reflection. The tubular field and the fogging test described earlier, among the positive signs, apply here too. In difficult cases, the electrical response of the visual cortex to a flashing pattern (the visual evoked potential) is normal.

It helps to set functional blindness beside three organic kinds of visual loss with which it can be confused, because each fails the examination in its own way. In optic atrophy the optic nerve has been damaged (by glaucoma, pressure from a tumour, inflammation or poor blood supply), the optic disc at the back of the eye looks pale, the pupil responds abnormally to light shone in the affected eye, and vision is lost progressively. A colour vision deficiency is usually an inherited defect of the cone pigments of the retina, far commoner in men because the commonest forms are carried on the X chromosome; acuity is normal, and only certain colours are confused. In cortical blindness, damage to both occipital lobes (usually from strokes or lack of oxygen) destroys the visual cortex. Here the front end of the system is intact, so the pupils react normally to light and the eyes look normal, which means the pupil reflex alone cannot tell cortical blindness from functional blindness. But optokinetic nystagmus is lost, the evoked potentials are abnormal, and a scan shows the damage. Some people with cortical blindness even deny that they are blind, a striking organic symptom called Anton syndrome.

The most elegant demonstration of what is happening in functional blindness comes from psychology rather than neurology, and it is told in the box below.

Key study: Celia and the triangles (Grosz and Zimmerman, 1970; Sackeim, Nordlie and Gur, 1979)

Celia, a 15-year-old girl, suddenly became unable to see. Some sight returned, but her vision stayed so blurred that she could not read. The psychologists who tested her used tasks that did not depend on her report of what she saw. In one, three triangles appeared on three screens and she had to press the button under the screen showing the upright triangle. A truly blind person would be right by chance, one time in three. Celia was right on every trial, while insisting that she could see nothing. If she had been faking, she would surely have made deliberate mistakes. In 1979 Harold Sackeim, James Nordlie and Ruben Gur hypnotised two volunteers and gave each a suggestion of total blindness; one was also told it was extremely important to appear blind to everyone. On a similar visual discrimination task, the second volunteer performed perfectly while reporting that she saw nothing, like Celia. The first, trying to appear blind at all costs, chose the wrong answer on almost every trial, far below chance. Grosz and Zimmerman had already met that pattern in 1965, in a man with apparent blindness who performed much worse than chance; other evidence confirmed that he was almost certainly malingering.

Figure 7.14 shows the logic. The truly blind person scores at chance, because no visual information gets in.
