The person with functional blindness scores well above chance, because the visual information is getting in and guiding behaviour, but it is cut off from awareness. The person who is feigning scores below chance, because to score that low you must see the correct answer and avoid it. The pattern is reminiscent of blindsight, studied by Lawrence Weiskrantz in people with damage to part of the visual cortex, who can point to objects in the blind part of their field while insisting they see nothing. Functional blindness looks like blindsight without the damage: vision that works but does not reach consciousness. This is exactly the "discontinuity in the normal integration" of function that ICD-11 places at the centre of its definition, and it is why the ICD tradition counts these symptoms as dissociative. You will meet the below-chance logic again in Section 7.9, where it becomes a tool for detecting malingering.

Exam trap

Functional blindness is blindness explained by psychological factors without organic damage. The distractors are all organic: progressive loss from optic nerve atrophy (pale disc, abnormal pupil response), colour vision deficiency (an inherited cone defect with normal acuity) and vision loss from a cortical lesion (cortical blindness: pupils react normally, but optokinetic nystagmus is lost and the scan is abnormal). On forced-choice tests, functional blindness scores above chance and feigned blindness below chance.

La belle indifférence and the question of gain

Seema's calm remark, "It is not really bothering me", is the most famous sign in the history of this disorder: la belle indifférence, "the beautiful indifference", a relative lack of concern about the nature or implications of a striking symptom. Section 7.1 tells who gave it its name. For most of the twentieth century it was taught as a hallmark of conversion. Freud's theory explained it neatly. If the symptom is the solution to an unbearable conflict, it relieves anxiety (the primary gain, Section 7.1), and so the patient has no reason to be upset by it.

The trouble is that when the sign was put to the test, it failed. In 2006 Jon Stone and his colleagues systematically reviewed every study since 1965 that had reported the frequency of la belle indifférence. Eleven studies qualified. The median frequency was 21 percent (range 0 to 54 percent) in 356 patients with conversion symptoms, and 29 percent (range 0 to 60 percent) in 157 patients with organic disease. If anything, the patients with real disease looked more indifferent. The studies were of poor quality, with no agreed definition of the sign and no blind ratings, and the authors concluded that la belle indifférence should be abandoned as a clinical sign until its definition and usefulness had been clarified. An older study had already pointed the same way. Malcolm Lader and Norman Sartorius (1968) compared patients with conversion disorder with anxious patients who had no conversion symptoms and found that the conversion patients showed equal or greater anxiety and physiological arousal. Whatever the calm surface shows, the body underneath is not calm.

Why, then, do some patients seem unconcerned? A person may be stoical, or putting on a brave face for the family, or relieved that the scan has shown no tumour. A person with depression may show little emotion about anything. And organic brain disease can produce real indifference: after a stroke in the right hemisphere, some people are strikingly unconcerned about a paralysed left arm, a phenomenon neurologists call anosodiaphoria, which may be one reason why Gould found the sign in patients with strokes. DSM-5-TR therefore states that la belle indifférence has been associated with the disorder but is not specific and should not be used to make the diagnosis.

The same verdict falls on secondary gain, the external benefits of being ill, such as attention, sympathy, money or release from responsibilities (Section 7.1). DSM-5-TR says that secondary gain is not specific to functional neurological symptom disorder either. Every illness brings some benefits (time off work, care from relatives, a pause in family demands), and Gould's stroke patients showed secondary gain as often as they showed the other "hysterical" features. Secondary gain can keep a symptom going, as you will see in Eloise's story, but finding it does not make the diagnosis. Section 7.9 explains how gain, deliberate production and motive together separate this disorder from factitious disorder and malingering.

Key study: Testing the hallmark (Stone, Smyth, Carson, Warlow and Sharpe, 2006)

The question was simple: does la belle indifférence actually distinguish conversion symptoms from organic disease? The Edinburgh group searched for every study since 1965 that had counted it, found 11, and compared the rates. The sign appeared in a median of 21 percent of 356 patients with conversion symptoms and 29 percent of 157 patients with organic disease. The conclusion: the evidence does not support using it to discriminate between the two, and it should be set aside until it is properly defined. The weakness of the study is the weakness of the literature it summarised: small, unblinded studies with no operational definition. That weakness is itself the point. A sign taught as a hallmark for a hundred years had never been properly tested.

Exam trap

When a question asks what la belle indifférence is classically associated with, the answer is conversion disorder (functional neurological symptom disorder), even though DSM-5-TR says it is not specific and must not be used to make the diagnosis. The distractors fail for a reason you can state. Panic disorder, specific phobia, generalised anxiety disorder and obsessive-compulsive disorder are defined by fear, worry or distress, the opposite of indifference (Chapter 6). Somatic symptom disorder and illness anxiety disorder are defined by excessive concern about symptoms or illness (Section 7.7). In mania or bipolar disorder the person is elated and may lack insight into being ill at all, which is not calm about one disabling symptom (Chapter 5). Major depressive disorder brings hopelessness and distress, and its bodily complaints are worried over. Borderline personality disorder is marked by intense, unstable emotion (Chapter 11). In factitious disorder and malingering the person, if anything, dramatises the illness to convince others (Section 7.9).

Getting it wrong: the fear of missing disease

Every clinician who makes this diagnosis carries a fear: what if the "functional" weakness is the first sign of multiple sclerosis, or the "functional" seizures are a rare epilepsy, or the "hysterical" headache a brain tumour? For a long time that fear seemed justified, largely because of one paper. In 1965 the British psychiatrist Eliot Slater (1904 to 1983), with E. Glithero, traced patients who had been diagnosed with hysteria at the National Hospital, Queen Square, in London in 1951, 1953 and 1955. They obtained follow-up information on 85 of them (32 men and 53 women), on average about nine years after the diagnosis. Twelve had died, four of them by suicide, and in several patients, among the dead and the living, a physical disease (vascular disease, tumours, a muscle disease) had been missed. Slater's conclusion, in a lecture published in the British Medical Journal the same year, became one of the most quoted sentences in psychiatry: "The diagnosis of 'hysteria' is a disguise for ignorance and a fertile source of clinical error. It is in fact not only a delusion but also a snare."

Later studies were far less gloomy. In 1998 Crimlisk and colleagues went back to the same hospital, now the National Hospital for Neurology and Neurosurgery, and followed 73 patients admitted in 1989 to 1991 with medically unexplained motor symptoms for about six years. Of the 64 traced, only three had developed a neurological disease that fully or partly explained their earlier symptoms. In 2005 Stone and his colleagues brought the whole literature together: 27 studies, 1,466 patients, a median follow-up of five years. The average rate of misdiagnosis fell from 29 percent in studies of the 1950s, to 17 percent in the 1960s, to about 4 percent in every decade from the 1970s onwards. Interestingly, the fall had happened before brain scanning became widespread, and the authors attributed it mainly to better-designed studies rather than to new technology. A Dutch study gives a higher figure for one group: Moene and colleagues (2000) carefully reassessed 85 patients with a diagnosis of conversion disorder and found that 10 (11.8 percent) had developed some evidence of a neurological disorder about two and a half years later.

So the error rate is real but low, about the same as for many other diagnoses in neurology and psychiatry, and DSM-5-TR sums up the modern position: after a thorough neurological assessment, an unexpected neurological cause is rarely found at follow-up, but the patient should be reassessed if the symptoms seem to be progressing, because functional symptoms can coexist with neurological disease and can even appear early in some progressive diseases. Gould's 1986 study adds the uncomfortable other half of the picture. Looking back at patients wrongly labelled as hysterical, his group found that women, people with psychiatric illness and patients who offered a plausible psychological explanation for their symptoms were the most likely to be misdiagnosed, and that movement disorders and paralysis were the symptoms most often mislabelled. The safeguard is the one built into Criterion B: the diagnosis is made on positive signs, by someone expert in neurological examination, and never because the tests are normal or the patient seems "the type".

Exam trap

Slater (1965) called hysteria "a disguise for ignorance and a fertile source of clinical error"; modern reviews (Stone and colleagues, 2005) put the misdiagnosis rate at about 4 percent since the 1970s. The lesson for a question that offers "physical examination and investigations reveal no abnormalities" as the defining feature of conversion disorder: normal results are necessary but never sufficient. The diagnosis needs positive evidence of incompatibility.

Who develops it, in India and elsewhere, and what happens to them

Brief functional symptoms are common: most people have had a moment when a leg "went", or a voice vanished under stress. The disorder itself is less common, and because patients go to neurologists rather than psychiatrists, much of what we know comes from neurology clinics. DSM-5-TR estimates the incidence of persistent functional neurological symptoms at 4 to 12 new cases per 100,000 people a year, based on research in the United States and northern Europe, and reports that around 5 to 15 percent of patients in neurology clinics in Scotland and Australia have the diagnosis. In Stone's 2010 study of 3,781 new referrals to neurology clinics in Scotland, functional and psychological symptoms (16 percent) were the second commonest category of diagnosis after headache (19 percent), ahead of epilepsy (14 percent). Barlow and Durand give a higher figure, about 30 percent in neurological settings. The difference lies in what is counted. The Scottish study behind that figure (Stone and colleagues, 2009) found that 30 percent of new neurology outpatients had symptoms that the neurologist rated as "not at all" or only "somewhat" explained by disease, and that group was a mixture: people with a real neurological disease that did not explain their symptoms (26 percent of the group), headache disorders (26 percent) and many others, with conversion symptoms (weakness, sensory loss or non-epileptic attacks) making up only 18 percent.

The disorder is two to three times more common in women for most symptom types. One large clinical study cited by DSM-5-TR found more cognitive symptoms and weakness in men, and more past sexual and physical trauma in women. Onset can occur at any age; as the discussion of functional seizures noted, non-epileptic attacks begin most often in the twenties and motor symptoms in the thirties. Personality traits, especially emotional instability, are common, as are histories of childhood abuse and neglect and of stressful life events, including physical injury. Having a neurological disease that causes similar symptoms is itself a risk factor: the person with epilepsy has a ready model for functional seizures. Like somatic symptom disorder, the disorder is commoner among less educated and poorer people, whose knowledge of how disease works may be limited. Binzer and colleagues (1997) found that only 13 percent of 30 adults with conversion motor disabilities had attended high school, compared with 67 percent of patients whose motor symptoms had a physical cause. The disorder seems to have become rarer over the twentieth century, which Barlow and Durand attribute to growing public knowledge about real illness, and it is not uncommon among soldiers exposed to combat, as the shell shock of the First World War showed (Section 7.1).

Culture shapes the form of the symptoms and the meaning given to them. DSM-5-TR notes that episodes of unresponsiveness (including seizures) and motor symptoms are the commonest functional symptoms across cultures, and that changes resembling functional and dissociative symptoms are part of many culturally sanctioned rituals. Barlow and Durand describe seizures, paralysis and trances in some rural fundamentalist religious groups in the United States, where they are taken as signs of contact with God and earn the person respect. When such symptoms are fully explained by the cultural context and cause no significant distress or disability, the diagnosis is not made.

Indian context

Functional and dissociative presentations remain common in Indian clinics. At the Child and Adolescent Psychiatry Unit of NIMHANS, Bengaluru, Shoba Srinath and colleagues (1993) found that hysteria was diagnosed in 30.8 percent (44 of 143) of inpatients and 14.8 percent (95 of 640) of outpatients seen in one year; most inpatients were past puberty, boys and girls were affected about equally, and "pseudo-seizures" were the commonest presentation. The authors suggested that in Indian culture a "medical" illness may be one of the more acceptable ways of seeking psychiatric help. At JIPMER, Puducherry, R. Chandrasekaran and colleagues (1994) followed 38 women with hysterical neurosis for five years and found 63 percent completely free of symptoms. And P. J. Alexander, S. Joseph and A. Das (1997) found that half of the inpatients with dissociative (conversion) disorders in their general hospital unit had brief episodes of unresponsiveness that fitted no ICD-10 or DSM-IV subcategory; they called it "brief dissociative stupor" and argued for a category of dissociative non-epileptic seizures. Clinicians in India often see the "fits" of a young woman like Nazia against a background of family conflict, an arranged marriage, or a new bride's first months in a joint household, and families commonly consult faith healers before, or alongside, doctors.

Sometimes functional symptoms spread through a group. In mass psychogenic illness (the older term is mass hysteria), symptoms with no physical cause, such as fainting, dizziness, headache, shaking, laughing, crying or abnormal movements, spread rapidly through a close-knit group, most often of girls and young women in schools, convents or factories, passing by sight and sound and usually clearing quickly. In 1962, in what was then Tanganyika (now Tanzania), uncontrollable laughing and crying spread from a girls' boarding school to other schools and villages, and schools had to be closed. In 1965, 85 girls from a school in Blackburn, England, were taken to hospital after an outbreak of fainting. In 2011 a group of high-school girls in Le Roy, New York, developed sudden tics and jerking movements, which were eventually diagnosed as conversion disorder spreading through the group. In July 2022 news reports from a government school in Bageshwar district, Uttarakhand, showed schoolgirls screaming, crying and banging their heads; a local priest was called, which tells you how the episode was understood. Publicity tends to make outbreaks worse. The dancing manias of medieval Europe are taught in Book 2, Section 1.2.4.

What is the outlook? Symptoms may be brief or may persist for years. In a study by Alan Ettinger and colleagues (1999), described by Barlow and Durand, 56 patients with psychogenic non-epileptic seizures (16 men and 40 women), who had had the disorder for an average of eight years, were followed for 18 months after diagnosis. Only about half recovered, rehospitalisation was common even among those who improved, and about 20 percent had attempted suicide, a proportion that was the same whether or not the seizures had improved. Patients who accepted the diagnosis when it was given, and who saw themselves as otherwise healthy and functioning well, were more likely to recover. Children do better: in a Turkish study (Pehlivanturk and Unal, 2002), 85 percent of 40 children had recovered four years after diagnosis, those diagnosed early doing best. DSM-5-TR lists the prognostic factors. Short duration of symptoms and agreement with the diagnosis predict a good outcome; maladaptive personality traits, physical disease and receiving disability benefits predict a poor one. Cohort studies mostly show raised rates of suicidal thoughts and attempts, and in neurology clinics people with functional symptoms report suicidal thoughts more often than people with recognised neurological disease; the disability can be as severe as that of comparable organic conditions.

The disorder rarely comes alone. Anxiety disorders, especially panic disorder, and depression commonly accompany it, and personality disorders are more common than in the general population. Dissociative symptoms are especially frequent, as the ICD placement predicts. Carsten Spitzer and colleagues (1999) compared 72 patients with conversion disorder with 96 psychiatric patients matched for sex and age and found dissociative experiences, such as feelings of unreality, significantly more common in the conversion group; Karin Roelofs and colleagues (2002) replicated this with 54 conversion patients and 50 matched patients with mood or anxiety disorders.

Exam trap

Know the numbers in their right places. About 30 percent of patients referred to epilepsy centres have functional seizures by Barlow and Durand's figure (Benbadis and Allen Hauser's own calculation assumed 10 to 20 percent); about 5 to 15 percent of neurology clinic patients carry the diagnosis (DSM-5-TR), while Barlow and Durand's "about 30 percent in neurological settings" counts every symptom poorly explained by disease; the disorder is two to three times commoner in women; about one in five people with functional seizures also has epilepsy. Accepting the diagnosis predicts recovery; disability benefits predict a poor outcome.

Causes: Freud's four steps and what the evidence says

Freud described four processes in the development of a conversion symptom, and Barlow and Durand use them as a checklist against which to test the evidence. First, the person experiences a traumatic event, in Freud's view an unacceptable unconscious conflict. Second, because the conflict and its anxiety are unacceptable, the person represses it. Third, as the anxiety threatens to break into consciousness, the person converts it into a physical symptom, which relieves the pressure of facing the conflict directly; this relief is the primary gain that keeps the symptom going. Fourth, the person receives more attention and sympathy and may be allowed to avoid a difficult task or situation; this is the secondary gain. Barlow and Durand's verdict is that Freud was probably right on three of the four counts, and that the doubtful one is primary gain, although firm evidence for any of them is sparse.

Trauma and escape. Most often, people with the disorder have faced a traumatic or impossible situation that had to be escaped at all costs: combat, where death is imminent, or an intolerable family situation. Running away is unacceptable, so the socially acceptable alternative of falling ill takes its place; but falling ill on purpose is also unacceptable, so the motive is detached from awareness. And because the symptom does remove the person from the situation, it continues until the underlying problem is resolved. A study by Elaine Wyllie and colleagues (1999) partly confirms this. Of 34 children and adolescents (25 of them girls) with psychogenic non-epileptic seizures, 32 percent had mood disorders and 24 percent separation anxiety with school refusal, and most had experienced substantial stress: sexual abuse, the recent divorce of their parents or death of a close family member, or physical abuse.

Learning and the family. In another study (Wynick, Hobson and Jones, 1997), 15 adolescents who had had visual problems of psychological origin in childhood were compared with adolescents whose childhood visual problems had a physical cause. The functional group had experienced more stress and more adjustment difficulties, such as serious school problems or the loss of an important person, and they rated their mothers as overinvolved and overprotective, which suggests that the symptoms had been strongly attended to and so reinforced. Patients also tend to adopt symptoms they are familiar with, often from the real illnesses of relatives; a person who has nursed a parent through a stroke knows exactly what a paralysed arm looks like.

The weakest link: primary gain. The idea of primary gain is what explains la belle indifférence: if the symptom solves the conflict, the patient should not be upset by it. But as the evidence on la belle indifférence showed, formal tests do not support this. Lader and Sartorius found conversion patients as anxious and aroused as anxious controls, and Stone's review found no more indifference in conversion than in organic disease.

Social and cultural factors. Lower education, rural life and poverty raise the risk, and the decline of the classic dramatic forms over the twentieth century suggests that when people know more about how real diseases behave, the possibility of an unconscious "medical" escape narrows. In cultures where distress is more acceptably expressed through the body than in words (Section 7.7), a functional symptom may be the only language available.

The brain. If the symptoms are not consciously produced, something in the brain must be producing them without the person's sense of authorship. In 2010 the neuroscientist Valerie Voon and her colleagues tested this directly, and their study is told in the box below. A wider theory came in 2012 from Mark Edwards, Rick Adams, Harriet Brown, Isabel Pareés and Karl Friston, who offered "a Bayesian account of hysteria". In their model the brain constantly predicts the sensations and movements it expects. If attention becomes fixed on a part of the body, and a strong expectation forms (for example, that a limb injured in a fall will not work, or that a shaking will come), the expectation can generate the very symptom it predicts, and because the symptom was not consciously willed it is experienced as happening to the person. The model explains neatly why positive signs work: draw the person's attention away, as Hoover's test or the entrainment test does, and the expectation loses its grip and normal function returns. Barlow and Durand add that brain imaging increasingly shows strong links between conversion symptoms and emotion-regulating regions such as the amygdala. Whether these differences are causes or consequences of the symptoms is not yet known.

Key study: A tremor that does not feel like one's own (Voon and colleagues, 2010)

What makes a movement feel voluntary? Voon's group studied eight patients with conversion tremor using functional magnetic resonance imaging. Each patient's brain was scanned twice: once during the conversion tremor, and once while the patient deliberately mimicked the same tremor on purpose. The conversion tremor was associated with lower activity in the right temporoparietal junction, where the temporal and inferior parietal lobes meet, and with weaker connections between this region and the motor and limbic (emotional) areas. The right temporoparietal junction is thought to compare the brain's internal predictions with what actually happens. Normally, when you decide to move and then move, this comparison tells you that you caused the movement. If the comparison fails, the movement may be felt as involuntary. The study was small, and it cannot say whether the brain difference causes the symptom or results from it, but it added to the evidence (an earlier brain-imaging study by Sean Spence and colleagues in 2000 had compared hysterical with feigned weakness of movement) that a conversion movement differs in the brain from a deliberate imitation of it.

Suggestion and dissociation. Finally, the old link with hypnosis has survived testing. Charcot believed that only hysterics could be hypnotised (Section 7.1). Roelofs and colleagues (2002) compared 50 patients with conversion disorder with 50 matched patients with affective disorders and found the conversion patients significantly more responsive to hypnotic suggestion, with more conversion symptoms in the more hypnotisable patients. Put together with the high rates of dissociative experience, this supports the ICD view of these symptoms as a form of dissociation. The most defensible account is a diathesis-stress model (Book 2, Section 1.7): a vulnerability made of personality, early adversity, suggestibility and a model of illness, triggered by stress or physical injury, and kept going by attention to the symptom, the expectations around it and the reinforcement it brings.

Exam trap

Freud's four steps: trauma or conflict, repression, conversion (primary gain), secondary gain. The evidence supports trauma, escape and reinforcement; it is weakest on primary gain, which was meant to explain la belle indifférence. Conversion patients are not calm underneath (Lader and Sartorius, 1968). The brain finding to remember is lower right temporoparietal activity during a conversion tremor than during a deliberately mimicked one (Voon and colleagues, 2010).

Telling it apart from the conditions it resembles

The main differential diagnosis is a recognised neurological disease, and the discussion of misdiagnosis above has shown how to approach it. Among the mental disorders, the comparisons DSM-5-TR draws are each instructive.

Illness anxiety disorder and hypochondriasis. The two disorders sit in the same DSM chapter, and both are called "somatic", but they are almost mirror images. In functional neurological symptom disorder the person has a striking loss or alteration of function that looks neurological (a paralysis, a fit, a blindness) and, on the evidence of the examination, has no organic basis; the person may or may not be worried about it. In hypochondriasis, now illness anxiety disorder (Section 7.7), the person has few or no symptoms but a persistent fear or conviction of having a serious illness, such as cancer or heart disease, despite reassurance. One has a dramatic symptom and may be unconcerned; the other is consumed by concern with little to show. ICD-10 kept them in different categories, dissociative (conversion) disorders in F44 and hypochondriacal disorder in F45.2, and ICD-11 moved hypochondriasis to the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders.

Somatic symptom disorder. The two can coexist. Most somatic symptoms in somatic symptom disorder (pain, fatigue, bowel symptoms) cannot be shown to be incompatible with disease, and they need not be: what defines that disorder is the excessive thoughts, feelings and behaviours around the symptoms. In functional neurological symptom disorder, demonstrated incompatibility is required.

Factitious disorder and malingering. Functional symptoms are genuinely experienced and not produced on purpose. Definite evidence of feigning, such as a marked gap between the disability shown in the clinic and the activities observed at home, suggests malingering if the person's aim is an obvious external reward, and factitious disorder if there is no such reward. Section 7.9 sets out the full comparison.

The other neighbours. When a person has both functional symptoms and a dissociative disorder, both are diagnosed. People with body dysmorphic disorder are preoccupied with a perceived defect in their appearance, not with loss of movement or sensation in the body part (Chapter 6). The weakness of depression is a general heaviness of all the limbs, whereas functional weakness is focal and prominent, and depression brings its core mood symptoms (Chapter 5). Panic attacks can bring tremor and tingling, but with a pounding heart and breathlessness and with awareness kept (see functional seizures).

Exam trap

Conversion disorder differs from hypochondriasis in that conversion involves neurological-like symptoms without organic basis, while hypochondriasis involves fear of having a serious illness. The wrong options are wrong for clear reasons. Neither disorder requires delusions or hallucinations; a fixed false belief of illness would be a somatic delusion (Chapter 4). It is hypochondriasis, not conversion, that is defined by somatic preoccupation, so "conversion involves somatic preoccupation, hypochondriasis does not" reverses the truth. And hypochondriasis is often accompanied by depression but is not "always" associated with it.

Treatment

Treatment has changed more than any other part of this story. During the First World War, at the same Queen Square hospital where Slater later worked, Lewis Yealland (1884 to 1954) treated soldiers with hysterical mutism, paralysis and tremor with painful electric shocks combined with forceful suggestion, and described his cases in Hysterical Disorders of Warfare (1918); such treatment would now be regarded as coercive. Between the wars and after, psychiatrists used abreaction, helping the patient to relive the traumatic memory, sometimes under hypnosis or after an intravenous barbiturate (the amytal interview); these methods are taught as history in Book 2, Sections 3.10.5 and 3.10.8. Modern treatment rests on four elements.

Giving the diagnosis well. The first treatment is the explanation. The clinician names the disorder, explains that it is common, real and potentially reversible, shows the patient the positive signs (as Seema's neurologist did), and offers a model of the problem, such as the software fault described at the start of this section. Phrases like "there is nothing wrong with you" or "it is all in your head" do harm. The explanation matters because, as Ettinger's study and DSM-5-TR both show, accepting the diagnosis is one of the strongest predictors of recovery. The second part of this step is to stop what does not help: unnecessary drugs, such as the anticonvulsants Nazia had been given (withdrawn carefully, and only when epilepsy has been excluded), and repeated investigations that keep the search for a hidden disease alive.

Physiotherapy for motor symptoms. Because a functional movement works better when attention is directed away from it, physiotherapy for functional weakness and movement disorders retrains movement through automatic, distracted activity rather than effortful exercise of the "weak" limb. The evidence is encouraging but not yet decisive. In the largest trial to date, Physio4FMD, Glenn Nielsen and colleagues (2024) randomised 355 adults with functional motor disorder in England and Scotland to specialist physiotherapy designed for the disorder or to standard neurological physiotherapy. At 12 months both groups had improved and there was no difference in self-rated physical function, but more of the specialist group reported that their motor symptoms had improved (59 percent against 38 percent), and the specialist treatment was likely to be more cost-effective.

Psychological treatment. Cognitive behavioural therapy (CBT), which targets the fears, avoidance, attention to the body and reinforcement that keep the symptom going, is the best-studied psychological treatment (its methods are taught in Book 2, Sections 5.4 and 5.6). In a Dutch inpatient programme for 45 patients with mostly motor conversion symptoms, Moene and colleagues (2002, 2003) found that 65 percent responded well to a cognitive-behavioural treatment package, and that hypnosis, given to about half the patients, added little or nothing. For functional seizures, W. Curt LaFrance Jr and colleagues (2014) found that a manual-based CBT-informed psychotherapy reduced seizures by 51.4 percent, and by 59.3 percent when combined with the antidepressant sertraline. The largest trial, CODES, led by Laura Goldstein (2020), randomised 368 adults with dissociative seizures to seizure-specific CBT plus standardised medical care or to standardised medical care alone. At 12 months the CBT group did not have significantly fewer seizures, but it did better on several secondary outcomes, including how bothersome the seizures were, everyday functioning and satisfaction with treatment. In India, Srinath's group reported rapid remission of childhood presentations with a structured, intensive inpatient package.

Removing secondary gain. Whatever started a symptom, the consequences that follow it can keep it going, and treatment must change them. Eloise's story shows how hard that can be. Her mother found it convenient for Eloise to stay in one place most of the day while she ran the shop at the front of the house, so Eloise's immobility was reinforced by her mother's attention and concern, and any unnecessary movement was discouraged. In the clinic, with intensive daily practice and considerable support, attention and praise from the staff, Eloise learned to walk again. When her mother visited, she said she was pleased with Eloise's progress, but the staff noticed that her face said something different. She promised to continue the programme at home. She did not. Six months after discharge Eloise had relapsed and was again spending almost all her time in a room at the back of the house while her mother attended to business out front. Treatment must work with the family, not only with the patient.

Drugs. No drug treats functional neurological symptom disorder itself. Antidepressants are used when depression or an anxiety disorder accompanies it, which is often. The trials on functional seizures illustrate the point. In a pilot trial (LaFrance and colleagues, 2010) of 38 patients, seizures fell by 45 percent with sertraline and rose by 8 percent with placebo, but the difference between the groups was not statistically significant; in the 2014 trial, sertraline alone did not significantly reduce seizures, while psychotherapy with or without it did.

Exam trap

There is no specific drug for functional neurological symptom disorder; antidepressants treat the depression and anxiety that accompany it. The core of treatment is a clear, positive explanation of the diagnosis, then physiotherapy for motor symptoms, CBT, and removal of the reinforcement that maintains the symptom. Adding hypnosis did not improve CBT in Moene's trials. Abreaction and the amytal interview are history, not modern practice.

7.9 Factitious Disorder and MalingeringMedium yield · 6 Qs

Medicine runs on trust. A doctor who hears that a patient has had chest pain for an hour, or a seizure last night, or blood in the urine, acts on the story; there is rarely time to doubt it, and almost always no reason to. Every functional symptom in Section 7.8 was genuinely experienced by the patient, however puzzling it looked. This section is about the small number of patients for whom that is not true: people who deliberately fake, exaggerate or even cause illness. They fall into two groups that look alike from the outside but are separated by one question. What does the person get out of it? Some deceive for an obvious external reward: money, time off work, escape from military service or a criminal trial, a prescription for drugs. That is malingering, and it is not a mental disorder. Others deceive with no obvious reward at all, apparently for the sake of being a patient. That is factitious disorder, and it is. Telling them apart, and telling both apart from the genuine symptoms of Section 7.8, is one of the most delicate tasks in clinical work, because a missed deception can cost a child its life, and a false accusation can wreck a genuine patient's care.

Factitious disorder: the criteria and their logic

DSM-5-TR defines factitious disorder imposed on self with four criteria. Criterion A requires falsification of physical or psychological signs or symptoms, or induction of injury or disease, associated with identified deception. Criterion B requires that the person present himself or herself to others as ill, impaired or injured. Criterion C requires that the deceptive behaviour be evident even in the absence of obvious external rewards. Criterion D requires that the behaviour not be better explained by another mental disorder, such as delusional disorder or another psychotic disorder. The clinician then specifies a single episode or recurrent episodes (two or more events of falsifying illness or inducing injury).

Each criterion closes a door. The word "identified" in Criterion A is the most important in the definition. DSM-5-TR emphasises that the diagnosis rests on the objective identification of falsification, a doctored sample, an impossible laboratory result, a syringe found in a locker, and not on a clinician's guess about the person's motives. Nobody can see a motive, but a falsified temperature chart can be seen. Criterion B separates factitious disorder from deliberate self-injury that is not disguised as illness: a person with borderline personality disorder who cuts her arms in distress, and says so, is not presenting the wounds as a disease (DSM-5-TR draws this line in its differential diagnosis: self-harm without suicidal intent occurs in borderline personality disorder, but factitious disorder requires that the injury be induced in association with deception). Criterion C is carefully worded. It does not say there must be no external reward of any kind; it says the deception is evident even when obvious rewards are absent, so that it is not fully accounted for by them. ICD-11 says the same thing in its own words: the behaviour is "not solely motivated" by obvious external rewards. Criterion D rules out the person whose "evidence" of illness springs from a delusion, such as a man with a persecutory delusion who brings in a bottle of tap water he believes has been poisoned.

DSM-5-TR names four methods of falsification: exaggeration, fabrication, simulation and induction. A person might report depression and suicidal thoughts after the death of a spouse who never died or never existed; report seizures, dizziness or blackouts that never happened; add blood to a urine sample; falsify medical records; swallow insulin or warfarin (a drug that stops blood clotting) to produce an abnormal blood test or real illness; or inject faecal material under the skin to produce an abscess or into a vein to cause sepsis. Heating a thermometer or tampering with a temperature chart is the classic ward example of the simulation of fever. A real illness may also be present and made to look worse: a person with diabetes may manipulate her blood sugar. Psychological symptoms can be falsified as well as physical ones: feigned grief, hallucinations, memory loss or traumatic stress. And DSM-5-TR notes a modern variant, people who mislead members of their community, in person or online, about illnesses they do not have, without necessarily involving doctors at all; the American psychiatrist Marc Feldman called this "Munchausen by Internet" in 2000.

Where does the disorder sit in the classifications? DSM-III (1980) gave the factitious disorders a diagnostic class of their own, and DSM-IV (1994) kept them in a separate chapter; it placed "factitious disorder by proxy" in its appendix of criteria sets provided for further study. DSM-5 (2013) moved factitious disorder, now with "imposed on self" and "imposed on another" forms, into the somatic symptom and related disorders. Its reason, repeated in DSM-5-TR, is that in the great majority of reported cases people present with somatic symptoms and a conviction of medical disease, and that, like the other members of the chapter, the disorder involves persistent problems of illness perception and identity. Factitious disorder was never a somatoform disorder: the somatoform disorders of DSM-III to DSM-IV-TR were defined by symptoms that were not intentionally produced, which is the opposite of factitious disorder. ICD-10 placed it among the disorders of adult personality and behaviour (F60 to F69), as "intentional production or feigning of symptoms or disabilities, either physical or psychological [factitious disorder]" (F68.1). ICD-11 gives it a grouping of its own, the factitious disorders: factitious disorder imposed on self (6D50), which includes Munchausen syndrome, and factitious disorder imposed on another (6D51), with malingering explicitly excluded.

Exam trap

Munchausen syndrome is placed under factitious disorder, not somatoform disorder, anxiety disorder (Chapter 6) or sexual disorder (Chapter 9). The somatoform trap is the tempting one, because DSM-5 moved factitious disorder into the somatic symptom and related disorders chapter. But the old somatoform disorders were defined by symptoms not produced on purpose; factitious symptoms are deliberately produced. DSM-III and DSM-IV gave factitious disorders a class of their own; ICD-10 coded factitious disorder as F68.1, among the disorders of adult personality and behaviour; ICD-11 has a separate factitious disorders grouping (6D50, 6D51).

Who develops factitious disorder, and why: the sick role

If there is no external reward, what is the deception for? The usual answer is that the person seeks the sick role. The American sociologist Talcott Parsons described this role in 1951 (Book 2, Section 9.1.4): society excuses a sick person from ordinary duties and does not blame them for being ill, in return for their wanting to get well and cooperating with competent help. For most people the sick role is a temporary inconvenience. For a person with factitious disorder it seems to be the goal itself: the attention and concern of doctors and nurses, the care of family and friends, the identity of a patient, the drama of the emergency and the intense, if one-sided, relationship with powerful professionals. Clinical writers have suggested several roots for this need, among them childhood experiences of illness and hospitals as the only times of being cared for, early abuse or neglect, and an urge to master or control frightening medical experiences, but these remain clinical hypotheses rather than established causes. DSM-5-TR is careful to keep motive out of the criteria.

How common is it? Nobody knows, for the obvious reason that successful deceivers are not counted, and DSM-5-TR adds that even recognised cases are seldom recorded. Its best estimate comes from a study of general hospital inpatients in the United States referred for psychiatric consultation, almost 1 percent of whom met the criteria; the disorder is met more often in tertiary hospitals than in primary care. A pooled analysis of case series finds two-thirds of patients are women and one-third men. Onset is usually in early adulthood, often after a hospital stay for a medical condition or a mental disorder; when imposed on another, it may begin after the hospitalisation of the person's child or other dependent. The usual course is one of intermittent episodes, but in people with recurrent episodes the pattern of deceptive contact with doctors may become lifelong.

Two large studies show the face of the typical patient. At the Mayo Clinic, Lois Krahn and colleagues (2003) reviewed 93 patients diagnosed with factitious disorder over 21 years. Seventy-two percent were women; the women's mean age was 30.7 years and the men's 40.0, and the mean age at onset was 25. Health care training or jobs were far more common among the women (65.7 percent) than among the men (11.5 percent). Most often it was an inexplicable laboratory result that gave the game away. In 2016 Yates and Feldman reviewed 455 cases published in the professional literature. Two-thirds (66.2 percent) were women, the mean age at presentation was 34.2 years, and a health care or laboratory profession was the occupation most often reported. Depression was described more often than personality disorder (41.8 against 16.5 percent of cases that reported psychiatric comorbidity), and more patients induced illness or injury in themselves (58.7 percent) than merely simulated or falsely reported it. The commonest presentations were endocrine (such as hypoglycaemia from secretly injected insulin), cardiac and skin problems.

Why are health workers over-represented? They know which symptoms are convincing, how laboratory tests work and how to obtain syringes, insulin or anticoagulants. A 29-year-old staff nurse in a city hospital, for example, may be admitted again and again with dangerously low blood sugar, until blood taken during an attack shows a high insulin level with a low C-peptide, the pattern of insulin injected from outside rather than made by her own pancreas. It may also be that the same need for closeness to illness and care draws some people both to nursing and to the deception.

Exam trap

The motive in factitious disorder is to occupy the sick role (Parsons, 1951), and the hallmark is deception without obvious external reward. Prevalence: almost 1 percent of general hospital inpatients referred for psychiatric consultation. Two-thirds are women; onset is in early adulthood, often after a hospital stay; health care workers are over-represented. Factitious disorder and malingering are not mutually exclusive: DSM-5-TR notes that motives can be multiple and can shift.

Munchausen syndrome

Joseph arrived at the emergency department of a large Chennai hospital a little after midnight, doubled up with abdominal pain. He was 41, articulate, and gave a detailed history in confident medical language: he was a chief engineer in the merchant navy, he said, just off a ship at the port, with a history of "adhesions" and two previous operations in Singapore. His abdomen was crossed with old surgical scars. He asked repeatedly for a CT scan and said that only an operation had ever helped him. Over two days the tests showed nothing to explain the pain, and he grew irritable with the junior doctors. A ward nurse recognised his face. A few phone calls established that he had been admitted under three different names to hospitals in Bengaluru, Kochi and Hyderabad in the previous year with the same story, and that the shipping company he named had no record of him. When the consultant gently raised the inconsistencies, Joseph insisted that he was being insulted, and by morning he had discharged himself against medical advice.

Joseph's pattern was named in 1951 by Richard Asher (1912 to 1969), a London physician with a gift for memorable medical writing. In a short paper in The Lancet titled "Munchausen's syndrome", he described patients who travelled from hospital to hospital with dramatic, plausible and false stories of illness, submitted to investigations and operations, and moved on when they were found out. He named the condition after Baron Munchausen. The real baron, Hieronymus Karl Friedrich, Freiherr von Münchhausen (1720 to 1797), was a German nobleman who had served in the Russian army and was famous for telling extravagant stories of his adventures. In 1785 Rudolf Erich Raspe published, anonymously, Baron Munchausen's Narrative of His Marvellous Travels and Campaigns in Russia, and the baron's tall tales became proverbial. Asher's point was that, like the baron, his patients had travelled widely and their stories were both dramatic and untruthful. He described presentations of three kinds: acute abdominal, haemorrhagic and neurological.

Munchausen syndrome is now the name for the severe, chronic and wandering form of factitious disorder imposed on self, and it has four features that clinicians learn to recognise. The first is wandering from hospital to hospital and town to town, often under different names, which is sometimes called peregrination. The second is pseudologia fantastica, pathological lying in which elaborate, often grandiose stories mix truth and fantasy (taught as a sign in Chapter 2): the merchant navy engineer, the decorated soldier, the surgeon's son. The third is a striking readiness, even eagerness, to undergo painful investigations and operations, which leaves many patients with abdomens criss-crossed with scars. The fourth is what happens when the deception is discovered: anger, denial and sudden departure, often in the night. Krahn's series shows the last feature in numbers: 18 of the 93 patients left hospital against medical advice.

Two cautions keep the term in its place. First, Munchausen syndrome is the extreme, not the typical, form. Most people with factitious disorder are not wanderers and liars of Joseph's kind but people like the staff nurse with hypoglycaemia, who stay with one set of doctors, work and have families, and deceive quietly. Second, the word is a nickname, not a diagnosis. In DSM-5-TR the diagnosis is factitious disorder imposed on self, recurrent episodes; ICD-11 lists Munchausen syndrome as an inclusion term under factitious disorder imposed on self (6D50).

Exam trap

Richard Asher, 1951 (The Lancet) named Munchausen syndrome; Roy Meadow, 1977 (The Lancet) named Munchausen syndrome by proxy. Do not swap them. Munchausen syndrome is the severe, chronic, wandering form of factitious disorder imposed on self, with pseudologia fantastica (pathological lying with fantastic stories) and repeated hospital admissions. Some Indian coaching notes label the by-proxy form "Munchausen syndrome" itself; it is not: the by-proxy form is factitious disorder imposed on another.

Factitious disorder imposed on another

In 1977 the British paediatrician Roy Meadow (born 1933), then at the University of Leeds, published a paper in The Lancet with an unforgettable subtitle: "Munchausen syndrome by proxy: the hinterland of child abuse". He described parents who, by falsification, had caused their children innumerable harmful hospital procedures. One mother had introduced her own blood into her baby's urine samples; another had poisoned her toddler with excessive quantities of salt. Meadow saw that this was child abuse of a new kind, in which the parent used doctors as the instrument of harm. His later career as an expert witness, and the overturned convictions of mothers in cases where he gave evidence, are told in Book 2, Section 10.6.1; they are a warning that the label can be misapplied as well as missed.

DSM-5-TR calls the condition factitious disorder imposed on another (previously factitious disorder by proxy). Its criteria mirror those for factitious disorder imposed on self, with one crucial change: the falsification or induction of illness is carried out in another person, and the perpetrator presents that person (the victim) to others as ill, impaired or injured. DSM-5-TR adds a note that is easy to forget: the perpetrator, not the victim, receives the diagnosis. The victim may be given an abuse diagnosis, and if the perpetrator also falsifies his or her own illness, both forms of factitious disorder can be diagnosed. The victims are usually children, but they can also be dependent adults, such as elderly parents or patients, and even pets. In Britain the same pattern in children is called fabricated or induced illness, and in the United States medical child abuse; the old name "Munchausen syndrome by proxy" is now discouraged.

The methods are chilling in their ingenuity. Barlow and Durand, drawing on Check's 1998 review, describe one mother who stirred her child's urine specimen with a vaginal tampon obtained during menstruation, and another who mixed faeces into her child's vomit. Others suffocate children to produce "fits" or breathing pauses, poison them with salt, laxatives or drugs, or tamper with intravenous lines. DSM-5-TR adds that some parents falsely allege educational deficits or disabilities in their children and demand special attention for them. In child cases the perpetrator is almost always the mother. What makes the abuse so hard to detect is that she usually builds a warm relationship with the ward staff, who see her as remarkably caring, cooperative and involved in her child's care; she is often the last person anyone suspects. Check's comparison with typical child abuse (Table 7.24) shows why ordinary abuse screening misses it.

Table 7.24 Typical child abuse compared with factitious disorder imposed on another (Munchausen syndrome by proxy), after Check (1998) as reproduced by Barlow and Durand.
FeatureTypical child abuseFactitious disorder imposed on another
How the child presentsInjuries from direct physical contact, often found on examinationA falsified acute or "accidental" medical or surgical illness, not usually obvious on examination
Getting the diagnosisThe perpetrator does not invite discovery of the abuseThe perpetrator brings the "illness" to the health care system
Role of the childObject of frustration and anger, or of undue punishmentThe means of gaining the attention the parent desires; anger is not the main cause

How is it caught? Barlow and Durand list two helpful procedures: a trial separation of the mother and child, to see whether the illness stops when she is absent, and video surveillance of the child in hospital. The most important study of surveillance was published by Hall and colleagues in 2000. Forty-one children with chronic, puzzling illnesses were monitored by covert video during their hospital stay. In 23 of them the diagnosis turned out to be factitious disorder imposed on another. The video was required to make the diagnosis in 13 of these 23 cases and supported it in another 5. In one case a child had been suffering recurrent infections with Escherichia coli, a bacterium of the gut, and the cameras caught the mother injecting her own urine into the child's intravenous line. Two other findings of the study matter just as much. In 4 patients the surveillance established that the parents were innocent. And the "profile" so often taught, a parent who is excessively familiar with the medical staff, eager for invasive tests and has a history of health care work, did not reliably pick out the guilty: 55 percent of the mothers had a history of health care work or study, but these characteristics were not sensitive indicators and were never compelling enough to make the diagnosis on their own.

DSM-5-TR draws one more boundary. A carer who lies about how a child was injured solely to protect himself or herself from blame is not diagnosed with factitious disorder imposed on another, because protection from liability is an external reward. If, on observation, records and interviews, the carer is found to lie far more extensively than self-protection requires, the diagnosis applies.

The perpetrator need not be a parent. Between February and April 1991 Beverley Allitt, a state enrolled nurse on the children's ward of Grantham and Kesteven Hospital in Lincolnshire, England, attacked children in her care with injections of insulin and potassium chloride. Four children died and others were seriously harmed, and in 1993 she was convicted of murder and given 13 life sentences. Her case is often described as Munchausen syndrome by proxy, though her motives were never fully explained. Consider a version of the same story: a nursing assistant who secretly injects insulin into elderly patients to create medical emergencies and enjoys the attention that follows. There is no external reward, the illness is deliberately induced in others, and the satisfaction comes from the emergencies and the attention around them: this is factitious disorder imposed on another.

Exam trap

The nursing assistant who injects insulin into elderly patients for the attention the emergencies bring has factitious disorder imposed on another. It is not malingering (there is no external incentive), not conversion disorder (the symptoms are deliberately produced, and in someone else), and not, on this evidence alone, antisocial personality disorder, which needs a pervasive pattern of disregard for others' rights since age 15 with conduct disorder before 15 (Chapter 11). Factitious disorder imposed on another is distinguished from malingering by the absence of obvious external incentives; the motive is the sick role, experienced at one remove. It is not distinguished by dissociation, substance use or onset in adolescence. And the perpetrator, not the victim, receives the diagnosis.

Managing factitious disorder

Treating factitious disorder is difficult, because the person has not come for help with deceiving; they have come, as far as they will admit, for help with an illness. The traditional step was confrontation, and Krahn's study shows its limits. Of the 93 Mayo Clinic patients, 80 had psychiatric consultations and 71 were confronted about their role in the illness, but only 16 acknowledged it; follow-up information was available for less than a third, few took up psychiatric treatment, and two were known to have died. The authors concluded that confrontation does not appear to lead to acknowledgement and should not be considered necessary for management.

What helps is quieter and more structured. Once the evidence is secure (laboratory results, outside medical records, direct observation), the team meets, agrees a plan and appoints one coordinating doctor. Unnecessary investigations, operations and drugs are stopped, because the first duty is to prevent further harm. The findings are shared with the patient in a non-punitive, face-saving way: the doctor explains what the tests show, makes clear that the team will keep caring for the person, and offers help with whatever distress lies behind the behaviour, without demanding a confession. Coexisting depression, anxiety or personality disorder is treated in its own right; there is no medicine for factitious disorder itself. Regular scheduled appointments with the same clinician give the person contact with care without the need for an emergency.

When the disorder is imposed on another, the priority changes completely. The victim's safety comes first, and the clinician's duty of confidentiality to the parent gives way to child protection. The child is separated from the suspected perpetrator while the evidence is gathered, and the case is reported through child-protection procedures. These, and the POCSO Act that applies when abuse is sexual, are taught in Book 2, Sections 10.6.5 and 10.6.6.

Clinical link

A clinical psychologist on a general hospital liaison team is often the person asked, "Is this patient faking?" The useful answer is rarely yes or no. It is a careful account of what the evidence shows (inconsistencies between reports and observations, results that are physiologically impossible), what alternative explanations remain (a functional disorder, an undiagnosed disease, an anxious and inconsistent historian), and what plan will protect the patient and others whichever explanation is true. Record observations in plain factual language, "the thermometer read 40 degrees Celsius while the patient's skin was cool and the pulse was 72", rather than labels such as "patient is faking", which follow a person through every later admission.

Malingering

Sunil was 35, a delivery rider in Pune, when a car knocked his scooter over at a traffic signal. He was bruised and shaken, and an X-ray showed no fracture. Four months later, with a compensation claim pending before the Motor Accident Claims Tribunal, he came to a psychiatric clinic with his lawyer's referral letter. He said he could no longer remember his own address, could not count money, had constant headaches and could not lift his right arm above his shoulder. He walked slowly with a stick. On a simple memory test in which he had to say which of two pictures he had seen before, he chose the wrong one far more often than a person guessing at random would. A neighbour's phone video, later submitted by the insurance company, showed him carrying gas cylinders up a flight of stairs.

DSM-5-TR defines malingering as the intentional production of false or grossly exaggerated physical or psychological symptoms, motivated by external incentives such as avoiding military duty, avoiding work, obtaining financial compensation, evading criminal prosecution or obtaining drugs. The word comes from the French malingre, sickly or frail. Two features of the definition matter. Both elements, the deliberate production and the external goal, must be present: deliberate production for no obvious reward is factitious disorder, and genuine symptoms that happen to bring rewards are not malingering at all. And malingering is not a mental disorder. DSM-5-TR lists it (code Z76.5) in the chapter of other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention (Chapter 1 explains these codes), and ICD-11 codes it QC30, in the chapter on factors influencing health status, outside the mental disorders altogether.

Why is it not a disorder? Because there is nothing wrong with the person's mind that needs to be explained. A disorder is a dysfunction; malingering is a strategy, chosen to reach a goal the person understands perfectly well. It can even be sensible. DSM-5-TR gives the example of adaptive malingering: feigning illness while a captive of the enemy in wartime, which nobody would call pathological. Most malingering is less heroic, but it is still a matter of conduct and, sometimes, of law, rather than of psychiatry.

DSM-5-TR says malingering should be strongly suspected if any combination of four clues is present:

  1. A medicolegal context: the person is referred by a lawyer, or comes while litigation or criminal charges are pending, as Sunil did.
  2. A marked discrepancy between the person's claimed stress or disability and the objective findings and observations.
  3. Lack of cooperation during the diagnostic evaluation and in complying with the prescribed treatment.
  4. The presence of antisocial personality disorder (Chapter 11).

These clues are reasons to look harder, not rules for a verdict, and each has a weakness. Many people with genuine injuries are in litigation, because genuine injuries are what lawsuits are about. A discrepancy between complaint and findings is also the defining feature of functional neurological symptom disorder, where the inconsistency on examination is the positive sign of a genuine disorder (Section 7.8, positive signs); the discrepancy that suggests malingering is of a different kind, between the disability displayed in the clinic and the activities observed in everyday life, such as DSM-5-TR's example of clear evidence that a loss of function present during the examination is absent at home. Poor cooperation can come from depression, fear, mistrust of doctors or side effects. And treating antisocial personality disorder as a clue risks prejudging people because of their history. DSM-5-TR adds a final caution: malingering and factitious disorder are not mutually exclusive, and the motives in any one case may be multiple and may shift with circumstances.

Exam trap

Consciously faking illness or symptoms of disability to achieve a specific nonmedical objective is malingering. The distractors describe phenomena with no faking in them. Altered identities are the personality states of dissociative identity disorder (Section 7.6), which are not consciously produced. False identification is a misidentification of a person or place, as in the Capgras delusion (Chapter 2). Confabulation is the filling of memory gaps with false memories without any intent to deceive, as in Korsakoff syndrome (Chapters 2 and 10). Malingering is coded Z76.5 in DSM-5-TR (ICD-11 QC30) and is not a mental disorder.

Detecting feigned illness

How can a clinician tell a feigned symptom from a real one? There is no lie detector in psychiatry, and DSM-5-TR says flatly of amnesia that no test, battery of tests or set of procedures invariably distinguishes the genuine condition from the feigned one. What clinicians have are principles, and the most powerful of them you have already met in Celia's story.

Performing worse than chance. In 1965 Grosz and Zimmerman tested a man who appeared to have conversion blindness on a visual discrimination task and found that he performed much more poorly than chance; other evidence confirmed that he was almost certainly malingering. The logic, shown in Figure 7.14, is simple and very hard to defeat. When a task offers a fixed number of choices, a person with no ability at all scores at chance. To score far below chance, the person must know the right answers and avoid them. Sunil's performance on the two-choice memory test was exactly this pattern: a man who truly remembered nothing would have been right about half the time. This kind of forced-choice testing is the basis of many modern symptom validity tests, and of the validity scales of personality inventories such as the Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory, which Barlow and Durand report is highly accurate in revealing people who fake serious mental illness; the tests themselves are taught in the Assessment book. Forensic practice is covered in Book 2, Section 10.9.5.

Inconsistency across settings. The second principle is to compare what the person shows in the clinic with what the person does elsewhere: the reports of ward staff, the observations of family or employers, and occasionally, as with Sunil, recordings. A single inconsistency proves little, because genuine symptoms fluctuate; a consistent pattern of disability that appears only when the person is being assessed is much more telling.

Feigned amnesia. Claims of amnesia for the offence are common among people charged with violent crimes, especially homicide, and the psychologist Daniel Schacter reviewed how little was known about them in 1986. DSM-5-TR lists the circumstances in which feigned amnesia is more common: acute, florid dissociative amnesia; financial, sexual or legal problems; a wish to escape stressful circumstances; a desire to seem a more interesting patient; and plans for litigation over "recovered memories". But it warns that genuine dissociative amnesia can arise in the same circumstances and can coexist with deliberate feigning. Many people who malinger amnesia confess, spontaneously or when confronted. The genuine forms of amnesia are taught in Section 7.5.

Feigned psychosis. A person facing trial may claim to have been hallucinating at the time of the crime, in the hope of being found not responsible. The forensic psychiatrist Phillip Resnick (1999) stressed that no single sign settles the question: the decision is made by assembling clues from a thorough evaluation of past and present functioning, corroborated by records and other people. The clues that Resnick and James Knoll (2005) set out follow from one idea: malingerers describe what they imagine psychosis to be like. They may report hallucinations that are continuous rather than intermittent, vague, or always obeyed, call attention to their illness, and produce a sudden psychosis with an obvious motive, while lacking the less dramatic signs, such as negative symptoms and subtle disorganisation of thought, that they do not know to imitate. Simon McCarthy-Jones and Resnick (2014) added that because genuine hallucinations are so varied, it is safer to look for an accumulation of atypical features than to compare a claim with a single "typical" picture. DSM-5-TR's guidance is that in malingering with apparently psychotic symptoms there is usually evidence that the illness is being feigned for an understandable goal.

Feigned PTSD. Posttraumatic stress disorder is especially easy to feign, because its symptoms are subjective, its criteria are published, and it can bring compensation and disability pensions. B. Christopher Frueh and colleagues (2005) checked the military records of 100 consecutive American veterans who reported Vietnam combat in a PTSD clinic: 93 percent had documented service in the war zone, but only 41 percent had objective evidence of combat exposure in their records, and there was virtually no difference between those with and without documented combat on the clinical measures. The authors concluded that a significant number may misrepresent their combat involvement, although missing records do not prove that a story is false. The question of compensation goes back further. In 1961 the British neurologist Henry Miller described "accident neurosis", arguing that symptoms after accidents often persisted until compensation was settled and then cleared; in 1982 Mendelson's follow-up of compensation claimants, titled "Not 'cured by a verdict'", found that many did not recover after their cases were settled. Compensation and litigation can also maintain genuine symptoms: in one of Ettinger's studies of non-epileptic seizures, patients with pending litigation were significantly less likely to improve. Feigned dissociative identity disorder, including the case of the "Hillside Strangler" Kenneth Bianchi, is discussed in Section 7.6, and Ganser syndrome, with its approximate answers, is taught there as well.

Exam trap

On a forced-choice test, a truly impaired person scores at chance, a person with a functional deficit (Celia) may score above chance, and a malingerer often scores below chance (Grosz and Zimmerman, 1965). The four DSM-5-TR clues to malingering are a medicolegal context, a marked discrepancy between claimed disability and objective findings, poor cooperation, and antisocial personality disorder; they raise suspicion but do not prove it. No test invariably separates feigned from dissociative amnesia (DSM-5-TR).

Intention and motive: the comparison that settles the questions

You now have every piece needed to set the conditions of this chapter's somatic family side by side, and most of the distinctions between them can be settled by two questions. First, are the symptoms produced on purpose? Second, is there an obvious external incentive? Figure 7.15 places each condition on the grid.

Many textbooks add a third question, which gives a classic and still useful scheme: is the person aware of the motive? In conversion, neither the production of the symptom nor its motive is conscious. In factitious disorder the production is conscious (the person knows they are adding blood to the sample) but the motive is not fully understood, even by the person; they could not tell you why they need to be a patient. In malingering both the production and the motive are conscious: the person knows exactly what they are doing and why. DSM-5-TR deliberately does not build its criteria on these inner states, because nobody can observe them, but the scheme explains why the three conditions feel so different to the clinician. Table 7.25 brings everything together.

Table 7.25 The somatic disorders, factitious disorder and malingering compared by intention, motive, incentive and status.
ConditionSymptoms produced on purpose?Aware of the motive?Driven by an external incentive?Mental disorder?
Functional neurological symptom disorderNo: genuinely experiencedNo motive assumedNo; any gain is a by-productYes
Somatic symptom disorderNoNot applicableNoYes
Illness anxiety disorderNo (symptoms absent or mild)Not applicableNoYes
Factitious disorder imposed on selfYes, with deceptionUsually not fullyNo: the sick roleYes
Factitious disorder imposed on anotherYes, in the victimUsually not fullyNo: the sick role at one removeYes (the perpetrator)
MalingeringYesYesYesNo: a focus of clinical attention

The language of gain, defined in Section 7.1, maps onto the same grid. Primary gain is the internal relief of anxiety that Freud believed a conversion symptom brings by keeping a conflict out of awareness; it is the classic explanation of conversion, though the evidence on causes in Section 7.8 showed it is the weakest part of Freud's theory. Secondary gain is the external benefit of being ill: attention, sympathy, money, release from duties. Every illness brings some, so its presence diagnoses nothing (DSM-5-TR); what distinguishes malingering is that the external benefit is not a by-product but the whole purpose. Tertiary gain is the benefit that other people draw from the patient's illness. Eloise's mother, who could run her shop undisturbed while her daughter stayed in the back room, had tertiary gain from Eloise's disability, and it was strong enough to undo the treatment. In factitious disorder imposed on another, the perpetrator's need is met through the victim's illness, which is why the sick role is said to be sought "by proxy".

Exam trap

Four distinctions settle most questions. (1) Malingering versus factitious disorder: the presence or absence of external rewards. Not whether the person believes the symptoms are real (in both, the person knows they are false; a genuine belief in illness belongs to the somatic disorders or to a delusion), not severity, not age of onset. (2) Conversion versus malingering: conversion involves unintentional neurological symptoms not feigned for gain. "Conscious production of symptoms for the sick role" is factitious disorder; "intentional production for external gain" and "faked symptoms in legal proceedings" are malingering. (3) Factitious disorder imposed on another versus malingering: no obvious external incentive. (4) Factitious disorder versus the somatic disorders: deception. In somatic symptom disorder and care-seeking illness anxiety disorder there may be excessive treatment seeking, but no false information (DSM-5-TR).

The ethics of suspecting deception

Suspicion is a powerful act in medicine, and it can go wrong in both directions. Miss a deception and a child may be poisoned again, or a patient may undergo a fifth needless operation. Make a false accusation and a genuine patient may be denied care for years, because a note saying "factitious" or "malingering" travels through every later record and invites every later doctor to doubt the next complaint. The history of this chapter is full of warnings. Slater showed how often "hysteria" had hidden real disease; Gould's group found that women and people with psychiatric histories were the most likely to be wrongly labelled; Hall's surveillance study cleared four parents while confirming abuse by others; and the overturned convictions in cases where Meadow gave evidence (Book 2, Section 10.6.1) showed what happens when confidence outruns evidence.

A few principles follow. Suspicion must rest on evidence, not on a patient's manner, sex, history or apparent "type". The evidence should be gathered openly where possible and documented in factual terms. Covert surveillance of a parent is an intrusion on privacy and is justified only when a child is at serious risk, under the hospital's agreed child-protection procedures. Clinicians should consult colleagues before acting, because a second opinion protects both the patient and the doctor. The clinical role (caring for this patient) should be kept separate from any forensic role (reporting to a court or insurer), and the person should know which role the clinician is in. And even when deception is proved, the person remains a patient: someone who injects himself with faeces to stay in hospital is gravely ill, whatever the label in the record says.

Remember

Three groups, two questions. Not on purpose: functional neurological symptom disorder, somatic symptom disorder, illness anxiety disorder. On purpose, no obvious external reward: factitious disorder, imposed on self (Munchausen syndrome is its severe, wandering form; Asher, 1951) or on another (the perpetrator is diagnosed; Meadow, 1977). On purpose, for an external reward: malingering, which is not a mental disorder.
