  9. In factitious disorder imposed on another, the diagnosis is given to the , not the victim.
  10. Feigning illness while a captive of the enemy in wartime is an example of malingering.

Answers: (1) functional neurological symptom disorder, (2) dissociative, (3) Hoover's, (4) tubular, (5) 29, (6) five, (7) below, (8) Richard Asher, (9) perpetrator, (10) adaptive.

The case of Blanche Wittmann, revisited

Look again at Brouillet's canvas and the woman leaning back into Babinski's arms. You can now name almost everything it shows. Blanche Wittmann's grand attacks, with their epileptoid phase, their arc de cercle and their passionate attitudes, would today be called functional seizures (psychogenic non-epileptic seizures), one form of functional neurological symptom disorder (Section 7.8.5): episodes that resemble epilepsy but are not caused by abnormal electrical discharges in the brain, and whose strongest proof is a normal EEG recorded on video-EEG during a typical attack. ICD-11 would call her condition dissociative neurological symptom disorder with non-epileptic seizures and keep it, as ICD-10 did, among the dissociative disorders. That placement is Janet's legacy: Janet, who worked under Charcot at the Salpêtrière, read such states as désagrégation, ideas split off from a narrowed field of consciousness (Section 7.1.3). Charcot's own answer, a dynamic lesion of function rather than structure, is almost the way neurologists describe functional disorders now. Freud, who had watched these demonstrations and kept a print of the painting, went on with Breuer to explain such symptoms as physical reactions to emotional trauma, conflict converted into the body (Section 7.1.4). And the young assistant who catches her went on to give neurology the toe sign that bears his name (1896), one of the first positive tools for telling an organic paralysis from a functional one; the modern diagnosis rests on positive signs of the same kind, such as Hoover's sign (Section 7.8.3).

The painting also carries the warning that runs through the whole chapter. Blanche passed through the three stages of grand hypnotism with a regularity that later critics found suspicious, and Bernheim's Nancy school, and in the end Babinski himself, argued that the Salpêtrière's stages and attacks were produced by suggestion rather than revealed by it; pithiatism, Babinski's word of 1901, meant disorders that suggestion can reproduce and persuasion can cure. The same argument came back with the hidden observer, whose reports followed the instructions given (Spanos and Hewitt, 1980), with Sybil, whose case Herbert Spiegel judged "was hysteria", and with Spanos's sociocognitive model of multiple personality (Section 7.6.4); and la belle indifférence, the calm that was supposed to show that a symptom had solved a conflict, failed when Stone's team finally tested it (Section 7.8.7). Shaped by attention and suggestion is not the same as faked, however. Whether a symptom is produced on purpose, and whether an external incentive drives it, are the two separate questions of Section 7.9, and nothing in Blanche's story shows that she was pretending. Behind Charcot's patients, too, lay frightening events: his working men fell ill after accidents, sometimes trivial ones, and his traumatic hysteria leads through railway spine, traumatic neurosis and shell shock to posttraumatic stress disorder (Section 7.2.2). A clinician meeting Blanche today would ask first what had happened to her, look for the positive signs, explain the diagnosis as real and potentially reversible, and change the consequences that keep a symptom going instead of putting it on a stage. By 1905, working in the hospital's new X-ray service rather than performing in its amphitheatre, Blanche no longer had attacks. One woman's story proves nothing on its own, but it fits what Section 7.8 teaches about the attention and reinforcement that keep a functional symptom alive.

Chapter summary

7.1 Hysteria: The Common Root of Three Families of Disorder. Hysteria, from the Greek hystera (womb), was blamed by the Hippocratic writers on a wandering womb; Sydenham (1682) saw it imitate almost any disease, in men too. Charcot made it a disease of the nerves, a dynamic lesion, and from 1878 showed that symptoms such as loss of feeling could be produced and removed by hypnosis and suggestion; Bernheim's Nancy school called this ordinary suggestibility, and Babinski renamed hysteria pithiatism (1901). Janet (1889) explained symptoms as fixed ideas split off, by désagrégation or dissociation, from a narrowed consciousness; la belle indifférence is usually credited to him. Breuer and Freud's Studies on Hysteria (1895) made hysteria physical reactions to emotional trauma; Freud added repression, conversion and primary and secondary gain. Shell shock (Myers, 1915) showed hysteria in men, and Hilgard's hidden observer (1977) revived dissociation. DSM-III (1980) split hysteria into somatoform and dissociative disorders; ICD-10 kept them together as F44.

7.2 Posttraumatic and Acute Stress Disorders: Diagnosis and Clinical Picture. DSM-5 (2013) moved PTSD from the anxiety disorders into a new chapter, trauma- and stressor-related disorders, whose criteria require a stressor; ICD-11 has disorders specifically associated with stress. The lineage runs from Pepys (1666), Erichsen's railway spine (1866) and Oppenheim's traumatic neurosis (1889) through shell shock and Kardiner's The Traumatic Neuroses of War (1941) to DSM-III (1980). Criterion A requires actual or threatened death, serious injury or sexual violence, experienced, witnessed, learned about in someone close, or met repeatedly at work. Four clusters (intrusion, avoidance, negative alterations in cognitions and mood, arousal and reactivity) must last more than 1 month, with dissociative and delayed expression specifiers and separate criteria for children 6 and younger. Acute stress disorder needs nine of fourteen symptoms from 3 days to 1 month. ICD-11 PTSD has three core elements, complex PTSD (after Herman, 1992) adds disturbances in self-organisation, and acute stress reaction is normal.

7.3 Posttraumatic Stress Disorder: Who Develops It, Why, and How It Is Treated. Lifetime PTSD affects 6.1 to 8.3 percent of American adults, women about twice as often as men, and Indian disaster studies show the same dose-response gradient. Most people exposed never develop it: Saigh's Beirut students showed that close, personal exposure matters, and Breslau's conditional risks are highest after captivity or torture and rape. About half recover within 3 months, resilience is the commonest outcome (Bonanno, 2004), and PTSD raises the risk of suicide. Peritraumatic dissociation raises the risk; social support protects. Biology shows modest heritability, a dysregulated HPA axis, an overactive amygdala, an underactive medial prefrontal cortex and a smaller hippocampus, partly pre-existing (Gilbertson, 2002). Conditioning, Barlow's learned alarm, Ehlers and Clark's sense of current threat and Janoff-Bulman's shattered assumptions explain persistence. Trauma-focused psychotherapy comes first; SSRIs and venlafaxine are the first-line drugs, and debriefing and benzodiazepines are not recommended. Tedeschi and Calhoun described five domains of post-traumatic growth.

7.4 Adjustment Disorders, Prolonged Grief Disorder and the Attachment Disorders. Adjustment disorder is a disproportionate or impairing reaction to an identifiable stressor of any severity; no Criterion A event is needed. In DSM-5-TR symptoms begin within 3 months and end within 6 months after the stressor or its consequences end, so a continuing stressor allows a persistent (chronic) form; there are six subtypes. ICD-11 (6B43) adds preoccupation with the stressor and failure to adapt. The treatment of choice is crisis intervention with brief psychotherapy. Prolonged grief disorder, new in DSM-5-TR (2022), needs yearning or preoccupation at least 12 months after the death in adults (6 in children); ICD-11 needs more than 6 months. Shear's complicated grief treatment helps; citalopram does not treat the grief. Reactive attachment disorder (withdrawn) and disinhibited social engagement disorder (indiscriminately sociable) follow extremes of insufficient care, need a developmental age of at least 9 months, and respond differently to good care (Rutter; the Bucharest project).

7.5 Dissociation, Depersonalisation and Dissociative Amnesia. Dissociation, first given scientific form in Janet's désagrégation (1889), is a disruption of the normal integration of consciousness, memory, identity, emotion, perception, body representation, motor control and behaviour. Positive symptoms are intrusions (identity division, depersonalisation, derealisation); negative symptoms are losses (amnesia). Mild dissociation such as absorption is normal, but Waller, Putnam and Carlson (1996) found pathological dissociation to be a separate taxon. Déjà vu is not a hysterical dissociative state. In depersonalisation the self feels unreal, in derealisation the world, and in depersonalisation/derealisation disorder reality testing stays intact; mean onset is 16 and no drug has been shown to work. Dissociative amnesia is a retrograde, potentially reversible failure to recall important autobiographical information, in localised, selective, generalised, systematised and continuous forms, with fugue now a specifier. The organic amnesias, such as transient global amnesia and Korsakoff syndrome, keep identity and lose new learning.

7.6 Dissociative Identity Disorder, Possession and Trance. Dissociative identity disorder, called multiple personality disorder until 1994, needs two or more distinct personality states (or possession) and recurrent gaps in recall of everyday events. The host carries the legal name, the alters are the others, and a change between them is a switch. Thigpen and Cleckley's Eve (1957) and Schreiber's Sybil (1973) shaped the diagnostic boom that Pope traced as a bubble. The trauma model links the disorder to severe early abuse, corroborated by Lewis (1997); Spanos's sociocognitive model treats alters as roles, often iatrogenic. Loftus showed that false memories can be planted, while Williams (1994) found that 38 percent of women did not recall documented abuse. DID is not schizophrenia, bipolar disorder or borderline personality disorder, and it is treated with phase-oriented psychotherapy. Possession within accepted ritual is not a disorder; ICD-10 has F44.3 trance and possession disorders, and Ganser (1898) described approximate answers in prisoners.

7.7 Somatic Symptom Disorder and Illness Anxiety Disorder. The somatoform disorders of DSM-III to DSM-IV-TR had symptoms suggesting a medical condition without the physical pathology they suggest. Their ancestors were Briquet's syndrome (Paul Briquet, 1859), renamed somatisation disorder in DSM-III, hypochondriasis, pain disorder and Beard's neurasthenia (1869). Because "medically unexplained" was unreliable, DSM-5 defined somatic symptom disorder positively: distressing somatic symptoms with excessive thoughts, feelings or behaviours, typically for more than 6 months, whether or not a medical condition is present. Illness anxiety disorder is preoccupation with having or getting a serious illness, with absent or mild symptoms, for at least 6 months, in a care-seeking or care-avoidant type; ICD-11 files hypochondriasis with the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders and replaces most of the rest with bodily distress disorder. Warwick and Salkovskis's vicious circle (1990) explains the persistence, CBT is the best-supported treatment, and chronic fatigue syndrome has Institute of Medicine (2015), not DSM-5-TR, criteria.

7.8 Functional Neurological Symptom Disorder (Conversion Disorder). Conversion disorder, named after Freud's idea of conflict converted into a bodily symptom, is now functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder) in DSM-5-TR and dissociative neurological symptom disorder (6B60) in ICD-11, which keeps it among the dissociative disorders. It needs altered voluntary motor or sensory function with clinical findings incompatible with recognised disease; a stressor and the exclusion of feigning are no longer criteria, and there is no minimum duration. The diagnosis rests on positive signs of internal inconsistency, such as Hoover's sign (1908), tremor entrainment and the tubular field. The forms include weakness, astasia-abasia, globus, functional seizures (diagnosed by video-EEG) and functional blindness. La belle indifférence is not specific (Stone and colleagues, 2006). Slater (1965) called hysteria a snare, but misdiagnosis has run at about 4 percent since the 1970s. Treatment is explanation, physiotherapy, CBT and removing reinforcement; no drug treats the disorder itself.

7.9 Factitious Disorder and Malingering. Factitious disorder imposed on self requires falsified symptoms or induced injury with identified deception, presentation of oneself as ill, and deception evident even without obvious external rewards; the usual motive is the sick role that Talcott Parsons described in 1951. Two-thirds of patients are women, and health workers are over-represented (Krahn, 2003). Munchausen syndrome, named by Richard Asher in 1951, is its severe, wandering form, with peregrination and pseudologia fantastica. In factitious disorder imposed on another (Roy Meadow's Munchausen syndrome by proxy, 1977) the perpetrator, in child cases almost always the mother, receives the diagnosis, and covert video surveillance aids detection. Malingering, the intentional production of false symptoms for external incentives, is coded Z76.5 and is not a mental disorder; below-chance forced-choice scores suggest feigning. Two questions, whether the symptoms are produced on purpose and whether an external incentive drives them, separate the conditions.

From the past papers

  1. Jean Charcot demonstrated that hysterical phenomena such as loss of feeling in limbs or fainting could be produced by: (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Hypnosis (b) Suggestion (c) Both (A) and (B) (d) Relaxation

    Answer: (c) Both (A) and (B). From 1878 Charcot hypnotised his hysterical patients, and in the hypnotised patient a suggestion could produce loss of feeling, a paralysis, a contracture or a faint, and another suggestion could remove it. Hypnosis created the state and suggestion created the symptom, so neither option alone is complete. Relaxation, as in Jacobson's progressive muscle relaxation, lowers arousal and produces no symptoms at all.

  2. The term 'la belle indifférence' seen in hysteria was first used by: (RML 2024)

    (a) Sigmund Freud (b) Anna Freud (c) Pierre Janet (d) Hippocrates

    Answer: (c) Pierre Janet. This is the keyed answer and the attribution most textbooks repeat, so give Janet whenever he is an option: he described how hysterical patients seem untroubled by their paralyses and anaesthesias in The Major Symptoms of Hysteria (1907). Know the fine print too (Section 7.1.3): Stone and colleagues (2006) found that the earliest well-known printed use of the phrase is in Freud's Studies on Hysteria (1895), and Freud credited it to Charcot, so (a) is the option the historical evidence favours, but it is not the key. Anna Freud catalogued the defence mechanisms, and Hippocrates is linked with the word hysteria and the wandering womb.

  3. According to Freud, the term hysteria describes: (RML 2021)

    (a) Physical reactions to emotional trauma (b) A phobia of horses (c) An unresolved Oedipus complex (d) A tendency towards oral-type behaviour

    Answer: (a) Physical reactions to emotional trauma. In Breuer and Freud's Studies on Hysteria (1895) the bodily symptom grows out of an emotionally traumatic experience, and Freud proposed in 1894 that the emotional energy of a repressed, unbearable idea is transformed into something bodily, a transformation he named conversion. The other options come from other parts of Freud's work: a phobia of horses is the case of Little Hans (1909), and the Oedipus complex and oral-type behaviour belong to his psychosexual theory (Personality book).

  4. In view of most psychodynamic theorists, dissociative symptoms are produced by which of the following? (RML 2022)

    (a) Repression (b) Ego state (c) Conflict (d) Gender identity issue

    Answer: (a) Repression. In the psychodynamic account an intolerable memory, wish or conflict is pushed out of awareness, and the amnesia, fugue or conversion symptom is the result. Conflict is what gets repressed, not the mechanism that produces the symptom; ego states are Paul Federn's different idea, later the basis of ego-state therapy and of transactional analysis; gender identity belongs to Chapter 9. Remember that Janet's dissociation was a deficit theory, the mind too weak to hold itself together, not a motivated forgetting.

  5. The treatment of choice for Adjustment disorder is: (RML 2024)

    (a) Psychoanalytic psychotherapy (b) Group psychotherapy (c) Cognitive behaviour therapy (d) Crisis intervention

    Answer: (d) Crisis intervention. Adjustment disorder is a time-limited reaction to an identifiable stressor: in DSM-5-TR it begins within 3 months of the stressor and ends within 6 months after the stressor or its consequences end. Help is therefore aimed at the stressor and at coping now, through crisis intervention with brief, problem-focused psychotherapy. Psychoanalytic psychotherapy, group psychotherapy and CBT may each help some patients, but none is the keyed treatment of choice.

  6. In which disorder there is a change in the awareness of one's own identity and patient feel that he is no longer his normal natural self? (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Derealisation (b) Depersonalisation (c) Stress (d) Depression

    Answer: (b) Depersonalisation. "I am no longer my normal natural self" is the classic description of depersonalisation, the experience of unreality, detachment or being an outside observer of one's own thoughts, feelings, sensations, body or actions. Derealisation is the same unreality felt about the surroundings. Stress and depression are not experiences of self-unreality, although either can trigger one.

  7. Which term describes the experience characterized by a profound sense of disconnection or estrangement from oneself, often leading to feelings of unreality regarding one's thoughts, feelings, or sense of identity? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Derealization (b) Depersonalization (c) Dissociative amnesia (d) Fugue state

    Answer: (b) Depersonalization. Estrangement from one's own thoughts, feelings or identity is the self pole of the pair; derealisation would be the world feeling unreal. Dissociative amnesia is a loss of memory and a fugue is travel or wandering with amnesia, and neither is defined by feelings of unreality. In both depersonalisation and derealisation reality testing stays intact: the person knows that it only feels as if she is unreal.

  8. A client reports experiencing persistent and frightening experiences of their surroundings seeming unreal, distorted, or dreamlike. This symptom is best described as: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Derealization (b) Depersonalization (c) Deja vu (d) Hallucination

    Answer: (a) Derealization. Surroundings that seem unreal, dreamlike, foggy, lifeless or visually distorted are derealisation; depersonalisation is the same experience about the self. Déjà vu is a feeling of false familiarity, a disturbance of recognition memory, and a hallucination is a perception without a stimulus. In derealisation nothing is perceived that is not there, and the person knows the world is real.

  9. In hysterical dissociative states, all of the following are seen EXCEPT: (RML 2024)

    (a) Multiple personality (b) Fugue (c) Amnesia (d) Déjà vu

    Answer: (d) Déjà vu. DSM-II's hysterical neurosis, dissociative type, produced amnesia, somnambulism, fugue and multiple personality, the classic hysterical dissociative states (today dissociative amnesia, the fugue specifier and dissociative identity disorder). Déjà vu, the feeling that a new experience has been lived before, is a paramnesia, a disturbance of the sense of familiarity (Chapter 2), common in healthy people and also an aura of temporal lobe epilepsy.

  10. When an individual has dissociative amnesia it is usually associated with several types of memory disturbances. In contrast, in generalized amnesia: (RML 2022)

    (a) There is a failure of recall of the entire past week (b) There is a failure of recall that encompasses the person's entire life (c) There is a failure of recall that encompasses the person's childhood (d) There is a failure of recall that for traumatic events

    Answer: (b). Generalised amnesia is a failure of recall for the person's entire life history, often including identity. The distractors are the other forms: a circumscribed period, such as the past week or childhood, is localised amnesia; forgetting some events of that period, often the traumatic ones, is selective amnesia; systematised amnesia loses one category of information, and continuous amnesia forgets each new event as it happens.

  11. Which disorder is characterised by the inability to remember important personal information, often following trauma? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Schizophrenia (b) Dissociative amnesia (c) Bipolar disorder (d) PTSD

    Answer: (b) Dissociative amnesia. DSM-5-TR defines it as an inability to recall important autobiographical information, usually of a traumatic or stressful nature, that is inconsistent with ordinary forgetting. PTSD can include amnesia for part of the trauma, but it is defined by intrusion, avoidance, negative changes in cognition and mood and altered arousal, not by memory loss; schizophrenia and bipolar disorder are not memory disorders.

  12. A 28-year-old woman presents with periods where she exhibits completely different personalities, each with distinct names, memories, and behavioral patterns. During these episodes, she has no recollection of events that occurred while the other personality was present. She reports finding clothes in her closet that she doesn't remember buying. Which disorder is most consistent with this presentation? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Dissociative Amnesia (b) Dissociative Identity Disorder (c) Depersonalization/Derealization Disorder (d) Dissociative Fugue

    Answer: (b) Dissociative identity disorder. The vignette has both requirements: two or more distinct personality states, and recurrent gaps in recall of everyday events, shown by the unremembered purchases (inter-identity amnesia). Dissociative amnesia has gaps without distinct identities, a fugue adds purposeful travel or bewildered wandering but no alternating identities, and depersonalisation/derealisation disorder brings unreality without amnesia or identities.

  13. A person with multiple distinct personalities within one body likely suffers from: (RML 2025)

    (a) Dissociative identity disorder (b) Schizophrenia (c) Bipolar disorder (d) Borderline personality disorder

    Answer: (a) Dissociative identity disorder, once called multiple personality disorder. The "split" in schizophrenia, as Eugen Bleuler meant it, is between thought, emotion and behaviour, not a division into several people; bipolar disorder alternates moods that the person remembers; borderline personality disorder has an unstable self-image but no separate identities and no inter-identity amnesia.

  14. In dissociative identity disorder, the identity that is frequently encountered, carrying the person's real name is: (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Alter Identity (b) Host Identity (c) Ghost Identity (d) Mixed Identity

    Answer: (b) Host identity. The host is the identity most often met in the clinic and carries the person's real name; the other identities are alters, and a change from one to another is a switch. "Ghost identity" and "mixed identity" are not terms at all.

  15. A group of conditions manifested by symptoms and complaints suggesting the presence of a medical condition but without any evidence of physical pathology: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Dissociative Disorder (b) Somatoform disorder (c) Alzheimer's Disease (d) Parkinson's Disease

    Answer: (b) Somatoform disorder. This is the definition of the somatoform disorders of DSM-III to DSM-IV, which DSM-5 replaced with the somatic symptom and related disorders. Dissociative disorders disrupt memory, identity and consciousness rather than producing bodily complaints, and Alzheimer's and Parkinson's diseases are the opposite case: neurodegenerative diseases with clear physical pathology (Chapter 10).

  16. What is a defining feature of somatic symptom disorder? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Hallucinations (b) Physical symptoms without a medical cause (c) Delusions of grandeur (d) Obsessive behaviours

    Answer: (b), as keyed, with a warning. It is the only option that belongs to the somatic family, so choose it; but "physical symptoms without a medical cause" is the old somatoform idea, which DSM-5-TR explicitly rejects. The DSM-5-TR feature is one or more distressing somatic symptoms with excessive thoughts, feelings or behaviours about them, typically for more than 6 months, whether or not a medical condition is present. Hallucinations are perceptions without a stimulus, delusions of grandeur belong to mania and psychosis, and obsessive behaviours to OCD.

  17. According to DSM-5-TR criteria for Chronic Fatigue Syndrome (Systemic Exertion Intolerance Disease), which of the following symptoms is NOT a required criterion for diagnosis? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Profound fatigue lasting at least 6 months that is not alleviated by rest. (b) Post-exertional malaise. (c) Unrefreshing sleep. (d) Clinically significant weight loss or gain.

    Answer: (d) Clinically significant weight loss or gain. The stem has a false premise: DSM-5-TR has no criteria for this condition, and the criteria are the US Institute of Medicine's (2015). They require a substantial reduction in activity for more than 6 months with profound fatigue not substantially relieved by rest, post-exertional malaise and unrefreshing sleep, plus cognitive impairment or orthostatic intolerance. Weight change is not a criterion, in these or in the older 1994 criteria of the US Centers for Disease Control.

  18. Conversion disorder differs from hypochondriasis in that: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Conversion requires delusions, hypochondriasis requires hallucinations (b) Conversion involves neurological-like symptoms without organic basis, while hypochondriasis involves fear of having a serious illness (c) Conversion involves somatic preoccupation, hypochondriasis does not (d) Hypochondriasis is always associated with depression, conversion is not

    Answer: (b). In conversion (functional neurological symptom) disorder the person has a striking loss or alteration of function that looks neurological, with no organic basis on examination; in hypochondriasis, now illness anxiety disorder, there are few or no symptoms but a persistent fear of serious illness. Neither requires delusions or hallucinations, (c) reverses the truth because somatic preoccupation defines hypochondriasis, and hypochondriasis often comes with depression but not "always".

  19. The term functional blindness refers to: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Progressive loss due to optic nerve atrophy (b) Color vision deficiency (c) Blindness explained by psychological factors without organic damage (d) Vision loss due to cortical lesion

    Answer: (c). Functional blindness is a conversion symptom: the person cannot see although the eyes and visual pathways are undamaged. The other three are organic: optic atrophy (a pale disc and an abnormal pupil response), colour vision deficiency (an inherited cone defect with normal acuity) and cortical blindness (pupils react normally, but optokinetic nystagmus is lost and the scan is abnormal). On forced-choice tests functional blindness tends to score above chance and feigned blindness below it.

  20. Under which type of disorder would you place Munchausen syndrome? (RML 2022)

    (a) Somatoform disorder (b) Anxiety disorder (c) Sexual disorder (d) Factitious disorder

    Answer: (d) Factitious disorder. Munchausen syndrome, named by Richard Asher in 1951, is the severe, chronic, wandering form of factitious disorder imposed on self. Somatoform disorder is the tempting distractor, because DSM-5 moved factitious disorder into the somatic symptom chapter, but the somatoform disorders were defined by symptoms not produced on purpose, and factitious symptoms are deliberately produced. Anxiety disorders (Chapter 6) and sexual disorders (Chapter 9) have nothing to do with it.

  21. Consciously faking illness or symptoms of disability to achieve some specific nonmedical objective is: (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Malingering (b) Altered identities (c) False identification (d) Confabulation

    Answer: (a) Malingering, the intentional production of false or grossly exaggerated symptoms for an external incentive such as money, avoiding duty or work, evading prosecution or obtaining drugs. It is coded Z76.5 in DSM-5-TR and is not a mental disorder. The distractors involve no faking: altered identities are the personality states of dissociative identity disorder, false identification is a misidentification of a person or place (as in the Capgras delusion), and confabulation fills memory gaps without intent to deceive, as in Korsakoff syndrome.

  22. The difference between malingering and factitious disorder lies in: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) The presence or absence of external rewards (b) Whether the individual believes the symptoms are real (c) The severity of symptoms (d) Age of onset

    Answer: (a). Both conditions involve deliberately produced or falsified symptoms. In malingering the motive is an external reward; in factitious disorder the deception is evident even in the absence of obvious external rewards, and the motive is usually the sick role. Belief does not separate them, because in both the person knows the symptoms are false (a genuine belief in illness points to a somatic disorder or a delusion), and neither severity nor age of onset defines either one.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Dissociative identity disorder (called multiple personality disorder until DSM-IV, 1994) needs two or more distinct personality states (or an experience of possession) plus recurrent gaps in recall of everyday events, personal information or trauma. Distinct named personalities and clothes she does not remember buying is the classic vignette. It is not schizophrenia (Bleuler's split is between thought, emotion and behaviour), bipolar disorder or borderline personality disorder. asked 6 times
  2. Depersonalisation = the self feels unreal: detachment, or being an outside observer of one's own thoughts, feelings, body or actions ("I am no longer my normal natural self"). Derealisation = the surroundings feel unreal, dreamlike, foggy or distorted. In both, reality testing stays intact; déjà vu and hallucination are different phenomena. asked 4 times
  3. La belle indifférence, a relative lack of concern about a striking symptom, is classically associated with conversion disorder (functional neurological symptom disorder), but DSM-5-TR says it is not specific and must not be used to make the diagnosis: Stone and colleagues (2006) found it in a median of 21 percent of conversion patients and 29 percent of patients with organic disease. asked 5 times
  4. Malingering is the intentional production of false or grossly exaggerated symptoms for an external incentive (money, avoiding duty or work, evading prosecution, drugs); it is coded Z76.5 and is not a mental disorder. Factitious disorder (imposed on self or on another) is deception without obvious external rewards, usually for the sick role. The difference is the presence or absence of external rewards, not belief, severity or age of onset. asked 3 times
  5. DSM-5-TR PTSD has four symptom clusters: intrusion, avoidance, negative alterations in cognitions and mood, and alterations in arousal and reactivity (DSM-IV had three). Magical thinking and ideas of reference are not a PTSD cluster; "flashbacks, denial, anger, bargaining" splices in Kübler-Ross's stages of dying. asked 4 times
  6. Dissociative amnesia: inability to recall important autobiographical information, usually traumatic or stressful, inconsistent with ordinary forgetting; it is mainly retrograde and spares new learning. The organic distractors: transient global amnesia (identity kept, new learning lost for hours), Korsakoff syndrome (thiamine deficiency, confabulation), Alzheimer's disease and anterograde amnesia due to a medical condition. asked 3 times
  7. Functional neurological symptom disorder (conversion disorder): one or more symptoms of altered voluntary motor or sensory function, with clinical findings incompatible with recognised neurological or medical conditions, not intentionally produced (unlike factitious disorder and malingering). The classic picture is sudden onset after a psychosocial stressor, but DSM-5 no longer requires the stressor. ICD-11 calls it dissociative neurological symptom disorder and keeps it among the dissociative disorders. asked 4 times
  8. PTSD needs symptoms in all four clusters for more than 1 month; with delayed expression means full criteria are not met until at least 6 months after the event. Acute stress disorder lasts 3 days to 1 month after the trauma, with nine of fourteen symptoms from any of five categories; DSM-5 dropped DSM-IV's requirement of dissociative symptoms. asked 3 times
  9. Charcot (1825 to 1893, the Salpêtrière) showed from 1878 that hysterical phenomena such as loss of feeling in a limb or fainting could be produced and removed in hypnotised patients by suggestion: both hypnosis and suggestion. Bernheim's Nancy school called it ordinary suggestibility. asked once
  10. The phrase la belle indifférence is credited in exams to Pierre Janet (1859 to 1947), the keyed answer, not Anna Freud or Hippocrates; its earliest well-known printed use is in Freud's Studies on Hysteria (1895). Janet's own terms are désagrégation (dissociation, 1889), psychological automatism and the fixed idea. asked once
  11. According to Freud, hysteria is physical reactions to emotional trauma: Breuer and Freud's Studies on Hysteria (1895), with Freud naming the transformation of a repressed idea into a bodily symptom conversion (1894). Little Hans (phobia of horses), the Oedipus complex and oral behaviour are distractors. asked once
  12. Most psychodynamic theorists hold that dissociative (and conversion) symptoms are produced by repression; conflict is what is repressed, not the mechanism. asked once
  13. The treatment of choice for adjustment disorder is crisis intervention with brief, problem-focused psychotherapy. DSM-5-TR: onset within 3 months of an identifiable stressor, ending within 6 months after the stressor or its consequences end. asked once
  14. Déjà vu is not a hysterical dissociative state; multiple personality, fugue and amnesia are (DSM-II's hysterical neurosis, dissociative type). Déjà vu is a paramnesia, a disturbance of familiarity. asked once
  15. Generalised amnesia = failure of recall for the person's entire life history. Localised = a circumscribed period; selective = some events of that period; systematised = one category of information; continuous = each new event as it happens. asked once
  16. In DID the host is the identity most often encountered and carries the person's real name; the others are alters, and a change between them is a switch. asked once
  17. The somatoform disorders (DSM-III to DSM-IV) were conditions with symptoms and complaints suggesting a medical condition without evidence of physical pathology; their ancestors include Briquet's syndrome (Paul Briquet, 1859), later somatisation disorder, and neurasthenia (George Miller Beard, 1869). asked once
  18. Somatic symptom disorder (DSM-5-TR): one or more distressing somatic symptoms with excessive thoughts, feelings or behaviours about them, typically for more than 6 months, whether or not a medical condition is present. "Physical symptoms without a medical cause" is the old somatoform idea. asked once
  19. Chronic fatigue syndrome (systemic exertion intolerance disease) has no DSM-5-TR criteria; the US Institute of Medicine (2015) requires a reduction in activity for more than 6 months with fatigue not relieved by rest, post-exertional malaise and unrefreshing sleep, plus cognitive impairment or orthostatic intolerance. Weight change is not a criterion. asked once
  20. Conversion versus hypochondriasis: conversion involves neurological-like symptoms without organic basis; hypochondriasis (now illness anxiety disorder) involves fear of having a serious illness. ICD-11 lists hypochondriasis among the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders. asked once
  21. Functional blindness is blindness explained by psychological factors without organic damage; optic atrophy, colour vision deficiency and cortical blindness are organic. On forced-choice tests the functionally blind tend to score above chance and malingerers below chance. asked once
  22. Munchausen syndrome (Richard Asher, 1951) belongs under factitious disorder, as the severe, wandering form of factitious disorder imposed on self, not under somatoform, anxiety or sexual disorders. asked once
  23. Factitious disorder imposed on another (Roy Meadow's Munchausen syndrome by proxy, 1977): a carer, such as the nursing assistant who injects insulin into patients for the attention, induces illness in someone else; the perpetrator receives the diagnosis. asked once
  24. Dissociative fugue: purposeful travel or bewildered wandering with amnesia for identity, as in the man found in another city; in DSM-5-TR it is a specifier of dissociative amnesia. Transient global amnesia keeps identity. asked once
  25. ICD-11 PTSD sits among the disorders specifically associated with stress and requires an extremely threatening or horrific event; its three core elements are re-experiencing in the here and now, avoidance and a persistent sense of current threat. Complex PTSD adds disturbances in self-organisation. asked once
  26. Post-traumatic growth (Richard Tedeschi and Lawrence Calhoun, 1996) has five domains: appreciation of life, relating to others, personal strength, new possibilities and spiritual change. asked once
